&ldquo; And yet people speak in this working age, when they speak from their hearts, as if houses and lands and food and raiment were alone useful, and as if light, thought, and admiration were all profitless, so that men insolently call themselves Utilitarians, who would turn, if they had their way, themselves and their race into vegetables ; men who think, as far as such men can be said to think, that the meat is more than the life, and the raiment than the body ; who look to the earth as a stable and to its fruit as fodder ; vinedressers and husbandmen, who love the com they grind and the grapes they crush, better than the gardens of the angels or the slopes of Eden ; hewers of wood and drawers of water, who think that it is to give them wood to hew and water to draw, that the pine-forests cover the mountains like the shadow of God, and the great rivers move like his Eternity .&rdquo;&mdash;John Buskin.
PREFACE.,-&diams;&amp;gt;&mdash;,What is written and published periodically is apt to be laid aside and forgotten when the day or month of its appearance is gone by. I.put a few words of preface to this volume chiefly to remind the Teachers and Students in &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt;, that whatever interest they may have taken in the papers here collected, that same interest ought to belong to them, one, two, or any number of years hence. Besides serving as a medium of current communication, the Magazine may become of threefold use by its past issues being put into the hands of new students. Let me urge this very strongly as one of the best means of impressing on men entering our Colleges the principles on which they have been founded. Bv spending an hour over this little volume, a man will, I believe, learn more of what a College means than if he were to attend a class half-a-dozen times, without thinking much about the object which has brought him there. Tor he will here have put before him the &ldquo;differentia&rdquo; of a College, If he asks what is implied in that name, he will here find his question doubly answered : first, by the resolute assertion of the higher and nobler, as distinguished from the lower and selfish, ends of mental cultivation ; and, secondly, in the constant reliance on the principle of Human Fellowship acting in defiance of all class distinctions, as the true guarantee of healthy and effective work. He will find that there are working men who refuse to believe that the grand object of all learning is to raise some lucky genius, one in a thousand, above the level of the 999 who are not quite so clever ; though this poor fallacy has been preached to them almost daily for the past twenty-five years, in the leaders of popular journals, in the harangues of the friends of education, and the autumn rhetoric of philanthropic statesmen.,He will learn here what some men of his class have found to be the
sufficient reward of study, and can judge whether or not it be equal to that wherewith an ex-Chancellor of the Exchequer lately tried to stimulate the ambition of two thousand Lancashire working men, telling them that he, if he was an employer, should keep his eye on the lad who had just won the Arithmetic Prize, and perhaps advance his wages five shillings a week !*,&lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; will not be useless to the world, if they do no more than stand as witnesses against this contemptible imposture.,R. B. Litchfield.,See a late speech by Mr. Disraeli, at an aggregate meeting of Mechanics&rsquo; Institutes.

JANUARY 1, 1859.,INTRODUCTORY LECTURE ON THE STUDIES OF THE (LONDON) &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;WORKING&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;MEN&rsquo;S&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;COLLEGE&lt;/span&gt;.,&#39; BY THE REY. E. D. MAURICE.,{Delivered on the 21th December , 1858.),Several students have expressed a wish for some hints respecting a course of study. I believe our friends who undertook the Preparatory Classes have done something to meet the wish, more than we the original teachers of the College could have done. However, we, on our part, have appointed a Committee to consider how far it would be possible to draw up a general scheme of instruction ; how far a course could be marked out in some de&not; partments of study, if not in all. The Mathematical Section very speedily produced a course which satisfied the Committee, and I think will be very useful to the students.* In other Sections, as for instance the Historical, the difficulties are greater. But, as one of our body remarked, the greater the difficulty in any subject, the more help the students must need with reference to it. We fully hope before next Term to offer you some scheme which may be useful to you, even if it does not wholly content us.,The Report presented by this Committee contained a suggestion which I think at least as valuable as any regular arrangement of studies. It advised that there should be always some members of the Council to meet those entering the College as students, to answer questions respecting our programme, and to offer hints about the selection of lectures. I do not mean at all that we should pronounce such and such studies to be better for an ironmonger, such and such for a shoemaker. If we tried to make one thing fit into another after that fashion, we might forget the great principle of all, without which our College is worth nothing, that we are meeting you all as men , to give you the culture which will enable you to work together as men. But every one has certain capacities which point out the best road for him to this culture. Some may prefer to find it out for themselves : let them do so, by all means. Some may fancy we can give them help in finding it out : to those we ought to offer help.,The Magazine which we propose to bring out once a month may contri&not; bute something to this end. The plan was announced to you at a Students&rsquo; Meeting ; you proposed some changes in it, which were improvements. We were agreed, I think, that we should not have room for some of the ordinary contents of a Magazine. The few pages we can print must be devoted to business ; tales and reviews of books you must look for else&not; where. Our object is to establish a medium of communication between our teachers and our students, between this College and others of the same kind in different parts of the country. In general, we desire to begin with,* See post, p. 9.,в 2
a lecture on some topic of general interest connected with some of our studies, delivered either here or at any other &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; ; per&not; haps in answer to some question raised in the Magazine itself. For we wish that letters, especially concerning the difficulties or the wants of stu&not; dents in the College, should form another principal division of the Journal. You are too much occupied to make your letters very long ; therefore they will suit our space better. News from the different Colleges, lists of classes, etc., will occupy the remainder of the twelve or sixteen pages, all that we can at present afford for the twopence that will be charged for them. We do not suppose that the Magazine will be remunerative ; but if the experiment is not a ruinous one, it is worth making.,These lectures of mine will not the least interfere either with the scheme of studies which we are proposing to put forth, or with the Magazine. I have undertaken them partly in consequence of the desire expressed for that scheme, partly in the hope of throwing out hints which may lead to questions and answers in the Journal. I have felt, myself, that besides a plan of studies, I wanted to know what studies mean,&mdash;what they have to do with my life and the life of men generally. I may have an excellent map of a country, with every river and town and hill accurately marked, their relative distances faithfully expressed. But after one has studied the map, one would like actually to look at the river or the hill or the town, to stand near it and get into a sort of fellowship with it. I have often felt this when a great chart of human studies was before me. &ldquo; That is very good,&rdquo;,I have said to myself, &ldquo; very accurate and complete. But what has Logic or Mathematics or History or Political Economy to do with me ? Why should I enter,&mdash;who put any one upon thinking that he should enter, upon a course of it ? You can tell me, perhaps, how one part of this study is related to another part, how one rises out of another. But can you tell me how the thought of them, the need of them, rises up in a man&rsquo;s mind ? whereabouts they meet our daily business? &rdquo;,I am afraid some have not courage to propose these questions,&mdash;they sound so elementary. Half the ignorance in the world arises from this kind of cowardice ; from a man not fairly bringing out the difficulty that is in,him 5 _smothering it, lest he should seem foolish to others or to himself. I,am not speaking of working men more than of other men. This is the plague and curse of lawyers, doctors, divines. The lawyer is reminded every time that he goes into court, the physician every time that he visits a patient, the divine every time that he is consulted about a case of conscience, that what he is learning in his closet is connected with human life, and is good for nothing except as it is brought to bear upon hinnan life. Yet W e are continually tempted to think the two spheres wholly distinct ; that we are busy in our books with a set of technicalities which we must throw off that we&quot; may adapt ourselves to the circumstances of the people we have to do with in the world. So we get a set of clever tricky advocates, quacks who know the weak points of male or female patients, popular preachers who have certain arts of oratory by which they act upon mobs. These are despised by, and in their turn despise, the learned jurist, the scientific doc&not; tor the school divine, who seem as if they had no concern except with books, no power of conversing with men. We want all to be well cate&not; chized about the relation of our studies to the facts of the world. If you
working men put us to that catechism, you are doing us as great service as to yourselves. My friends and I have come among you in great measure because we want that help, and are sure that you, being engaged in the rough toil of life, will subject us to this inquisition, and will not let us go till you have wrung from us some not merely vague and evasive answers.,lou will ask me, why / undertake to grapple with these demands upon a variety of subjects? Just because I have been puzzled with these ele&not; mentary questions about the meaning of studies and their relation to life ; because I am still puzzled with them, and have not got a step beyond them, I may throw out a hint or two which would not occur to more learned peo&not; ple ; which their acquaintance with the details of particular studies, and with the compass and circle of studies, would lead them to pass by. My main qualification for being your Principal is, that I have no such acquaintance with art or with science or with languages as could make me partial to the professors of any one of those branches of study ; but have been com&not; pelled to ask each what help he can give which the other cannot, and by degrees have come to feel, very imperfectly indeed, but still perhaps more vividly than professors generally, what it is in me, because I am a human being, which craves for the special help of each.,I have another reason for taking this office upon myself, I have said that I, as a divine or clergyman, am exposed to the same danger as my legal or medical friends, of forgetting the connection between the studies of my profession and the business and the thoughts of men. I sometimes feel as if the danger were greater in my case than in theirs,&mdash;threatened more utterly the destruction of all I count highest and most sacred. At all events, I have been obliged to cast about for some protection against it And this is the one protection which has seemed to me adequate for mv necessities, has given me hope that all the efforts for educating the people of this country may not be vain. In the fullest, simplest, most practical sense of the words, I have been driven to acknowledge that God is the educator of His creatures, that we are only working under Him working successfully so far as we enter into His methods, failing so far&#39;as we are not able to discern them, or wilfully substitute for them some methods of our own. That there is a consolation in this thought, to one who looks out upon the multitudes who have either no school teaching or such as pro- fits them little, most will acknowledge. It is at least a reasonable faith that all the discipline of domestic life, even in its worst conditions the dis&not; cipline of work and suffering, the lessons of day and night, summer and winter, death and life, are brought, in ways that we cannot dream of to bear upon human beings and upon human societies. It is a reasonable faith, let people dwell as they will, let our own despondency dwell as it will, upon all the evils and contradictions in individuals and in societies h or how should there be any peace and order amidst these evils and con&not; tradictions, if there were not some Divine education counteracting them ? To me this thought has also been the explanation of those different studies which seem sometimes to stand so aloof from the ordinary transactions of men, then sorrows and joys. Each must be intended to form a part of this Divine education, because it must have to do with some side of that common human life ; and if this be so, then we shall be helped to perceive the different pomts of contact between them. What might be a desperate
undertaking if there were no living Guide and Teacher over men, becomes blessed and hopeful if He has placed us in an order which He wills that we should understand ; if we are under a government which expressly aims at awakening and cultivating every faculty and perception bestowed upon us. This is one of the lessons which has been strengthened and deepened in me by every&#39; reflection I have made upon the relations between those who teach and those who learn in the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;.,For this thought, if well weighed, brings the profouudest student and the most ignorant people into very close attinity, while it shows what bene&not; fits one may confer on the other. There is a book, of which many of you have heard, by one of the most accomplished scientific veterans in Europe. The author of it, Alexander Humboldt, unites the peculiar gifts of at least two great nations,&mdash;the earnest laborious search of the German ; the power of stating the results of his investigations with the clearness of the French&not; man, whose language he adopts. Englishmen will add, that he also had, at least in his youth, the enterprise and delight in personal observation which they sometimes claim as especially their own. This distinguished man, quite in his old age, but with his powers unabated, and still able as a youth to accept new information and discoveries, gathered up in this popu&not; lar book a sort of general result of what he had been learning, and called it Cosmos. Now Cosmos means, as many of you know, Order. Alexander von Humboldt would remind us that we are all placed in an Order, what we call a World, and would give us some hints for thinking about this Order, some help in ascertaining what we do know, or do not know, or may know hereafter about it. A great and honourable work for an old man to un&not; dertake, but one that frightens us when лее first hear of it. &ldquo; Here is a man,&rdquo; we may say, &ldquo; who has acquired more information about the things of which he writes than perhaps any of his contemporaries. What an im&not; measurable distance there must be between us and him !&rdquo; So far as that thought humbles us, I would try to cherish it in myself and cultivate it m you. &deg; But there is another thought which I would cherish with it. W &raquo;,,all of us are actually in this Order ; we open our eyes every morning to some of its greatest wonders. Here, at least, we are united both to Hum&not; boldt and to the poorest South American savage whom he encountered. Think what a world, or order, every woman who has a hut in the Andes is awakened to every morning ! I do not speak of peculiar wonders, of the o-io-antic forms of Nature which may present themselves to her eyes ; I speak ofthat which is common between her and the peasant-woman m Middlesex or Surrey. There is about them both the mystery of light and darkness, the succession of day and night ; a firmament over their heads wdnch that lieht makes visible ; the liquid and the solid, water and earth, ihe earth is&#39; 3 not barren as the waters seem, but prolific of life, sending up herbs, plants trees of every kind. In that firmament by day, one orb seems to o-ather up the light into itself, to dispense it; by its rising and setting, to mark the distinction of day and night. The night presents other visions which evidently do not come and go by chance,&mdash;which have to do with the same mystery of light and darkness,&mdash;which suggest, as each o&iacute; the others, thorn-hts of regularity and succession. All these luminous bodies must have to do with that peasant-woman in the Andes, or in Surrey or Middle&not; sex. But there are other objects nearer to her, and over which she can ex-
ercise control. The domestic animals which surround the one or the other, tell her of another kind of life than that of plants and trees ; she must speak of them by a different name. And her husband, who has bows and arrows oi a gun, hei brother, perhaps, who is a fisherman, tell her of other crea&not; tures like to these, flying over her head, or swimming in the waters be&not; neath. And then, strangest, most marvellous of all, there is herself and that husband, that brother, who look up to that firmament, are enlightened by that sun or moon, exercise powers over these creatures on the earth, m the ah, and in the water. An Order, a Cosmos is here surely ; the very one which Alexander von Humboldt would tell us of. For all that he can e us, let us be most grateful. But first of all let us remember that we are in it, have been put into it somehow, and that there must be some instigator of all our desires to know what each part of it means, and what we mean.,It was no wit of mine which put the facts together,-the common, mar&not; vellous tacts, of which tins Order consists. I brought them forward exactly m the sequence m which they are presented to us at the beginning of the oldest Book m the world ; not knowing any other so clear and good I should not have done so, if I had thought that book or that passage checked or prohibited inquiry into this Order, or any one of the facts to which I have alluded ; if I thought it began with settling all questions about light and darkness the years during which the earth has existed, the rela&not; tions of the sun and moon to the earth, the conditions and distinctions of animal and vegetable natures. I find there no dogmatism at all about any of these subjects. I find the facts grouped together in a beautiful succes- . I find, besides, the assertion that there is a living Author of this Oidei, who has not made it an order of death, but of life ; not a dead ma&not; chine cast forth from the hands of an artificer, but a mighty harmony of wing powers, to be renewed and quickened every hour by the Mmd and Spirit of a Creator. And as with this Order itself, so it is with this first lecord of it. It is a living, not a dead record. It contains the sug&not; gestion and warrant for all mvestigations respecting the laws of light &middot; the relations.of sky to earth, and earth to water; the earth&rsquo;s crust and all changes m it ; vegetable life, and the birth and growth of vegetables the motions of the heavenly bodies, and the earth&rsquo;s relation to tfem, alUb,iicties and 01 deis of animals, their uses, functions, habitations, their con&not; nection with the earth and with man.,Bins last is the real subject of that record. This explains why it is im possible that we should not ask questions about the earth and thİ heavenly,&Auml;C&#39;&Auml;&Auml; Ге у 10 а&deg;Г tľt f/,iľas^ominion!&#39;^ 6 If ^tlľat ^vere^l^hTndght^erlT ^ b &amp;amp;S aU f ma ^ salute, he pluck and kill and eat. cannot, any more than they, be satisfied with gazing at the beautiful ob&not; jects around him ; he must have intercourse with his own kind. Of Man it must be said, as of every other animal, Male and female created He them. And as they look up, so must he. To be a part of the same Order with them, cannot content him. There must be some Order besides, with which he has to do. It may be closely connected with their Order ; it will not, however, connect him with them ; but with the Being who made him and them, who made him in His own likeness.,Receiving this account of the Cosmos or Order to which we belong as authentic and Divine, I believe that there is a method in all human studies, that there is the highest encouragement to seek for that method, and to follow it so far as we have discovered it ; that it interests all human beings, and not a few learned people here and there ; that all human beings, so far as they can, have a right to profit by any light that learned men have received respecting it ; that that light must have come from the Source of light. With trembling indeed, lest I mislead you, but still with confi&not; dence, because we are pledged to help each other in this way, and because He who has laid the obligation upon us will help us to fidili it, and will turn our honest mistakes into means of discovering the truth, and not of hiding it, I shall try to ascertain what the studies in which we are engaged here have to do with one or another part of that Order. Seven or eight lectures, stretching through the whole of next Term, may be given to this inquiry.,1 should wish those lectures open to the whole College, and should like each to be the text for a conversation, either on the night upon which it is delivered, or the following one. I trust not to make them long enough to exhaust you, or leave no time for the dialogue afterwards. They will not be what I intend them to be,&mdash;they will not answer to the name Inquiry , which I have used in describing them,&mdash;if you do not take part in them as well as I.,With which of our studies, then, do I propose to begin ? Some of my friends on the Council, in trying to lay down a chart of our studies, found Art the province which it was most difficult to include, or at least to assign to its proper latitude and longitude. Now, from my point of view, Art presents itself before other topics. Why ? Because I am considering what each study has to do with you , how it comes into contact with your life. Now the words Artisan , Artificer , Artist , may be very distinct indeed. But they must have some close connection with each other, and it must be very desirable to trace out that connection, even for the purpose of finding out the difference. Trom this point, then, I should take my start. There is nothing, perhaps, of which I shall show my ignorance more ; an igno&not; rance not merely of the fine arts, but of the common arts ; not merely of what Mr. Ruskin is teaching you, but of what you are engaged in every day. But I believe I shall be doing you a service if I can tell you what kind of help I ask from both artisans and artists, and what aid I think each can give to the other. If I cannot add to your knowledge of your sepa&not; rate crafts, I may teach you to honour and reverence them as I do ; I may show you something of wherein their greatness consists, something also of their danger. I believe every artist,&mdash;in his measure and degree, every artificer,&mdash;proves that he is made in the image of the Creator, because he creates. I believe the great sin of every artist,&mdash;and, in his degree, of every
artificer,&mdash;has been, that he has tried to create, without having some true model to follow, and so has tried to create God Himself in his own image, or in the image of things that are below him. This has been the degrada&not; tion of the artisan, ot the artist, of the man. How this is to be counter&not; acted will be the question of the true artist, of the true artisan, that both may contribute to the elevation of man.,Men were makers and artists very early ; the Nineveh Marbles will tell us that. But a time comes when the artificer as well ns the artist begins to ask himself very seriously, &ldquo; What are these things which I am imita&not; ting ? Before I make, should I not find out what is made ? Must I not be a learner, before I can be rightly or effectually a doer?&rdquo; A learner? Tes. And is there not some way of learning, some mode of finding out &iacute;vhat these things actually are ? T his is an immense Order, in which we live ; but if we would get hold of some little corner of it, might that not help us to the meaning of the rest ? We call this method of learning Ma&not; thematics. Тош teachers in that branch tell you that the application of Mathematics extends from this table to the fixed stars. Perhaps they might go further, and tell us that Mathematics explain how we arrive at know&not; ledge in any direction. We connect them with Science , and rightly. They teach us how to search after that which is. They tell us that that which is will discover itself to us if we seek it. This, then, will be my second topic. My ignorance wifi not signify, if T can in anywise help you to seek for knowledge in the only way in which I believe it is to be found ; if I can teach you to reverence that which is, and to hate what is false,&rsquo; or that which is not. That is the true reverence for science, which makes us value every discovery, and hope for more. There is a worship of Science which is a worship of ourselves ; that, I believe, leads to no thankfulness for past discoveries, and will never lead to any. The more we connect science with human life, the more we shall shun that worship.,Both the artist and the man of science need words, though not as the chief implements of either. Our third lecture will bring us to those studies of which Words are the chief implement. First, we shall have to speak of Logic, which derives its name from &lsquo; word to consider how this study be&not; came necessary ; how we m&uuml;stafi be logicians in some way, whether we will or no ; how to study logic is only to find out how we are obliged to use words that they may convey a sense to other men. We shall hear what a tyrant Logic has become ; what need we have to watch that it does not rob us of all science, and make us the mere slaves of words ; we shall have to speak of the way in which Logic is connected with Rhetoric on the one side and Grammar on the other, and to consider how the study of Words (what we sometimes call Philology ) may lead us quite in another direction and may open to us thoughts about ourselves and about our fellow human beings, very deep and wonderful indeed.,For this Philology, or study of speech, leads us straight to the speakers to the natmus who have expressed their thoughts in different languages&rsquo; i he study of Languages is closely connected with that of Historv &amp;amp; T shall have to speak of both m my fourth lecture ; how the desire of a know&not; ledge of languages, and also for the knowledge of the life of different na tions, arises ; how this begins best from the knowledge of our own lan&not; guage and history ; how that demands other studies, such as Geography ;
what different topics it embraces ; how it looks backwards and forwards, connects us with the earliest races of the world and with races yet unborn.,\\ hat I have been speaking of is the history of Man. There is another subject, which we call Natural History, or the history of Vegetables and Animals, their growth and customs and ways. There is something in man, as the lecturer on Physiology will teach you, which is common between the bodies of animals and his, and something which is distinct. But there is a motto in one of our old public schools, Manners maketh Man ; there are habits, customs, ways, which belong specially and characteristically to man ; what we call Manners, Morals, or sometimes, from a Greek word, Ethics. Of this subject I shall speak in my fifth lecture.,History and this subject of Ethics will have taught us that men live in societies or cities, which have some resemblance to the societies of ants or bees, but also great and amazing differences ; so that we can understand something of the societies of ants and bees from those of men, but not much about men from theirs. So we are brought through History or Ethics to that subject in which all Englishmen have, and in which I wish you to have, the deepest and most practical interest, to Politics. This will be the subject of my sixth lecture.,You wiff see that in all these lectures I have been occupied with two subjects ; Nature, i. e. all in this Order or Cosmos which is not Man, and Man, And I believe we shall have been discovering the distinction between the two subjects,&mdash;that it can be only expressed in those words of the ancient llecord, that Man is made in the image of God. This will be the subject of my seventh and last lecture ; why I teach you Theology ; what that has to do with human life, with our natural and our human stu&not; dies, what justification it offers for them, what interpretation it gives of them. I am to show you how I suppose the study of Theology to be pos&not; sible ; why I think that no other study would be possible if that were not ; what I mean by a revelation of God to man ; why I think that it is that revelation which throws light upon all that artists, men of science, logi&not; cians, historians, moralists, politicians, think of or aim at. I must try to show you what that other Order or Kingdom is which the old Book speaks of when it says that we are in a Kingdom in which work and rest are marvellously united ; that every working man is intended in his work to be the image of his Creator, to find his rest in the House of his Father.,Applications for copies, advertisements, and other business communications, to be addressed to the Publishers, Messrs: Macmillan and Co., 23 , Henrietta Street, Covent Garden, W. UNIVERSITY DEGREES.,The (London) &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; has the privilege of sending its Students up for Degrees in Arts and Laws at the University of London.,The Examination for Matriculation at the University of London is held in July, in Arithmetic and Algebra (to simple Equations), Euclid (Book I.), Natural Philosophy (Elementary), English Language, English History to A.D. 1700, Erench or German, and one Greek and one Latin book,&mdash;for 1859, Homer&rsquo;s Iliad, Book У., and Cicero&rsquo;s &lsquo;De Amicitia&rsquo; and &lsquo; Pro Lege Manilia.&rsquo;,SCHEME OE MATHEMATICAL STUDY,,DRAWN UP BY THE MATHEMATICAL SECTION OF THE COUNCIL OF THE (LONDON) &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;WORKING&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;MEN&rsquo;S&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;COLLEGE&lt;/span&gt;.,The science of number and magnitude divides itself into the two depart&not; ments, commonly called Pure and Mixed Mathematics ; the latter being the application of the former to the investigation of the facts of the material universe. The aim of the course here presented is to introduce the smallest necessary amount of the first kind, and to ground on it the largest possible amount of the second ; to enable, in fact, the student to get the maximum of practical and scientific interest out of the minimum of abstract theory. With this intent, we give up, at least for the present, the idea of introducing the higher or differential calculus. The uses of Mathematics as a part of an educational method may be thoroughly attained without it, though per&not; haps we may have from time to time students who will pursue the subject by themselves. The scheme here given is such (from the natural connec&not; tion between its parts) that however far it is followed the results obtained up to that point are complete, substantial, and coherent.,In the elementary subjects, fresh classes are formed twice in the year ; in some of the higher ones their formation must depend on the number of students wishing to join the particular classes. Hitherto we have always had a higher Mathematical class, reading some subject from Trigonometry upwards, to the Principia. &deg; J,The order of the following arrangement must be understood subject to the optional variations hinted at in the table. In some cases a wider in&not; terest may be given to a subject by previous reading, which is at the same time not absolutely necessary. Others again are independent applica&not; tions of the elements of Mathematics ; and again it may be occasionally in&not; teresting to expand a subject beyond the minimum required as an introduc&not; tion to further progress.,The books mentioned are chiefly text-books used in the College. Most of our Mathematical work will be done in connection with classes &middot; and students reading by themselves or in a manner supplementary to the class- work, can always obtain special advice on the selection of books from the teachers.,I have quoted &lsquo; Goodwin&rsquo;s Course &rsquo; as a handy book for showing at a glance the extent of each subject, and as presenting in some cases the best manual.

Remarks.,To know Arithmetic thoroughly without the aid of Algebra (which includes it) is very difficult, if not impossible. The easiest plan is to learn Arithmetic, and afterwards to complete and consolidate the knowledge of it by reading Algebra. The reasoning about numbers is of one kind only, and therefore the same in both, the Algebraic form being the more simple and comprehensive.,Books I., II., III., IV., and part of VI., hut the last may he easily read out of the class. Certificates given for first four.,With a good class comes into four Terms. The introductory class in the Long Vacation very useful. The work has been occasionally continued in the vacation, and the teachers are always able to set papers, etc., after the conclusion of the course.&mdash;Alge&not; bra = Universal Arithmetic. Solution of problems by equations.,Measurement of triangles, heights, and distances. (Surveying.),Conic Sections are curves made by cutting a cone. They are of great importance, as they are the paths described by all planets, comets, etc. The reasoning quite the same as in Euclid,&mdash;an extension in fact of the geometry of lines, triangles, and circles, to the cases of an Ellipse, Parabola, and Hyperbola.,Theory of the Equilibrium of bodies acted on by forces. Machines. (Problems in&not; volving weights and resistances.),Theory of the Motion of bodies acted on by forces. [Three Laws of Motion : the basis of all Physical Astronomy.] Very little Algebra and Trigonometry wanted, but a familiarity with simple equations indispensable.,A short subject,&mdash;the Equilibrium of Fluids. Solution of problems about floating bodies. Machines involving the action of fluids. Air-pump. Barometer. Steam- engine. Reasoning very simple,&mdash;all founded on one principle.,Proof of the theory of the motions of the Moon, Planets, etc. Why the Earth goes in an Ellipse round the Sun. Connection between times of revolution, distances, etc., of heavenly bodies. Requires a sound understanding of the laws of motion. Calcu&not; lation not used, except as an incidental aid to Newton&rsquo;s reasoning.,An interesting introduction to Astronomy, being the theory of Optical instruments,&mdash; Telescopes, Microscopes, etc., and the Human Eye. Algebra and Geometry required only of the simplest kind, but Conic Sections assist in one or two points.,Astronomy may be made a purely &ldquo;popular&rdquo; subject, i.e. almost divested of Mathematics, and yet have abundant interest. But the knowledge thus got can be but partial. Geometry is the main thing required to explain the lines of motion of the heavenly bodies. Newton&rsquo;s Principia gives the mechanics of the motions, i.e. explains why they are what they are, and so adds much to the interest of the subject.

The following are its rules, which indicate a somewhat stricter discipline (attributable no doubt to the earlier age of admission) than has been found necessary in other &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt;.,Rule 1st.&mdash;The Students are divided, for the present, into Two Principal Sections, the Elementary and Intermediate.,2nd.&mdash;The Elementary Section is divided into Senior and Junior Classes ; and an upper division is formed, as preparatory to the Intermediate Section.,3rd.&mdash;Students in the Elementary Sec&not; tion are required to attend regularly and punctually; and in case of violating this rule are liable to be suspended or expelled by the Council.,4th.&mdash;No person under fifteen years of age can beadmitted a Studeutof theCollege.,5th.&mdash;In admission into the Interme&not; diate Section, previous examination, to the satisfaction of the Teacher, is necessary.,6th.&mdash;Students in the Intermediate Sec&not; tion absenting themselves, or being late in attendance, without cause which the Coun&not; cil may deem satisfactory, to be suspended during the pleasure of the Council.,The subjects taught in the &ldquo; Elementary Section&rdquo; are, in the first rudimentary classes, Phonetic reading for adults unable to read. 1st division, pupils from 15 to 18 years of age, reading, rvritiug, and arithme&not; tic. 2nd division, pupils above 18, the same studies. In the upper rudimentary classes, English grammar and composition, history, and geography. In the Interme&not; diate Section, arithmetic, algebra, and men&not; suration ; English composition and litera&not; ture ; German ; French ; Phonetic short&not; hand ; Latin ; Euclid ; and elementary free hand drawing and mechanical drawing. EEBE&Uuml;AEY 1, 1859.,EEPOET OE THE HISTOEICAL SECTION OE THE COUNCIL OF THE (LONDON) WOEKING MEN&rsquo;S COLLEGE, ON A COUESE OE ENGLISH HISTOEY.,1.It is proposed that there shall be two sets of Lectures in the College on this subject :&mdash;I. General ; II. Specific. The General lectures shall give a general view of the entire subject, from the time when the island was visited by C&aelig;sar, to the present reign. These shall be completed in one Term. Without dictating to the Lecturer what method he shall pursue, it is thought that by dividing the entire period to be travelled over into the following epochs, the task here proposed may easily be accom&not; plished. These epochs will be the Eoman, Anglo-Saxon, Norman, Plan&not; tagenet, Tudor, Stuart, Hanoverian; forming the materials for seven lectures, unless the Lecturer wishes to divide the Plantagenet into Plan&not; tagenet proper and Lancastrian; thus filling up the eight lectures into which the Term is divided.,It is very desirable that these lectures should be repeated every other Term. They may be equally useful to the uninitiated and to the mature student.,2.-The Special lectures will follow the division here marked out in the General lecture, with this difference, that in them each of the subjects will be expanded into eight lectures, which the general Lecturer has treated of in one. And as it will be the object of the general Lecturer to dwell only on the more salient characteristics of those epochs which come before him, and their relations to each other and the whole subject, the special Lecturer will work out each epoch more minutely, and with closer reference to its individual character.,Dividing the special lectures into six, we shall have for each consecutive Term the following portions of English History taught in as many Terms : 1. Erom the coming of C&aelig;sar to the Norman Conquest. 3. Norman Period. 3. Plantagenet. 4. Tudor. 5. Stuart. 6. Hanoverian.,By these means every Lecturer and every student will know precisely what portion of English History will form&#39;the subject of the lectures in any given Term ; and if he does not enter in the first Academical Term, he will know what portion he must get up to join the Lecturer at a future Term. All special courses and single lectures may be made subservient to this arrangement.,3. It is proposed that the Modern History course shall be arranged in relation to the same divisions ; so that a student attending the English lectures of any one period, and desirous of obtaining a more complete insight into the general European History of that period than the special English Lecturer is able to give, may enter the Modern History course, and thus study the same period from two different standing-grounds. Thus, during,c 2
the period that the English Lecturer is travelling through the first epoch, from the Roman to the Norman invader, the Modern History Lecturer might, by some such division of his subject as the following, move with him pari passu : &mdash;1. Rome under the Emperors. 2. Constantine and Con&not; stantinople. 3. The Barbarians, 4. The fall of the West. 5. Justinian. G. Gregory the Great. 7. Mahomet. 8. Charlemagne and the Carlo- vingians.,5. The lectures on English Grammar and Literature might wisely follow the same plan, and be included in six Terms. Supposing that whilst the English Lecturer was employed upon the period of history from the begin&not; ning to the Norman Conquest, the Lecturer on English Grammar would be employed in explaining to his pupils those elements of the language which first make their appearance during this epoch, illustrating them, if he think proper, by the writers of the time, e.g. the Keltic&mdash;Classical and Ecclesiastical Latin&mdash;Anglo-Saxon&mdash;fusion of all, etc. It will be for him to consider whether he will divide the next period into Norman and Plantagenet, or treat it as one. In the Tudor epoch he will take up the Tudor writers ; in the Stuart, the Stuart, and so on. But it is very desirable that the Lectures on English History, and English Grammar and Literature should be brought into close contact, and serve for mutual illustration.,It will be for the Lecturer on English Grammar and Literature to con&not; sider whether it will be advisable to have for English Grammar, or rather the English Language, a General Lecture corresponding to the General Lecture in English History ; so giving a brief outline of his subject in its full extent, within the period of one Term.,The Lecturer on Constitutional History will, as far as practicable, adopt the same divisions.,J. S, Brewer.,. Thomas Randle Bennett.,Frederic Harrison.,F. D. Maurice.,NEW REGULATIONS OF THE UNIVERSITY OF LONDON FOR MATRICULATION IN ARTS.,There shall be two Examinations * for Matriculation in each year ; one commencing on the second Monday in January, and the other on the first Monday in July.,No Candidate shall be admitted to the Matriculation Examination, unless he have produced a Certificate showing that he has completed his Sixteenth year.,This Certificate f shall be transmitted to the Registrar at least fourteen days before the commencement of the Examination.,* The Examinations will he held at Burlington House, London ; and also, under special arrangement, in other Cities, Towns, and Colleges of the United Kingdom. f A certified copy from the Baptismal Register, or a Certificate from the Registrar General in London, or from the Superintendent Registrar of the District, is expected.
A Fee of Two Pounds shall be paid at Matriculation. No Candidate shall be admitted to the Examination, unless he have previously paid this Fee to the Registrar. If a Candidate fail to pass the Examination, the Fee shall not be returned to him, but he shall be admitted to any sub&not; sequent Examination for Matriculation without the payment of any addi&not; tional Fee, provided that he give notice of his intention to the Registrar at least fourteen days before the commencement of the Examination.,The Examination shall be conducted by means of Printed Papers ; but the Examiners shall not be precluded from putting, for the purpose of ascertaining the competence of the Candidates to pass, viva voce questions to any Candidate, in the subjects in which they are appointed to examine.,Candidates for the Matriculation Examination shall be examined in the following subjects :&mdash;,Mathematics. &mdash; Arithmetic and Algebra. The ordinary Rules of Arithmetic. Vulgar and Decimal Fractions. Extraction of the Square Root. Addition, Subtraction, Multiplication, and Division of Algebraical Quantities. Proportion. Arithmetical and Geometrical Progression, Simple Equations.&mdash; -Geometry. The First Four Books of Euclid or, The principal properties of Triangles, and of Squares, and other Parallelo&not; grams, treated geometrically ; The principal properties of the Circle, and of its inscribed and circumscribed figures, treated geometrically.,Natural Philosophy.* &mdash; Mechanics. Explain the Composition and Resolution of Statical Forces. Describe the Simple Machines ( Mechanical Powers), and state the Ratio of the power to the Weight in each. Define the Centre of Gravity. Give the General Laws of Motion, and describe the chief experiments by which they may be illustrated. State the Law of the Motion of Falling Bodies.- Hydrostatics , Hydraulics, and Pneu&not;,matics. _ Explain the Pressure of Liquids and Gases, its equal diffusion, and variation with the depth. Define Specific Gravity, and show how the specific gravity of bodies may be ascertained. Describe and explain the Barometer, the Siphon, the Common Pump and Forcing Pump, and the,Air-Pump. Acoustics.. Describe the nature of Sound.- Optics.,State the Laws of Reflection and Refraction. Explain the formation of Images by Simple Lenses.,Chemistry.&mdash; Powers of Matter. Aggregation, crystallization, chemi&not; cal affinity, definite equivalents. Heat. Natural and artificial sources &middot; its effects. Expansion; solids, liquids, gases. Thermometer; conduction; radiation; capacity; change of form; liquefaction; steam. Combustion, llame; nature of ordinary fuel; chief results of combustion, i. e. the bodies produced. The Atmosphere. Its general nature and condition&middot; its component parts. Oxygen and Nitrogen; their properties. Water and Carbonic Acid. Proportions of these substances in the air. Chlorine and Iodine as compared with Oxygen. Water. Its general relation to the atmosphere and earth; its natural states and relative purity Sea&not; water, river-water, spring-water, rain-water. Pure water : effects of heat and cold on it; its compound nature; its elements. Hydrogen. Its nature and proportion in water ; its presence in most ordinary fuels &middot; its Product when burnt. Sulphur, Phosphorus, and Carbon generally. Nitric,, * bovvledge required of these subjects in Natural Philosophy will be such as may be attained by attending a Course of Experimental Lectures. ^

Ovid, one book of the Metamorphoses, and one book of the Epistles or Heroides.,The papers in Classics shall contain passages of the ancient Authors to be translated into English, and simple and easy sentences of English to be translated into Latin. and sent down expressly for the occasion ; who will be responsible to the Senate for the proper opening of the Papers, for the conduct of the Exami&not; nation, and for the collection and sealing-up of the answers at the close of each Examination The answers will be brought back to London in sealed packets, so as to be reviewed by the Examiners in London at the same time with the answers of the London candidates; and the total results (including the local as well as the central) will be made known by the Examiners in London on the same day as they are at present.,Applications from any City, Town, or College, desiring the institution of Provincial Examinations in the present year, must be transmitted to the Registrar before May 1st.,REPORT OF THE SALFORD &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;WORKING&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;MEN&#39;S&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;COLLEGE&lt;/span&gt;.,{As read at the Meeting of January 5, 1859.),This Institution has now been in operation during two Terms. It was established to afford to working men the opportunity of advanced culture, and to help those whose early education had been neglected to acquire the rudiments of knowledge, and to enter upon the work of self-culture. It aimed to establish also a point of union for working men, where the cultivation of social feeling should go hand in hand with the pursuit of knowledge.,Its promoters felt that success would greatly depend upon the earnestness with which working men might be prepared to co-operate in the formation of the College. And the first step taken was, to call a meeting of working men, over which a work&not; ing man presided, to obtain an expression of opinion as to the need for such an institution. This appeal to a meeting of the class for whose benefit the College is especially designed, met with a unanimous and hearty response. A provisional Committee of working men was appointed, and the draft of a constitution for the government of the College was submitted to them and approved of. Upwards of 100 students entered immediately ; and at a meeting of the members held in May last, the constitution was adopted, and the officers of the College appointed.,It may he proper to remark here upon a distinct feature in the constitution of the College&mdash;the admission to the Council of students elected by various Classes as their representatives. Doubts were at first expressed as to the expediency of this course but experience has made it clear that the representative element has done much to promote the healthy tone and vigorous working of the institution. Feeling that they are themselves responsible for the efficient management of the institution, the stu&not; dents manifest an active interest in its wellbeing. The unreserved communication between teachers and taught, secured by the admission of students to the Council is productive of the best results. The wants of the members get expression through those who know them best by constant and familiar intercourse. And though the teacher may be the best judge of the course to be taken by the taught, the taught themselves, at least when we have to do with adults, are not quite unconscious of the degree in which their attention is secured, and their minds enlightened, by the in&not; structions of the teacher. Silent dissatisfaction, which often leads to weakness and decay in similar institutions, is impossible where the students have a recognized right to speak through their representatives. Nor has this right, in any case, hitherto been unwisely used.,Early in June classes were opened for Writing and Book-keeping, French, Mecha&not; nical Drawing, Chemistry, Grammar and Composition, Latin, Arithmetic,&#39; Algebra and Geometry, Geology, Elocution, and Rudimentary Instruction. These classes were numerously attended. In July the number of students reached 180, and before
the close of the Term 190. Novelty may have had something to do with this rapid influx of numbers, and it is only after the experience of several Terms that any re&not; liable information can be obtained as to the number of earnest and persevering stu&not; dents that can be secured among the population of this district. But the following table will show the number of students entered in each class, and their average at&not; tendance during the first Terra :&mdash;,Numbers of Students, and their Average Attendance, during the First Term.,Number in,Average,Number in,Average,Class.,Attendance.,Class.,Attendance.,Elocution . . .,24 .,. 14,Rudimentary,. . 45 .,. 21,French . . .,42 .,. 24,Grammar .,. . 54 .,. 40,Geometry, etc.,41 .,. 22,Drawing,. . 24 .,. 17,Latin . . . .,12 .,9,Chemistry .,. . 27 .,. 19,Geology . . .,11 .,9,&mdash;,Writing and Book-,310,181,keeping . . .,30 .,. 16,Though the difference is considerable between the numbers entered in the various classes, and the average attendance ; it is not surprising that the long and genial summer evenings should have led many to prefer the open air to the classroom, after a day&rsquo;s work.,It may be remarked here, that the students admitted did not pass any preliminary examination. And it was found, in all the classes, that the instruction demanded by most of the students was of a purely elementary character. Had any arbitrary stan&not; dard been established as a test of fitness for admission, it is probable that this would have operated as a bar to the entrance of many of the students. Had it been the primary object to meet the wants of those only who sought advanced education, such an examination would have been essential. But it may be doubted whether any institution established to meet the educational wants of the working classes can be widely successful, where this is made the primary object, and it has been found essential to the extensive usefulness of this institution to dispense with any prelimi&not; nary test. It is hoped indeed that many who take here the initial steps in the ac&not; quisition of knowledge, will be led ultimately to advanced culture ; but it is felt that, in this district at least, it would be fatal to our success to demand more than the merest rudiments of knowledge as a test of fitness for admission.,Early in the first Term the Inaugural Soir&eacute;e was held in the Town Hall, Salford, at which E. R. Langwmrthy, Esq., President of the College, took the chair. About 300 persons sat down to tea. The meeting was addressed by F. J. Furnivall, Esq., of London ; J. G. Greenwood, Esq., Principal of Owens&rsquo; College ; William Harvey, Esq., Mayor of Salford, and others. A more detailed reference to this interesting meeting is rendered unnecessary by the full report of it already published.,The second Term shows a diminution in the number of students ; from 190 the number has declined to 170. The latter number may be taken to represent those who have been attracted to the College by something more substantial than its no&not; velty. And this view is supported by the fact, that while in the first Term a larger number entered than in the second, yet the average attendance at the classes in the atter period exceeds that of the former.,Numbers of Students, and their Average Attendance, during the Second Term.,Number Average in Class. The following Table shows the occupations of the students who attended during the second Term :&mdash;,Analysis of Trades and Professions.,No. of Members.,Warehousemen and Clerks . . .79 Printers and Bookbinders . . .10,Draughtsmen and Pattern-makers . 8,Millwrights, Engineers, and Smiths 18,Building trades.5,Engravers.2,Total,Shoemakers and Tailors,Packers.,Mill-hands . . . .,Labourers ..... Miscellaneous . . .,. 170,No. of Members. . . 6 . . 5 . . 5,. . 4,. . 28,In the second Terra there was a large accession of new members, and a large num&not; ber of those who entered in the first Term did not reappear in the second. The new men of the second Term were in the same want of rudimentary instruction as had characterized those of the first. While, therefore, it was needful that those who during the first Term had passed through a certain part of the course, should con&not; tinue to advance, it became necessary to make some provision for those who had newly entered. Few, if any, of the first Term students were sufficiently advanced to be entrusted with the instruction of the new comers, and the staff of teachers was not sufficiently numerous to enable us to form a second division to each class. The teachers provided for the emergency as best they could. But as this difficulty will constantly occur, it is important to meet it. Either we must double the present staff of teachers, and establish two or more classes in the same subject, or the length of the Term must be extended, so as to make possible the completion of the course before admitting new students. That it would be most desirable to meet the diffi- culty in the way first suggested is evident ; and the Council would gladly accept the services of earnest and competent men, willing to aid in this work.,But we have been embarrassed by another difficulty&mdash;want of adequate accommo&not; dation, and until we have more space at command, it will be useless to attempt the division of existing classes, or the formation of others, for the study of subjects of essential importance not yet included in the College course. But it is hoped that this hindrance will not long be permitted to exist. The Council have already de&not; cided that the building on the Oakfield property is admirably fitted to be the local habitation of our College, In close neighbourhood to Peel Park, and the Royal Mu&not; seum and Library, the establishment there of a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; would be an appropriate supplement to that admirable institution. It is in every way convenient in its internal arrangements, and is not too remote from the populous parts of Sal&not; ford and Pendleton. Already the sum of &pound;595 is promised by munificent friends of the College towards &pound;1800, the amount required for the purchase of the building. An active canvass for subscription is proceeding, and it is hoped that the earnest friends of education in this district will help us, by their contributions, to obtain that more extended accommodation which is absolutely requisite for the increased useful&not; ness and full efficiency of the Salford &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;.,It should not pass unreported, that during the summer months the severer duties of the class-room were relieved by rural excursions, in which large numbers of the students joined. These were so arranged as to combine the pursuit of knowledge with that of health and pleasure. The first was made for botanical study, under the direction of Mr. H. S, Grindon, lecturer on botany to the Manchester School of Medicine. By the invitation of R. N. Philips, Esq., M.P., a large party went on foot, along the bank of the Irwell, to that gentleman&rsquo;s park, near Prestwich, and were received by him in the most kindly manner. The second excursion was made to Alderley. The party were received and hospitably entertained at Alderley Park, by Lady Stanley of Alderley. A visit to the copper-mine gave the teacher of th&eacute; Geology Class an opportunity for some useful observations upon the geological pecu&not; liarities of the place, and our chemical lecturer explained the process of extracting the coppei fiora the ore. The third excursion was made to the Duckinfield coal&not; pits ; and a fourth to the Manchester Waterworks, at Woodhead ; by the kindness

The annual meeting of members of the Salford &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; was held on the evening of January 6th, in the Mechanics&rsquo; Institution, Great George Street, Salford; Mr. D. Chadwick, Treasurer of the Institution, presiding. Mr. G. Parry, Honorary Secretary, read the report of operations since June, when the College was established. He also read the report of the various teachers ; which, without exception, spoke warmly as to the average attendance of those on the books, and the general assi&not; duity of the students. The Chairman read the Treasurer&rsquo;s Report. He remarked that although &pound;100. 2s. had been received as donations, the balance-sheet unmistakably proved, that with the present number of members and rate of expenditure&mdash;and excluding expenditure for additional furniture, fittings, etc., or anything that would ordinarily be called expenditure on capital accouut,&mdash;the College was absolutely self-supporting. &Uacute;pon the motion of Mr. John Plant, seconded by Mr. Gibson, the Reports and Treasurer&rsquo;s account were adopted and ordered to be printed. There was some discussion as to whether the teachers reports should he printed in extenso ; but an amendment for having them condensed was negatived, it being shown that there was a misapprehension as to the amount of expenditure which the original motion would involve. Officers for the ensuing year were appointed (or rather re-appointed), the only change being the piacine of the Mayor of Salford, W. Harvey, Esq., in the vacancy caused by the death of Sir J. Potter, M.P. Mr. Alderman Langworthy, it was stated, had expressed a wish to retire from the presidency ; and the Chairman pleaded to the meeting that other pressino&middot; engagements compelled him to ask to be relieved from the treasurership, to the duties of which he could not attend as he wished, and as the importance of the institution demanded both gentlemen were, however, re-elected by acclamation, on the ground that they were essential to the successful development of the College, and must therefore serve in their old offices. ^ The Chairman still urged the benefit which he knew to result from change as compared with the system of having permanent honorary officers ; and he said it must he understood that he consented to serve now, upon a clear condition that he was released at the next annual meeting. He should not therefore feel less interest in the College Mr. Plant moved a motion effecting certain alterations in the constitution of the College. It was originally laid down as a fundamental law that the teachers must all be unpaid; and that the fact of being a teacher necessarily made a man a member of the Council. But it was now found that it would be necessary to modify this provision so as to guard against having at the Council teachers who were in the receipt of payment Another section provided that no person under eighteen years of age should be qualified to become a member of the College ; but it had been suggested that it would be in many ways desirable to substitute sixteen for eighteen, in doing which they would only be fol- lowing what had heen found most advantageous in other existing Colleges The onlv other alteration was a change of the time of holding the annual meeting from the first week in January to the first week in February; for it was found that it would be im possible properly to close the accounts and prepare the report, in future at so enrlv * penod &bdquo;s waa now fixed The Chairman said there would he another advantage &Icirc;Z &#39;&iuml;he last-proposed change At present the classes did not begin until the middle of January or later; so that when the annual meeting was fixed to he held, there were really no members hut those who subscribed annually; and the thin attendance at the meeting was due to a notion amongst the great bulk of the old members, that they were not entitled to be present because they had not yet renewed their subscriptions The Rev. J. N. Pockhngton as one of the teachers, warmly approved of the change in the age of admission. It would, apart from other advantages, give an opportuni nf hereatter attempting to establish a course of instruction which would enable student! to some extent to prepare themselves for competition at the Oxford Examinations Tb! motion was unanimously agreed to ; and the proceedings terminated with a vote of thanks to Mr. Chadwick for presiding .&mdash;Manchester (Guardian ?), Jan. 7. * thanks,MANCHESTER &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;WORKING&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;MEN&rsquo;S&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;COLLEGE&lt;/span&gt;:,JANUARY, 1859.,FIRST REPORT.,have professed to seek, and the measure in which they have attained it. У
In an Address issued at the end of 1857, they stated their conviction that while valuable results had been accomplished by Mechanics&rsquo; Institutions, other classes were still needed for the more advanced pupils and adults, and for the large number of working men who, from whatever causes, had not been generally reached by Mecha&not; nics&rsquo; Institutions. &ldquo; We think,&rdquo; they go on to say, &ldquo; that a necessity still exists for institutions which shall aim at performing for the working class what our higher schools and our Colleges perform for the middle and upper classes. . . . We propose, therefore, to establish a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, offering to &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;men&lt;/span&gt; education of the kind we have described, as Avide in range as our numbers will allow, but in every case aiming at what is thorough and systematic, rather than at what is extensive but superficial ; and, again, not engaging to lecture to a class of listeners merely, hut to teach and direct those who believe that they must be parties to their own education, and are willing to give the labour implied in this.&rdquo;,Such was the aim with which they began their work. In the measure of success which they have met with, they find much to confirm them in the belief that their aim was good, and not a little to reward them for the pains which they have been able to bestow. It will be seen from Table I., that in the first Term the whole number of students entered was 231, in the second Term, 195, and in the third, 163. It was to be expected, from the experience of similar institutions, that many would enter at first from curiosity, and after a time fall away, when they found that some labour was demanded from the learner. The number of students, again, was con&not; siderably raised by the admission, for the first two Terras, of all members of the Mechanics&rsquo; Institution without payment of any fee. Hence, in the first Term, 151, or two-thirds of the whole, were members of the Mechanics&rsquo; Institution, and in the second Term the members of this class were 126, or five-eighths of the whole. In the third Term it was judged expedient, in order to secure regularity of attendance, to require some slight fee from members of the Institution, and in consequence, in the Term just closed, only 71, or less than one-half, are derived from this source. So far, then, as a readiness to pay the low fees charged to the students constitutes a test, and allowance being made* for the influence of curiosity at the beginning of the operations of the College, a comparison of the figures in the Tables gives giound for believing that its work has not been without its appreciation. More than one- third of those who joined at the outset still continue members of the classes ; and of the whole number of enrolled students the actual attendance, in class throughout the year, evening by evening, has averaged more than 65 per cent.,- a proportion quite as high as any one experienced in such matters would have anticipated. It is to this not inconsiderable band of students, who, both by regularity of attendance and by conscientious performance of class-work, show that they have in them something of the student spirit, that the teachers look for encouragement and profit ; and they invite such by their active influence, as well as by their tacit example, to aid in draw&not; ing into their class-rooms others of like quality.,In Table I. will also be found an attempt at a classification of the students under their several occupations. The Council are glad that their efforts should be welcomed bv any of their fellow-townsmen, and they are quite aware that under the second heading (clerks, book-keepers, etc.) are enumerated many of the class to which they especially addressed themselves ; still they cannot but be gratified at observing that the proportion of students from the class of operatives has been steadily lising, and now constitutes one-third of the whole.,In Table II. are shown the numbers enrolled in the several classes. The language- classes (English and Latin) have been almost from the beginning the largest and the most regularly attended. The Council do not underrate the value of Language and Literatiľre as instruments of mental culture, but they attach no less importance to the exact sciences, and they would be glad to see those subjects more adequately cul&not; tivated. It is desired to introduce in the coming Term a class for the study of Tri&not; gonometry, and another, at the request of a considerable number of students, lor Greek. Of the Bible Class no record has been kept: the members of the class have been few in number, but regular in their attendance.,Examinations will be held at the end of J une, and prizes and certificates of merit,^During the year the want has been strongly felt of a Library, especially of books of reference, for the use of the students. As the funds of the College are not suffi-
cient to allow of any expenditure for this object, the Council will be thankful for do&not; nations, either of books or of money to be expended in their purchase. An appro&not; priate beginning has been made by the presentation, from the author, of Mr. Ludlow&rsquo;s History of British India, a work which originated in a course of lectures to the stu&not; dents of the London &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;.,Table L-,-Showing the Number of Students attending in the three Terms, from January to December, 1858, and their Occupations.,Total Number of Students.,Members of Mechanics&rsquo; Institution . . .,Other Students.,Occupations of Students :&mdash;,Operatives.,Clerks, Book-keepers, Warehousemen, etc.,Shopkeepers and Assistants.,Teachers, etc.,Miscellaneous.,Table II.,Arithmetic.. . 1,Algebra ..r,Geometry.. . . .,Mechanics.,English Language and Literature.,Latin.,English History.,Logic.,Political Economy.,Common Law.,Chemistry.,Geography, Physical and Historical.,Human Physiology.,Political Philosophy.,First,Second,Third,Term.,Term.,Term.,231,195,163,151,126,71,80,69,92,70,57,54,121,107,89,20,20,15,8,8,5,24,3,e Several Classes.,First,Second,Third,Term.,Term.,Term.,99,42,16,14,39,29,21,38,25,13,95,87,57,63,72,67,37,30,11,&mdash;,&mdash;,16,9,10,_,8,11,6,58,36,27,30,16,5,20,22,10,22,9,&mdash;,The cordial thanks of the Council are given to the Directors of the Mechanics&rsquo; In&not; stitution for the use of a portion of their building: by their liberality alone has it been possible to carry on the work of the College at so small a cost.,From the Treasurer&rsquo;s statement of Income and Expenditure during the year it an- pears that the total income, from donations and fees, has amounted to &pound;99 &#39;U 2 d and the total expenditure to &pound;95. 19.. Ы , a balance of &pound;3. 5.. 8 d. being left in the hands of the Treasurer. To those who have aided them by donations of monev the Council offer their best thanks ; but this is not the only kind of help which is needed by them. They cannot avoid calling attention to the great difficulľy thev find in se- curing the assistance of qualified teachers. They know what great demands are made in this busy community on the time of those who are abb to aid in such a,Ze f U^ rS; , bUt &#39;l f i U ľ e&Iacute;ght Were all0Wed t0 this considerati they might,have felt themselves absolved &iacute;rom taking up that work. For assistance in it thev address an earnest appeal to those who, having adequately studied the subjects taught in the classes, and being possessed of the necessary leisure, are also at one J with them in their estimate of the true end of education, as consisting mainly, not in adminis tering to the specul needs of any, whether artisans or scholars, tradesmen or manu- facturers, still less in fostering the spirit of rivalry between man and man or class
and class, but in the gradual and patient development of the true nature of the indi&not; vidual in his relation to the society of which he forms a part, with an ultimate view to &ldquo; the glory of the Creator and the relief of man&rsquo;s estate.&rdquo;,Olivkr Hey wood, Chairman.
