Secs.,Income.,&pound;.,s.,d.,Donations . . . .,. . 66,7,8,Students&rsquo; Fees . . .,. . 32,17,6,&pound;99 5 2,Expenditure.,&pound;. s. d.,Partitions in Rooms, and Fur&not;,niture . 46 18 6,Maps.2 18 6,Printing, Stationery, and Ad&not; vertising . 26 11 3,Salaries, Assistant Secretary,and Porter.14 7 6,Sundries .5 3 9,Balance in hand.3 5 8,&pound;99 5 2,NEWS.,London .&mdash;On the 6th of January was held, at the Rooms of the College in Great Ormond Street, the first General Meeting for the year 1859. The attendance was somewhat thin, owing, no doubt, to the circumstance of its being Twelfth-Night. After tea, the Principal gave a short ac&not; count of the visit w hich he and Mr. Hughes had paid to Manchester, and expressed the delight with which they had found three Colleges, founded on very different prin&not; ciples, yet able to work together on the most friendly terms. The position of these Colleges was very different from that of the London one : here, the teachers had had to go in search of students; there, students were in plenty, but there was a want of teachers, which would, however, he trusted, soon be supplied. Mr. Hughes and him&not; self had been received most kindly, and they found a great interest taken in the Magazine. Mr. Maurice also dwelt briefly on the improvements which he had found in the condition of Manchester generally, after an interval of several years ; on the magnificent Peel Park at Salford, with the Museum and Library, of which the poorest may enjoy the benefits, etc. He gave notice that a gentleman connected with Charing Cross Hospital, Mr. Heaton, had kindly offered to give a Course of Lectures on Chemistry applied to Manufactures, if a certain number of students would put down their names. He then called upon&mdash;,The Rev. Mr. Shepherd, of Kidder&not; minster, who regretted that hitherto he,and his friends had not been able to start a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; in that town, although Mr. Maurice had been kind enough to come down and lecture to them. Still it was a great thing that the London Col&not; lege had been established, and that other large towns had followed its example ; as this was, at all events, a sufficient answer to the first objection alw r ays started against any similar proposal : &ldquo; Your plan is a very fine one ; such an institution would be most desirable; but how is it to be done? Where can we see the like of it ?&rdquo; Mean&not; while they had done what they could at Kidderminster, in establishing fortnightly lectures, followed by a discussion on the subject treated of, and did not, of course, mean to stop there. The Magazine would necessarily be a great help to them, in putting them periodically in mind of what was being done elsewhere.,The Rev. Llewellyn Davies was next called upon as a comparative stranger (the room was hung round with coloured illus&not; trations of his Lecture two evenings before on &ldquo;A Visit to Algeria&rdquo;). He could not report the foundation of any &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; in the part of the world from which he had derived the subject of his last lecture ; though in Kabyli&agrave; there were some curious educational institutions, peculiar to that district, at the head of which was usually some &ldquo;maraboo,&rdquo; cele&not; brated at once for religion and learning (&ldquo;very like our Principal&rdquo;), and to which students of all ranks were admitted ; though, he believed, not much could be said of the
quality of the education which they re&not; ceived. He concluded by expressing his regret that he was not able to devote more time at present to the work of the College, as he had done when it was first set on foot.,Mr. Hughes briefly referred to the pleasures of his Manchester trip, and au- nounced that the Wednesday evening social tea-meetings, which had been proposed to be held only fortnightly, would, it had been decided, after due deliberation, con&not; tinue weekly, other &ldquo; teas &rdquo; to the contrary notwithstanding. The Gymnastic Class would continue its in-door evolutions, so far as space would allow, until spring allowed the members to turn out into the garden again.,Mr. Ludlow gave a short account of the present condition of the Magazine, the most prominent feature in which was that the first number had cost considerably more than was expected, owing chiefly to the expense of the lists of classes and of the table of mathematical studies. The pub&not; lishers were henceforth to provide it with a cover. He trusted that correspondents would be in earnest, and write about sub&not; jects which really concerned the welfare of the College, and would not cavil about this or that point in a lecture or a speech.,Mr. Roebuck pointed out one or two inaccuracies in the scheme of mathematical studies. The Trigonometry and the Prin&not; cipia, for instance, had never been com&not; pressed into one Term. As respects the Adult School under his management, the only observation he had to make was, that the students were beginning to be older at their admission, and of a class better fitted for subsequently entering the College.,Mr. Westlake spoke on the subject of the Mathematical scheme. Referring to the proposed Course of Lectures on Chemistry applied to Manufactures, he expressed the hope that progress would hereafter be made in the teaching of apphed mathema&not; tics. It was chiefly the theory which Cambridge men were occupied with, and they were unfortunately, for the most part, ill fitted to teach the simpler practical ap&not; plications of the science ; but there must be many points of connection between geometry and common trades, which it might be expected that &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; would aid in bringing out.,Mr. Litchfield gave some explanations as to the scheme of mathematical studies.,Mr. Cookson said a few words respect&not; ing the Latin Class, dwelling chiefly on the advantages of Latin grammar as a standard for grammar in general.,Mr. Eraslie, called on as representing the Preparatory Classes, spoke of the benefit which he had himself derived from teaching in them. He heartily agreed with Mr. Westlake as to the need of teach&not; ing the applications of science.,Mr. Furnivall referred to his late visit to the Norwood Educational Institution, set up by their friend Mr. Franks, in con&not; sequence of reading Dr. Arnold&rsquo;s Life and Mr. Maurice&rsquo;s &ldquo;Learning and Working.&rdquo; Mr. Arthur Anderson, of the Peninsular and Oriental Steam Company, had kindly given the Institution a building, and great good was being done even among the roughest navvies, though there was a want of teachers. As respects his own class in English Literature, he explained, that in&not; stead of taking a single early author&mdash;&middot; Chaucer or Piers Ploughman&mdash;as he had done hitherto, he intended this time, by way of experiment, to embrace a wider field, and to go over a series of writers in historical scquency.,The Principal, in calling upon Mr. Tansley to speak, expressed the satisfaction which the Council had felt in electing him and Mr. Roebuck as members of their body ; both of them having not only passed their examinations with credit, but having shown, by several years of work in teach&not; ing others, how fully they entered into the spirit of the College.,Mr. Tansley expressed briefly his cou currence with Mr. Emslie as to the benefit of the Preparatory Classes to the teachers. He felt that it was a duty of the students to help in teaching others. He was afraid to say how much he valued the College, which had, in fact, now become a second home to him.,Mr. Lowes Dickinson, called upon on behalf of the Art Department, protested against its being required to speak for itself ш the absence of Mr. Ruskin. He urged the students, in the Drawing Classes, if anyhow possible, to work at home as well as at the College.,&mdash;The Manchester meeting seems to have attracted considerable attention, reports of it, or leading articles upon it, or on the subject of &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; generally, were inserted in the &lsquo; Manchester Guardian &rsquo; (London) &lsquo;Daily News,&rsquo; and &lsquo;Express&rsquo;&rsquo;,Mommg Post,&middot; &lsquo; Daily Telegrafi,,&middot; &lsquo;Star.&middot; the Weekly Mail,&rsquo; and most of the weekly papers. Manchester. &mdash;A soir&eacute;e, preparatory to the second Terra of the present Session, was held on Wednesday evening, January 12th, in the rooms of the Manchester &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, Mechanics&rsquo; Insti&not; tution, David Street. Mr. Oliver Hey wood, Professor Christie, the Rev. J. Gwyther, of Staleybridge, several of the teachers, and a large number of students were present. After tea various objects of interest were laid upon the tables, and some time was passed in their examination, or in conversa&not; tion. Among the articles inspected were a number of microscopes contributed by Messrs. Thomas Turner, William Smith, J. A. Ransome, and J. B. Dancer; port&not; folios and books of etchings, pen-and-ink drawings and engravings, from the Rev. William Gaskell, Professor Greenwood, Mr. Alfred Neild, and Mr. J. H. Nodal ; cameras and stereoscopes from Mr. Arthur Ransome and Mr. Neild; a collection of water-colour drawings, and a series of geological specimens, from Professor Roscoe, etc. The proceedings were brought to a close by an address from the Rev. William Gaskell. It was announced, during the evening, that a class for the study of Greek would be commenced at once under the direction of the Rev. Mr. Gwyther; and we also learn that the Rev. Alfred Newth will undertake a class in trigonometry, should a sufficient number of students enrol themselves. Underwood, Eansome, and Parry.*,The Chairman : I do not know that there is a more beautiful feature in the progress of our social life, during the last ten or twenty years, than the increasing number of those who devote wealth and influence, talents,* The Report of the Ancoats College will be found on p. 15 of this Supplement: those of Manchester and Salford, in No. 2 of the &lsquo; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; Magazine &rsquo; VOL. and time, to promote the wellbeing and alleviate the sufferings of their fellow-creatures. Miss Nightingale among the soldiers at Scutari, and Miss Marsh among the navvies at Sydenham, are but conspicuous repre&not; sentatives of a band of earnest-minded Christian men and Christian wo&not; men, who, in all ranks and in all spheres, are working for the good of others. They are found in our large manufacturing establishments, in our workshops, charitable institutions, Sunday-schools, refuges, and refor&not; matories.,I believe that it was Mr. Maurice himself who remarked, &ldquo; That the trust and confidence of working men increase in proportion as the number in&not; creases of those who deserve to be trusted.&rdquo; How hopeful then the pro&not; spect of the gradual removal of those class jealousies which have marred so many earnest efforts after social reform ! I believe it to be Mr. Maurice&rsquo;s highest honour and best reward, that he has won the confi&not; dence and the sympathy of working men ; his next, that by his example he has aroused many to a right appreciation of the duties of their posi&not; tion, and to a cheerful and willing self-dedication to their performance. Advancing years and failing strength have neither caused his love for his work to grow cool, nor his zeal to flag in his pursuit of the object he has set before himself. That object Mr. Maurice has very clearly defined ; he has told us that we must look for the prosperity of our country in the elevation of its working class ; that we must seek to ameliorate their phy&not; sical condition as one great means to accomplish their moral improve&not; ment ; and more than all, he has bid us always remember that as children of one common Father, we should be united in one common brotherhood. These are high aims and generous feelings. I do not like to mar the influence they must have upon each one of us, by any added words of my own, and I content myself by asking you to give the hearty welcome you have prepared for a friend \vhom I now invite to give to us, who are seek&not; ing to follow his lead, some words of counsel and encouragement.,&#39;The Rev. F. D. Maurice, on rising, was greeted with prolonged and hearty cheers. He spoke as follows :&mdash;Mr. President, ladies, and gentle&not; men,! thank you most heartily for the veiy kind manner in which you have received me, and I thank the Committees of your different Colleges for giving me the great delight of being present at your meeting this evening. But I must strongly protest against the notion which уош President has so kindly suggested to you, that I have come here to act in any sense as a teacher of you, Ancoats and Manchester and Salford men, about your duties. I have no title whatever to that office. Our Institution is indeed some three or four years older than yours. I have known children of our age who have taken upon them to exhibit very patronizing and stately airs towards other little children of one or two ; and I may presume a good deal upon the strength of that difference. Still, I think that any advantage which I derive from the antiquity of our Institution is more than balanced by the fact that I am the repre&not; sentative of one College, and that I see before me the representatives of three. Now I should have taken shame on account of that difference if there had been no other ; but after what I have seen today, I should be very slow indeed in assuming the right of a Londoner to speak in Manchester about the right way of benefiting the working classes. Before
I should dare to do that, I should have to talk of our London Peel Park, Pree Libraries, and Pree Museums. I am sorry to say we have nothing of the kind to boast of. But though I cannot assume this dignity, I may possibly be of some little use if I tell you what motives induced us to take part in a London &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;. There were some beau&not; tiful words in the first Report that was read this evening. I do not know whether they reached you all, or whether they made the same impression upon you which they did upon me. The writer of the Report, earnestly exhorting those who had the ability, to come forward as teachers of working men, expressed his belief that the work might be as useful to themselves as to their pupils. I can respond to that sentiment,&mdash;I can more than respond to it. Before we opened our rooms to the Working Men, I was appointed to deliver an Inaugural Lecture at St. Martin&rsquo;s Hall. In that lecture I had to make the strange and humiliating con&not; fession that we had formed ourselves into a College, but that we did not the least know whether we should have any students. I will tell you how that came to pass. We were a knot of men of different profes&not; sions,&mdash;lawyers, doctors, parsons, artists, chemists, and suchlike. We felt that we had some common interests that were different from the pe&not; culiar interests of our different professions, and that the common interests were deeper and larger than the peculiar. We were afraid of falling into the little pedantries and the little jealousies which are sometimes apt to spring up among professional men when they are shut up within their own narrow circles. (Applause.) We believed we should do far more justice to those different works in which we were engaged,&mdash;that we should be better lawyer^ better doctors, better divines, better artists, better chemists,&mdash;it we really understood one another, and could realize the relations which bound us one to another. We felt this very strongly; but then what were we to do ? If we merely formed a club, we should soon tall to pieces again, or we should wrap ourselves in our club-conceit, and learn to scorn and criticize and condemn all that lay outside of&rsquo; our charmed circle. How could we be secured from that peril? We mudit be secured from it it we could form an alliance with men brought up in cir&not; cumstances altogether unlike ours,&mdash;if we could enter into sympathy with some great body of human beings who could be regarded in that cha&not; racter and that only. Now while we were thinking about these things and thinking earnestly of them, there came that awful year 1848, which I shall always look upon as one of the great epochs in history. 1 &#39; lo + no 1 t . s W. tIiat we coul(1 give it that importance if we considered the effect which it had in those countries on which it seemed likely to have most effect If we turned to the Continent, we might perhaps only see a tide which had been violently driven out of its channels, rushing back into those channels again. But I do say so, when I think of that country which seemed at first to be least influenced by the mighty convulsions of that year ; 1 do say so, when I think how it has affected the mind and the heart of the people of England ; yes, of all classes of Englishmen. I hear people taking that as a date from which they begin to reckon changes in heir own minds, and m the minds of those about them. I hear one intel- ligent man and another confessing: &ldquo;Ten years ago we felt differently, -but all of us have acquired, since that time, a new sense of our rela-,D 2
tion to the working class.&rdquo; I think T may in some degree interpret that feeling, from the way in which that year acted upon onr little body. It did canse us fear, I own : bnt it was not fear for our property and position ; it was the fear that we were not discharging the responsibilities&mdash;greater responsibilities than those which rank or which property imposes&mdash;that onr education laid upon us. AVe believed that what we saw was as a hand&not; writing upon the wall, clearly sent us by God himself; testifying that if either rank, or wealth, or knowledge is not held as a trust for men, if any one of these things is regarded as a possession of our own, it must perish. A\ e believed and felt that unless the classes in this country, which had received any degree of knowledge more than their fellows, were willing to share it among their fellows, to regard it as precious because it bound them to their fellows, England would fall first under an anarchy, then under a despotism, in which life would die, death would live, in which all classes would be crushed, in which nothing would be so crushed as speech and thought. That was the lesson which we gathered from the events of that year; therefore, for our own sakes, to preserve any little we might have of professional knowledge or of common knowledge, we sought for some method of binding ourselves to the working classes of the country. I will not speak of other plans to which we resorted for this end ; my business is with those which had direct reference to education. Apparently there were many methods by which we might, if we desired it, make our little educa&not; tion useful to those who had less. There were, for instance, the Mechanics&rsquo; Institutes, which that earnest and excellent man, Dr. Birkbeck, bequeathed to this country. Some of us had the opportunity of lecturing at these institutions, and whenever we did so we Avere m&sect;re than rewarded by the sympathy which was bestoAved upon us. But such sympathy made us desire a closer felloAvship than the mere effect of occasional lecturing could create. Besides these institutes, EA r ening Classes of a more regular kind have been commenced in London, and have been a vast blessing to over- Avorked clerks in public and private offices, and have happily conspired Avith the examinations for the Civil Sendee which have been recently established. But here, again, the relation Avas merely one between teacher and learner, and what Ave wanted, if possible, was to make our teaching a bond of intercourse with, the men Avhom Ave taught. How that could be, we might never have found out. But the working men themselves found it out. We heard, in 1853, that the people of Sheffield had founded a People&rsquo;s College. The news seemed to us to mark a new era in education. AYe had belonged to Colleges ; they had not merely given us a certain amount of indoctrination in certain subjects ; they had not merely prepared us for our particular professions ; they had borne Avitness of a human culture Avhich is the highest of all culture. AYe had formed, in those Colleges, friendships which Ave hoped to keep Avherever Ave Avent,&mdash; friendships that had had an influence upon our life and character through all the years that had passed since Ave left them. A man at the Anti&not; podes feels that his relation to a certain College is a relation not ordy to those who were his contemporaries there, but to generations back.,It enables him to realize far more perfectly his rights as an English citizen, to feel that they are not dependent upon place or time. AYas it not a glorious thing, then, that the working people should lay hold of this
name ; that they should say, we are determined we will have Colleges P TV hether you set them up among us or not, we will have them, because we want to connect all our education with our social life, with our fellowship as human beings.&rdquo; (Applause.) I repeat it, you will see then that we have no pretensions to originality in our London College : we were pla&not; giarists from the Sheffield people. And we have quite as little pretension to benevolence. We believed what Mr. Underwood has told you, that ive should get more good than we gave ; and we have not been disappointed in our calculations. Though the working people had no special reason for thinking that we were ftt to teach them,&mdash;though there were many prognostics of ill-success on the right hand and on the left, many fears in our own minds that we should deserve ill-success,&mdash;yet, from that day to this, I believe there has never been a want of the most cordial desire on their parts to further our object and reciprocate our sympathy. I be&not; lieve I may appeal to friends of mine who are here tonight, whether the working people have not asked more lessons than we offered them ? whe&not; ther they have not urged us to give them more systematic instruction than we thought it was possible for them to receive ? whether the advantages we have received from our intercourse with them was not greater than we hoped for when we hoped most fondly P I could go over every one of the studies in which we have been engaged, and ask the teacher in that subject whether it has not been so with him. I will speak of my own experience first. There was this difference between our position and&quot; that of the me&not; chanics institutes. We were not bound, as the mechanics&rsquo; institutes weie, to consider what knowledge it might be safe to give, or safe to with&not; hold, Our feeling was simply, &ldquo; we are men meeting human beings, and whatever knowledge it is good for men to have, whatever knowledge we feel to be most precious and most necessary to ourselves, we will offer to them ; they may reject it or not, as they please ; they are not the least bound to come to any one of our lessons if they do not like it, but at all events they shall have the chance.&rdquo; (Hear.) You will not be surprised therefore that our first class commenced (indeed, before the College was opened) was a Bible class. Over that class I presided, and the course which I pursued was simply this,&mdash;I read a portion of the Scriptures I explained what I thought was the meaning of it, then I invited all possi&not; ble questioning from those about me. (Hear.) I wished them to ask any&not; thing that came into their minds, anything that was pressing upon their minds. They met my wish ; and I have never had one cause to complain of want of reverence, nor yet of failure of boldness and freedom to state frankly what they were thinking and feeling. (Applause.) And in this way I affirm that I have learned more myself than I have imparted. A&deg;ain and,f t g w n V;v vi i l i ba f COrae into m T n,ind when 1 have left those* classes, W Quid to God that anything I have said to them has been as useful to them as what they have said to me has been to me !&rdquo; (Applause.) Our lawyers, I believe, have had the same experience. Some lectures, İ know which have been given on Constitutional History by an accomplished law- yer, have been especially interesting to the students,&mdash;for this reason in particular as they tell me themselves, because the lecturer questions them to find what they know already, and invites their questions, that he may fill up the blanks in their knowledge. And I feel sure that however much
lie may have known of Constitutional History before, he knows much more now,&mdash;that he knows better \vhat Law has to do with History, what he has to do with it. I have referred to this topic that I may speak of the part of our teaching in which we have departed most from the example of mechanics&rsquo; institutes. From a natural wish to avoid party irritation, they resolved they woidd avoid politics altogether. We have felt that we could not avoid tliem. If the nettle is apt to sting, we must grasp it hard, that it may not sting us. To have shunned politics would have been to shun the subject which gave the working men the strongest assurance that they were English citizens and English men. We, I think, feared party as much as the founders of mechanics&rsquo; institutes did ; for that very reason we resolved to tell the people that Politics, according to its true sense, is the great witness for the fellowship of man with man, the greatest witness against that which separates man from man. We could not confine ourselves in our teaching to the present or to the past. We wanted one to explain the other. Our men have a right to know that all the right or wrong acts of rulers in this day concern them ; they have a right to all the noble deeds that were done in other days ; they have a right to the experience and wisdom, not of one party, but of all. In claiming these rights for them, we were claiming them for ourselves ; we were breaking the shackles of party from our own limbs while we were trying to break them from theirs ; we were humanizing ourselves so far as we succeeded in humaniz&not; ing them. I am sure you will acquit me of any inclination to speak slight&not; ingly of our predecessors, of the scientific lectures delivered at so many mechanics&rsquo; institutions. &ldquo; These men,&rdquo; said the promoters of these insti&not; tutions, of the classes for which they were intended, &ldquo; are hard at work ; it would be exceedingly good for them to know the principles of that .work in which they are engaged, that they may not do their work mechanically. Therefore we will teach them physical science.&rdquo; Well, that is admirable. There wants but one thing to make the conception perfect, it is the recol&not; lection that the worker is not first of all a worker, but first of all a man. By all means tell him the principles which expound the nature of his work ; give your scientific lectures. I)o more than this ; give him scientific lessons. Educate him day after day, as the Manchester Report has so well told us that we should do. Never suppose that the working man will care less for your teaching because it is continuous. His common work tells him of succession and regularity ; an interruption will often destroy not the work only, but him. He therefore must be specially impatient of studies that are desultory and fragmentary ; besides that, he has not time for them. I say this to show you that we aie not less heedful of scientific teaching than the founders of mechanics&rsquo; institutes were ; that we desire to profit by their hints, and&mdash;as they would have wished to improve upon them. But we must view physical studies, of all others, as they affect the man ; we can omit none which make the worker feel that he is not a machine, but a man. I may illustrate this point by an&not; other of our studies. I said that in our little knot of professional men were some earnest and devoted Artists. ISow Art appears often to be regarded as an entertainment which may relieve the dullness of merely scientific lectures. We have never looked at it at all in this way. Ihe Artists that belong to our body would not allow us to look at it so. They
become very angry indeed if we dare to speak of it as an amusement. They tell us distinctly it appeals to some great thoughts and necessities in the heart of the working man as well as of every other man ; it connects itself with his business and his bosom ; with the world in which he is living, and with the objects which are presented to him hour by hour ; and they say, &ldquo;We will not present Art under that poor aspect in which rich men often regard it. For the sake of the working men we will not ; for the sake of the rich men we will not ; for our own sakes we will not. We hope to raise the standard of our art, of our profession, by show&not; ing our brethren of the unprofessional class what it may be to them.&rdquo;,All I have said, you see, tends to the same point. We came forward to help the working men that we might help ourselves. I do trust this lesson will not be lost upon those who have heard the cry of your dif&not; ferent Reports for help in teaching. I believe there are in every town&mdash; I am sure there are in this&mdash;all the faeidties that are required to form most efficient teachers. Only there is a certain shyness&mdash;not want of zeal and benevolence&mdash;which shrinks from the task. Possibly, also, there are some of the same doubts about the feasibility or wisdom of the scheme which encountered us when we began. To one or two of these I will refer. You have often had this remark made to you : &ldquo; There is really no chance of the College teachers persevering in their work. How can you expect men who are not paid, to come night after night?&rdquo; I have given the answer, &ldquo;We are not unpaid teachers. We are not paid in money, but we are paid in kind.&rdquo; We are paid just in the way in which a number of men in different professions pay them&not; selves continually. Look at those medical men who spend so much of their time in hospitals. It is not worth their while, surely, for any money that they get there. It is worth their while for the sake of all that is cultivated in them,&mdash;of the knowledge they acquire of their own pro&not; fession. It is worth while for all the benefit which is accruing to them&not; selves from the good they are doing to those around them. İlere is one instance which I have personally seen. It may be a strong one, but hun&not; dreds of the same kind are occurring. We have lately lost in London one of the most eminent of our medical men. He had a very large practice ; he had a European reputation ; he was besides a travelled man, and a man cultivated in all ways. Well, while he was in the height of his fame and practice, I saw him coming day after day, at eight o&rsquo;clock in the morning, from his dwelling at the extreme west of London to a hospital at the ex&not; treme east of London, to study a class of diseases which have since asso&not; ciated themselves with his name. Is not that a proof, if proof were want&not; ing, that you cannot measure services of this kind by a money standard ? Is it not a proof that we are establishing no new maxim, that we are only acting upon an old maxim, when we say that this return in kind&#39;is quite as precious to those who are bringing their goods as the return in money ? (Applause.) I could make another and a more careful answer to that objec&not; tion, if I could believe a story which has been very widely circulated. It sounds somewhat too romantic ; perhaps some of you may know whether it is worthy of credence. I have heard it said that there is a city some&not; where,&mdash;it may be in fairyland, but that was not the locality that was as- signed to it in my report ; the event may have happened in some far-gone
mythical period, but names were alluded to which identified it with a pe&not; riod not far from the reign of Queen Victoria. It is said, then, that the inhabitants of this city once waved an enchanter&rsquo;s wand, and that straight&not; way there came from every part of the land to which it belonged, pictures, most precious to those who possessed them, treasures that had been pur&not; chased, I know not at what price, that had been accumulating, I know not how long. However, they came together ; they were received into a palace in that city ; persons from all quarters of Europe came to gaze at them, to derive profit from them ; none gazed, it is said, with more interest than the working people who dwelt in that city. And as legendary writers are apt to mix details with their stories, that they may give them an air of reality, the story went on to tell that this was not a profitable speculation ; that when the accounts came to be balanced, this great scheme was found just, and only just, to have covered its expenses. Now, if I might sup&not; pose that that narrative had any claim to be a part of history, and if I ever met any natives of that wonderful city, surely I might say to them, &ldquo; You have demonstrated to all people&mdash;it can never hereafter be disputed&mdash; that money is not the measure by which you estimate the utility of your,labour, or of any scheme for humanizing your fellow-men.&rdquo;-Time is fast,going on, and &Iacute; will not detain you much longer. But there is one other objection which has been partly suggested by one o&iacute; your Reports. It is this, that not working men, but men of quite another class will avail them&not; selves of the benefits of these Colleges. So it has been, the objector says, with the Mechanics&rsquo; Institutes, and so it will be with you. To the first part of the charge, that &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;men&lt;/span&gt; will not join &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt;, I oppose not our experience, but yours. To the part of it which says that other people will come along with the working people, I answer, as the Manchester Report has answered, &ldquo;By all means, if they choose to come, let them come. For why? They write themselves down &ldquo;workingmen;&rdquo; at all events they claim fellowship and kindred with working men. W hat can be bet&not; ter? Do not they help to accomplish the very purpose which we aim to accomplish ? If you will turn to any biography ol the great Scotchmen of the last, I might say the present, age, Sir Walter Scott, or Lord Jeffrey, or Lord Cockburn, you will see that those eminent men were brought up in the Edinburgh High School, side by side with men who had hardly the means of getting daily bread. They were learning the same lessons with them ? Here again therefore we are not establishing a new maxim, we are going back to a very old and venerable practice, which has been most effectual for the education of Great Britain. But, ladies and gentlemen,,I am very far indeed from wishing to persuade any of you, least o&iacute; all you, my friends of the working class, that there will not be enormous difficulties in carrying the purpose of these Colleges into effect. I believe theie will be, there must be ; I should think them worth nothing if there were not. What difficulties there have been in producing all the great woiks which I see around me in Manchester ! What difficulties there have been in bringing any one good project to effect! What struggles men have had with opposition from without and within ; with their own indolence, cowar&not; dice, and despondency ! How good men have wrestled, not with flesh and blood only, but with principalities and powers, day after day, before they have succeeded in leaving the world at all better than they found it !
Has it not always been so? Do you wish that it should be otherwise ? If you do, you expect that which you will not get. You will have these same enemies to struggle with, this same indolence, cowardice, and de&not; spondency. They will invade teachers as well as pupils, and all alike will have to fight against them. Do we not meet in Colleges that we may fight against them with common hearts ; that when we are defeated we may raise up one another, and that when we are victorious we may rejoice together ; that we may never be contented with one victory, but always be looking for&not; ward to a final victory, which will come, though we shall not see it while we are on this earth ? Because I think so, I think that these &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; will be blessings to this city, will be blessings to all cities.,Tidings came to us, you will remember, about six months ago, that a mes&not; sage had been transmitted with the speed of lightning, across the Atlantic, &iacute;rom the ruler of one great nation to the ruler of another, and that an an&not; swer was returned. I believe you all rejoiced when you heard that news. I do not know whether there were any who did not rejoice ; I do not know whether there were any monarchs to whom it came, perplexing them with fear of change ; but I am sure the heart of the English people rejoiced when they were told that the bounds of sea were ineffectual to stop that intercourse. And when we were told that, from some cause or other, these messages could be transmitted no longer, we did not cease to believe that a science which had triumphed over so many obstacles would triumph over all that remained. (Applause.) Believe, I beseech you, that there is an&not; other electricity, that there is another communication between the hearts and spirits of men, which is more subtle than that by means of which those messages are conveyed. (Applause.) If, through any tubes whatsoever, however feeble, a message has passed from one class to another which has been long separated from it ; if that message has been heard, and a response, even a faint one, has come back, do not be cast down because there are hindrances to the transmission o&iacute; those messages, through any accident or any fault of ours. The accident will he removed. God will help us to repair the faults. For it is surely His Will that this communication should become firm and continuous. Many are looking forward to the day when the wars of nations shall cease. They cannot hope for it too earnestly. But let them believe that these wars will not cease till another has ceased. Let them join with us in praying, hoping, working, that the war of classes may come utterly to an end.,The Chairman : With our warmest thanks to Mr. Maurice for his very eftective, earnest, and hearty address, we are still under great oblm-a- tion to him for bringing, as his companion on this occasion, one of his best and most valued colleagues in the London &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; And we have also to express our thanks to Mr. Hughes for his kindness&deg;in consenting to accompany Mr. Maurice, and to attend at our meetm- this evening. Be have an impression, however, that Mr. Hughes is Sot a stranger to us (applause),&mdash;that under a different name he and we are already familiar ; and that on that point upon which Mr. Maurice has dwelt with especial force,&mdash;the fellowship of a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;,&mdash;he has an excellent representative m his friend on my left-hand. We recall the school-days of Tom Brown (applause and laughter), and although perhaps none of us were members of the Eugby community, we sympathize with
his jealousy for the honour of the &ldquo;School House;&rdquo; his cordial interest in the prosperity of the great school, and his warm attachment to that good and respected man who, for many years, presided over it with so great success. (Applause.) We will ask Mr. Hughes to be good enough, on this occasion, to add to the remarks which Mr. Maurice has made, the expression of a sympathy which we know he feels in our movement here. (Applause.),Mr. Hughes, on rising, was heartily cheered. He said:&mdash;It is no diffi&not; cult task for me to express my sympathy with this meeting. It is now ten years, as you have been told by the Principal of our College in London, since I and others have been working with him in many different ways. We made many blunders, and had many tumbles in our work, and we have found out a good many things since we started ; but I have never been so satisfied with any work, after being engaged in it for some time, as with this work in which we are engaged at present, this establishing of &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt;. (Hear.) I did not look at it at first with any great enthusiasm. I thought that we were doing what perhaps we might find too much for us ; we might be trying to fly too high ; and those venerable and great institutions in this country, of which we had had the benefit, might not yet be fitted for those for whom we designed them.,I am happy to say that I have found that that belief of mine was an entire mistake, and that the very feeling brought out by those institutions at Oxford and Cambridge,&mdash;that there was a common bond between us and the great men who had gone before us, a bond which you might realize, and which became a part not of your education only, but a part of your very man, a part of you,&mdash;was what our working men were desiring, and what they asked of us to try to give them. And in my humble endeavours to assist our Principal and those who have laboured with him to give these &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; to &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;men&lt;/span&gt;, I can only say, in corroboration of what he has told you, that no work in which I have ever been engaged has done me myself so much good, and has made me so much understand and feel my relation to every other Englishman ; that the work has given me hopes for my country which I did not entertain before, has made me rea&not; lize many truths of which I had only a glimmering, and has made me feel what this country may become when we have got not only these, but other such institutions, into full bearing, and have brought them to do the work which they are to do for us and for all future generations of Englishmen. Now, with respect to our College in London, ray work there is a work, alluded to tonight, which I am glad to hear you have found the need of in your Colleges, and which we certainly have found the need of in ours. It is not connected with instruction, technically so called, for my attempts at instruction in the College, I am sorry to say, did not meet with great success. You may perhaps be aware that my occupation in life is that of a Chancery barrister, and when the College was started, it was thought very desirable that there should be law lectures. I was selected to commence this series of law lec&not; tures, and accordingly I commenced them. My class was never very large : at first it numbered six, and we started with the law of associa&not; tions and clubs, and then we went on to the law of Mechanics&rsquo; Institu&not; tions and Joint Stock Companies; but when we got to the law-of Joint
Stock Companies, that seemed to be enough for the working men, (Laugh&not; ter.) After one Term of Joint Stock Companies my class dwindled to two, and from that time, with the advice of the Council, I discontinued my lectures on law, or rather, I should say, my lectures on law discon&not; tinued me. (Laughter.) Well, then, I being a loose hand, they cast about to see what was to be done with me. The Council saw that there was a part of the life of our Universities, of our older Colleges, for which we had no equivalent in our &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;. At Oxford and Cambridge there are common rooms, or a common hall, and there is a great deal of social life enjoyed in them. Men meet one another, not to get what is technically called instruction, but for all sorts of social purposes. We thought it would be a very good thing if we could get some equivalent for that in &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt;. We have now a common room in our College, where every evening there is tea for any members who like to go up there. Once a week it was agreed that I should attend there, and that we should have a sort of social tea-meeting. These meetings have been very successful. (Hear.) They have quite made up for the want of success in the Law class, and form an extremely interesting part of the College work. We meet there in very considerable numbers now; the members who make excursions partly for pleasure and partly for instruction, bring their geological and botanical specimens, and we discuss all questions under the sun (laughter) ; there is nothing that docs not come up in one way or another. In fact, the general effect has been, I think, very much to bind the members of different classes together, and to make us feel the truth and the meaning of the work which we are about in establishing a College. But sooner than continue to talk to you about my own work, I should like to turn to what I have been seeing today. I have been going about this wonderful town of yours, this fairy-land, as it has well been called tonight, and I have been witnessing those astonishing processes by which you convert the produce of other climes into the extraordinary fabrics which load the enormous warehouses of this town. It certainly is a most marvellous sight, those rooms fifty feet high, from which descend masses of cloth through the ceiling, you know not by what means. It is like a tale. As the President said today, it is the only place where you can realize the Arabian Nights. Everything is done with such marvellous precision and such marvellous power, that the dropping of one single thread stops a machine which could tear a man in pieces. I have been immensely impressed with all these wonderful sights ; for though I have been in Manchester before, I never saw any of these great manufac&not; tories. I think that the impression that is brought home to one in the first place most earnestly and deeply, is the enormous power of organi&not; zation which you have in this city and neighbourhood, and the extra&not; ordinary way in which order is brought out of seeming confusion. When you first go in, to the unaccustomed eye all things seem to be in confusion ; you know not where to look, or what result to expect ; yet as you stand there, everything comes out beautifully arranged and ordered and regulated, so that there is nothing wanting ; the very minutest circumstance has been attended to, in furthering the great end of production. Now this power of organization and order makes one wish that it could be used for other pur&not; poses. If we could only get the power of organization possessed by our
friend Mr. Langworthy, whose mill we visited, and whom we are so glad to see tonight,&mdash;if we could only get such a power of organization and produc&not; tion used upon other raw material ! If such raw material as one of us, could only be turned in at one end of a mill of education, and turned out at the other end, possessed of all the science and all the knowledge that we ought to have ! (Laughter.) That organization and power of bringing order out of disorder, is what I think will enable you to succeed in this ivork which we have in hand. The men of the last generation in Manchester have done their work in a way in which it has been done by no other community under the sun. Their work was to subdue raw material, and to bring forth out of that raw material the greatest possible amount of material wealth in the shape of useful fabrics. That has been done. The raw material is ready ; the most extraordinary production in the world is going on every day under your very eyes. All I hope is, that the work of the next generation may be done as well. The work of our generation and the generation after us is not to perfect raw material, not to produce wealth, but to perfect men. (Applause.) To some extent our fathers, in their earnest pursuit of the other work, have forgotten this. There is no doubt that in our relations with one another we are behindhand. There is not that relation between men of one class of society and men of another that there should be. We are not good citizens; we have not that sympathy which we ought to have with one another; and the bringing out of that sympathy, the making us realize the bond which binds us all together,&mdash;to do that is the work of our generation and the next. It has been going on for some years. The result is those noble institutions to which reference has been made tonight, your Free Li&not; braries, your splendid Peel Park. This is one of the ways by which our generation is to carry out the work which it has to do. For what is the means of doing this work of perfecting men ? The only means is education; for you know that education does not mean merely know&not; ledge of this thing and that thing, and doing hard sums, and being able to call a clock or a pudding-bag by twenty different names, but the making men good citizens, and the understanding what citizenship is, what the duties of citizens and men are to one another. If the power of those who have raised Manchester in fifty years from a common country town to be the great metropolis of the trading world has descended to you,&mdash;if you have got the same energy and power that they had,&mdash; remember how it is to be applied, remember the work that you have to do. A harder work, I allow, but a greater, because every age that passes away leaves a harder work for its successor, if that successor is worth anything. The strife between good and evil is getting more desperate and deadly, and you must have harder tasks, and you must not flinch from them. 1 do trust that in the work which you will have to do, you may show the same energy and the same thorough giving up of self to the thing in hand, as your fathers in subduing the material universe ; sacri&not; ficing ease and pleasure and everything, to doing that which is set before you to do. I believe that one of the noblest ways of doing this work is in helping in every way these &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; for &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&lt;/span&gt; which we are now establishing. (Applause.) I earnestly urge upon you all to do this, not to blink the difficulties, but to throw yourselves into the work, and to
do it with all your hearts and all your minds. Then I am sure that a much belter time will come,&mdash;and I trust many of us will live to see it,&mdash; when there will be no longer that division, that uncomfortable feeling which exists between men of different classes, so that when you sit down with a man of one class you hardly know how to begin to talk to him, or upon what subject you can safely address him, or how you can interest him, and make him feel that you are a fellow-man with him. This time of the year naturally makes one review the past of one&rsquo;s life, and look for&not; ward to the future, and I think that one may do it with great hope. I do think that we are to see, and that we are now seeing, a very much better time than any that has gone before us ; and I think so simply because of the feeling of sympathy which is rising up and giving birth to such insti&not; tutions as those we are endeavouring to found. I thank you extremely ioi the kind reception you have given to us. It is with sincere gratitude that we meet you tonight, and with the greatest pleasure that we see the work which we are doing in London done here so heartily by you men of Lancashire. (Loud applause.),The Lev, Canon Lichson, M.A., said he had a most agreeable task to peiform, to move that the best thanks of the meeting be given to their friends Mr. Maurice and Mr. Hughes, for their presence and addresses. Much as he is to be admired&mdash;con- mued Mr. Gaskell-m other respects, I quite agree with our Chairman in thinking that there is none in which he is so admirable as in the deep interest which he has always taken in the elevation of the working classes of the community. He has tried to persuade us that it was from selfish motives that he and his colleagues entered upon this particular plan foi then&middot; improvement. _ I have only to say, that would be a corľtra- diction of his whole spirit in every other part of his work (Applause 1 At al events, unlike too many, he has shown that he did notfi.ke up this plan in some little temporary fit of enthusiasm, but from deep prin&not; ciple, and that it is that which has enabled him to go on patiently and strenuously labouring at it for a long series of years, and which has

REPORT OF THE COUNCIL OF THE ANCO&Aring;TS &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;WORKING&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;MEN&rsquo;S&lt;/span&gt;,&lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;COLLEGE&lt;/span&gt;.,Before entering more minutely into particulars, the Council of the Ancoats &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; is desirous of making some general remarks. For though they have but taken up a note which has been struck boldly and clearly in other quarters and by other men (one of the most eminent of whom they are proud to join in welcoming this evening), yet there are circumstances peculiar to the neighbourhood of Ancoats and the infancy of its College, which may be interesting to this audience. And first, as to the field of their efforts. It may not be generally known that the quarter of Manchester styled Ancoats is included in the important wards of St. Clement&rsquo;s and New Cross, with a population of 76,896 persons, according to Police statistics of 1857. Many of the largest cotton- mills, foundries, machine- and engine-works, dye-works, and other manufactories, lie within its range, and have drawn together vast masses of labourers. Indeed, with the exception of the shopkeepers and small tradesmen, whose tenements make a somewhat showy fringe to the closely-woven network of streets behind, the inhabitants of this neighbourhood are almost solely composed of the working classes. Thence comes a great proportion of the manual dexterity and skill, the mental power of conception and ex&not; ecution, the pith and energy and endurance, which have raised Manchester to sucha lofty position in the world of commerce, and have contributed their quota to swell the might of England. There, and in the district of which it is but the centre, are gathered toge&not; ther great numbers of persons, who have either received no education whatever, or have been taken from school at the earliest possible age, eagerly availing themselves of such helps as the desultory efforts of individuals might afford, yet without avy solid, organized, continuous system of instruction within their reach. So that whatever advantages a more central position in Manchester might present,&mdash;though it was likely that men of influence and ability would more readily offer themselves as friends and teachers in an institution more centrally placed,&mdash;there could be no doubt that Ancoats, and the district surrounding Ancoats, had an abundant supply of the material most wanted, and that no better site than Ancoats could be selected for a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; for &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&lt;/span&gt;.,I ersuaded of this, the Rev. Canon Richson, Rector of St. Andrew&rsquo;s, Ancoats, was most anxious that some decided step should be taken. At this time Mr. Henry Duffy,&mdash;who had written to one of the daily papers under the signature of &ldquo; A Mill Operative,&rdquo; complaining of the neglected state of Ancoats,&mdash;and Mr. N. Underwood, who had for some time devoted his spare hours after work to the Working Men&rsquo;s Educational Institute and Phonetic School in Tame Street, Ancoats, volunteered their services in any plan that might prove feasible. The friends and assistants of both combined. The establishment of a branch free library for Ancoats was the first object. A meeting was held to memorialize the Town Council to this effect, and was very numerously attended by working men. At the conclusion of the,meeting, Canon Richson placed the large and commodious school&not; rooms in St. Andrew s Square at the disposal ot those who were in earnest in wdshin 0, for self-improvement, consented to become their President, guided them in the organization of the College, and taught two of the classes so long as his health would permit. And the Council feel that they would be wanting in a right appreciation of his kindness if they did not acknowledge it publicly and gratefully.,The College was opened on the*27th of January, 1857, and therefore, without any assumption, lays claim to be the first of its kind established in Manchester The teachers w&rsquo;ere mainly working men themselves, who voluntarily set the learning they possessed at the sei vice of others less advanced than they; and those who were duly qualified as teachers of the Elementary Section became Members of the Council.,It was found necessary to divide the students into two sections, a lower or elementary and an upper or intermediate, with the prospect of a section still higher in the ran-e of its subjects, as candidates sufficiently qualified should gradually be advanced from the in- ermediate classes The register of the first Session, dating from 27th January, 1857, to May, 1857, showed that 382 persons, between the ages of fifteen and forty, had entered --Г&trade;!; &middot; &Iacute;J the follo &#39; v &#39; n S record of their occupations shows unmistakably the rank from which they sprang, 86 being employed in machine- and engine-works, 36 mill or factory operatives, 51 boys in shops or offices, 18 engaged in building-trades 158 in dye- works, print-works, or as stonemasons, labourers, weavers, packers, and one a policeman.
From amongst these 382 persons there was an average attendance&mdash;,In the Elementary Classes, of In the Mathematical Class . In the Latin Class In the Drawing Classes,100,18,10,24,The Drawing Classes are taught by Mr. John Rowe, Certificated Master of the Government Department of Science and Art.,It should be stated, also, that during this time the Phonetic element, contributed by the Tame Street Institution, had not lain dormant ; for unlettered adults, of ages vary&not; ing between sixteen and forty, have been taught to read on the Phonetic system in a period estimated at forty hours&mdash;a system proved to be exceedingly successful in such cases.,Nor must the Council omit to mention the existence of a Gymnastic Section, which, during the summer months, has contributed to the physical development, health, and enjoyment of about forty-five persons on an average, and has helped materially to keep the members of the College together. They consider that the renting of a field adapted for Cricket and other manly sports, is a most important feature in their scheme.,Since the commencement of the third and present Session there have been 154 names enrolled.,Of these, the average attendance in the Elementary Classes is Algebra and Mensuration .....,Euclid.,Latin ......&middot;&middot;,German ..... ...,French .....&middot;&middot;&middot;,English Grammar and Composition Mechanical Drawing ......,Free hand Drawing ......,62-6,8,9,5,4,7,15,11,8,Looking however at the present position of the College, it is obvious that there has been a considerable falling off in the attendance of students. But these points must be taken into consideration: the pressure of last winter took from many the ability to pay the fees, from lack of work, while the great increase of work this winter has occupied the overtime of many, and has prevented their attendance.,Besides, the mental discipline which enables a person to apply his thoughts per- severingly to a given subject, is a part of education as much deficient among working men as any other part.,The teachers of the Elementary Section, as stated, have also been almost exclusively volunteers from those whose time is mainly spent in manual labour. And though their efforts have been most valuable and praiseworthy, yet they have been subject to the dis&not; tractions which press of business, change of situation, etc., necessarily produce, and to the fluctuation of feeling which is to be expected in all voluntary undertakings. Add to this the fact that, with the exception of Mr. Fletcher, Solicitor, Mr. B. Templar, Master of the Model Secular School, Dr. Zimmerman, Mr. James leket, and Mr. O&rsquo;Cullaghan, who have rendered their services gratuitously, the Ancoats AVoi king Men s College has received no assistance whatever from the scientific and influential men of Manchester. All has been effected by the energy and perseverance of those residing in or n^ar the,neighbourhood of Ancoats. ,,The Council therefore would take courage at what has already been achieved ; they would freely congratulate their fellow&middot; labourers in Manchester and Saifoid on the success which has attended their labours; they would look around at the rapid progress which such comprehensive, practical, and time-honoured schemes arc making throughout the land and they would appeal confidently to those who have the leisure and the ability m Manchester to help tliem. The building is there ; the students are there ; there is abundance of talent among our educated men, and abundance of room for ten Colleges. Let it not be said that there are not to he found men sufficiently alive to the interests and advancement of their fellow-citizens,&mdash;riot to say their own ,&mdash;to spare one hour on one or two evenings in the week for such a noble purpose.
