MAECH 1, 1859.,THE WOLYEEHAMPTON WOEKING MEN&rsquo;S COLLEGE.,[Nothing is likely to promote more effectually the formation of new &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; than the telling how existing ones have grown up. The following paper will, it is hoped, he only the first in a series of historical sketches of the different Colleges.&mdash; Ed.],This College, though not quite the youngest, is among the smallest of our &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt;, and though it has little to say for itself in the shape of history, it gladly bears its emphatic testimony to the blessed&not; ness of the work in which it is engaged. Our College owed its origin to no one individual, but was the result of a general feeling that something of the kind was necessary. A few of &quot;us, chiefly young men, of widely differ&not; ent habits and occupations, met together privately to confer upon the advisability of commencing a College. The question was at once settled without discussion as an absolute necessity&mdash;a necessity for ourselves, as well as for those whom we hoped to procure as students. Whether we should have students or not, we knew not ; but we resolved we would make a beginning. Acting upon a suggestion we had received from Cambridge, we held small meetings in different parts of the town, principally in the national schools, where our plans were freely talked over, and the names of intending students were taken. These meetings proved very successful, and helped us greatly in shaping our future plans to the actual wants of our intended students ; we had no speechifying ; no formality of any kind, only free, plain chat. Our preliminary arrangements being completed, we held our Inaugural Meeting, October 16, 1857. One of our number read an Address, and others delivered speeches on some special subject bearing upon our work. That meeting, with the bright beaming faces of those who had responded to our invitation, was one never to be forgotten. We felt sacredly pledged to success.,In one respect we differed from, I believe, all our sister Colleo-es. Among our Council of Teachers, there was none whose age or position pointed him out as Principal. While we were anxiously debating how we should supply this deficiency without begging for patronage, we heard that a neighbouring nobleman, the recent accomplished President of the Eoyal Society, Lord Wrottesley, had expressed the deepest interest in our move&not; ment ; we at once invited him to become our President, and he at once acceded. Thus one great purpose of our existence was accomplished : we had shown that there was a common work in which all classes mio-ht unite without the compromise of the dignity or duty of any. We wished to meet and work together as men, and we found a ready response to our movement from each class in society. In January, 1858, our President met the teachers and students of our College, and delivered an Address to them full of weighty counsel. This Address was published, and drew increased attention to us and our work. Several friends suggested the importance of a Library, and while we were debating the matter, another nobleman and,E 2
neighbour, the Earl of Dartmouth, and a Member of our Council each pre&not; sented us with &pound;10-worth of books as a foundation. We have now about 200 volumes in our Library. A Geological collection is in process of forma&not; tion, which, we hope, may some day grow into a Museum. During last summer we had a day&rsquo;s excursion to the Wrekin, and, though the day was not altogether favourable, it proved a happy and merry one for us all.,On Monday, Oct. 1857,19th, we opened our classes. The following tabu&not; lar statement will best show r what has been done up to the present time :&mdash;,1st Term. Oct. 19 to Dec. 20, 1857.,2nd Term. Jan. 21 to March 31, 1858.,3rd Term. April 12 to June 13, 1858,I 4th Term.,1 August 2 to Oct. 3, 1858,5th Term. Oct. 11 to Dec.19,1858.,Bible Class ....,29,18,13,11,22,Arithmetic, Section I.,14,13,7,6,8,Arit hmetic, Section 11.,&mdash;,8,10,4,7,Latin.,15,14,14,12,12,English Gram., Sect. I.,19,12,6,&mdash;,5,English Gram. Sect.II.,&mdash;,9,3,3,6,Logic.,5,&mdash;,&mdash;,&mdash;,&mdash;,Vocal Music . . .,15,14,11,&mdash;,&mdash;,Bookkeeping , . .,&mdash;,&mdash;,6,9,10,Physiology ....,2,&mdash;,&mdash;,&mdash;,4,Algebra .,12,No return.,8,6 .,French, Section I.,5,3,5,11,6,French, Section II,8,14,5,&mdash;,5,Geometry ....,4,&mdash;,&mdash;,&mdash;,&mdash;,English Literature,14,8,7,7,&mdash;,Geology and Mining .,&mdash;,7,6,6,5,Number of Students .,93,90,63,50,58,It will be seen from the foregoing tabular statement that our numbers are small, though we expect an increase during the present Term, which has lately commenced. A Preparatory Class was formed last Term for those who were unable to enter the College, which proved very successful, and which will, tve expect, prove a great feeder to us. We have everything to encourage us. The spirit which seems to animate and bind us all together is indeed a blessed one. As teachers and students we have all gained a lore which cannot be measured by any money value. Grave fears were expressed that if the teachers were not paid they would soon run away, for &ldquo; is not the labourer worthy of his hire ? &rdquo; But supposing we are not hired at all, supposing we engage in a work which we feel essential to us, as helping to save us from that intense mammon-worship which we know to be so near to our own hearts,&mdash;supposing tins is our reward, that we are not paid in money, but in the kindlier flow of sympathy and human fellowship ; I think we shall none of us be disposed to grumble, or think the time is lost which we spend at our &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;.,Yet, after all, our College may come to nought ; we do not pretend to know what is in store for us in the future. Students, teachers, friends may fail us, but of one thing we are quite sure, that already a good has been done which cannot die. Though we look forward to many years of happy toil together, already our past history, short as it is, is full of happy memories, which have amply repaid us for our anxiety and efforts.&mdash;J.N.L.
MEETING OF THE CAMBRIDGE &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;WORKING&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;MEN&rsquo;S&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;COLLEGE&lt;/span&gt;.&mdash;THE DEAN OF ELY&rsquo;S ADDRESS.,On Wednesday evening, February 16, a tea-meeting of the Students of the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&lt;/span&gt; s &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; and their friends took place at the Town Hall. The Principal, the Rev. Dr. Goodwin, Dean of Ely, presided. There were also present,&mdash;The Mayor, W. Hopkins, M.A., Esquire Bedell, F. S. Warren, Esq., Travelling Secretary of the National Society, C. H. Cooper, Esq., Town-Clerk, Alderman Brimley, R. M. Fawcett, Esq., Revs. Ť. Fuller, T. B. Pearson, and S. G. Phear, of Emmanuel College, Revs. W. T. Beamont and 1. B. Lightfoot, and R. Burn, C. Gray, H. M. Butler, and &mdash; Fisher, Esqs. of Trinity College, E. G. Hancock, and E. A. Hadley, of St. John&rsquo;s, A. Macmillan, Esq., and other gentlemen connected with the Town and University.,After the company had partaken of tea, and grace had been sung, Mr. John Mouel (Chairman of the Committee) spoke. He expressed his great surprise that such a small number had availed themselves of the advantages to working men which that institution offered advantages which were not to be equalled by any other institution in the town&mdash; the instructors of the members of the College being gentlemen connected with the Univer&not; sity, and eminent for their knowledge ; and after some further remarks, read the follow&not; ing address ;&mdash;,t( &#39; _ &ldquo;To the F ery Rev. JDr. Goodwin, Dean of Ely.,&ldquo;Rev. Sir,&mdash;We, the students of the Cambridge &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, venture to express to you as our Principal the great satisfaction with which we regard your elevation to a high and important post. No one who has known the strong, practical spirit which has characterized your life, and which has influenced so many during your residence in Cambi idge, can have any other feeling than that your efforts in your new position will ever be directed to the true w T ell-being of those around you, and that vou will never tire in the &icirc;mbie work of uniting manto man and helping them to be better men and better Christians.,&ldquo; In your high position, it will not be a small source of comfort to you to know that you have done what you could to destroy class feelings, and to make every man know that his true position, whether he be a worker of the brain or of the hand, is to do his duty lovingly and honestly, in charity to those around him.,&ldquo;9 l ! r is &amp;gt; and we hope always will be, the exponent of these view&#39;s and we,would testify the sincere pleasure which we all feel in having teachers who, while they are thoroughly able to direct our studies, do also considerately sympathize with us in our difficulties ; and we trust they know that we feel deeply grateful for their disinterested labour, although we do not often express it.,i &deg; U 1 fe &reg; lin S mars . our satisfaction&mdash;that your elevation may in some decree loosen the tie which has united you to the College. There are many in this towm, as well as ourselves, who will keenly miss your kind and affectionate counsels and vour cheerim- words ; but we trust we may hope for your presence at many of our meetings. One thing \ve are always sure of&mdash;your kindly sympathy and ready help, ihat you may be long spared to usefulness, is our fervent desire.,&ldquo; Si S aecl on behalf of the students, by eight members of the Committee of Working Men.&rdquo;,i he Principal was received with loud and prolonged applause. He observed that when he came there that evening, he felt somewhat embarrassed in respect to what he should say. The difficulty he felt to be increased when he remembered that he was no longer a resident of the town of Cambridge, and, consequently, not familiar with what was going; on. He was however, relieved when he considered what had taken place since he had entered the room that evening. After such a kind-hearted and noble- spinted address he had, as it were, greater facilities given to him for opening his mouth Still, a burden had been put upon his shoulders, for fear he should fail to reply in ade&not; quate terms to the address, and to the spirit iu which it had evidently been conceived C0 Y n0t help tlunklI &lsquo;g thatwas Q0t deserving of all the kind sentiments whicli,&Icirc;he MnľC ή I h | Y taken any troub!e ia Promoting the welfare of,the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&lt;/span&gt; s &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, he had done no more than his duty, and sure he was that,it deserved all the trouble bestowed. If trouble he had taken, &lsquo;he was now amply re warded by the receipt of such an address, containing the generous and noble expressions of a body of working men, conveying to him the gratitude of them all, and muindim-
him of hia position in connection with the College, the interest of which it had been his pleasure to advance during his residence at Cambridge (cheers). They would, perhaps, allow him to say a few words upon the more immediate business of the evening. When assembled together for similar purposes, it had been his privilege to say something rela&not; tive to the progress and prospects of the College, and he thought to deviate from that practice would be unwise (hear, hear). Although separated from them, and away from Cambridge, he had not been absent from a College, because at Ely there was one also, and it truly was a Working Man&rsquo;s College, for, during the past few weeks, there had been workmen of all sorts employed from top to bottom&mdash;carpenters and bricklayers, and all kinds of real workmen (laughter) ; but the noise, dust, aud confusion, had been so great, that he hated the very sight of the workmen. He came that evening amongst workmen of a dilFcrent class, where there was no dust (except learned dust), dirt, or dis&not; comfort, and where he hoped that nothing but happiness prevailed in the mind of each member before him (loud cheers). Mr. Gray, their valuable secretary (hear, hear), had put into his hands a paper, containing a great number of figures, and which he would have explained if he could only understand them (laughter). The tabular form had been so arranged, that he was afraid if he attempted to give an explanation of the whole, he should only be confusing the subject. His position reminded him of an old lady, who was said to have read the &lsquo; Pilgrim&rsquo;s Progress&rsquo; with notes, aud said she understood the text very well, but could not understand the notes (laughter). There were, notwith&not; standing, two or three things to which he wished to called their attention. He believed that with but a small increase, the numerical strength of the last term (1858) was very much the same as that of the corresponding term of 185?. There was oue class insti&not; tuted which he thought called for a remark or two&mdash;the class of gymnastics. There were no less than 23 working men attending that class; aud that was a very satisfactory re&not; sid&iacute;, &mdash;not that he wished to be thought as encouraging pugilistic tendencies (laughter). Still, muscular education \vas very important to working men; the class was a useful one, and he hoped to hear that it had continued to flourish. With the exception of that gymnastic class, there was no very great discrepancy in comparison to the classes of last term, but a curious resemblance in regard to the diminution of numbers in one class and another ; in accordance with the season, oue class was patronized, while another was not, and vice versd. The stationary condition of the College was by no means a healthy condition. The number of members ought to have increased, because one hundred members were but little, when considered with the extent and jmpulation of the town, and the advantages which the College offered. It would be an extremely desirable thing if the members could by any means make the advantages known to their friends, aud so endeavour to bring in a greater number of the working men of Cambridge to share in those advantages (cheers). He was aware that there were many difficulties in the way, great, and such as many had no manner of conception of, but still a strong and earnest resolution must be formed, in order to overcome those difficulties, and that the time now otherwise employed might be devoted to the accomplishment of glorious and noble pur&not; poses (hear, hear). It was possible that a very great many might embrace the privileges and share in the solid advantages of the College, if they would only make the effort (cheers). It would, he believed, be extremely desirable and beneficial if the teachers were to have an examination of the students, in order to test the amount and extent of their knowledge. That would give a greater amount of interest both to the teacher and student ; and would no doubt cause greater exertion aud sterner endeavours to be made. The Council of Teachers w r ere very anxious to carry this out, and he was sure that the arrangements might be such as to meet the convenience of the students, aud if so, he felt convinced that by the examinations taking place at stated times, a great number of students would submit themselves for those examinations. Dr. Goodwin pioceeded to advert to his being invited to assist in the examinations by the Society of Arts. Ihat Society consisted of what was called a union of Institutes. They invited persons to compete for the examinations in history, geography, and other branches of education ; and appointed examiners from the different bodies of the Universities. A list was at the close of the examination published, containing the names of those who had distinguished themselves. He could not help feeling that there was no reason why there should not have been some candidates from the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; ot Cambridge (hear). He regretted to say there were no candidates from Cambridge at all. The matter was one well worthy of their consideration. An apprentice in the Portsmouth Dockyard had
distinguished himself in the first class at these examinations, and at the Society&rsquo;s meet&not; ing at which the Earl of Carlisle presided, near to him sat the apprentice lad, as a mark of distinction and honour for his abilities. He (Dr. Goodwin) was much pleased at that fact, for two reasons especially. First, because the lad had been marked first on his (Dr. Goodwin&rsquo;s) paper&mdash;a matter he did not know then ; and secondly, because he was so humble in his character&mdash;simple-minded and no boaster. At the 500th anniversary meeting of Caius College, Archdeacon Mackenzie, who was declared second Wrangler, when his health was proposed in very complimentary terms, said that &ldquo; he had done nothing but what was natural under the circumstances.&rdquo; That was an observation which he should never forget, and he would simply apply it, by observing that the Portsmouth dockyard apprentice-boy had done nothing but what was natural uuder the circumstances (hear). But did not that show what could be done? and in a place like Cambridge, where the advantages were so great, he could not see any reason why the young men of the town should not be candidates, especially those who were members of that College. Lhe Principal advocated the granting of certificates of merit; and, proceeding, adverted to the general principles of the College, stating that if provision was made for the young men ot Cambridge, which should act as an antidote to idleness, and to inculcate in them industrious habits, they did a most valuable thing ; but at the same time, it would be unfair to the persons connected with that College if the lectures or amusements were to be regarded merely as antidotes to idleness, and to say that the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; of Cambridge was simply to keep young men out of mischief. Well, then, in what re&not; spect did it differ from other plans for the same purpose ? Why, by undertaking to give men not merely entertainment and amusement for the moment, but to supply them with solid instruction, which should be durable, and last them till their lives&rsquo; eud (cheers). If they were to have a system of lectures, that would not be productive of much good un&not; less the persons who heard them thoroughly understood the subjects. If lectures simply amused^ they might lead to mischief by people fancying they understood when they did not. But it young men could be persuaded to study dry hard books, to pursue a course of solid study, to spend their time in searching out facts, such a course must be produc&not; tive of lasting good ; in fact it was impossible to estimate the value of such study and the use ot it in time (hear, hear). lie looked upon the College as valuable in all manner of ways : not merely for the spreading forward of education, but because it had also a very important social bearing upon the community; and in support of this view, he quoted some remarks made by Mr. Maurice upon the subject at Manchester, who, it ap&not; peared, had looked upon the establishment of &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; as the&rsquo;great&rsquo; means by which the social ties of mankind were to be cemented.(?) This was the feeling that was desii ed to be carried out at Cambridge, and he was glad to say had been; still, there was no reason why it should not be carried out still more extensively, as it had been in other parts of the kingdom. The Town, once separated and on the other side of the line, had since been joined to the University, and the two now felt that they were one body cheers). They knew very well that in a University Town there was a verv con&not; siderable tendency to separation ; and they knew very well, in times past, that this feel&not; ing had led to a disseverance, and therefore how essential it was that there should be the same feeling and the same exertion shown for the attainment of one point and to re&not; member that, after all, they were working men (hear, hear). In thus working together they had but one master to serve, and but one great purpose to gain. In&#39;this&#39;social aspect, the idea of separating one class from the other because the one were members of the University, and the others were not, ought to be kept out of sight (cheers) &middot; and therefore it was very pleasant to see the University and Town united in the cause of education. The Principal next alluded to a subject which, he said, required careful and delicate handling. They knew very well that when he was at Cambridge, he had under his own immediate superintendence, a Bible class; although he found very soon after he undertook that class, that at the close of the labour he had gone through in the day (Sunday), it was more than his strength would admit of, to do the work with ease However, there being no one else who could be found, and who could spare the time to undertake the duties, during a period of two years he continued to conduct that class but at the end of that period he gave it up, because he discovered that he was taxing his btiength too much, which he thought he had no right to do. Now, since he had given up that part of the work, he very much regretted that there had been no class formed corresponding with it. He knew very well that there was a difficulty in connection with
it, and that an effort had been made to get over it, but still it had not been conquered. &raquo;Supposing that class to be resumed, it would not be possible for every one to attend that class, nor was it desirable that they should ; but what was their position without such a class? What was their character before the world, and what would they be charged with by those outside? The simple construction put upon it would be that while every attention was paid to their secular duties, no attention had been given to their religious. The object was not merely that of calling that a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, but also a Christian Men&rsquo;s College (cheers). How was this to be done without a Bible class? without it, all their attainineuts and abilities for the accomplishment of secular know&not; ledge would be nothing more than vanity. He spoke upon this subject with the greater confidence, because it had been hinted to him that the difficulty was in a very fair way of being got over. He had been informed that a gentleman of the University had taken upon himself a portion of the work, and he hoped to hear of the re-formation of this class. The rev. gentleman proceeded to advert to his being separated from Cambridge, observing, that no place in the world was more dear to him than the town of Cambridge (cheers). When he came into the town that day, notwithstanding that he had a kind friend to go to, he felt, as he passed by the house recently occupied by him, a sadness, to think that he was no longer master of the house, and no longer connected with it. Those feelings he knew were to be got over in time ; but it was not the mere fact of being happy or miserable, it was the separation of friends and ties most dear, and the painful recollection of being separated from a number of spiritual works and duties,&mdash;and, amongst those works, the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; was not the least (loud cheers). He felt, however, that he had done but little for their College, inasmuch as he was not the promoter, but simply asked to join a number of others in carrying it out, and he did so from a sense of duty; he admitted that he had taken an active part by consenting to be&not; come its Principal ; but he simply acted as one to help, and he did so w r ith very great pleasure, and should continue to do so (cheers). It afforded him great satisfaction to think that, after all, he was not separated by any great distance from his neighbours,&mdash; it was but a half-hour&rsquo;s ride ; and they would allow him to say, that if any of the mem&not; bers of the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; came over to Ely, he should be most proud and happy to see them (cheers), and would show them over his Cathedral (renewed cheers). If there was anything that made him proud, it was the fact that he was essentially a work&not; ing man. He had received his present appointment, not because he was second cousin to the Prime Minister&rsquo;s friend ; not because he was a strong partisan ; not because he put himself forward for the office ; but because he came up to this University as a work&not; ing man, and continued so (cheers). He came to this University with no greater advan&not; tages, and with no greater talents, than others ; but, from the time he came, he applied himself thoroughly to the work put before him. It was because he did work, and under very good auspices, that he had been enabled to accomplish what he had, and which had led to the result of his addressing them that evening as the Dean of Ely (loud cheers). He said that, merely to stimulate them to increased exertion in their several spheres of duty. He hoped they would continue to think and prize much the advantages offered by this institution. If a man would only work, do his duty, and lay hold of the advan&not; tages within bis reach, he would be enabled to raise himself in his social position, and might give to himself a name which should be respected and honoured as a working man (applause). The Dean concluded by expressing his gratification at seeing certain visitors present that evening, and among them the Mayor of Cambridge (cheers). His Worship had not come there as a member of the College, but in his official capacity as Chief Magistrate of the Town, and therefore delegated with the authority of the Queen ; and he came there in approbation of the College, and to bear his testimony to its value (applause). The Principal then sat down amidst renewed acclamations.,The Mayor expressed the pleasure it gave him to be present, and alluded to the very admirable manner in which the address had beeu presented to the Principal by a workiug man, a test of the abilities of a member of the College. He tendered his thanks for the way in which they had received his name. After what he had heard he should go away a debtor to the College, and he should at all times feel it his duty to promote its welfare.,If he had done anything to increase the interest that had been manifested he should be most grateful, and thankful to think that he had in any manner contributed to an institu&not; tion which he conceived to be of great benefit to the community.,The Chairman said, that as they had just heard some remarks from the chief repre-
seutative of the town, it was but right they should hear something from a representative of the University, and therefore he should call upon his esteemed Tutor,,William Hopkins, Esq., M.A., of St. Peter&rsquo;s College, aud Esquire Bedell, who was received with loud applause. He felt proud of representing the University on that occa&not; sion. He had now heeu a resident of the town for nearly thirty years, and he rejoiced at the establishment of an institution like the present, which was the means of uniting the University and Town. He referred to the good feeling which now existed between the two bodies, aud he thought that the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; was calculated to pro&not; mote social intercourse. Those who taught had their minds improved as well as those who were being taught ; aud the more they could encourage the intercourse between class and class, the more they were performing a great social duty. Mr. Hopkins ad&not; verted to Dr. Goodwin being in every respect a working man, and entering the University as such ; and he thought that if the students generally would consider themselves work&not; ing men, there would be a greater number of men distinguishing themselves than now. He concluded by saying that he was sure the University sympathized with the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&lt;/span&gt; s &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;; aud though his own occupation prevented him from taking any active part in the working of the institution, still it had his hearty co-operation, aud he hoped that it would continue to do good.,H. Cooper to deliver a lecture upon &ldquo; The History of Cambridge,&rdquo; next Term .&mdash;Abridged from the Cambr&igrave;dge Independent Press.,ORATORIOS TOR THE WORKING CLASSES.,[We heartily commend to our readers the following account (extracted chiefly from the &lsquo; Manchester Guardian &rsquo;) of a late most interesting experi&not; ment foi the benefit of the working classes, which our Manchester friends seem to have carried out with perfect success.],The admirable performance of &ldquo; The Messiah,&rdquo; at one of the recent Con&not; certs of the Manchester Choral Society, suggested to a number of the Subscribers who then heard it, the desirableness of securing a repetition thereof for the gratification of the working classes, in the belief&#39; that a num&not; ber of the large employers of labour would readily co-operate in supplying the necessary funds, with a view to the production of an entertainment of so high a character for those in their employ. A preliminary meeting was held in the Town Hall, at which the Mayor (Ivie Mackie, Esq.) presided, and a Committee (the Chairman of which was Robert Rarnes, Esq and Honorary Secretary, Henry Charlewood, Esq.) was formed to make the ne&not; cessary arrangements. The same Principals (Mrs. Sunderland, Mr. Mon&not; tem Smith, Miss Lascelles, and Signor Belletti) and the same splendid Band and Chorus were engaged as at the former performance, the Amateur Mem&not; bers of the Society readily promising their gratuitous services. The whole was confided to the able management of Air. Charles Hall&eacute; who entered cordially into the undertaking. The performance took place at the Free irade Hall, on Wednesday, February 9, the band and chorus exceeding &iexcl;&iquest;50 performers. &deg;,The Subscribers received 13 tickets for every guinea subscribed, for gratuitous distribution, available for either the body of the hall or the
gallery. Many of the large employers of labour took as many as 50 or 00 tickets. Most of the teachers in the Manchester &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; subscribed, and distributed their tickets among their classes. No fewer than 4200 tickets were subscribed for and distributed ; and it is believed that about this number of persons were present. The members of the Committee and other subscribers acted as stewards durino&middot; the evening, to see to the accommodation of the audience.,1 he noble hall was crowded in every part with an assemblage consisting mainly of the working classes, and nothing could be more admirable than their deportment during performances occupying nearly four hours. Judg&not; ing by their earnest and rapt attention ; their quiet and subdued de&not; meanour ; their manifest delight, bursting forth into enthusiastic and un- checkable plaudits ; this great experiment on the influence of the grandest music must be regarded as a complete and splendid success.,Mr. R. Barnes, chairman of the Executive Committee, introduced the Rev. Dr. Hook, of Leeds, who had kindly and most readily complied with the request of the Committee to deliver a short introductory address.,Dr. Hook, after some prefatory remarks, said he felt that in seeking to provide for the innocent and rational amusement of the people, he was labouring in his vocation as a minister of the Gospel&mdash;that he was pro&not; moting the cause of virtue by diminishing the temptations to vice. (Hear, hear.) Our work, whatever it might be, was for the most part assigned to us by circumstances over which we had very little control ; but with respect to our amusements much more was left to our freedom of choice : so that, to ascertain the character of a man, one must learn how he amused his leisure hours. A man might be a very skilful artisan ; but if his hours of leisure were passed in places of intemperance, dissipation, and de&not; bauchery, his skill might be beneficial to the public, but it would not be advantageous to himself or to his family (hear, hear, and applause). &#39; During the last half-century, through the spread of education, a taste for music had been created and cultivated ; and where could we find amuse&not; ment more elevating and civilizing than that provided in the concert- room ? (Hear, hear.) In these manufacturing districts, not only did a taste for music especially prevail, but a taste for what had been called the national amusement of England&mdash;a taste for Handel. Eor it was only in England that Handel&mdash;as his Erench biographer said&mdash;was well and widely known ; only here was his music sung, and played, and estimated as it ought to be. In these districts, it appeared that those who devoted their minds, in the way of business, to the material world found an outlet for the poetry of our nature in that art which raised us from the stern realities of life to the regions of sentiment and feeling. (Applause.) The concerts for the people established in Leeds, Manchester, and elsewhere, had created an artistic taste and feeling, so that the difficulty had arisen&mdash; How to reconcile low-priced tickets, which at such concerts were neces&not; sary, with the legitimate demands of first-rate artists ? Every true musical artist was such not from talent alone, but from hard and unremitting study and labour ; and such artists were therefore entitled to demand ample re&not; muneration from the public whom they served. To the honour of Man&not; chester she had shown how to meet the difficulty ; and he believed that Leeds would not be slow to follow the example. (Applause.) The em-
ployers o&iacute; labour desired that their &ldquo; hands&rdquo; should share the enjoyment of which they had partaken from the performance of the &ldquo; Messiah and they did well in this, for it showed that they remembered that their &ldquo; hands &rdquo; had minds and hearts. (Applause).&mdash;The oratorio closed, amidst loud cheers, about eleven o&rsquo;clock.,NEWS.,London .&mdash;The following resolutions have been come to by the Council of the Work&not; ing Men&rsquo;s College :&mdash;,&ldquo; tached to particular classes as tutors, tak&not; ing , charge of the students for practice on one evening or more per week whilst the class is being carried on.,&ldquo; That preparatory class teachers, and students who have gained certificates of competency, be admitted as tutors on the written request of the College Teacher to whose class they are to be attached.,&ldquo; cur with the Council in seeking openings for them.,&ldquo; That the Students&rsquo; Committee be re&not; quested to open a list for the names of all students willing to teach in the Preparatory Classes, and of the subjects which they would undertake, and that the names oň such list be reported from time to time to the Council.&rdquo;,A &ldquo; College Record Book &rdquo; has been lately opened, in which are to be inserted, by order of date, &ldquo; all documents, printed or written, which emanate from the Col&not; lege, are put up on its board, or are other&not; wise placarded or advertised within it.&rdquo; ( This step, too long delayed, is strongly re&not; commended for adoption to such of the other Colleges as may not have taken it already.),Ancoats .&mdash;The second anniversary of the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, established two years ago, under the auspices of the Rev. Canon Richson, was celebrated by a tea meeting in St. Andrew&rsquo;s schoolroom, on Thursday evening, January 37. A party numbering about 200 assembled on the occasion. After tea the Rev. Canon, who is President of the College, was called to the chair, and delivered an address. He,said he believed some good had already re&not; sulted from the establishment of the Col&not; lege, and hoped that in future still further benefits would accrue from it. Some might ask why they called it a College. It was a mere assemblage of classes for the purpose of imparting to young men in&not; struction in various branches of learning ; therefore, why designate it a College ? Why not be content with an humbler title ? What was a College ? A College had been represented as a body, but a Col&not; lege was really a number of colleagues,&mdash; persons bound together by the same law and rules, and governed by the same regu&not; lations. But take it as a body, actuated by motives, regulated by principles, with feet for progress, hands for work, a head for thought, and a heart to feel. Apply that to working men. Would not such a body be very desirable&mdash;a body of men united for one purpose, with the same feelings and aspirations, availing themselves of every opportunity of securing their own intellectual advancement and promoting their best and highest interests, consistently with the law of God and the welfare of so&not; ciety&mdash;was not such a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; desirable ? Did &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;men&lt;/span&gt; despise &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; ? No. Did &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;men&lt;/span&gt; neglect every kind of associations ? No. Man was a social be&not; ing, and as such must have opportunities of gratifying his desire for social intercourse with his own species. It was, he believed, the want of opportunity for working men to assemble together in such Colleges as this, for the promotion of objects that would conduce to their real advantage, that caused them to have recourse &amp;amp; to those abominable colleges, the beershops. (Cheers.) He could not believe that the working men of this country would adopt the courses they too frequently did, to the great, detriment of themselves and their families, if it were not that they sought the gratification of that natural impulse for association to which all were more or less subject. But what was the aim of the promoters of this College P They desired to promote the improvement of the work&not; ing classes. Why ? Because they wanted to help to make them what God intended
them to be&mdash;men. The tendency of a krge proportion of society at the present day was to make them anything but men,&mdash;he would not say beasts, for the habits of many of the working population of this district would disgrace the brute creation. He was not dealing ont wholesale condem&not; nation upou the working classes ; far other&not; wise. Some of the most respectable men living, be their class what it might, were to be found amongst the working men of that neighbourhood. He knew working men careful and anxious to bring up their families in the fear of God and honesty to&not; ward man. But on the other hand, there were men earning good wages, who ap&not; peared in ragged dirty coats on Sundays, and whose children could not go to school, or any place of worship, because they had no clothes to wear. The pawnbroker was the constant resort on Monday morning, and the few things necessary to put on on Sunday had to be fetched out of pledge ou Saturday night. Drunkenness was the cause of all this. Could any mere philan&not; thropist, not to say any religious man, contemplate such a state of things and not desire to apply a remedy ? It was said it was impossible to improve such men by education. It was said they must correct their habits, and get them to take the pledge. lie was not a teetotaller ; hence he could scarcely appear consistent in ad&not; vocating any stringent feature of the sys&not; tem ; but he knew its value and its merits. Any man given to habits of drunkenness, in his opinion, never would correct them unless he became a total abstainer for a considerable period. Though not himself a teetotaller, he was an earnest advocate of total abstinence under such circumstances. He did not say a man who knew how to govern himself ought to be subject to such rules, but every man who could not go&not; vern himself ought to be subjected to them. (Cheers.) It was obvious that any man who wished to do what was best for him&not; self and his family, would not pursue a course of drunkenness, and to prevent men from falling victims to such evil habits was one of the objects of this institution. But did the promoters want to raise men above their station in life? By no means. He did not think a man a bit more respectable because he happened to have &pound;5000 a year, and ride in a carriage. He did not think that the possession of wealth made any dif&not; ference in regard to personal respectability. Neither did he consider that it made the slightest possible difference in respect to,personal enjoyment. He believed there were more men happy upon 30i. a week than upon &pound;3000 a year. Therefore he was not desirous to raise men out of the station in which it had pleased God to place them. But he did wish to see every man able to bring to bear, for the highest possible purposes, and his own individual advantage, the intellectual powers with which he was endowed. He and the pro&not; moters of the College desired that all per&not; sons in that neighbourhood should have an opportunity of acquiring such knowledge, of obtaining such an amount of mental en&not; lightenment and moral culture, and such an appreciation of their social and religious duties, as would make them good members of society. Two of the most distinguished men in Lancashire, both of whom w r ere now living, one very nearjto the place in which they were assembled,&mdash;men whose names were famous throughout the world &mdash;certainly throughout England&mdash;men who were consulted by the curators of the Bri&not; tish Museum&mdash;had maintained a position as working men as long as they were able. He spoke of the English botanists Buxton and Nowell&mdash;Buxton living in a street close at hand, and Nowell at Todmorden, the former being by trade a shoemaker, and the latter a weaver. Buxton was a thoroughly independent man, and Nowell was one of the noblest specimens of human nature, entirely superior to everything worldly, of the highest possible respecta&not; bility, beloved by his family, happy in his home, and considering himself more bless&not; ed than a monarch. In this College were facilities for instruction which might en&not; able the members to improve themselves, to cultivate their powers, lead them to feel themselves to be men, and to become a source of happiness to all who depended upon them, or with whom they were as&not; sociated. It was true that in establishing the College they had laboured under great difficulties. Ancoats was a considerable distance from the centre of the town, and there were not many persons living in the neighbourhood who could be of service as teachers. The consequence was that the Arithmetic and Geometry classes had been discontinued ; but he was happy to say that Mr. Atkinson, the Principal of Daisy Bank House establishment, in Victoria Park, had undertaken to give instruction in both classes, and they would be imme&not; diately resumed. (Applause.) Then in order to promote a union^of sentiment and a community of feeling amongst the
members, a common room would be opened every Monday evening, for the benefit of all who chose to come; and Mr. M&lsquo;Cann, Fellow of the Geological Society, would give them the benefit of his knowledge of practical science, and communicate scien&not; tific knowledge in a familiar style. It was hoped, too, that under his guidance some botanical excursions would be organized in the course of the summer, for the purpose of affording the members an opportunity of acquiring scientific knowledge in a practical way. (Cheers.),Mr. Salkeld next came forward and recited a poem he had himself composed on the subject of the College. It contained allusions to the services rendered to the College by its esteemed President.,Mr. Underwood next read the report of the Council of the College for the past year. {See Supplement to this Magazine for February.),Councillor Curtis, in a practical and telling speech, proposed a vote of thanks to the Council and Teachers of the College, which was seconded by Mr. Powell.,Mr. Dueey, in the name of the Council and Teachers, presented to the Rev. Akroyd, Esq., M.P.,On Friday evening, February 4th, a Lecture on Self-Education was delivered to the students and friends of the Work&not; ing Men&rsquo;s College, by the Rev. A. Barry, Head-Master of Leeds School. The Lee* turer was introduced by the President of the College. He observed that it was a common mistake that the great men who had figured largely in the world were men who had educated themselves. Even the men themselves,&mdash;such as Watt, Hugh Aliller, and Stephenson, acknowledged that their education was not self-derived. There were powers within that corresponded with powers without ; and each acted upon the other. The man who boasted that he was self-educated, generally turned out to be presumptuous and proud ; whilst the really clever men, such as Hugh Aliller, had to acknowledge that they had &ldquo; schools and schoolmasters,&rdquo; and were men of humility and kindness. The Lecturer noticed the folly of cultivating one part of the human system, and leaving the other uncared for. AVhat was wanted was an equally balanced education ; a due attention to the body, the mind, and the heart. If one was at&not; tended to too much, it was at the risk and expense of the other. He alluded to the manly games encouraged in the College. At the close of the lecture a vote of thanks to the Lecturer was enthusiastically passed.,The following is the attendance of the

No. IV. APEIL 1, 1859.,OUE WOEK.,hat is the substance, what the method, and what the objects of our teaching ? These are questions that are being asked us every day with a more and more pertinacious demand for full, clear, and honest answers. To furnish such answers, dealing with each subject separately, will be one of the immediate purposes of this Magazine. Of the general reasons for от - existence as a College, we* have said perhaps enough. What we have not done yet, and what Ave have to do, is to set forth in what Avay each particular thing that we teach can be used in forwarding the true ends of a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;. We have avowed that our enterprise, considered as a whole, is an experiment. Let-us confess that every separate bit of our work is an expeiiment too. Not that the nineteenth century English workin&deg;&middot; men are radically different from the men of any other age, country, or rank, but simply because we have to adapt our &ldquo;learning and working&rdquo; to a set of outward circumstances for which we can find no precedent in the past We dare not build on the traditions of other Colleges and other ao- es . It will not do to say to working men, Come and learn Latin, becausefor ten generations a qualified knowledge of the Odes of Horace has been the maik of an educated man ; or, You must learn Geometry, because tri&not; angles and circles have been used to clarify the brains of mankind for twenty centuries. Such presumptions, strong as they are, may be met by the natural question, &ldquo; How do we know that these studies have any other value an to produce an educated class ; to develope professional faculties &middot; to Lain, in short, a superior order of craftsmen,&mdash;lawyers, doctors, priests ? How do we know that they will do any good to us, who live not by our brains, but by our hands ?&rdquo; These queries give us the chief test, to Avhich we must submit all that we teach. We must ask, What is there in it of in- tnmw non-professional value? What does Geology teach besides where to sink our coal-shafts? What can Mathematics give men besides the Nautical Almanac ? We have given m general terms replies to these ques- tions We have indeed been constantly asserting that in the answer to them lies the foundation of all we are doing, but have not yet worked out the principle m word or thought. The attempt to do so will certainly benefit teachers and learners. It will force us to grapple with difficulties often piesent to the minds of both It will raise many questions closely affecting our work : questions as to the relative values of different studies^-Wh^h best achieve particular ends ? Which combine best as mutually corrective or supplementary? And questions not less important of a plain practical &icirc;^ d \ a u What subjects suit the limited opportunities of working men? W Inch bear their fruit quickest ? and so on. I hope therefore to be^ble to give the readers of this Magazine one or more papers every month written,F 3,B &amp;gt; we &rsquo; 1 mean generally the Loudon &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;.
with this view. They will of course abound in contradictions of opinion, not to speak of differences of style. We do not aim at producing a &lsquo; Philosophia Prima,&rsquo; or Theory of Human Knowdedge. Nothing here said will be &ldquo; published by authority.&rdquo; To serve its ends, the &ldquo; Council &rdquo; will be &ldquo;open,&rdquo;&mdash;a debating ground free to all who are doing &ldquo; College work,&rdquo; and have anything serious to say about it. We especially desire our friends at Manchester, Wolverhampton, and elsewhere, to understand that the Magazine belongs as much to them as to us. We shall receive with the greater interest the thoughts of those with whom wc are not in daily intercourse. The Editor.,MATHEMATICAL STUDY.*,Why arc mathematics taught at a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; P The answer to this question must comprise both the reasons for making mathematics a part of general education, the application of which to &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;colleges&lt;/span&gt; we shall claim in virtue of our principle of the fundamental identity of edu&not; cation for all classes, and the special reasons for making mathematics a part of the instruction of working men. Another subdivision of the answer,is, into the improvement of the mind due to the study of mathematics, and the positive knowledge gained thereby, each of which considerations is of great importance for men in all conditions of life. In the present article we shall not have space to speak of more than the improvement of the mind, if even to exhaust that part of the subject.,The end of education is to think, feel, speak, and act rightly on all sub&not; jects presented to a man. With feeling, speaking, and acting, mathematics have to do only indirectly, so far, namely, as rightness in each is promoted by clearness of thought ; though, indeed, rightness does in all these de&not; pend on clearness of thought to a prodigious extent. With rightness in thinking mathematics are concerned directly, and it is their tendency to promote this that we wish at present particularly to impress on working men, and to impress it on them too in such a manner that they may see for themselves the truth of what we say, and not take our mere word for,it. But in doing so there arises a great difficulty, so far as concerns those of our readers who are as yet entire strangers to mathematics ; for it is of course impossible to discourse intelligibly of the effects of a thing, to one who does not know what the thing itself is ; nor is it many degrees more possible to describe in general terms what mathematics are, so as to be intelligible to one who is not the least of a mathematician, for the power of deriving clear impressions from descriptions couched in general terms is itself in no small measure dependent on the study of mathematics. Happily, however, a slight acquaintance with mathematics is sufficient to show their nature, and that of the reasoning used in them, and thereby to render a person competent to understand many of the arguments for and,* The Mathematical Course was the first scheme of study offered to our readers. To explain in general terms the use of the Course so suggested is the object of the present Paper, written by one of the mathematical teachers of the London &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;.
against their educational value : a fact which is probably due to their deal&not; ing with number and form, two of the primary conceptions of men, and at least latent in all, so that much of the truths and methods of reasoning concerning those conceptions becomes pretty soon evident when once the attempt is made. If, therefore, we throw what we have to say into the form of answers to the objections usually made to the educational value of ma&not; thematics, we shall be sure of being understood by a large part of the students of the London &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, to many of whom probably the objections will have already occurred ; and perhaps, with a little care in statement, we may be as intelligible in this way to those who have never entered upon mathematics, as if we had prefaced this article with a general account of that science.,The first objection then to mathematics as a means of mental training is, that mathematical truth is certain,&mdash;that the reasoning by which it is established is all on one side,&mdash;that there is simply nothing whatever to say against it ; while in politics, law, social economy, and all other human af&not; fairs, the truths which men are said to know, and on which they are obliged to act, are arrived at by balancing arguments and probabilities, for which a temper of mind is required the very opposite to that dogmatic and hasty temper which, it is alleged, results from the habit of contemplating such truths as, when once they are really understood, give no room for hesi&not; tation in admitting them. That there is something in the effect of mathe&not; matics of which this is the distorted representation, we allow, and will presently show what it is : in the meantime, it is certainly not that which the objectors imagine it to be. &ldquo; Truths such that, when once they are really understood, they give no room for hesitation in admitting them.&rdquo; A.nd of how many truths, besides those of mathematics, is not this an ac&not; curate account ? How often, in human affairs, is not the contest between those who will not, 01 cannot, understand each other? between arguments and probabilities which are not, though they profess to be so, directed to opposite sides of the same question, but to different questions which, from duine ss oftener than from malice or from interest, men pertinaciously con&not; found P Now it is one great benefit of mathematics that they correct this evil, by training men to conceive clearly what they propound as arguments and venture assertions upon. Thus, in the solution of problems, the main dif&not; ficulty is to conceive clearly the various facts involved in the conditions of the problem presented, to separate each of those facts from the others of them, and to state the algebraical equation which expresses each of those facts &middot; the last step being nothing more than the construction of a mathematical sentence asserting the particular fact&mdash;a sentence of which the construction is as grammatical as that of any sentence in English or French while the symbols which form its words, being rigidly defined beforehand,&rsquo;convey the meaning with a precision scarcely known to ordinary speech This beino&middot; done the solution of these equations, that is, of the problem, is generally tound far easier than the statement was : thereby illustrating at once the necessity in human affairs of a clear statement or conception of questions as often more than half the difficulty in their solution, and the discipline which algebra furnishes towards that end. 1,Nor is it only in the statement of particular problems that this discipline ot clearness is provided by mathematics, but also in the whole method
pursued in them. The principles of mathematics are self-evident, at least as soon as the reflection is fixed upon them. Thus, in geometry, reflection makes it plain, and no demonstration can make it plainer, that the whole of any space is greater than any portion of it ; that two straight lines cannot enclose a space ; that through the same point there cannot pass two straight lines each parallel to a third straight line ; and that a figure, of which every point is equidistant from a certain fixed [mint called the centre, will return into itself, or be a closed figure,&mdash; what in fact we call a circle. Upon these foundations is reared that whole department of geometry which is known by the name of Euclid, the author of the book upon it which, after twenty-one centuries, is still a standard work : and all the more advanced truths of the subject, as that all triangles on the same base and of the same height are equal in area, can be conceived as clearly and definitely as those above cited, being only not self-evident because they are complex, while their certainty is secured by their being connected with those first simple truths through chains of reasoning against no one link of which, as truly remarked above, can anything whatever be so much as advanced. Nor is algebra, which is the science of number, or universal arithmetic, one whit behind geometry, or the science of form, either in the self-evidence of its principles, or in the cogency of its reasoning. Now what an educa&not; tion is this, both in the power and in the habit of representing to one&rsquo;s- self with scrupulous exactness the strict question at issue in every case ! In mathematics &ldquo; every term,&rdquo; as De Morgan says, &ldquo; is distinctly explained, and has but one meaning, and it is rarely that two words are employed to mean the same thing. Also there are no words whose meanings are so much alike that the ideas which they stand for may be confounded. Be&not; tween the meanings of terms there is no distinction except a total distinc&not; tion, and all adjectives and adverbs expressing difference of degrees are avoided. Thus, it may be necessary to say &Lambda; is greater than B, but it is entirely unimportant whether A is very little or very much greater than B. Any proposition which includes the foregoing assertion will prove its con&not; clusion generally, that is, for all cases in which A is greater than B, whether the difference be great or little.&rdquo;,Again, in illustrating the clearness of mathematical truth, we have inci&not; dentally illustrated also what is called the deductive character of the rea&not; soning by which it is reached; that is, every portion of such truth is deduced from some simpler portion, by reasoning which shows it be not superadded thereto, but actually contained therein ; to have been, in fact, involved from the first in the very meanings of the words by which that simpler portion was expressed. And thus, passing successively from portion to portion, all mathematical truth is seen to be the mere development of such first principles as those geometrical ones above cited, the mere unfold&not; ing what is contained in them. Now this is excellent practice in an art which is very necessary for forming a sound judgment in human affairs,&mdash; the art, namely, of determining what assertions are necessarily connected with other assertions ; what, if Ave say this or that, we are or are not thereby obliged to say besides. It is essential that, before we adopt or reject an assertion, we should consider it in all its bearings, and unravel, as far as we can, what is implicitly contained in it. Perhaps it may neces&not; sarily lead to some other assertion, the truth or falsehood of which we know
from other sources, and which will therefore reflect on the original asser&not; tion submitted to our judgment a light which was wanting to it when considered in itself. Every candid man tries to test his opinions in this manner, but the common snare is to suppose a conclusion to follow neces&not; sarily from the opinion which is being tested, when, so far from being con&not; tained in it, it is not otherwise to be reached than by introducing some new supposition ; and then, if the conclusion be notoriously false, it may be because of the falsehood of this new supposition, and not, as our mis&not; taken, though candid, reas&ouml;ner supposes, because the first opinion pro&not; pounded was false. Now deductive reasoning, on whatever subject, is per&not; formed by the same method, the nature of which is explained in the science commonly called logic. And here we meet a second objection, that, grant&not; ing the reality of the benefit we have contended for, it may be more easily got from the study of logic. But inasmuch as example is better than precept, and practice better than either, an accurate reas&ouml;ner is more likely to be made by reasoning out some thousands of mathematical truths, many of them of great complexity, than by learning the theory of deductive logic. That theory can after all be explained in ten words to a man whose reasoning powers are already trained by a course of mathematics ; while on the other hand any attempt to explain, or at least to illustrate, it to one who has not passed through such a course is seriously cramped by the inevitable paucity of examples; for, except in the abstract sciences of number and form, there are but few truths which are at once true and suffi&not; ciently large and general to be made the basis whence, through deductive reasonings, any considerable number of more limited, and therefore more interesting and practically useful, truths may be established. In mathe&not; matics, indeed, we pass in this manner from such elementary and self-evident principles as the geometrical ones above cited to all such special and im&not; portant facts as those concerning the forms and dimensions of particular figures, which, moreover, though an approximation to them might in some cases be obtained by actual measurements, could in no case, independently of the mathematical deduction, be known with accuracy, and in many cases not even roughly. But, we repeat, in no other science is the metho d of deductive logic used to extend the bounds of our knowledge ; and the stu&not; dent who will learn reasoning from the logical theory is therefore obliged to content himself with examples drawn from barren classifications, such as, that all birds are animals ; eagles are birds ; therefore eagles are animais .,The mention of logic suggests to us that something in the effect of ma&not; thematics, of which the first objection above noticed was the distorted representation. Chains o&iacute; reasoning, like chains of iron, require a peg to be hung from. There must be true premisses, as they are called, to start from, or the best of reasoning will lead to no true conclusion. Now deduc&not; tive logic is not at all concerned with premisses, for it is not concerned with positive truth, but only professes to teach you how to reason out the con&not; clusions . contained in such premisses as you may chance either to find or to imagine ; and mathematics, so far as we have yet considered them though concerned with the positive truths of number and form deduce those truths from premisses which, being self-evident, arrest but little the attention of the student. Hence it is undeniable that those in whose train-
ing either logic or pure mathematics &mdash;as algebra and geometry are called &mdash;have unduly predominated, show, in their arguments upon human affairs, a great recklessness in the assumption of the facts which they make the premisses of their reasoning. It is not that they are consciously in&not; different to truth, but that their minds have acquired the habit of attend&not; ing more to the reasoning than to the facts it sets out from ; more to the chain than to the peg on which it is hung ;&mdash;a habit which is fatal in human affairs, where on almost any topic many assertions may be made plausible enough for a clever arguer to start from, and dazzle himself and his audience by the acuteness and brilliancy of his reasoning, though at the utmost only one of those assertions can be true. Which assertion is true must be determined by balancing arguments and probabilities, a process possessing nothing in common with that by which, in the pure science of number and form, conclusions of absolute certainty are aimed at through reasoning in which not only all that ought to be said, but all that can be said, is on one side. When the true view is reached by such balancing, all the further consequences which are involved in it may be developed by reasoning of mathematical rigidity. But there is less risk of error, even for an untutored mind, in the latter process than in the former,&mdash;less risk of reasoning wrongly from the premisses than of assuming wrong premisses to reason from.,This, then, is the truth which was distorted in the first objection. It is not the fact that a dogmatic or hasty temper is cultivated by contemplating the absolute certainty of mathematical truth, nor is the temper we allude to dogmatic or hasty, or found more among pure mathematicians than among logicians. It is a temper delighting in long and subtle trains of reasoning, by which, if the assertion ultimately appealed to in proof be only removed to a sufficient distance from the assertion to be proved, with&not; out involving obvious falsehood in any of the intermediate links, the person whose mind is so constituted may easily be dazzled into forgetting altogether the necessity of examining what support in facts there is for the last link. Now it is perhaps the greatest of all the educational advantages to be gained from the study of mathematics, that another portion of them, mixed mathematics , corrects this tendency which might result from the pure branches, by furnishing a discipline in the weighing of premisses not infe&not; rior to that which algebra and geometry furnish in the conduct of reasoning.,All natural phenomena may be mathematically calculated, which depend on the combination or variation of other phenomena in number and form. Thus, if a ship float two miles an hour north, with a current, and a wind springs up which, if there were no current, would blow her ten miles an hour west, her position at the end of an hour will be determined by combining the spaces and directions of two miles north and ten miles west, according to their mathematical relations merely as spaces and directions. In the same way, all other effects of what is called mechanical force are subject to mathe&not; matical calculation. But when water is produced by the mixture of oxygen and hydrogen, the oxygen and hydrogen undergo no change known to us as possessing any relation to number or form; they are not to be de&not; tected in the water, combined or varied in number or form, and thus chemical effects, of which this instance is a type, are not subjects of mathe&not; matical calculation. Now Mixed Mathematics are those natural sciences
which admit in this manner of mathematical calculation, and which are chiefly mechanics, optics, and astronomy. The first process in them is to ascertain the laws, or fixed order, according to which the phenomena under consideration are combined or varied in number and form. When the laws are obtained, it is a question of pure mathematics what, according to such laws, will be the result of any given set of circumstances. Thus mixed mathematics are the application of pure mathematics to certain natural sci&not; ences, the peculiar laws of each science being first ascertained ; and there are two causes why their study disciplines the mind to look carefully to the selection of the premisses from which it reasons.,First. The student is invited, at the threshold of each branch of mixed mathematics, not to assume the natural laws on which his calculations will have to be founded, but to examine the evidence for them, which of course must in every case be derived from experiment. Now it is not too much to say that there never was or will be an experiment of which a wrong ex&not; planation might not be given, so as to satisfy all the circumstances of the experiment itself, and which can therefore only be shown to be wrong by its failing to satisfy the circumstances of some other experiment partly similar and partly different ; consequently the establishment of natural laws is always an affair of balancing arguments and probabilities, just as are the practical problems which have to be solved in life.,Secondly. Many of the natural laws which are dealt with in mixed ma&not; thematics are so far from being self-evident that, when they have to be applied in circumstances of any complexity, the student is much more troubled to apply them accurately than he is with the mathematical calcu&not; lations based on their application. It is well known at Cambridge that in any complex and difficult arrangement of solid or fluid masses which may be invented to test the student, more will fail in conceiving rightly what are the forces and pressures called into operation, than in calculating rightly what will be their effect if the true nature of the forces and pressures is suggested to them.
