MAURICE, ON &ldquo;THE STUDIES OF THE COLLEGE.&rdquo; (SUMMARY.),(It has been thought that where the space at our command did not allow of our publishing Lectures at full length, some effectual help or useful hints might be supplied to teachers by inserting summaries of them, and sylla&not; buses of Courses. The following summaries are those of Lectures delivered at the (London) &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; by the Principal, during the pre&not; sent College Term, in continuation of the one printed in No. I. of this Magazine.),LECTURE I.&mdash;ART.,Examination of the words Art, Artful, Artifice, Artificial, Artificer, Arti&not; san, Artist.&mdash;The good and bad senses which have been given to each,
illustrated from different English authors. Main object of the lecture to determine the relation between these words ; but especially between the Artist and the Artisan. What Arts belong to a pastoral state of society. What Arts belong to the hunting state of society. The Nineveh sculptures ; what they indicate. Sense of the relation of men to Beings above and below themselves. Arts belonging to the agricultural state of society. Egyptian Arts, what is expressed in the Egyptian sculpture. Inference from all the cases hitherto considered. How this inference derives new force from the consideration of Greek Art. Homer&rsquo;s feeling about the Muses. How Greek history illustrates Greek Art in its glory and in its downfall. How modern history illustrates the meaning of Greek Art. Christian Art, its objects and its perils. How the Artist and Artisan may help each other.,LECTURE II.&mdash;SCIENCE.,Science and Art contrasted, yet necessary to each other. The art of Counting ; why it seems very natural and is very wonderful. Speculations about number. How the science of Arithmetic developes itself out of the art of Counting. The discovery of a law which is the same for all people everywhere. How Arithmetic becomes connected with Music. How it connected itself with the idea of society. Reference to the history of Py&not; thagoras. The art of Measuring ; how out of it is developed the science of Geometry. The Greek search in different directions after Science. How the Greek Sophist professed to teach all things. How Socrates led the young Greeks to seek for a Science beginning in the confession of igno&not; rance. How he made use of the facts of common life as an induction to Science. Why Plato wished all who frequented his schools to study Geo&not; metry. Use of Geometry to us in making us feel that we are to seek for that which is, not to be content with appearances. Why people in our day denounce Mathematics. Their worth to the working man.,LECTURE III.&mdash;WORDS.,The Rhetorician found in all stages of society, from the most barbarous to the most civilized. The art of producing effect by means of Avords pre&not; ceded any reflection upon their power. Homer&rsquo;s &ldquo; winged words.&rdquo; The speeches of his heroes. Different feelings of the two great tribes of Greece about the use of Words. How the Athenians began to look upon Words merely as powers, and so became the slaves of words. How they acquired the sense of Words being merely conventional. The fallacy and mischiev&not; ousness of that opinion. The teaching of Socrates respecting Words. Dif&not; ference between his method and what is called the logical method. What that is, and what at first it seems to be. Aristotle the great Logician, and also the great systematize!&rsquo; of all studies. How he became the great oracle in Christendom. How Logic began to usurp power over all other studies. How its authority was overthrown. How unfairly it was afterwards dis&not; paraged. What danger there is of its resuming its tyranny. How that danger may be averted.
ACCOUNT OF THE ORIGIN AND PROGRESS OF THE PEOPLE&rsquo;S COLLEGE AT SHEFFIELD.,The People&rsquo;s College at Sheffield originated seventeen years ago, with the Rev. R. S. Bayley, an Independent minister then residing in the town. In his Prospectus, he stated it to he his conviction, that the Mechanics&rsquo; Institutions generally had fallen far short in their efforts at popular education, and that the time was come when studies of a higher range, and moie likely to be really useful, ought to be placed within the reach of the youth of the Middle and Working Classes. To carry out his views, he proposed to establish classes embracing Latin, Greek, French, and German, Mathematics, English Literature in its various branches, Logic, Elocution, and Drawing, in addition to the more elementary subjects that commonly formed the curriculum of night schools. The classes were opened August 11th, 1842 ; the times of meeting were before and after the hours of labour. The morning classes met from half-past six to half-past seven ; the evening, &iacute;rom half-past seven to half-past nine. The fees charged amounted to about ninepence per week each. Perhaps the greatest novelty was the admission of women to the classes. Many were the doubts and fears entertained of this step, even by the most ardent friends of popular education. But Mr. Bayley thought that the men and women who had sufficient aspiration and self-denial to engage in the work of their own education, would not be the people to frustrate their own efforts by levity or indiscretion. Experi&not; ence has amply proved that, so far from the admission of women into classes, under proper restrictions, having an injurious effect upon instruction and discipline, a very opposite one is produced, an increased degree of decorum and self-respect being exhibited by both sexes.,The principal portion of the work in the classes fell upon Mr. Bayley, but he bore it with a resolution and an enthusiasm that can only be felt by a man thoroughly devoted to the carrying out of his own ideas. For several years he laboured thus indefatigably, and with marked success. He had a peculiar aptitude for engaging the attention of youth, and by his example and precepts, succeeded in raising the students almost to a pitch of enthusiasm in their work. And this was indeed necessary ; for many were the difficulties and disagreeables they had to encounter. The class-room, for the&rsquo; first year and a half, was a ghastly, whitewashed, uuplastered garret, not fitted up with the neces&not; sities, much less the conveniences of study. In this place the morning classes in winter were especially uninviting, and it required considerable devotion to study, to travel through snow at half-past six in a morning, before breakfast, to find a room probably without fire, or one but newly lighted by the monitor-student, to whose lot it had fallen for the week to perform that and kindred duties. But there was no trial that the Prin&not; cipal himself did not willingly endure, and no morning, however rough and uncomfort&not; able, but found him in attendance. This self-denying constancy on the part of Mr. Bayley, told beneficially upon the students at large ; they were attached at once to their studies, to the place, and to the man. They had found an &ldquo; Intellectual Home : &rdquo; ay, to them those bare walls were a palace. In December, 1848, the College removed to better rooms in Orchard Street. Here the same labours were renewed, and attended for several years with success ; but from a succession of untoward circumstances, which it is not necessary to particularize here, the members began gradually to fall off, till, at length in September, 1848, on Mr. Bayley leaving Sheffield for London, the College was all but extinct, and must have entirely ceased to exist, had it not been for the resolute efforts of a few of the remaining students.,These young men&mdash;sixteen in number, many of them under age&mdash;felt that with them rested the question of the continuance of the Institution, and that if it were not pos&not; sible for them to carry it on, they were at least bound to make the effort. They knew the benefit they themselves had derived from it, and felt assured that the town would not willingly let die an institution that had been of such signal public benefit. But they soon found that it was one thing to entertain a generous impulse, and another thing to put it into practice. The difficulties that beset them were immense ; they had neithei money nor friends. They cast about for the countenance and patronage of such men as were publicly known to be favourable to Popular Education, only to find out that their sympathy was just ш the inverse ratio to the obtrusiveness of their public profession But these young men possessed an experience that was more valuable than patronage In the first place, several years toil in the same classes had taught them to make an
accurate estimate of each other&rsquo;s powers and characteristics. This mutual knowledge was invaluable, as it enabled them at once to assign to each man the duty to which he was most peculiarly fitted. And in the second, their experience of the working of the system and discipline established by Mr. Bayley, enabled them to select only those parts which were likely to be suited to the altered circumstances of the Institution, and to introduce such new regulations as were thought to be demanded by the growing wants of the town. They at least knew what they wanted, and had had some inkling of the means of carrying it out, if the support of the town could in any moderate measure be depended upon, and, in spite of the croaking of friends and the sneering of enemies, they were determined at least upon a trial. Anxious to animate the new Institution with the general discipline and objects of the old one, it was determined generally to adopt the same classes, together with the methods of conducting them. Women were to be admitted, Party Politics and Sectarianism eschewed. The age of the students to be at least sixteen. The Institution to be self-supporting and, as a consequence, self- governing . The programme appeared taking and plausible on paper, at least to youth and hope; but to the sober judgment of experience it seemed only another of those attempts that had so frequently ended in failure. This doubt Time could alone determine, and as the People&rsquo;s College, as then established, has lived, hearty and vigorous, to its tenth year, it is evident there was a vitality in the principles propounded by those young men, that must modify largely all future speculations concerni ug adult Popular Educa&not; tion. How established, how these principles have been carried out, and what results have been reached, remains to be told.,T. Rowbotham.,(To be continued.),CORRESPONDENCE.,MR. GASKELL AND HIS ENGLISH LITERATURE CLASS AT MANCHESTER.,My dear L.,&mdash;As you propose to put into our Magazine papers on the ways of teaching different subjects, I beg you to let me ask Mr. Gaskell to give us a Paper on his method of teaching English Literature. His class on that subject is the most numerously attended one in the Manchester College ; and I have long heard of the great success of his ladies&rsquo; and other classes in English Literature. Our classes at the London College in it have not succeeded, and at Wolverhampton, I see that the E. L. Class began with fourteen pupils, ended with seven, and then disappeared.,It would be a great help to every teacher of this subject, if Mr. Gaskell would tell us how he teaches it ; and I trust that he will be able to do so in next month&rsquo;s Magazine, though we all know how much his time is taken up in his efforts to rid his city of the dirt and smoke and the ignorance and sin that are in it, and to help forward all the goodness and purity that are in it too. Yours,,F. J. SUPPLEMENT,то,THE &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;WORKING&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;MEN&rsquo;S&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;COLLEGE&lt;/span&gt; MAGAZINE.,APRIL 1. 1859.,FOURTH ANNUAL REPORT OF THE LONDON &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;WORKING&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;MEN&rsquo;S&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;COLLEGE&lt;/span&gt;.,It will be observed that the Report of the London &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; appears this year, for the first time, in a Magazine. That Magazine has been established for the purpose of recording, from month to month, the progress of the College ; of pointing out when it comes short of its pro&not; fessions ; of receiving hints concerning it from within and without ; of asso&not; ciating the students more closely with the teachers, and our own body with similar bodies in other parts of the country. An annual scrutiny therefore may be less necessary. The Council hope that the Magazine may be re&not; gularly circulated among those who take an interest in popular education ; so they wih judge far better of proceedings than from the mere notices of a Report.,During the last year (1858) the Council have been urged by the Students to explain more fully how their different lessons may form a regular course of study. That a desire of this kind should have proceeded, not from the teachers, but from the learners, is a proof that some good has been done. The Council had always wished to avoid desultory instruction, believing that those who have only a few hours to spare from their work should eco&not; nomize those hours most carefully. It has now been proved that working men understand this principle themselves, and are as impatient of mere scraps of irregular information as we could wish them to be. The Council at once appointed Committees to consider what might be done for the pur&not; pose of organizing more perfectly the lessons in their different sections. The Mathematical Section very speedily printed a report, which was published in the first number of the Magazine ; one from the historical section ap&not; pealed in the second, ihe hints contained in them will be acted upon in the ensuing Term, And the Council also resolved that certain members of their body should meet those who were entering the College at the begin&not; ning of each term, for the purpose of giving them any advice they might desire respecting the classes, then&middot; relation to each other, and with which class it would be wisest for any particular student to begin.,Still more important than the arrangement of studies, is the adoption of the students themselves into the work of teaching. A great step was taken in this direction by the establishment of Preparatory Classes, each superin&not; tended by one of the advanced pupils of the College. This plan has worked very successfully. The preparatory teachers have not shrunk from the task they imposed upon themselves. They have fitted many students for the higher instruction. Still this arrangement is not sufficient for the wants of the College. Permission has been given to every teacher of a class to as&not; sociate with himself a Tutor chosen from among his pupils, who shall assist the members of it privately as well as publicly.,The Council has this year adopted two of the certificated students into,VOL. I.
itself. The intention to do so was announced in one of our early pro&not; grammes. They hope in due time that the students will form not less than a third of the governing Body. The Committee of Students is also in frequent communication with them.,The accompanying tables will show what the attendance has been upon the respective classes, what are their occupations, and which of them have received certificates of competency.,The finances of the College are best explained by the accompanying abs&not; tract of accounts.,Two friends have lately placed at our disposal for a time the interest of a sum of &iquest;900, which &lambda;&nu;ϊ&Pi; be devoted, while it lasts, to the Building Fund. By gradually extinguishing the debt still remaining on our house (&pound;772), we shall be making ourselves very nearly independent of external aid. To become so, is our great wish. &quot;Until we accomplish this object we shall be ready to accept the help of any who may think (as we think) that it is a good thing to have a place in this great city \vhere whatever advantages education can give are offered to all who can afiord houseroom, gas, and firing.,The Library has received additions during the past year, partly from gifts, partly by purchase, to the amount of 498 volumes.,The Museum has benefited still more by the generosity of friends. But the Council wish to record with especial pleasure how much it is also in&not; debted to the diligence of the students themselves, in collecting and arrang&not; ing specimens.,If the funds permitted it, they would rejoice to enlarge this department of the College, to build rooms for it in their garden, and to connect it with an exhibition of works of industry.,The Council have rejoiced in the movements which have been made by the Universities and by the Society of Arts during the past year for the general education of the country. The rules and limitations which the former have laid down respecting age, shuts out a great majority of our students from the examination. With the latter, we have felt that we cannot unite without giving up our claim to the name of College as distinguished from that of &ldquo; Mechanics&rsquo; Institute.&rdquo; It is important on all accounts that we should retain our own distinctive character ; if that could be secured, co-operation with powerful and established Bodies would of course be desirable. In the meantime, we have drawn more closely our ties to those institutions which do adopt our name. Nothing has been more gratifying than the intercourse we have maintained with the Colleges at Manchester (including Ancoats and Salford), Wolverhampton, Caraoridge, Sheffield, Oxford, and Halifax ; we earnestly hope to see them far surpass&not; ing us in their labours, and presenting an example for our imitation.,1858.,Certificates of Competency have been granted in the following Sub&not; jects :&mdash;-,Arithmetic. &mdash;W. THE MUSEUM.,Report .&mdash; Two years and a half have i now passed since the contributions of a few students laid the foundation of our College Museum. Its growth since then has been unexpectedly rapid, donations having been literally showered upon it. Its increase, however, has been impeded by one considerable drawback : our funds have not been able to provide suitable accommodation for the specimens as fast as they have arrived, and though every&not; thing we have is safely housed, not more than three-fourths of the collection can be exhibited. During the first year there were received about 600 specimens, the second year brought about 1700 more, and the first four months of the third (current) year have added nearly 1000; so that now, exclusive of the Botanical portion, there are upwards of 3000 fossils, minerals, recent shells, and antiquities, in the drawers of the Museum. Of these, nearly 1000 fossils are mounted on ta&not; blets and named, and about 750 minerals are placed in cabinets and in process of naming. Among the teachers who have given us donations there are Mr. Ruskin (to whom we owe a noble collection of minerals), Mr. F umivali, Mr. Slade, Mr. Litchfield, Mr. Ludlow, and Mr. Hughes. And among those unconnected with the College are noticeable, the South Kensing&not; ton Museum, the United Service Institu&not; tion, Mr. N. Wetherell, Mr. R. Trench, F.G.S., Mr. S. P. Woodward, F.G.S., Mr. J. Prestwich, P.G.S., Miss Spottiswoode, and Miss Lushington. Of the students, from thirty to forty in number, who have given specimens we can give no exact list, but their donations are looked upon as our most interesting acquisitions, and as an evidence that the wish to have a real museum, and, if possible, to give the na&not; tural history sciences a fair position in the College studie : in illustration of this it may be mentioned that of 17 dona&not; tions received in the last four months, 13 were from students. Yet, notwithstanding this sympathy and willingness to give up private collections for the common good, it must be admitted there has always been far more indifference than is desirable shown as to what becomes of these gifts ; and although a register has not been kept, it may be safely said that there are not more than 500 visitors to the Museum during the year. Students have been very remiss too in coming forward to take charge of and arrange the collection. Two terms ago four students, who from the be&not;,ginning had shown an interest in the Mu&not; seum, took on themselves the responsibility of becoming curators of the Geological di&not; vision. Each curator has allotted to him&not; self a class of animals in a particular for&not; mation, mounts on tablets all fossils be&not; longing to that class, and writes cata&not; logues of each order, to each of which is prefixed an account of its anatomy, phy&not; siology, general habits, and geological his&not; tory, and a list of the genera it includes. A list of the specimens, or index to the Museum, is also in preparation. The cu&not; rators think that the advantages they thus obtain are important beyond estimation, because they could not be had in any other way. They would wish to impress this particularly on their fellow-students, whom they heartily invite to undertake like charge of the plants, recent shells, minerals, and coins. It has long been a matter of sur&not; prise that curators in Botany have not come forward, considering there was once a good Botanical Class. The natural his&not; tory of the mollusca would give excellent application for a knowledge of physiology ; and the antiquities might well be taken charge of by a student in history. It may be remarked that it is not so much a knowledge of these subjects that will qualify any one for taking charge of the specimens mentioned, as a desire to re&not; ceive the knowledge, and the mental train&not; ing, they are able to give. We have ar&not; ranged for the systematic distribution of duplicates, and as specimens accumulate, notices will, from time to time, be given of their nature. A Geological and Ueneral Natural History Library in connection with the Museum is also in course of formation, of which a statement will hereafter be given. The foUowing is an analysis of the contents of the Museum -.&mdash;Fossils, 2,040 ; minerals, 800; recent shells, etc., 270; plants, etc., about 150 ; sponges, etc., 70 ; and antiquities, 110. The fossils consist of&mdash;,Crag, mounted, Eocene &bdquo;,Chalk,Green-sand &bdquo; Oolite &bdquo;,Carboniferous Devonian ,, Silurian,60,285,176,135,220,5,0,0,unmounted, 260 20 34 80 55 60 240,Miscellaneous, not yet classified, 410.,Henry Seeley, Student, Secretary to the Museum.

THE,IMorl&uuml;ng ||[eiťs College Hlagaţine.,No. У. MAY X, 1859.,PHYSICAL EDUCATION.,Every man is born into this world with a body, as well as an intellect and a soul. This is perhaps an unfortunate fact, nevertheless a fact it is, and these bodies are quite as sensitive to good or bad treatment as our intellects. They will repay careful training and resent neglect in the same way, and you can no more let any muscle of your body lie idle without weakening it than you can your memory or reasoning powers. The body and mind act and react on one another ; a vigorous body gives a healthiness to a man&rsquo;s mental work, and enables him to throw it off with a freshness which will make it go twice as far, and affects every one who comes within its influence.,So every one engaged or interested in education (and who doesn&rsquo;t fall within the category ?) should have an eye to turning out the body of every boy, girl, man, and woman, thoroughly well educated&mdash;trained to as high perfection as it is capable of. Depend on it, no better start in life can be given than this, into whatever profession or trade a man may go. He has made a good servant of his body for life instead of a slip-shod grumbler ; and the good servant will carry him about in an easy and re&not; joicing manner in gratitude for his early care, instead of dropping him at every pinch, and hindering instead of helping his work. Apart from all connection between body and mind, a consciousness of bodily power, of being able to put out his whole strength at any moment in the most effectual way, gives a man a sense of independence and self-reliance which is worth a good deal in this struggling world. Not a day passes in the life of any man in which a quick eye, a strong and steady hand, or swift feet, may not stand him in good stead or enable him to help or protect some weak or helpless person. The life of those dearest to a man may depend upon whether he can run a mile in five minutes, and so catch a train and fetch a doctor. He may tumble off a steamer or pier, or see some one else tumble off ; he may find himself in a field with an uncomfortable bull, or have a dog run at him ; he may see a woman struck in the streets ; he may get a lift in a butcher&rsquo;s cart, and be run away with and upset&mdash;and let me tell him that if this vulgar accident should happen, the chances will be ten to one in his favour if his limbs are supple and well educated, for they will then gather themselves up together by instinct, and fall at the best possible angle, while constrained limbs will scatter themselves about and get broken in the most perverse manner.,Now all the best educators in the history of the world&mdash;the old Greeks in particular&mdash;have recognized these facts, and taken great pains to de- velope the body properly. And as we, the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; of England, mean to take the shine out of all the old Pagans who ever edu&not; cated, and (due allowance being made for hindrances, arising from the,h 2
position of our Colleges in the centre of huge towns, from the necessary daily occupations of our members, etc. etc.) to turn out a better article than the world has yet seen, we must set ourselves seriously to work on this matter of Physical Education.,What hints and help then can we get from our contemporaries, the Uni&not; versities and Public Schools of England ? Well, nothing can be better than rowing, cricket, foot-ball, rackets, and swimming, and many of the other games which are popular at these places. There is little need there for encouraging these studies ; the public feeling at every school and col&not; lege is so strongly in favour of them, that a man or boy who is not reason&not; ably proficient in some or more can never have his full weight in the little commonwealth, and this leads many to cultivate them more or less who might otherwise have neglected them, and had torpid livers at twenty-five. But at the first glance we see that our position is hope&not; lessly unlike theirs. They have more leisure than they want ; we, less. They have the best and brightest hours of the day at command, and can bring fresh, well fed and cared-for bodies to their sports ; we can very rarely meet for these or any other purposes till late in the evening, and after a hard day&rsquo;s work of head or hand at other matters. They have rivers and cricket-grounds and racket-courts at their doors, in fact belong&not; ing to them, and plenty of money to make the most of these advantages ; we have no money, and are hours away from any such luxuries. So that we must fall back on our own resources, and strike out a path for our&not; selves&mdash;resolving, however, as soon as possible to get, by hook or by crook, all the advantages we can honestly lay our hands on, which our elder bre&not; thren have got before us.,And полу to come to the case of our London College, which is the only one with whose doings I am conversant, let us see what steps have been taken, and what are possible, in this matter of physical education. Amongst us public opinion is as yet scarcely sufficiently alive on the subject ; and no doubt the want of space, and the difficulty of getting any proper means and appliances Avithin reasonable distance, is much against us. However, we have now a regular cricket-club, which has taken a ground, not at a hopeless distance from the College, for two evenings in the week, and the members are bent on working it well and getting all they can out of it.,Nothing can be better than this as far as it goes; cricket is an exercise which developes eveiy part of the body, and makes it ready for anything, and as an education of the eye is perhaps the best that can be named. I remember well, in the Eussian war, reading some most admirable letters from an officer, a cricketer, who was doing engineering work in the trenches before Sebastopol, in one of which he said that the round-shot which were for ever coming at him were very much like cricket-balls from a moderately swift bowler ; he could judge them quite as accurately, and by just turning round when the gun, which bore on him, was fired, and marking the first pitch of the shot, he could tell in a moment whether to move or not, and so got on with his work veiy comfortably. But there are great difficulties about cricket for our students. First, grounds in the neighbourhood of large towns are very dear ; we pay &pound;5 for the privilege of playing two evenings a week, on a very moderately good ground, which we share with two other clubs. The tools also are costly, and we have
not enough spare time. You can&rsquo;t get enough cricket under two or three hours. Again, you want numbers to play cricket to advantage ; unless we &ccedil;an muster thirteen at least, and play almost every day, we shall never turn out very skilful players. Nevertheless, cricket is very popular with us, and rightly so ; but that is no reason why the enthusiasts should take to practising in our little garden, at the back of the College, on the summer evenings ; for there is not room enough, and a gravel walk, with tan laid down at one end, is not, and never will be, a decent cricket-ground.,Besides, the little garden is wanted for other exercises ; it is the only space we have for our gymnasium ; and, considering its capabilities, T think it is made as much of as any part of the College premises. The garden (be it known to our provincial readers) is some lifty yards long by thirty feet across, and is happily divided from our neighbours by a reason&not; ably high wall. At the further end of it is a high plane-tree, sheltering an old summer-house, in which coats and waistcoats may be stowed away, and under the shade of the tree is a small raised platform, edged with brickwork, with a seat against the wall, on which tired athletes may re&not; pose, and look complacently on the performances. This brickwork though is out of place, and we purpose removing it very shortly, for exactly in front of it is our arena, which we have carefully laid down with tan over the hard gravel ; and here the boxing-class is held, and the gymnastic poles are set up. We propose to add wrestling to our curriculum, and this cannot be done until the bricks are removed ; for in wrestling you must foil, and you can&rsquo;t choose whether you will fall two feet this way or that wa y an d it is all very well to lall on tan, but to come with anything but a very soft part of your body on to the edge of bricks is quite another affair, and more likely to hinder than to help the education of the body.,Does anybody object to our boxing class ? Well, if they do, all we can say is that it has been willingly sanctioned by the College Council ; so it is their business to defend it and not mine, and the objectors can address them on the subject.,It has been going now for nearly three years, and from all appearances is likely to become more, rather than less popular. At first we were a select few, in the first year numbering, I think, only some fourteen mem- beis, but now we have at least forty members, the earnestness and constancy of whose practice the battered state of our gloves proves most satisfactorily. We have members of all sorts, who join from all sorts of motives,&mdash;from the steady, sober men, engaged all day in sedentary employments, who come because they find that they can here get the hardest exercise in the shortest time, and who have found out what good they get from it, down to the youngsters, who come for the fun of the thing and the general propensity which marks the youthful Briton of every class for pummeling mammals of like age and growth with himself. But, so far as I have seen (and I ave been a very regular attendant), nothing whatever has happened which could induce I will not say our Council, but a peace-at-any-price man&mdash;- to wish that the class had not been sanctioned. I think that, both in ac&not; tivity and strength, and in temper and good feeling, the regular members have benefited markedly, and all the members more or less. The beauty ot boxing is that you can get your exercise in a very short time, and that all the muscles of the body and the lungs are used at once. Many other
kindred sports devclope some few muscles at the expense of others,&mdash;for instance, fencing and single-stick, which work a man&rsquo;s right arm and left leg almost exclusively. Wrestling is as good as boxing in this particular, and for this reason we mean to cultivate it.,Then, for change, we have horizontal and parallel bars, whereon the usual feats of turning, travelling, touching the chin, shoulder, etc., are performed, and these are all good in their way ; and between the cricket, the boxing, and the poles, it is a student&rsquo;s own fault if he does not get a fair allowance of good exercise for his body at the College.,But in this, as in all our other branches of education, the greater part of the work must be done by students themselves, away from the College. So now for a few words on this subject.,Of course the first condition, the sine qua non, of having a body fit for anything, is temperance in all things ; the second, cleanliness. Of these I have no space to speak, and besides it is not so directly my business.,The next is, as much fresh air as you can get. If you are obliged to work in close, badly ventilated workshops or other places all day, it is all the more needful for you to get as much fresh air as you can elsewhere. Therefore always get five or six miles&rsquo; walk in the course of the twenty-four hours; the night is just as good as the day for this purpose. I used, when 1 first came to town, often to run or walk round the Regent&rsquo;s Park at night, and a very good course it is ; and when you are walking, don&rsquo;t dawdle. Choose quiet streets, so as not to attract notice, and then go the pace, never less than four miles an hour, and the more you can put on the top of that the better. A walk, to do you any great good, ought to make you feel a little uncomfortable about the muscles between the knee and ankle.,If you can get running (as suggested above, at night or in any quiet place), don&rsquo;t miss the chance.,Always sleep with your window open. Directly after washing in the morning take five minutes, at least, of the extension motions&mdash;the com&not; mon ones which are taught in the ordinary drill&mdash;doing them vigorously till you hear your muscles, and feel every limb perfectly loose. Then a few minutes with a light pair of dumb-bells or clubs ; and in working these work them upwards and round your head, which is far the most telling way. If you can&rsquo;t get dumb-bells or clubs, take two chairs and put them two feet apart, and then use them as horizontal bars, leaning one hand on each, and going down and touching first one shoulder and then the other, till you feel that your muscles have had enough. Then drink a glass of fresh water and make your toilet, and if you are not fit for work two stone above your usual weight that day, why then don&rsquo;t take my word again.,Since this article was written, events have happened which make it very probable that we Englishmen of this generation may, in a few months, be &#39;engaged in a life-and-death struggle with half the Continent. I be&not; lieve that every man who reads this will come forward then, and give his goods, his body, his life, if necessary, for the old country and her women and children.,Now, my dear readers, you will be of just twice as much use at that pinch if vou have* bodies inured to fatigue, open air, and violent exercise ; or rather,
if you can&rsquo;t sleep out at night without getting ill, or march twenty-five miles without knocking up, you will be most likely (nine out of ten of you at any rate) of no use at all. So set about the matter in good earnest at once, and notwithstanding our great disadvantages, let our students at the end of this summer lay their account to being as fit for a hard cam&not; paign as any equal number of men at Oxford or Cambridge.,ENGLISH COMPOSITION, AS A STUDY IN A &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;WORKING&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;MEN&rsquo;S&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;COLLEGE&lt;/span&gt;.,It is because we feel how important English Literature has been and is to ourselves, that we, the Council, have given it a place among the studies of the College.,What do we mean by &ldquo;English Literature&rdquo;? In English Literature lie History, Philosophy, Science,&mdash;every kind of knowledge : English Lite&not; rature is a vast, miscellaneous thing. Putting aside however all books on particular sciences requiring knowledge of a special kind, and seeking only special classes of readers, there remains a mass of multiform writings which address almost all readers, because they deal chiefly with the fami&not; liar facts of our common daily life ; this is the English Literature we have in view. Poetry is the most important department of this literature ; other departments are novels, tales, essays, leading articles, etc. etc. Even so limited, &ldquo; English Literature &rdquo; is vast enough, miscellaneous enough ; and methodical instruction concerning it, after much experience and with many appliances, would still be a very imperfect work.,Much, I dare say, might be done in time. We might take author by author, book by book, century by century ; our classes might be more frequent, our lectures more systematic ; and, in short, English Literature might be taught in a more determined and satisfactory manner than, so far as I know, has ever yet been anywhere so much as attempted. But this is far from my mark. At present I wish only to state, in few words, how a weekly class in English Composition may&mdash;for this I say again is our object&mdash;help our men in the study of English Literature, and towards that which is the end of such study, the formation of a sound and manly judgment.,Writing, then, makes a person think out his subject in hand ; think it out, if it be a matter of reflection ; see it fully, if a matter of description : there is no one who does not feel his subject grow under his pen ; more&not; over it grows clearer also. &ldquo;Writing,&rdquo; said and says Lord Bacon, &ldquo; maketh an exact man.&rdquo; To unfold his thoughts on this subject and on that, bring them out into distinct consciousness, may well be a delight to the student, and profitable in many ways, as thinking, or even trying to think, always is. Again, to put together his thoughts, arrange them, and,produce them in a finished shape, every comma right, this further effort_,partly painful, partly pleasant&mdash;is a true piece of literary work for him, and will help to make him, as nothing else will, a workman with his pen. As for the thing produced, that will probably be dissatisfying to our student,
very dissatisfying, even as the expression of his own thoughts, but this result will follow : he will be set upon inquiry, he will be led to observe with far more intimate interest than before the thoughts and workmanship o&iacute; great authors dealing with topics at all similar to those upon which he himself has been busy. This is perhaps the chief benefit to be expected from the practice of English Composition ; it will lead to a closer study of good books, and generally to a habit of more critical reading. But, be&not; sides this, the student in our class, if only he work well and produce his exercises time after time to be criticized, will continually be brought face to face with the mind of the Teacher ; the Teacher will freely bandy opi&not; nions with him on the quality of his work, and will moreover find com&not; fortable opportunities of speaking his experience, his knowledge, or belief concerning many things, not literary only, which could not otherwise be conveniently approached.,On the whole, a class in English Composition ought, I think, to be a pleasant class, and profitable too, especially to those of our students (nu&not; merous now) who take an interest in books, and feel a natural desire to be able to express their thoughts on paper with more facility than they now can do. The facility will come in time, no doubt, for as in speaking, so in writing, there is a certain craft which can be learnt by practice (long practice) ; and this facility will for common purposes be useful and plea&not; surable, say for writing letters to friends ; but how are the thoughts to come? especially the good thoughts, of which good writing finally con&not; sists. That is another question ! That problem lies almost entirely outside of any class-room whatever. Perhaps the first lesson we have to teach, and the last, is that good thoughts come of natural gifts and manful lives, no otherwise,--and that our business is to recognize with grateful joy where the gifts have been bountifully bestowed and nobly used.,A few words as to the conduct of the class in English Composition, which I have carried on in the College. We begin with a piece of dicta&not; tion, prose or verse, as the case may be,&mdash;a piece chosen for its value and delight ; I set some importance upon this. Then we read in class some book together, of compass such that it can be finished in the Term ; this Term it was Shakespeare&rsquo;s &lsquo; Macbeth :&rsquo; we read perhaps not very critically, but with criticism more or less, and question, and remark, and make a social work of it. This occupies the first hour of the class. The remain&not; ing hour is given to looking over the exercises sent in during the week ; sometimes they are read aloud and criticized, sometimes otherwise ; and in that case the rest of the class engage themselves meanwhile in looking- over each other&rsquo;s work. Two subjects are set weekly for Composition ; and I sometimes give a few hints to assist the students, to be followed or not, as they choose,&mdash;indeed I invite them to choose their own subjects alto&not; gether, if they prefer. The following fist of some already given may indi&not; cate the nature of our work :&mdash;,Eeasons for and against early closing.,Westminster Abbey and St. Paul&rsquo;s.,Reasons for and against the ballot.,The different influences of commercial and agricultural employments.,Describe the course of the Thames, and the principal places on the banks.
Name the parks of London, stating tlie position, etc., of each.,Define the words &ldquo; Monarchy,&rdquo; &ldquo; Aristocracy,&rdquo; &ldquo; Democracy,&rdquo; &ldquo; Con&not; stitution,&rdquo; &ldquo; Parliament,&rdquo; adding remarks.,Name and illustrate the force of the various stops and other symbols used in writing.,April 38 . Vernon Lushington.,THE ENGLISH LITERATURE CLASS AT MANCHESTER.,To the Editor of the &lsquo; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&#39;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; Magazine .&rsquo;,Plymouth Grove, Manchester, Apr&igrave;l 18 , 1859 .,My dear Sir, &mdash;For fear lest you write me down too unlettered a man to have charge of a class in Literature, I must send a short and hurried answer to the request you make respecting my mode of conducting it, and reserve a more detailed account for some calends which I am always looking forward to, but which prove Greek ones, when I may have a little spare time.,The first object which I aim at, is to inspire my students with a liking for the subject, feeling that without this there will be little of that true spontaneous work on which, of course, we are obliged to depend. If I have had any success whatever, I attribute it mainly to the sympathy which I may have excited with my own feelings of reverence and love for our grand old writers. I dread anything which wears the appearance of a puff, but, simply in order to show that this object may be attained, I will copy a few lines out of a note which I received a short time ago from one of my students, who was obliged, &ldquo; with extreme reluctance,&rdquo; to discon&not; tinue his attendance on the class. He says :&mdash;&ldquo; In taking this step, I should be wrong not to express my sense of the great obligations under which you have laid me. Your lectures in the first Term on the English language, revealed, as it were, a new world to me; whilst the history and examples of early English literature, as given in your later lectures, have afforded me a clearer notion of its value than I could possibly have gained by my own unaided study.&rdquo;,As your own experience will have shown, it may be necessary, in some degree, to lure on learners at first, and adopt the plan of sprinkling &ldquo; di soave licor gli orli del vaso ;&rdquo; but still, I trust, we keep in view the great end which the College is designed to answer, and do not forget that&quot;real knowledge can only be bought at the price of real work.,During the first Term we confined our attention to the history and com&not; position of the language. This proved so interesting to the generality of the students, that I was induced to go somewhat more minutely into the subject than I at first intended. One of our exercises was taking some Latin or Greek root, and trying to trace it through all its variations and compounds. In order to do this more readily, I got the class to make themselves familiar with the Greek alphabet. And you cannot think what a benevolent eagerness was shown in hunting out stray members of a fa&not; mily, and restoring them to their relatives.,We commenced the Literature at the beginning of the second Term,
starting with the Anglo-Saxon period ; and we have now worked onr way steadily down to the reign of Charles II. We take as a text-book &lsquo;Spalding&rsquo;s Outlines.&rsquo; Onr method of using it is this:&mdash;One of the stu&not; dents reads out a portion, and I take the opportunity of asking the strict meaning of the more remarkable words (an exercise to which I attach much importance), of tracing their derivation, and of throwing in freely such remarks and illustrations as may seem to be required, or help to keep up the attention. I then go over the same ground more carefully in my own lectures, dwelling at length on works that are of peculiar value, or not likely to be accessible to the students generally. At the close of the last Term, for example, I gave such an analysis and characterization of the writings of Bacon as could be brought within the hour.,A few of the students have paid some attention to composition, and several of the essays given in have shown considerable power both of thought and expression. These I look over at home, making in pencil such remarks and corrections as are called for. And, now and then, the essays have been read aloud in the class.,In conclusion, 1 may say that though, on the whole, I have had reason to be satisfied with the working of the class, I feel that it ought to be divided into three&mdash;for Language, Composition, and Literature&mdash;with a separate teacher to each ; and I could then labour with more closeness and efficiency in one of these departments, than I can at present in all three.,I fear this is but an imperfect reply to your wish, but it is the best which my time will allow me to give ; and I must remain, my dear Sir,,Yours very faithfully,,William&quot; Gtaskell.,LECTURE ON &ldquo;DECORATIVE ART AND ITS RELATION TO MANUFACTURES.&rdquo;,[Mr. Ruskin lately opened the Bradford School of Design by an Intro- ductorv Lecture. The following extracts from the sketch of the Address, given in a local paper, will probably interest the members of the three Colleges established in the great manufacturing metropolis.],Portable pictures are not decorative Art ; only that can be so called of which decoration is the main intent, whether of the interior of a dome or the base of a candlestick. All Art may be decorative ; the greatest Art hitherto produced has been decorative. Thus Phidias produced his great&not; est works for the adornment of the Pantheon ; Raphael, for that of the Vati&not; can ; Michael Angelo lavished his genius on a ceiling ; Tintoretto, on a wall and ceiling ; Correggio, on home interiors ; while Titian and Veronese threw out some of their finest thoughts on the outside of walls. I have seen at Venice some of Veronese&rsquo;s work actually effaced by the placarded tribute of gratitude to a curate, stencilled in great black letters ! But there must be a distinction observed between the less and the greater kind of decora&not; tive Art. The last is appropriate only where the work cannot be disturbed and can be fully seen. Thus on walls Correggio painted arcades, with clus&not; tering vines and children between them as if leaping into the room. And
Tintoretto at tlie end of a certain room imagined Paradise. But where work is liable to injury, or cannot be distinctly seen, or is subject to be altered by use, as in the folds of a dress, a general effect of richness only can be attained. Hence an inferior kind of Art, characterized by simplicity and continuousness, by less of the natural and perfect form, by zigzag, checks, and conventional patterns. The true rule is to put into your Avork as much nature and beauty as it will rightly bear, not to aim too high from a fear of degrading Art. Such degradation is a wise degradation. Compare a Greek vase with a piece of Dresden china. The finished landscapes of the latter give a feeling of incongruity, while the simple yet perfect forms of the classic model harmonize beautifully with the material and its use. A want of a sense of propriety in this respect is an essential vulgarity, but a vulgarity into which many are led from a desire to show what can be done with their material. Hence styles of decoration which spurn a becoming conventionalism, and overlay all material alike with the results of a natural&not; istic Art, which is entirely bad. How then is the true rule to be reached ? Is the designer to keep to his zigzags and his checks ? Not so ; the true way of approach to a system of wise conventionalism in decoration is from the knowledge and practice of a higher Art. The designer may approach his task from above, or from below : in the former case, applying the laws of the noblest Art ; in the latter, regarding only the dispositions of lines and colours. Savage or half-civilized nations universally approach it from below. Hence the patterns of Indian shawls, Indian maidens in the drawing- school of Madras create patterns by the sweep of the hand, letting sand fall between their fingers in the graceful curves thus fonned. But the cultiva&not; ted artist has always approached his part from above. Witness such forms as those most to be found among the models of Schools of Design, as Eaphael&rsquo;s ornamentation of the Loggie of the Vatican; or the cinque&not; cento work given as models in Schools of Sculpture. Here we have the play of great minds formed on a true knowledge of nature, and espe&not; cially of the figure. This representation of the figure was the life of all Egyptian, of all mediaeval ornament. . . . Look again to the Greek power of outline, gained by study of the figure, to Byzantine ornament, to Norman&mdash;still figure, figure, figure ! In the Bayeux tapestry Matilda has depicted every kind of circumstance connected with the Norman invasion, even to the style and material of the Conqueror&rsquo;s dinner. We need then the power to delineate life, together with a necessary conventionalism. At present there is no good ornamental design in Europe. The French indeed have a more delicate eye for colour than any nation. Still neither they nor any other people have such a knowledge of design as the artist&rsquo;s study of life would give.. One good result of that study would be that we should get rid of that notion of symmetry as consisting in formal balance, which has become the bane of Art. The Greeks understood that beauty lay in connected proportion, not in the balance of equal things. We, on the other hand, draw a leaf on one side of the stalk, and then to correspond a pre&not; cisely similar leaf on the oteher. We want to understand that for true harmony there is needed not a reverse, but a companion.,[Mr. Kuskin afterwards adverted to what might seem to many a ground of hopelessness in the depressing circumstances of manufacturing life.] lu such circumstances high design is often simply impossible, design not being the offspring of idle fancy, but the accumulated result of the ob-
servation of beautiful things,&mdash;a condition not to be attained apart from peace and pleasurable occupation. And yet one ground of hope lies here. In the Middle Ages, the highest Art did but minister to the pride of life among a nobility who lived by robbery and oppression. Hence, in the very triumph of Art, there ever came a prestige of ruin. No lesson of history is surer than this. The names of great painters sound like passing bells. Velasquez lived in the time of the downfall of Spain ; Leonardo, in that of &Lambda; enice ; Raphael, in that of Rome. We in modern times may yet attain a loftier and a lovelier privilege than ancient nobles knew, in bringing the power and charm of Art within reach of the poor. The splendour of Art may be past. Let us seek a greater glory in its humility. Another con&not; dition of things may yet appear in which men, neither oppressed by labour nor wasted by vanity, may associate with the tenderness and holiness of domestic life the pursuit of all the arts of peace. England shall then neither be blasted by furnaces nor encumbered by palaces. Manufac&not; turers have great power in their hands. Let them only act with nobleness and independence,&mdash;not following the demand of the market, cringing to the caprices of fashion, pilfering from each other whatever attracts the popu&not; lar whim of the hour : let them rather form and lead the market, by pre&not; senting what in the end will elevate and refine, disregarding the vulgar cry for the showy and tawdry and adorning stuffs, suitable for every-day, homely use, with devices both beautiful and quaint. This was the work of Wedg&not; wood for his own peculiar ware. In truth the manufacturer may be re&not; garded as an author, with power to direct the taste and form the mind of his fellow-men. Talk against ambition ! why the fault is that we are none of us ambitious enough. We may be rulers if we only will !,CORRESPONDEN CE.,Mr. Editor, &mdash;Permit me to occupy a little space in the next number of the &ldquo; Maga&not; zine,&rdquo; in order to correct that portion of the (London) &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; Report&mdash; your April Supplement&mdash;which refers to the Associate Examination. Instead of &ldquo;Dy&not; namics,&rdquo; in the list of subjects, should have appeared &ldquo; Mechanics&rdquo; (including Statics and Dynamics) ; and in the immediately succeeding portion, credit should have been given to me for the possession of a Certificate in Euclid, in addition to the one men&not; tioned. My Euclid Certificate (the first in that subject granted by the College) is dated &ldquo; Michaelmas, 1856,&rdquo; a fact which I beg to recommend to the notice of the com&not; piler of the &lsquo; College Record Book,&rsquo; to which your correspondent, Mr. Dryasdust, refers.,Permit me, also, Mr. Editor, to suggest the occasional insertion of examination papers on the subjects taught in the College, if your space will allow. They should be the actual papers used in past examinations, for a fair proportion of correct answers in which Certificates of Competency have been given. I think this, if adopted, will serve more than one useful purpose. It would, in the first place, stamp with something like its fair value the rather mysterious-looking &ldquo; C. S.,&rdquo; which some of us have appended to our names. It would (and this is much more important) enable the various &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; throughout the country to assimilate their tests of competency to a common standard ; and also it would prove, to such as care to inquire, that real work is being done in such Colleges as our own in Great Ormond Street.,Other reasons might be adduced in support of the course suggested, hut if you agree with me, that it would serve the purposes I have already indicated, I am sure I need not trespass further upon your space. Yours very truly,,April, 1859. John Roebuck.
MANCHESTER &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;WORKING&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;MEN&rsquo;S&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;COLLEGE&lt;/span&gt;.,OPENING OF THE THIRD TERM.,A General Meeting of the Council and students was held in one of the upper rooms at the Mechanics&rsquo; Institute, David Street, on Wednesday evening, March 30th. The meeting partook principally of a social cha&not; racter, and was intended primarily to pro&not; mote more intimate social relationship amongst the members of the College ; a subsidiary object being to impart informa&not; tion to the students respecting the course to he pursued in the various classes during the ensuing term, commencing on the 4th inst. A large number of the council, the teachers, students, and others interested in the success of the College, were present. Tea and coffee having been served, the ta&not; bles were spread with a series of beautiful lithographs, and experiments were made with electrical, chromatic, and other appa&not; ratus, calculated to interest and instruct those present.,Mr. Oliver Heywood, the chairman of the College, having been called upon to preside, stated that it was not intended to detract from the essentially social charac&not; ter of the gathering by a set of formal speeches ; but some information respect&not; ing the working of the College during the past term would be communicated by Mr. Greenwood ; Professor Scott would deliver an address ; and, subsequently, the teachers of the classes would state the course they intended to pursue in the coming term.,Mr. Greenwood said that the number of students had been slightly less than in previous terms, but the attendance at the classes would, he believed, bear favourable comparison with any previous term. As term after term passed by, he believed the College was gradually falling into a normal condition ; and the students were not in&not; duced to attend by any vague feeling of curiosity, which probably operated in the earlier terms, but by higher motives, and a sincere desire to prolit by the facilities afforded them. During the term a Greek class had been formed, and was, he be&not; lieved, progressing favourably ; the num&not; ber of the students was twenty-three. In the coming term, one or two changes would be made. The College would lose the benefit of the services of Dr. Guthrie in the Geometry class, but there would be no intermission in the class, Mr. Oats hav&not; ing kindly undertaken to supply his place. I he College would also lose, temporarily, the services of Mr. Edwards, who was,leaving for London ; hut it was hoped his place would be supplied. At the end of the term it was intended that an examina&not; tion should be held in the various classes, and that prizes and certificates of honour should be given to those who might distin&not; guish themselves. It was also intended that a number of the students who attained a certain position in several of the classes should be elected associates of the College, and be permanently associated with the Council and teachers in the government of the College. It was believed their counsel and advice wovdd be of great value ; and it was also considered that if the College were to be greatly extended, and the num&not; ber of students largely increased, it must he through the efforts of those who, hav&not; ing availed themselves of the benefits to be derived from it, would exert themselves to impart to others the same advantages. The Council were at present unable to an&not; nounce the accomplishment of an impor&not; tant object broached at the annual meeting &mdash;the provision of a permanent place of assembly for the members of the College, a library and coffee-room, and rooms where the members might meet either for the purpose of pursuing their studies or the enjoyment of social intercourse, and other most important subsidiary advantages and pleasures of a college life. But the sug&not; gestions made on the subject had not been wholly without effect, and the Council hoped to be able to accomplish the object in course of time. The delay must not be attributed to any want of interest in the matter on the part of the Council, but to the great difficulties which necessarily surrounded such an undertaking.,Professor Scott then delivered an ad&not; dress, in the course of which he said : Pro&not; bably the chief advantages of these Work&not; ing Men&rsquo;s Colleges consist not so much in the amount ot knowledge actually im&not; parted, as in teaching the students what kinds o&iacute; knowledge will be most valuable to them, and enabling them to systematize the knowledge they already possess. The manner in which we are drawn together is also of itself one of the most interesting results of these institutions. We should, probably, have remained apart and un&not; known to each other hut for the attrac&not; tions which this institution has presented to us. And great benefit may he derived from this. (Hear.) No doubt considera&not; ble advantages have been offered in this in&not; stitution ; but not, we believe, without a demand for them existing. What are the advantages proposed ? I venture to place,
first of all, those advantages which are pro&not; perly individual. The highest gains are those which can be understood aud mea- suied iu contemplating the individual man &mdash;those which make you more men, if I may so speak, which make your lives fuller and richer, present higher interests to you, raise you more and more above the temp&not; tations to indulge in lower gratifications for want of those that are nobler&mdash;those which make you more and more objects of respect and confidence to those that are near aud dear to you, that will make your minds nurseries of thoughts and sentiments ennobling to yourselves and those that dwell with you. These are some of the objects we propose in associating you toge&not; ther in a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;. (Cheers.) Whether you will rise higher in the world, whether you will be richer for it, whether you will win more praise or become more conspicuous in consequence of what you acquire here, I cannot tell. But if you determine thoroughly to avail yourselves of the advantages of that higher education which is here provided, a better thing than any of these will befall you&mdash;you will be able to do better without any of them. (Applause.) So much in regard to the individual influence of these institutions. But &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; are but one of a thousand agencies whose general ten&not; dency is the same. Whether men like the name of social revolutions or not, a great social revolution is going on rapidly, aud these institutions are contributing their share to it. Though I would not of course say a word calculated to provoke reflec&not; tions on party politics here, I believe &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; are one means of carrying into effect a Reform Bill of pro&not; digiously greater value than either that of the Government or the opposition. (Cheers.) Working men&mdash;L take the class in a very wide sense&mdash;I desire to be of it&mdash;working men have long constituted the preponde&not; rant portion of the national force ; they con&not;,stitute the preponderant portion of na&not; tional happiness ; the greater their happi&not; ness, the greater the nation&rsquo;s happiness ; what does most to promote their benefit will do most for the whole ; by their shar&not; ing largely in the advantages of intellec&not; tual culture they will also form a larger proportion of the national mind ; and then their convictions, however expressed, whe&not; ther by the legal machinery already exist&not; ing, or by legal machinery provided ex&not; pressly for the purpose, cannot fail to be duly attended to, and not they only, but all classes of the community will be gainers.,Dr. Koscoe, Mr. Gaskell, Mr. Scuda&not; more, Mr. GreenwOod, and other gentle&not; men, subsequently explained to the meet&not; ing various matters of detail connected with the practical operation of the different classes.&mdash; From the Manchester Courier.,Cricket. &mdash;The &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; Cricket Club (numbering about a fifth of the whole body of students) has arranged to play on Tuesdays and Saturdays, through&not; out the season, 1859, on the Gospel Oak Cricket Ground, lying near the end of the Malden Road, Kentish Town.,The Club is open to all members of the College, on payment of an entrance-fee of sixpence, and a subscription of sixpence j)er month. All communications and in&not; quiries respecting the Cricket Club should be addressed to Mr. Roebuck, Secretary, &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; Cricket Club, at the College.,The first match of the season was played in the Easter Vacation, on the beautiful common at Chigwell Row, Epping Forest. Sides were chosen by Mr. Bromhill and Mr. Taylor, the latter winning by 112 runs against 107. Mr. Bromhill made the leading score, 28. Mr. Greenwood.,Students must be sixteen years of age, must be able to read and write, and must know the first four Rules of Arithmetic. No Class will be formed for less than six,Students, unless at the desire of the Teacher,,Each Student will pay a permanent Entrance-fee of 2s. 6d., not to be renewed after absence. The Fee for each Class will be 2s. for the Term, to be paid in ad&not; vance. Admission to the Bible-Class will be free to all members of the College. Examinations will be held and prizes and certificates awarded in June, 1859. Prospectuses are issued before the commencement of each Term, giving details relative to the Classes and hours of meeting. They may be obtained at the Free Libraries, Camp Field, Hulme, and Ancoats; at the Mechanics&rsquo; Institution, David Street ; and from the Secretary, at the College.,J. G.
