JUNE 1, 1859.,A COLLEGE HALL.,We have been busy at the London &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; during the past month, on a project the interest and importance of which will be felt almost as much by the sister Colleges as by ourselves. It has been pe- termined to establish as a part of, or in close alliance with, the College, a &ldquo; Hall,&rsquo; or place of residence for our students.* The house next-door to our own being for sale, a member of our Council has signed a contract for its purchase at the price of &pound;1,400. By means of a sum of money lately lent to the College, without interest, by two friends, the purchase can easily be carried out, and the estimates of income and expenditure&mdash;made after a careful survey by an architect (a member of the Council)&mdash;seem to remove all fear of any addition being made to the pecuniary liabilities of the College. There are, on the contrary, special reasons for thinking our financial position will be improved by the proposed step ; and the only doubt arising on this head, is as to whether it will be best to place the &ldquo; Hall&rdquo; in the hands of a &ldquo; Company&rdquo; pecuniarily independent of the College, though formed from among its members ; or to make it an addition to our present property. It is proposed to convert two floors of the building into sleeping-rooms, on the dormitory plan ; to let other rooms to a mar&quot; ried student and a teacher (if possible), and to devote the remainder of the house (after providing a common room for its tenants) to the general purposes of the College.,We confidently believe that the happiest results will flow from this at&not; tempt to supply in some degree the want of that precious element of a community of daily life to which the ancient Universities owe most of their value. We are, indeed, quite certain that we have among us plenty of the true college feeling (meaning by this the sense of being knit together for a common purpose, and &oacute;f working for a common end). Nothing can so well serve the natural and healthy growth of this feeling as the exist&not; ence among us of a certain amount of what is called in other places &ldquo; col&not; lege life, even though but a small circle can partake of it. The effect wifi undoubtedly be felt beyond that circle, and will lead, we hope, to a larger application of the same principle : the only real difficulties being of a physical and material kind, those, namely, due to the circumstances of mo&not; dern life and modern labour, in vast and crowded cities. To carry out the plans thus shortly indicated, we have several months before us, as the present tenancy of the house in question may not expire before next March. Many questions will no doubt arise on various practical points of organi-,i 2,* The precise mode of carrying out this intention account is given merely as a piece of current news, and Council&rsquo;s intentions. being still under discussion, this not as a formal statement of the
zation and management. One or two such are well remarked upon by a correspondent in our present number. R. B. Litchfield.,&ldquo; RIFLEMEN, FORM !&rdquo;,An excellent cry, but to whom addressed ? Where are there to be found this coming million of deadly marksmen that are to line every hedgerow between Kennington Common and Pevensey Bay, before the first thousand of invaders have formed upon the beach ? If they are to appear at all in such multitudinous fashion, we surely have not much choice as to whom to select for the service. The target and the drilling-ground must be open to &ldquo;all sorts and conditions of men.&rdquo; A sharp eye and steady arm are all the qualifications we want. To such as have these, and care to train them for the benefit of their country, let the defence of the &ldquo; tight little island &rdquo; be freely entrusted. These simple notions however seem to be all a mis&not; take. From what the Government (P) has said, and still more from what it has not said, we infer that the privilege of so defending our common country is to be confined exclusively to gentlemen who are in society ! That this is plainly the sentiment now governing the official mind, any&not; body who reads the &ldquo; Memoranda,&rdquo; etc., must see. Perhaps when the country has had a few weeks more to consider the question, and can explain itself in Parliament, the authorities may discover that a body of 10,000 well-dressed young gentlemen from the clubs, government offices, and up&not; per classes generally, is not all that will be wanted if the contemplated crisis presents itself. Yet this is all that the &ldquo; arrangements &rdquo; hitherto announced can ever produce. The Volunteer Service must include some sacrifice on the part of every man who undertakes it. Superadd to this the demand of some &pound;10 or &pound;20 as an entrance-fee to pay necessary expenses, and you practically exclude nine out of every ten men otherwise fit for the work. Just one-tenth of our population are males between the ages of twenty and forty. A certain proportion of these would, from various reasons, be absolutely prevented from offering themselves. Some would be physically inefficient :&mdash;many most ardent patriots could not see their commanding officer at a hundred yards&rsquo; distance, how then pick off an invader at trvo thousand?&mdash;allowing a deduction of one-half (surely not too much) for these various preventing causes, we may reckon on having five per cent, of the population in a condition to be able, if willing, to do something for the defence of their country. Of such candidates England, Wales, and Scot&not; land would thus furnish about a million. But what would become of this number if you select only those who have &pound;20 to spare ? Half of one of your invader&rsquo;s caps ď аттее would hold in check the entire body of your volunteers. The readers of this Magazine will not wish to see discussed here the general question suggested by this Rifle Club controversy. We need not stop to refute, in a working man&rsquo;s paper, the dandiacal supersti&not; tion that &ldquo; the masses &rdquo; (hideous phrase !) are not to be trusted. Let it suffice to remind suchas tremble at their own vision of coming &ldquo; anarchy, that it is to the real or assumed fear of this poor spectre, fear of &ldquo; anar&not; chy,&rdquo; that Europe owes its present humiliation. It is the &ldquo;peace esta&not; blishment &rdquo; of three millions of armed men that supplies two or three despots
with the means of setting the world in a blaze, from St. Petersburg to the Nile ; and these monstrous armies exist solely on the plea that they are to protect the &ldquo; cause of order&rdquo; and check the spirit of revolutionary terror&not; ism, etc. etc. But if this red phantom frightens our neighbours out of their liberty, don&rsquo;t let it frighten us out of our strength. The heart of the &ldquo; lower orders &rdquo; is true to England&rsquo;s best interest. The grief that remains among us will never be cured by a war of classes. This at least we are all convinced of, however much more we have to learn ; and never could there be a better time than the present for strangling by the bond of a common citizenship, the suspicions that divide us from each other. The attitude which &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; should assume in reference to the present condition of public affairs, is therefore sufficiently obvious : so at least the members of the London College have felt. A few weeks back the idea of a Rifle Club in connection with the College was suggested and considered ; it was determined to form one. The names of men willing to join were obtained, and rules were agreed upon. For evident reasons it was then thought proper to submit the proposal to the Lord-Lieutenant of the county, with the view of eliciting, if possible, some information as to how the Go&not; vernment would be likely to entertain the idea of volunteer corps not pledged to defray their own expenses. The answer to this application is of im&not; portance (his Lordship being a prominent member of the present Cabinet) to all would-be volunteers in a position similar to that of our students. For their benefit, therefore, I append the following note, which certainly requires no commentary. It may be worth observing, however, that the number of volunteers mentioned is about the same as that suggested as a fair complement for such a corps in the last War Office Memorandum (see &lsquo; Times,&rsquo; May 38th). &szlig; L.,&ldquo;Privy Council Office, May 20th, 1859.,&ldquo; S 1R &amp;gt; I am directed to acknowledge the receipt of your letter, addressed to the Marquis of Salisbury as Lord-Lieutenant of the county of Middle&not; sex, oftering your services in the formation of a Volunteer Rifle Corps among the members of the W orking Men&rsquo;s College, and informing his Lordship that you have a list of more than sixty names of those readv to join.,&ldquo; As no arrangements have been made for the formation of Rifle Corps in the county of Middlesex, I have only to inform you that a note has been taken of your application.,&ldquo; I have the honour to be, Sir, your obedient Servant, &ldquo; Thomas Hughes, JEsg. &lsquo; &ldquo; W. Franks.&rdquo;,LORD BACON ON MATHEMATICS.,This subject being before our readers, who will have next month the remainder of Mr. Westlake&rsquo;s paper, the following sentences from the &lsquo;Ad&not; vancement of Learning&rsquo; may be quoted as a statement of what must always be the four chief points in the discussion ;&mdash;,&ldquo; Mathematics are either pure or mixed. To the pure mathematics are those sciences helongiug which handle quantity determinate, merely severed from any axioms of natural
philosophy ; and these are two, Geometry and Arithmetic ; the one handling quantity continued, and the other dissevered.,&ldquo; Mixed hath for subject some axioms or parts of natural philosophy, and considereth quantity determined, as it is auxiliary and incident unto them.,&ldquo; For many parts of nature can neither be invented with sufficient subtilty, nor de&not; monstrated with sufficient perspicuity, nor accommodated unto use with sufficient dex&not; terity, without the aid and intervening of the mathematics ; of which sort are perspec&not; tive, music, astronomy, cosmography, architecture, enginery, and divers others.,&ldquo;In the mathematics I can report no d&eacute;ficience, except it be that men do not suffici&not; ently understand the excellent use of the pure mathematics, in that they do remedy and cure many defects in the wit and faculties intellectual. For if the wit be too dull, they sharpen it ; if too wandering, they fix it ; if too inherent in the sense, they abstract it. So that as tennis is a game of no use in itself, but of great use in respect it maketh a quick eye and a body ready to put itself into all postures ; so in the mathematics, that use which is collateral and intervenient is no less worthy than that which is principal and intended. And as for the mixed mathematics, I may only make this prediction, that there cannot fail to be more kinds of them, as nature grows further disclosed.&rdquo;,[A prediction notably verified seventy years afterwards by Newton&rsquo;s discovery of the great modern engine of calculation,&mdash;Fluxions, or the Differential Calculus.],ACCOUNT OF THE ORIGIN AND PROGRESS OF THE PEOPLE&rsquo;S COLLEGE AT SHEFFIELD.,(Continued from page 72.),In spite of the difficulties however that at first beset these young men, they were too far committed to the attempt to think of giving up before at least it had had a fair trial. The rooms were temporarily engaged, and a Committee of twelve from the sixteen was chosen : and at the first meeting the officers. President, Treasurer, and Secretary, were elected, and constitutional laws were passed. As these laws are few and short, and have served every contingency in the experience of the Institution to the present time, it may not be uninteresting to give them in extenso.,Law 1. That this Institution shall adopt the name and style of the People&rsquo;s College.,2. That the People&rsquo;s College shall be a self-supporting and a self-governing Educational Institute.,3. That all persons above sixteen years of age shall be eligible to become Members of this Institution.,4. That subjects bearing upon party politics or sectarianism shall not be introduced in the Classes.,5. That the executive power shall be vested in a Committee of twelve Students, with the power of electing its own President, Treasurer, and Secretary; seven to form a quorum.,6. That every six months one-half of the Committee shall retire, and an election take place for the vacancies ; and that retiring members be eligible for re-election.,7. That the surplus funds shall be applied exclusively to increase the efficiency of the Institution.,The eighth, shortly afterwards considered a necessary supplement, enacted &ldquo; That no motion affecting the constitutional laws of the College shall be considered, except at the Annual Meeting of the Students (to be held in January), and by giving fourteen days&rsquo; notice to the Committee and Students.&rdquo;,On the 16th of October&middot;, 1848, a public meeting was held in the Rooms of the Insti&not; tution, Wilson Overend, Esq., J.P., in the Chair, when a full statement of the objects of the future People&rsquo;s College, and the plans whereby they were to be secured, was read on behalf of the Committee, by the President, Mr. Thomas Rowbotham, and resolutions of approval were passed ; and thus the Institution was fairly launched upon its future career. It was stated at the meeting that the Committee did not possess a single book and had not a single farthing to invest in furniture. Indeed to outside observers things looked so hopeless that Mr. Overend and his brother earnestly asked the Committee to
estimate the probable expenses of the first year, that they might subscribe a portion, and beg the rest. Although the generosity of this offer was perfectly appreciated, it was nevertheless respectfully declined. A principle had been laid down, and it must be abided by. On the day following the public meeting the classes were opened, and it was soon found that the appeal made to the &ldquo;young men and women of Sheffield&rdquo; had not been made in vain. Before the end of the month there were nearly two hundred students in the classes. This rush at first taxed to the utmost the efforts of the Com&not; mittee and Monitors. Order was the first thing to be established ; and the following General Rules were adopted :&mdash;,General Rules.,Students.,Rule 1. The College will be opened on Monday, Tuesday, and Wednesday mornings, from 6.30 to 7.30, and every evening from 7 to 9.30. The Classes to begin each evening at 7.30.,2. Each Student is requested to pay the weekly fee, if possible, on Monday or Tues&not; day evenings, and the quarterage during the first week in the months of January, April, July, and October.,3. The females shall sit in any part of the room that the Committee may appoint, and retire first at the close of each Class.,4. No Student shall leave his seat until the females have retired,,5. Any Student being guilty of misconduct in manner or language, or damaging the property of the Institute, to be fined at the discretion of the Committee, or (if necessary) expelled the College.,Monitors.,6. Every Monitor is requested to be punctual in opening and closing his Class.,7. Any Monitor being unable to attend to his Class, must notify the same to the Class-Monitor as soon as possible.,8. No person must interfere with any Class when the appointed Monitor is present, unless at the request of the said Monitor.,Class-Monitors.,9. The Class-Monitor must be in attendance at the opening of the Classes every evening, to see that the Classes are conducted in their proper order.,10. In the absence of the appointed Monitor of any Class, he may appoint another to the charge of the same during the absence of the regular Monitor, to teach the Class himself.,11. He must give notice to the Monitor of each Class five minutes before the time of closing, and announce the Class which follows.,12. The Class-Monitor must see that those Students not engaged in Classes do not loiter round the fires, talk, or continue in the room to the annoyance of any Class then in operation.,13. He may receive complaints of any irregularity or misconduct, and report the same to the Committee, who have the general superintendence of the Institution.,The Committee pledge themselves to use their best efforts to promote the advantages of the College, and desire the earnest co-operation of the Students.,It will be seen that great attention was paid to the general conduct of students, and particularly to the location and arrangement of the women. It was felt that full public confidence should be established and maintained in the discipline of the Institu&not; tion, if its ultimate objects were ever to be realized. And as Mr. Bayley was gone, there was greater danger and more need for watchfulness, now that the &ldquo; young people,&rsquo;&rsquo; so to speak, had it all to themselves. The unwearied attention that has always been paid to this point, has been rewarded with the happiest results. For after ten years&rsquo; experience, there has not been a single occurrence to call into question in the remotest degree the discipline of the Institution and the decorum practised by the students towards one another,,^ Discipline being established, wants&mdash;sudden and great&mdash;were clamouring on all sides. Books, maps, furniture, etc., must be had : necessary alterations must be made. Such faith however had the Committee in the success of the scheme, that in less than six
months they had incurred liabilities of upwards of &pound;200. Extensive alterations were made, and the whole of the rooms were properly fitted up with new furniture. At the end of the first forty-seven weeks it was found that there had been 530 students on the books ; 426 of these were young men ; the rest, 104, women. The average attendance had been 135, and the amount received for fees had been &pound;203. l&iacute;. bd.,This was a success far beyond the most sanguine expectations of the Committee. They however thought that some portion of it was due to novelty, and began in the second year, to devise plans for establishing the Institution upon some firmer basis, enabling it to do its work more truly and thoroughly, and thus putting it beyond the influence of mere impulsive action, which might at any moment be diverted or arrested altogether. The plan which seemed most likely to promote these ends was the establishment of Day Classes in connection with the Institution. It was thought that regular and valuable assistance might be obtained in the Night Classes from the Day Masters, and that the children brought together by these means, would in course of time form a valuable source of supply, inasmuch as they would probably imbibe an early predilection for the place, and receive an education specially adapted to the Night Class course. The sequel has proved that these were not idle speculations : very valuable and regular has been the assistance rendered by those engaged in the Day Classes. And some of the most talented and persevering of the present night students have originated from the same source.,It would be useless and foreign to our aim in the present paper, to recount year by year the events characterizing each, as most of them are necessarily recurring. It will suffice if we just seize upon a few of the leading events, which will serve to show how the energies of the Institution have been generally directed, and what has been the measure of success. Indeed it may be said that all the endeavours of the Committee from the commencement have centred in one object, viz. to improve and perfect the action of the Classes. The machine was necessarily at first uncouth in its form, uncertain in its action, and unfinished in its details ; but constant attention has in a great measure remedied these defects, and the result is an increase of effectiveness with a less applica&not; tion of force.,It was an axiom of the Committee never to establish a lending library ; but rather to aim at the possession of a few necessary books of reference, and then to spend any surplus money in purchasing such scientific or other apparatus as could not be obtained with facility elsewhere. There were several good libraries in the town, and easily accessible ; consequently there was no necessity for collecting another lot of books, which must for the most part have consisted of duplicates. Holding these views, then, it is not a matter of surprise that the Committee seized the first opportunity of possess&not; ing philosophical instruments. In 1853 a gentleman of the town had a splendid col&not; lection to dispose of in one lot, and the Committee bought them for &pound;100. Following out the idea indicated by this purchase, it w&#39;as intended to establish Industrial Classes&mdash; for, to quote from the Fifth Report, &ldquo; They considered there was something more re&not; quired for the artisans of Sheffield than purely mental discipline, however excellent in itself that might be, and that to be really a College for the people, it must include in its classes studies that would have a direct bearing upon the industrial pursuits that distinguish the town.&rdquo; A very promising prospect of success in this department was held out by the kind assistance of Mr. James Haywood, a distinguished chemist in the town, and the Industrial Classes were inaugurated by a public course of twelve lectures delivered in the Council Hall, by Mr. Haywood. The aim will be best gathered from the subjects, w r hich were as follows :&mdash;&ldquo; Fire, and the various methods of producing Heat;&rdquo; &ldquo;The effects of Heat ou Metals, Liquids, and Solids;&rdquo; &ldquo;Electro-Gilding, Sil&not; vering, Bronzing, etc.;&rdquo; &ldquo;Water, audits Impurities;&rdquo; &ldquo;Preparation, Properties, and Uses, of Mineral Acids;&rdquo; &ldquo; General Properties of Metals ;&rdquo; &ldquo;Lime, Land, Clay, Fire&not; bricks, and Furnace Materials ;&rdquo; &ldquo; Iron and Steel &ldquo; Cobalt, Nickel, Zinc, Copper and Lead ;&rdquo; &ldquo; Tin, Antimony, Bismuth, Silver, Gold, and Platinum.&rdquo; Soon after de&not; livering the last of these lectures, and before the arrangements were complete for com&not; mencing the Industrial Classes, Mr. Haywood w r as cut off by an accident, in the midst of a life of great usefulness. His loss to the Institution was irreparable, and further pio- gress in the Industrial department was suspended.,As time rolled on, and the Committee acquired additional experience, they began to find the prospects for the general spread of education among the masses were not so
bright as had at one time been ardently imagined. Ignorance, deep and general, was found to prevail ; and, what was worse still, a pervading apathy on the subject of edu&not; cation seemed to possess the multitude. Small indeed was the proportion that could be got to take the least interest in the subject ; and of these, again, by far the greater number were possessed more with a vague wish to become learned for some reason, they knew not why, than impressed with a due sense of the nature and importance of know&not; ledge, or willing to make the requisite sacrifices for its acquirement. It was even found that too often those who were able and willing to work, would study indiscriminately, and without method. To remedy this, and to supply a healthy stimulus to exertion, it was proposed to establish a graduated system of Honours, to be obtained only by exa&not; mination. So fully were the Committee convinced that something should be done to systematize and direct the studies of the students, that a Subcommittee was formed to draw up the particulars of a plan, and submit it for approval. This was done ; but attention was drawn to what the Society of Arts proposed to introduce among the Institutions in union ; and it was ultimately agreed to fall in with their system of examination. To the first examinations, held in London, in 1856, the People&rsquo;s College sent one candidate ; to those in 1857, held in Huddersfield, two. But as travelling to other towns was objectionable on the score of time and expense, it was not until last year, when General Local Boards were formed, that full advantage could be taken of the Society of Arts Examinations, when out of fourteen candidates, twelve obtained certificates ; three of the first class, two carrying first class prizes of &pound;5 each, for Latin and Roman History, and for English Literature. The number of candidates for this year is twenty-three. Thus it will be seen that every year ground has been steadily gained&mdash;a sufficient indication that these examinations are answering in a great measure their purpose.,Besides the Classes, Lectures are delivered monthly to the students and friends of the Institution. They are mostly supplied by gentlemen of the town and neighbour&not; hood, and are generally very interesting, and not so frequent as to interrupt materially the studies in the classes. For if there be one thing more than another that has con&not; tributed to the success of the People&rsquo;s College at Sheffield, the Committee believe it to be the great scrupulousness with which anything tending to interrupt the work in the classes has been kept out of the Institution.,During the course of ten years the People&rsquo;s College has been the means of receiving and dispensing more than &pound;4,000 for the promotion of popular education, and, through many temptations, has steadily held to the principle of self-support. And what is very encouraging, when the last Annual Statement of Accounts was read, there was a balance in the hands of the Treasurer of upwards of &pound;20.,This much, then, has been accomplished by a handful of young men in the cause of Education. At the first meeting of the Committee the room was illuminated by a half&not; penny caudle inserted in an earthenware inkstand.,Thomas Row botham.,NEWS.,Halifax.&mdash;Annual Report .&mdash;Since last Easter the College has gone on steadily working, and w r e think we may say that from increased experience, and from a more free and unrestrained intercourse between all its members, many improvements have been brought about in the working, so as to render the Institution much more generally efficient. And we believe that the more the collegiate character of the Institution (in the strict sense of the term) is kept up, the more will the Institution answer the end for which it was founded, viz. :&mdash;the raising of the social and intellectual con&not; dition of working men. One important reform that has been brought about is&mdash;the,students themselves have attained a direct voice in the management of the affairs of the College, two of their number being elected to sit on the Committee. The great advantage of this has already appeared in a variety of ways, especially in matters re&not; lating to the convenience of the students, such as the manner of collecting fees, in the subjects studied and the manner of study&not; ing them, etc. We have had, too, more frequent social gatherings, where all the Members of the College&mdash;Principal, Presi&not; dent, Tutors, and Students, male and fe&not; male, the Committee and their friends&mdash;have been drawui together by one object, viz. mutual improvement. Any person who has been present at one of these meetings,
will admit that the name which we have chosen for ourselves&mdash;a College&mdash;is the only one which could correctly describe our Institution. Such meetings as these, where all classes are drawn together on an equal footing, where the teachers and the taught, the educated and the uneducated, the em&not; ployer and the employed, the master and the man, meet in a kindly spirit for social intercourse and conversation, have a great effect in keeping before the mind the ob&not; jects for which they are associated, and convincing each member that he is a fellow- worker with the rest. We consider such means&mdash;in connection with the steady study of the classes&mdash;much better adapted to the end we have in view than ordinary lectures ; though these last wc do not despise. Of these we have only had one during the year, by Mr. Barry, Head Master of Leeds School.,We have somewhat altered during the year the grand divisions of the subjects to be studied. The original plan, however, of dividing the subjects has not been de&not; parted from. Wc have a course of instruc&not; tion adapted specially to handicraft trades&not; men ; Chemistry and Natural Philosophy form also a distinct branch. And we have found it necessary during the year, to arrange a course specially adapted to an&not; other class of students, the want of which has gradually become apparent,&mdash;and that class consists of such young men as aspire to raise themselves from the ranks of manual labourers. This class comprises some of the best and most intelligent of the students. For such there is a special course in English Language, French, Book&not; keeping, Letter-writing, and Composition. And while we think that the Society of Arts has made a mistake in holding out inducements chiefly to this class, and while we maintain that the great end of the edu&not; cation of working men is not to make them dissatisfied with their rank in life, and to lead them to hope to get appointments as clerks and civil servants under Government, still we cannot but admire the industry and perseverance which mark young men of this class among us, and we have felt bound to afford them every facility to attain their object. In this last course, great aid has been given by a member of our Committee, Mr. J. Wainhouse, who gives gratuitous instruction in the practice of Book-keeping.,In Chemistry Dr. Paley has continued his gratuitous services, and we are happy to be able to report that the students in Chemistry have kept up the high character they gained in the Society of Arts Exami&not;,nation, when one of their number carried off the highest certificate but three. He stood fourth on the list, as against all Eng&not; land. The Report of our Examiner, Dr. Brinton, of Loudon, which will be found below, amply testifies to this fact. The Laboratory has been greatly enlarged.,In the other great division of subjects, viz. that adapted for the handicraft trades, the staff of tutors remains the same, and the Report of the Examiner (Rev. G. M, Gorham) will show that the College has not lost ground in their hands. The re&not; moval of the senior tutor, in consequence of his appointment to an important post at Madras, has somewhat iuterfered with the w r ork of the College in this department, but Mr. Gibb, his successor, has by his un&not; tiring zeal and steady perseverance,re&not; paired the injury, and he promises to be in every way competent to carry on the work which Mr. Anderson had so well begun. The List of Certificates gained at the Society of Arts Examination last Whit&not; suntide (16 in number) w&#39;ill show that real and telling work has been done. In the coming Examination of the Society of Arts, too, we hope to exceed the success of the last one.,Another alteration in the course of in&not; struction made during the year, is the aban&not; donment of Art Instruction in the College, Through the cordial co-operation of the Mechanics&rsquo; Institute, we have succeeded in establishing a School of Art in the tow r n. Mr. Stevenson, our late teacher of drawing, has been appointed Master of that School ; and we are glad to say that it is in a very promising state.,Then with regard to special subjects&mdash; there are 30 students learning Vocal Music, who have made great progress. In the study of Theology we cannot report much progress. Though tbe class assembling on Sunday afternoons, conducted by the Senior Tutor, is open to all, free of charge, not one student has passed a sufficiently good exa&not; mination to gain a prize. Mr. Gibb in&not; tends, during the coming year, to do his best to make this lecture interesting as well as instructive. Portions of Scripture His&not; tory, or Church History, or some kindred subject, will be taken up in such a manner as not to bias the opinions of the students. There surely can be no reason why a stu&not; dent should not be taught the opinions of different religious sects, without being forced to adopt any one of those opinions.,The class for adults in the Senior Di vision, who have not had the advantages of a day- school education, has to a great extent
failed. It is of course a very difficult thing to carry on.,The Recreation Ground, notwithstanding the prizes offered in manly games, has not attracted the students as it ought. It is difficult to convince a man who earnestly desires to educate his mind, that the best aid in doing that is to educate his body at the same time. Chess and Draughts in&not; troduced into the Reading Room have met with more favour.,In the Young Women&rsquo;s Institute great progress has been made.,A School of Cookery has been instituted, in which young women are taught the art of plain cooking, such as will increase the comforts of the home of the working-man, and economize his limited means. They are also,taught the art of cooking for the sick, which not only teaches them a lesson of charity, but also provides them with the means of usefulness in their own homes when sickness visits them. There is no idea of training them for domestic servants. The classes for Vocal Music are well at&not; tended, as well as the Sewing Classes. In these classes also, great care is taken to teach them only what is useful, viz. mak&not; ing and mending their own dresses, etc. The detailed Reports will be found below.,The following particulars we give in an abridged form from the Report :&mdash;,Attendance.&mdash;Number on the books, 171; admitted since Easter, 1858, 80; average weekly attendance, 118 ; average evening attendance, 73.,Classes.&mdash;,Algebra: senior, 12; junior, 12. These lectures have been ar&not; ranged in order to meet the deep interest taken in the present war by the working classes, and it is hoped they will attend iu large numbers, with their wives and fami&not; lies.,Cambridge .&mdash;An examination will be held during the week beginning June 5th. The examination has been instituted in com&not; pliance with a wish conveyed to the Couu- cil by the Committee of the students. The Council, cordially sympathizing with this wish, deliberated on the best meaus of car&not; rying it into effect. Their choice seemed to lie between a single general examina&not; tion aud a series of separate examina&not; tions. They might have fixed upon a course of three or more subjects, aud required every candidate to pass iu each ; or they might offer to examine separately in all the subjects on which Lectures were being given, and to give certificates of compe&not; tency to those who should pass satisfacto&not; rily in any one or more of these.,In rejecting the former alternative, the Council were guided mainly by reasons of a temporary character. It seemed very doubtful whether &rsquo;many of the students would be able to find time to prepare for an examination iu a regular coarse of sub&not; jects. It seemed wiser to test the accu&not; racy of their knowledge of the particular subject on which they had been lately en&not; gaged, than to invite them to enter upon new studies. Should it be found that there are many candidates taking in many of the separate subjects at once, the result of the examination will be a guide to the Council in providing for future years.,The examination in June will be held in,each of the following subjects ;&mdash;Religious Knowledge; English Grammar; English History ; Arithmetic ; Algebra ; Euclid ; Trigonometry ; Latin ; French. The stu&not; dents have been informed of the range in each subject within which their knowledge will be tested. The Certificate will, as far as possible, specify this.,The subjects for which the largest num&not; ber of candidates have entered are French and Arithmetic , the numbers being ten aud seven respectively.,London.&mdash;The Curators of the Museum hope that they may be able to make some mutually profitable exchanges of fossils and mineral specimens with the Colleges in the North. They can furnish any number of characteristic shells from the London clay, chalk, greensand, etc., in return for the produce of the carboniferous, and whatever other strata their Northern friends are working in.,&ldquo; A Student&rdquo; writes as follows :&mdash;&ldquo; &lsquo; The rules and limitations which the former ( Universities) have laid down respecting,age , shut out a great majority of our stu&not; dents from examination. &rsquo; The foregoing is an extract from the Report of the Lon&not; don &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, printed in the Supplement of our April Magazine. Is the Matriculation Examination of the Univer&not; sity of London referred to ?&mdash;and if so, what are the rules and regulations respecting age ?&rdquo;,[The examination referred to in our Lon&not; don College Report, is not the &ldquo; Matricu&not; lation&rdquo; Examination of the University of London, but the Examination of youths under sixteen, and not members of the Uni&not; versity of Cambridge or Oxford, by Exa&not; miners appointed by one of those Univer&not; sities. See the Regulations, printed at,pp. 14-17 of the February number of our Magazine (No. 2).],CORRESPONDENCE.,THE LONDON WOKRING MEN&rsquo;S HALL.,London, May, 1859.,Sir,&mdash;I learn by a circular I have received, that a scheme is on foot to establish a &ldquo; Working Men&rsquo;s Hall,&rdquo; or place of residence for students of the Working Men&rsquo;s Col&not; lege. I am so strongly impressed with the importance of this movement, as tending to the completion of the idea of a College which should educate socially as well as men- tallv that I cannot resist the temptation to put a few thoughts respecting it on paper.
The precise mode by which this end is to be attained is as yet, it appears, undetermined : whether the Council of the College will assume the responsibilities which the scheme will carry with it, whether these responsibilities will be borne by a Company formed for the purpose, or a combination of the two Bodies, each sharing in the responsibility and ma&not; nagement. How this matter may be settled is a minor consideration. My impression is, that it would be well if a Company, with student-shareholders, were formed, as likely to perpetuate the interest with which students may regard the &ldquo; Hall but the real points of interest are those of discipline and management.,&ldquo;Discipline&rdquo; may be a harsh word perhaps, yet no intending resident must regard this scheme without taking into consideration an amount of, it may be self-imposed, but still necessary, discipline. The London &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, since it enforces no test but one of an educational kind,&mdash;and that of the most elementary nature,&mdash;necessarily contains within it men of all varieties and shades of habits and opinions, and it will be the duty of any man in whom a true college feeling exists, to avoid all offence to the habits and opinions of his fello w-residents. There can be nought in this but what is wholesome and good for a man; indeed, in my opinion, it will be the best training he can have, the life of all others which will best fit him for doing his duty in the world. On the other hand, there must be a wide toleration : men must be allowed to live out their individuality; there cannot possibly be a stamp of uniformity impressed upon the residents in the Hall, and men who enter it with strongly formed opinions must be pre&not; pared to distinguish between real and seeming antagonism ; indeed, where antagonism really exists, they must be prepared to regard it with respect and tolerance.,A word or two about management. The Hall must be a &ldquo; home &rdquo; to the men who enter it, in the fullest sense in which it is possible to make it so, and to this object the efforts of the management must be directed ; but as in every home there is a &ldquo; head,&rdquo; and upon the personal influence of the &ldquo; head &rdquo; depends the character of the home, so in this case much&mdash;very much&mdash;will depend upon the choice of a resident manager, for that I believe to be indispensable to the successful working of the projected Hall. The term,resident manager &rdquo; may sound formidable, but it may, I think, be narrowed into a small compass. I presume, amongst the number of students in the College, many are married, and there would be little difficulty in inducing one of these students, who should be well known and respected by his fellow-students, to reside on the premises as the &ldquo; head &rdquo; of the family there congregated (and this for a slight consideration beyond the sacrifice of two or three rooms on one of the lower floors of the house), while his wife could super&not; intend the house arrangements.,The circular or prospectus forwarded to me has certainly a very promising look in a commercial sense, and the gentlemen who have signed it deserve credit for the manner in which they have made capital out of the success of other similar societies ; but it strikes me the Company or the Council, whichever body carries out the project, will discover that the Prospectus does not very accurately estimate either receipts or disbursements ; yet I have little hesitation in saying the actual working of the scheme will, with judicious management, yield a fair profit, though not nine and a half per cent. ; and I have every confidence that the &ldquo; Hall&rdquo; will recommend itself to the men for whom it is intended as a home.,I trust the consideration of such schemes as the &ldquo;Working Men&rsquo;s Hall Company&rdquo; will not be thought beyond the province of your Magazine ; if so, and this should find a place in your pages, I hope others who have any suggestions to offer on the subject wifi follow my example, and that the scheme will be thoroughly discussed in this way. It is a matter in which all are interested who find &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; a source of interest and for ray part I hope the time is not far distant when every town in England will have a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, every &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; a &ldquo;Hall.&rdquo;,I am, Sir, yours very respectfully,,A Student.,ETYMOLOGIC AND GRAMMAR CERTIFICATES,,о у a -n May 24sth, 1859,,Dear Sir &mdash;In the Examination for certificates of competency in English grammar, at the London &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, do the questions bear much on Etymology ?,And how much is a student required to know of it ? I for one do not recognize the
Etymology, &ldquo; as we are taught it,&rdquo; as essential to the knowledge of English Grammar ; but rather as the history of the language, the thorough knowledge of which ought to command a separate certificate. At the commencement of all Grammar-hooks one is taught that Grammar is the art of speaking and writing the English language with pro&not; priety, and I think that the knowledge of the rules of Grammar, the correct application of those rules, and the ability to detect any grammatical errors in composition is the true definition, and ought to be sufficient to obtain a certificate of competency,,Yours respectfully,,F. W. Murray.,[The Secretary, Mr. Shorter, keeps a book which contains, or should contain, every Examination-Taper for a Certificate of Competency set in the College. As I know there is at least one Grammar-Paper in this book, I refer Mr. Murray to that. Generally two papers arc set, one by each of the two Examiners, and good answers to half the questions ensure a Certificate. Perhaps one-fourth of the questions are etymological,&mdash; but these latter never comprise anything which has not been dealt with in the course о the year&rsquo;s lessons, and our experience has shown that they are fairly answered.^ Mr. M. would apparently define Grammar as the art of usina; w&#39;ords 4 with propiiety. &rsquo; &reg; then is it not desirable to know what a thing is before you begin to use it ? In other words, must not Etymology, which teaches you what words are, come before (or be included in) Grammar, which teaches you what to do with them?&mdash; F. J. Furnivall.J,INCE AND gilbert&rsquo;s 4 OUTLINES OF ENGLISH HISTORY.&rsquo;,[The necessity of getting a good text-book for his Class is well known to every Teacher. His teaching is only in spoken words, which, being winged, too often fly through the pupil&rsquo;s head; the printed text remains, and the student, referring to it atrain and again after his lesson, houses its statements in the nest of his mind, and is almost sure to produce them, instead of his teacher&rsquo;s brood, when a questioner comes to examine his progeny. A bad text-book may almost destroy the effec о good teaching, and must confuse and hinder the progress of every student.
