JULY 1, 1859.,RECKU ITING FOK A COLLEGE.,I ventured ţ to tell our friends at Manchester last January that our Work&not; ing Men&rsquo;s College was established first for the benefit of us the Teachers, secondly for the benefit of those whom we taught. The statement sounded discreditable ; but I maintain that it was true, and therefore it should not be concealed. Young professional men felt that they wanted employment ; co-operation among themselves, co-operation with human beings who were not of their class. They founded a College, thinking it the best and most reasonable way of realizing these objects ; if the working men refused to enter it, the design would in a great degree be frustrated ; but it would be a pretence to say that benevolence to them was the primary impulse of those who sought their alliance.,1 am speaking only for our London College. Others may have been started&mdash;the Sheffield notoriously was&mdash;with a quite different intention. But for our own sakes, and for the sake of all, I wish that the fact should be stated fairly, because it may lead some to join a Working Men&rsquo;s Col&not; lege, or at least to think about joining one, who have hitherto stood aloof. It does not become us to beg for help ; there are many young men and old men in London, and in the other towns of England, with excellent dispositions, and more than average talents, whose dispositions and talents may be better employed in some other labour than that of teaching classes ; there are many whose assistance we should decline, because we are sure it would be precarious, given one week, withheld another, and because in such an institution as ours an irregular teacher is worse than no teacher at all. But there are many who come up year by year, ahnost month by month, to London, from Oxford and Cambridge, or from travelling abroad, to enter the Inns of Court, to study in the Hospitals, to take &rdquo;cures in London districts ; there are&mdash;of course not as many but&mdash;some who settle for one purpose or other in country towns. These crave for some other fellowship than that which a Club can give them ; they want some occu&not; pation which will not withdraw them from their regular work, but will rather remind them of the principle involved in that work, and may secure them from becoming mere drudges. To such as these a College may be a blessing far greater than they know. It may keep alive a hundred lessons which they are forgetting ; it may connect the dreary bustle of a city with regions which appear to be hopelessly divided from it.,This language will be too bold if I supposed our Colleges were to be no better than they are at present. We in London have very little ful&not; filled our own dream : we have met together for purposes of business ; our intercourse has not been for mutual instruction, encouragement, connected as it might have been. If we had been more of a College of Teachers, we should have been far more profitable to our scholars and far more united,к 2
with them than they have been. I desire that fresh blood should be put into our system, on purpose that we may be compelled to reform ourselves in this respect. The work quickly enough becomes mechanical, torpid, solitary. We need continually to be reminded that we are a body of workers, and that every piece of work will be ill done if it is not connected with every other. If the hope which our name holds out appears to any who join us to be belied by facts, let them put forth all their remonstrances and all their exertions that it may be accomplished ; let them insist upon more frequent meetings for comparing the methods we have adopted and devising better. If we fall, or have fallen, into a premature senility, they will push ns from our stools, and restore life to the Society. Tor a Society it is, with all capacities for healthy action, for continual growth and ex&not; pansion ; our pupils, 1 am sure, think so ; they feel that their union, how&not; ever imperfect and hindered by English reserve and shyness, is a reality and not a fiction. If we had as much faith as they have, &mdash; if we were as zealous to break do\vn the barriers which separate us, and w r ere determined to cultivate those gifts that have been bestowed upon us,&mdash;I believe the effects would be as visible among Churchmen, Lawyers, Physicians, as among Mechanics. We might be doing something to establish a Man&not; hood Suffrage in the best sense of the word, by first making ourselves men to exercise it ; we might be forming a true and efficient Eitle Corps for the defence of our own land and of all lands. E. H. M.,[It may be as well to add here that it is only in the Natural Science department that any positive want of teachers has yet been felt. Most of our teaching material comes from the old Universities, where, despite the noble efforts of late years, such studies as Geology and Chemistry still hold a pitifully low position. Cannot we find some one among the less busy members of the Geological Societies to help a class of very earnest devotees of that grand Science?&mdash; Editor.&#39;],METHODS OE TEACHING VOCAL MUSIC.,No apology is needed for including music among the studies of a Work&not; ing Men&rsquo;s College. We may call it an art, an amusement, a science, or by what name we like, but the thing &ldquo; music,&rdquo; in some shape or other, must be recognized as one of the great Divine powers holding sway among men. To attempt to define its position as among other arts or sciences would be a difficult and quite useless speculation, plunging us inevitably into I know not what slough of vain aesthetic and metaphysical muddle. Suffice it to note the one feature which places music apart from all other arts, though in sympathy with them all, namely this ; that whereas their power is reflex and derivative , that of Music is direct and individual in its action. Upon this difference, not easy to state shortly in words, depends the entire cha&not; racter of music as an art. In painting, in sculpture, and to a great ex&not; tent in poetry, it is the representation of something else that produces the effect. It is because we care about trees and clouds that we care about pictures of trees and clouds. The shadow of the thing pictured works upon us by appealing to the same sensibilities which are called into action
by a sight of the thing itself. Music, on the other hand, presents to us an absolutely new mode of beauty, not otherwise existing in Nature.* How or why this new mode of beauty affects us it is vain to ask ; we might as well puzzle ourselves to find out why we are delighted by a rain&not; bow or a primrose ; let it be enough to rejoice that the ivory chambers of the ear are the entrance to a new world of beauty ; that out of mechanisms the most humble (as mean as the mud-pigments of the painter), horsehair and catgut, brass and iron, we can summon at will visions of ethereal splendour.,Ihat this divinest of arts has of late been gathering increased life and power among the multitudes o&iacute; the people, is one of the most prominent social facts o&iacute; our time ; our present business is to see how we can widen still further the domain of its purifying and ennobling influence,,r ihe best of all music is that of the human voice ; how to teach people to make this sort of music for themselves is the question now before us. A complete discussion of it I cannot attempt within the limits of these few pages. I must confine myself to the single point on which any serious controversy arises, and on which all the practical importance of the ques&not; tion centres. To persons entirely ignorant of the subject it would be im&not; possible to make the following remarks intelligible without writing a trea&not; tise on music. Let my non-musical readers apply to their musical friends for such explanation as they need.,The object of a popular system of vocal music is to teach people to,sing at sight, that is, to produce at will the sounds indicated by a set of written signs placed before them. There are at present only two me&not; thods prominently before the English public which profess to do this. An examination of the comparative merits of these two is all that I have now space for. One of these systems is that adopted some years ago in France by M. Wilhem, and introduced into England under the Government pa&not; tronage by Mr. Hullah. The other is called the &ldquo; Tonic Sol-Fa &rdquo; method, and is the one which, provisionally at least, we have adopted in the Work&not; ing Men&rsquo;s College. Why this latter method is to be preferred I will try now to show. J,All music consists of a succession of different sounds : the passage from any one sound to another is called an interval. To sing at sight, one must learn to recognize sounds and intervals from their wntten signs&rsquo;. But wherein does one sound differ from another, produced on the same instrument? In being &ldquo; higher &rdquo; or &ldquo; lower,&rdquo; that is, in pitch. How then is this difference of pitch to be measured ? how exhibited to the eye ? The true reply to this question (on which hangs the entire difference between the two methods) will appear from the following considerations. The sounds used m any piece of music, when arranged in order of pitch, form a &ldquo; scale : in this scale there will be found one note, which seems to o- 0 _ vern all the rest ; this is called the &ldquo; key-note ; &rdquo; its properties are to the ear as unmistakably evident as they are indescribable in words Now it is found that the musical effect of any note depends not upon its absolute pitch but upon its position in this &ldquo; scale.&rdquo; It is the fact of its being,. * Nl &sect;| ltin S ales &amp;gt; cuckoos, and other non-human musicians, are no exceptions to this &Iota;Ϊ&Gamma;&Gamma;. &Lambda; Л mUS1C 1 S w\&deg; far 38 ? g0es &rsquo; the same in kiad as ours, and gives us the same kind of pleasure. When we imitate it, as it is called, we in fact reproduce it.
such and such a number of degrees distant from the key-note that gives it its musical character. This fact is perfectly indisputable : it rests on uni&not; versal experience. For instance, let two people sing a tune successively; the second may sing it higher or lower than the first, using an entirely different set ot sounds : the musical effect of the whole, and of the note sung to any particular syllable, will nevertheless be the same in both cases. Suppose the tune to be &ldquo; Oh where, and oh where is my Highland laddie gone?&rdquo; The word &ldquo; is &rdquo; may have been sung by the first person to a cer&not; tain note, say &ldquo; D,&rdquo; on a pianoforte, while the second person may have used the note &ldquo; C &rdquo; for the same word. The absolute pitch of the two notes, estimated by comparison with a fixed standard, was different ; how then was the musical effect of each the same ? Because in both cases its pitch relatively to the key-note was the same ; it was the 6th of the scale. &#39;the same is true of any interval or succession of two sounds. For in&not; stance, the first two notes of the tune just mentioned, when played in the key of 1), are A, D, the 5th and 8th of the scale ; but suppose you first play or sing a tune in the key of A, and while the ear is full of the sensa&not; tion so produced, play then two notes A, I) ; they will not sound the least like the first tw r o notes of the tune&#39; in question. Why not ? because they are not the 5th and 8th, but the 1st and 4th notes of the scale just pre&not; sented to the ear. The interval is identically the same as before, but its position on the scale being different, its character is entirely altered. All this is so obvious to anybody who is familiar with the sound, not to say the theory, of music, that one might fairly have assumed at once that in the principle I have tried to illustrate&mdash;the principle, as it is called, of &ldquo;tonic&rdquo; or &ldquo; key relationship &rdquo;&mdash;is to be found the explanation of all the differences , in the musical effect of sounds and intervals. The truth of this is too simple and self-evident to be formally denied ; but yet, wonderful to relate, one of these two popular methods, the one dignified with Government patronage, and therefore of course accepted as orthodox by all innocent people, actually ignores this one principle whereon depends the possibility of recognizing the difference between one sound and another.,In the &ldquo;Hullah&rdquo; system the student is taught to sing every note by mea&not; suring its interval from the note that preceded it , irrespective of the place of either in the scale. He is taught to call each sound by a name indicating its absolute, not its relative pitch ; and he is given the hopeless task of learning by practice the sound of every possible interval that can occur in music, the only characteristic which distinguishes one from another being at the same time carefully concealed from him. I call the task hopeless, for observe what he has to do : the number of different intervals which may occur in vocal music, without introducing &ldquo;accidentals,&rdquo; is, say six&not; teen (from a semitone to a tenth). Now most of these may occur in dif&not; ferent positions on the scale, and in each of these positions it has a different effect on the ear. For instance, a major third may occur, read backwards and forwards, in six different ways ; a major second and a major ninth in ten different ways, and so on. To recollect and reproduce at pleasure any one of these multifarious vocal effects, without the aid of the principle of tonic relationship, may safely be pronounced impossible. &ldquo; But,&rdquo; says an objector, &ldquo; this impossibility is perpetually being done by Mr. Hullah&rsquo;s classes,&rdquo; Yes, but to what extent, and how? Only by a very small pro-
portion of tlie total number of learners ; and by them it is achieved not by means of, but in spite of the method employed. This conclusion may seem paradoxical and ungracious ; I can only say that a considerable familiarity with the performances of Mr. Hullah&rsquo;s chorus, and some experience of sing&not; ing classes generally, has convinced me of its truth. The true principles of music are so beautifully clear and self-asserting, that they are unconsciously seized by the ear and voice of an intelligent student, though most carefully omitted from his &ldquo; Manual.&rdquo; He may sing in a dull mechanical way IVI. Wilhem&rsquo;s exercises, but when he opens liis &ldquo;Messiah&rdquo; he finds he has to deal with something very different from those dismal sequences of thirds and fifths. He is met by the principle of key-relationship, which soon enables him to sing &ldquo;by the light of Nature&rdquo; all the common intervals of the scale, and he thenceforth forgets, as he ought to do, the barbarous jargon about augmented fourths and imperfect fifths which has made his first fifty lessons a mass of complexity and dullness. For not the smallest evil about this yVilhem system is, that it begins by presenting the subject to the student m its driest and most repulsive aspect. It is not too much to say that a person must be a reasonably good musician before he can sing freely the iiist few exei cises iu the Manual. Why ? Because they are composed of salies of intervals which taken in sequence are not music ; to sing them is a mechanical feat only to be achieved by a resolute suppression of all the natural impulses of the ear. Let any one, to prove this, try a series of consecutive fourths on the pianoforte ; without the tonic principle, which the pupil is told nothing about, nobody could sing them. And yet they are written for the children in village schools ! No wonder that, as a matter of fact, the Committee of Council&rsquo;s big sheets are generally to be found re&not; posing unused, in the dust of the schoolroom cupboards. This fact, which leard testified by a large majority of a meeting of schoolmasters, would be of itself sufficient to show the futility of the method. Its irrationality rs indeed astounding. Take as an example one feature in the construction of these exercises Their whole virtue, if they have any, must be in ena&not; bling tue pupil, by the incessant reiteration of the same musical interval to know it again, and sound it when it meets his eye. Instead of this we Una whole pages of sequences headed by one name, as &ldquo;sixths,&rdquo; in which no distinction is made between the &ldquo;major&rdquo; and &ldquo;minor&rdquo; forms, which &middot; m 1 us ^ ans know, two radically distinct intervals. The major i * (t) * s physically and arithmetically different from the minor 6th (A) and yet the two occur intermixed in the same line, with nothing to show &iacute;e pupil tuat he is singing one thing one moment and another the next . bl &trade; der 80 monstrous, almost incredible, in a treatise professino- to be scientific, is enough to condemn the whole method without appeal P ro - bably, however, the author saw that an attempt to carry out his principle,İft &lsquo;T &deg; las , s s ; n 8 on , e &lsquo;bing at a time would produce results too &ldquo;t - distracting to be endured ; that such would be the case any one may convince himself by trying on any instrument a sequence of really similar intervals, e.g. the major thirds, 1,C, E ; D, F sharp ; E, G sharp ; F, A ; G, В ; A. C sharp, etc.,w a fi&#39;&#39;threVt&iacute;l S f n S a &iacute;b S &deg; &lsquo;ri thi .&lsquo;i Whe &rdquo; one &#39;ibrates backwards and for. ľľľufi? fi u о ю1е the 0lher does the satne five times, they are said to be timed in the lati&oacute; ň-. lhe 3 represents the shorter string and the deeper note.
But how, it may be asked, if this method is so hopelessly irrational, could it have obtained such sanction as has been given it ?&mdash;how can it have maintained its ground so long, and ivith some degree of practical suc&not; cess?&mdash;and how could such intelligent men as M. Wilhem and Mr. Hullah have fallen into such an abyss of unreasonableness P These things would be mysterious indeed if the world was not fidi of like puzzles ; but in fact they have all arisen from causes peculiar to the subject. Music has hitherto existed in the world in two forms. First, the Ancient and Popular, coeval no doubt with man&rsquo;s appearance on the planet ; secondly, the Scientific. This last is comparatively recent ; it was born, say about nine centuries ago, and has perpetually been in a state of conflict with its parent. 1 he history of the art during many ages may be summed up as Science versus Nature. Men were constantly trying to erect some petty invention of their own into general forms of composition, and to lorce all human ears thenceforward to own allegiance to these assumed eternal laws of the &ldquo; Science.&rdquo; It was discovered, for instance, by Guido, of Arezzo, A.D. 1000, or about his time, that a progression of &ldquo;fourths&rdquo; made purer harmony, arithmetically speaking, than a progression of any other intervals. Thenceforth it became a &ldquo; law &rdquo; that the harmony of con&not; secutive fourths or fifths, and that alone, should be admitted into the services of the Church. Now, and for some centuries past, as most people know, this has been the only sort of sequence absolutely forbidden to com&not; posers. Thus has the so-called &ldquo; Science &rdquo; been encumbered, age after age, by all manner of vain rules, invented by one generation to be rejected by the next. Spiritual analogies, among other things, served, in those strange times of logic and Aristotelianism to guide the theories of the monkish professors. We dare not scorn, but we must smile at, the devout simplicity which decreed that three-part harmony was the utmost that could be al&not; lowed in the music of the ritual, &ldquo; because of the Holy and Undivided Trinity.&rdquo; But all this while there was flowing on the ceaseless stream of popular music, springing fresh and clear out of the untutored instincts of the race, and fertilizing from time to time the barren domains of the pro&not; fessors of the art. So, in double shape, has grown up what we now call Music ; the conflict is nearly over, the resistless inspirations of genius, aided by the common-sense of the many, having almost demolished the pre&not; tentious sciolism of the theorists. Up to this time, nevertheless, only the very slightest advance has been made towards constituting music a true science. There does not even exist yet one rational book on harmony and compo&not; sition.* How silly then in this regard is M. Wilhem&rsquo;s pretence of con&not; structing a &ldquo;synthetic&rdquo; as distinguished from an &ldquo;analytical&rdquo; treatise on the subject ; as if a science could ever become &ldquo; synthetic,&rdquo; but in its most mature and perfect shape. Musicians, as distinct from composers, have as yet formed a craft and nothing more ; and it is only very recently that the task has been undertaken of revealing to the vulgar the mysteries of this craft. No wonder therefore if the earlier attempts to do this bear traces of the irrational confusion in which the subject has so long been enveloped. No wonder, in particular, that men should have dropped,* This is I believe tolerably notorious, but I should not have ventured to make so sweeping an assertion if I bad not heard it very lately from the lips of the greatest of living English composers.
into the error of mistaking a certain mechanical apparatus, the symbo&not; lical language namely ot music, for music itself. It is in this grand mistake that lies the irrationality of the &ldquo;Wilhem&rdquo; system. He professes to teach you the thing music, but instead of that he forces your attention upon the complex and cumbrous mass of signs whereby the thing music is represented. Before you know what a key-note is, you are pestered with ugly definitions of &ldquo;chromatic&rdquo; and &ldquo;diatonic&rdquo;&#39; scales; &ldquo;chro&not; matic scale being the name for something which does not appear at all in substance till the second part of the course.,That this is a total inversion of the first principles of all science and of all scientific teaching it is surely needless to prove. The Wilhem and Hul- lah &ldquo; Manual is a determined attempt to evolve in the mind of the learner the substance from the sign. In aiming at this impossibility it displays perhaps as much ingenuity as is to be found in any like treatise ; and in laet is a better handbook of the meaning of musical terms than any which I have ever met. There is probably no serious error of fact in the whole book : the controversy is not about the facts, but about the method of pre&not; senting them to the minds of beginners ; and considering that the English representative of the method is a gentleman who, by the concurrent testi&not; mony of all, is said to be endowed with singular gifts as a teacher, and to have used them with singular energy and perseverance, we need not wonder that the &ldquo; Wilhem 5 system, bad as it is, should have produced some results. As being one of the first to challenge the public attention, under the sanction of authority, to the possibility of teaching vocal music in classes, and as having devoted many years of a professional life, with no hope ot pecuniary gain, to the furtherance of this great movement, Mr. Hullah has most worthily earned the title of a public benefactor. But there is little doubt that it it had seemed fit to Sir Bob&eacute;rt Peel, Sir James Graham, and the other members of the Committee of Council of Education, in 1841, to submit the question to the judgment of some competent musical authorities, their choice would have fallen, not on the Wilhem system, but on one of the other methods which were then before the world, professing the same object. For it must not be forgotten that the Wilhem method is, in the critical point ot its application to sight&middot; singing, intrinsically new. lire names I)o, Be, Mi, etc., used by him to indicate notes of a fixed pitch corresponding to C, D, E, etc., on a pianoforte, can be shown to have been used tor three centuries past in England to represent the notes of the scale ; i.e. the key-note was always called Do, the second above it Be, and so on. Nobody who knows the vast progress made in a very few years by the Tonic system, can doubt that if such a system, or any reasonable develop&not; ment ot the old English method of the &ldquo; Moveable Do &rdquo; (a principle iden&not; tical with that adopted by the Tonic Sol-Fa-ists), had been patronized by the same high authority, and worked with the same energy which Mr. Hul&not; lah has devoted to his enterprise, the present wide diffusion of popular vocal musre would have been equalled at least ten or twelve years ago.,. us turn now to the Ionic Sol-Fa method. Its great merit is that, as its name implies, the doctrine of key-relationship is made from the first the guiding principle. The student is in the first lesson plunged into the study of the scale, thus at once grappling with that which is the essence ot all music. He rs shown the different musical effects of the different
notes of the scale, and is taught to call them by the names Do, He, etc., По always representing the Tonic ; Re, the second above, and so on. - o\\ theie is no doubt that this principle might be most thoroughly worked out without discarding our present musical notation ; but for the sake of saving the pupil in the first stage of the subject from the multi&not; farious perplexities necessarily attaching to the old notation; a new one is adopted, consisting merely of the initial letters of the names Do, Re, etc,, a&iacute; ranged with graduated spaces between them to indicate the duration of the notes. Ihus &ldquo; God save the Queen&rdquo; would be written as follows ;&mdash;,d : d : r J tj* : --d : r J m : m : f I m : -r : d I r : d : Q ( d,and so on. It is the introduction of this new notation which constitutes the first objection to the Ionic Sol-Fa method. This notation is, however, only meant as an introduction to that now used, and the question of its propriety can only be decided by ascertaining whether or not the art of reading music can be acquired more quickly by this means than by using the old language. In practice it is found that the extreme simplicity of the new notation enables even the youngest children to learn to &ldquo; read &rdquo; from it with astonishing ease and rapidity. This being so, it is surely pedantry to cavil at its introduction. When seen in use, its effectiveness becomes abundantly manifest. It saves the pupil the confusion sure to arise in the mind of a beginner from the cumbrous apparatus of flats and sharps as key-signatures ; but its chief virtue is, that it excludes every pos&not; sibility of his attempting to recognize the character of the different notes in any way but the right one. A resolute teacher might certainly make his pupil read on the tonic principle from the stave of five lines ; but in doing this he would have to be perpetually cautioning him against the false ways of measuring intervals : the Tonic Sol-Fa notation leaves the learner with no guide at all but the only true and infallible one. The cha&not; racters of the several notes of the scale with reference to the tonic are so distinct, that they can be made manifest to the ear and mind of a child in one or two lessons. He has then only seven distinct things to recollect, and Avhen he has learnt these well, he is in effect a &ldquo;sight-reader.&rdquo; For the occurrence of &ldquo; accidentals &rdquo; proper, i. e. of notes not forming part of the natural scale, and not implying a change of key, is comparatively rare in the familiar kinds of vocal music. When they do occur, the Tonic notation deals with them by a very simple change in its signs. &ldquo; Modu&not; lation,&rdquo; or &ldquo; change of key,&rdquo; on the other hand, is stripped of all its difficulty by a very ingenious artifice. On a transition into the key of (say) the fifth of the scale, or Soli, a sign is used to indicate that the Sok becomes a Doh, or a temporary key-note, and any phrases that follow in this new key are written with a Doh for this key-note as simply as if no change of&middot; key had occurred ; on reverting to the original tonic, the change is similarly indicated ; the Fa, or fourth note of the transition-key, becoming once more the original Doh. But -simple as is the new notation, it may fairly be asked, Why teach a language which you are afterwards obliged to discard ? Why not use the old notation at first, if you have eventually to,* The seventh of the scale is called &ldquo; Те,&rdquo; instead of Si, the j beine; already used for the fifth (Sol).
recur to it P Tlie answer is, that in practice thousands can be induced to learn the simpler system who never could be got to attack the more com&not; plex ; and of these, all who care to pursue the study beyond the point at which the new notation begins to get cumbrous (as it undoubtedly does it applied to the more elaborate kinds of music), can master the old nota&not; tion in a lesson or two, at a less expenditure of time on the whole than if they had tried to learn it at first. In adopting the new notation, they took the shortest road to the knowledge of the thing music, and when the thing is once known in substance, it can easily be recognized under any variety of form. It is then only as a subsidiary adjunct to the tonic principle that I am inclined to admit the efficacy of the new notation, the practical utility of this adjunct may be matter of controversy, but let it be clearly understood that the truth and supremacy of the tonic principle admits of no controversy or contradiction whatever; it is the principle which forms the root and basis of all music, equally irresistible, and equally applicable, by whatever different kinds of language musical sounds be represented. To compare then further a system which is built upon this principle with one which neglects or ignores it, is needless. It is self- evincut that one must be right and the other wrong. It is upon this issue that I prefer to rest the case for or against the respective systems. It would be easy to try the matter on other grounds ; for instance, the torpid stagnation of the Wilhem singing-schools might be contrasted with the signal and enormous success achieved in a very few years by the Tonic Sol -Та Association. Individual experience again might be appealed to, and its verdict would certainly lie to the same effect. Personally I never met a single reasonably good sight-reader who had acquired the faculty by the &ldquo; Wilhem &rdquo; method. On the other hand, among members of the choir which challenges public criticism as the best example of the training given by that method, there are certainly some whom I know to be incapable of singing securely the plainest musical phrase ; and although it may seem ungracious to quarrel with a body of amateurs to whom the people owe so much real pleasure of the highest kind, I cannot help saying that the style in which that choir performs any unfamiliar work (as Mozart&rsquo;s Re&not; quiem), is so hopelessly bad in comparison with its handling of the more often practised pieces, as to prove that the method produces&#39; 3 more singers by ear and memory than independent readers.,Such are some of the reasons why the elementary singing-class at the Lnn don &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; is being taught on the Tonic Sol-Fa method! ihat in this great city there must be many teachers adopting other ra- tional ways of using the tonic principle, I have no doubt. One at least Mr. O. W Martin has achieved a singular success in the organization and teaching of large choirs of children ; but his plans do not, so far as I know assume the shape of a system. Having heard him describe his manner of handling his classes, 1 am inclined to think that his method if deve&not; loped, would prove more thoroughly rational and satisfactory than any now before the world We have had to choose one from among those systems which have had the greatest visible share of success, and are at the same time most complete. The decisive progress made by Mr. Phillips&rsquo;s class ш their first dozen lessons, shows that we have not chosen badly.,P. B. Litchfield.
AFTERNOON CLASSES FOR WOMEN.*,There are afternoon classes for women at our London &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;. But who attend them ? I should like to hazard a few guesses on that subject. If I do not hit the truth, I may at least provoke an answer from some one who knows better than myself.,I shall begin by saying that of course they are not attended by those who are commonly called Working Women. That is to say, not by paid workers, engaged in trades or crafts. For such, the only time for study must be not afternoon, but night.,Is it right, however, to apply the name &ldquo; working woman,&rdquo; exclusively to these hired workers in shops or factories ? Are we not unnecessarily narrowing the class by so doing? Why not rather acknowdedge all Lon&not; don as being one great factory in which &ldquo; women &rdquo; are &ldquo; working,&rdquo;&mdash; working at housekeeping for the men of their families? In London, surely the number of paid working women must be very small in compari&not; son with the number of women belonging to the working classes,&mdash;wives, sisters, or daughters, of working men. I suppose, then, that these wo&not; men, who are working, not for wages, but in the houses of their own rela&not; tions, can find time to attend the afternoon classes. Their business in this world is to clean rooms, to cook, to mend clothes, to tend children ;&mdash; dreary mechanical work, even that last mentioned can become, if their minds are not now and then roused out of the thoughts connected with its routine, if they are not occasionally allowed to feel that they belong to another world besides the world of pinafores and perambulators. Men tell them that nothing is more likely to rouse this consciousness in them than the cultivation of their intellects. The man who has felt himself benefited by the study of mathematics, or philology, or natural science, desires that his wife, or his sister, or daughter, should share the gain with him. It is for her own sake, as well as for the pleasure which will accrue to him from her learning to sympathize in his tastes, that he urges her to go to the afternoon classes at Ids own College.,Her housework is done, and dinner is over, I suppose, by two o&rsquo;clock ; and the &ldquo; neat-handed Phillis &rdquo; can leave the washing-up to some younger or inferior hand, while she devotes a couple of hours to her classes,&mdash;or to preparing for them, if it happen not to be the day allotted to her special branch of study. And a very happy couple of hours it is for her ! She enjoys the quiet of Great Ormond Street, enjoys the notion of having got into an intellectual atmosphere for a brief space of time. It is true it is not always quiet : grinding organs sometimes invade the sublime neigh&not; bourhood, and even profane Ethiopian serenaders. But what is their noise compared to the perpetual setting of saws in &ldquo;father&rsquo;s warehouse,&rdquo; or the continual hammering, squeaking, and groaning that issues from &ldquo; my bro-,[* We welcome with particular pleasure the first appearance of this subject in our pages. Surely the teachers and students of these classes must have forgotten that they are part owners of this magazine, or we should have heard from them before. Their branch of the College work is surely as difficult as our own ; and on the mere score of its greater novelty, and the greater shock which it offers to common prejudices about education, might demand more ample discussion.&mdash;E d. W. M. C. M.]

Instead of doing this, we have done, I fear, the reverse of this. We have led the labourer to think that the wisdom we possess, and of which we would give a portion to him, can be gotten for gold ; and that its value can be represented very accurately in gold or silver or copper measures. Whatever kind of instruction we have offered and recommended to him, we have tried by this scale. c If &lsquo; you will only acquire these fragments of physical information, who can tell &lsquo; you that you may not be as rich as Sir Richard Arkwright ? If you only &lsquo; make yourself master of these dogmas of political economy, what advan- &lsquo; tage you will have over those about you, who know nothing of the laws &lsquo; which regulate wages ! If you will but let your children swallow these &lsquo; lumps of divinity, there are examples without end of well-behaved boys,* who were sent to College, and became in due time Prebendaries and Deans,,* to say nothing of richer stalls that may be reserved for them in some other &lsquo; state of existence.&rsquo; With these and the like pious frauds, together with the comfortable assurance, in the last case, that if they will receive our wisdom they will be saved from the danger of being too wise, we have stuffed the ears of those to whom we might have held out promises and hopes of quite a different kind,&mdash;promises and hopes which would not have proved, as these do, utterly disappointing in the millionth case in which they are lite-

We have no space for more than a bare mention of this very interesting meeting.,Cambridge .&mdash;On Monday, June 6th, about 50 of the teachers and students paid a visit to Ely, on the invitation of the Dean (Dr. Harvey Goodwin), who is still Principal of the College. The chief feature in the day&rsquo;s proceedings was a visit to the Cathedral. The Dean ac&not; companied the party over the building, and gave all the required explanation as to its history, architecture and restoration, past and present. After fully exploring the splendours of this grand old place, the party met for dinner at the Angel Inn. The Dean occupied the Chair, Mr. Gray taking the post of Vice-President. Mr. Butler made a few remarks after dinner, congratulating the College on the success of its first excursion. Mr. Morel thanked,the chairman on behalf of the students for his kindness in showing them over the Cathedral, and proposed his health, which was drunk with hearty goodwill. The party re&not; turned to Cambridge much pleased with their day&rsquo;s excursion.,Cricket .&mdash;A match was played on Satur&not; day, June 4th, on the &ldquo;Gospel Oak&rdquo; ground, between the College and an eleven of the &lsquo;Belmont Club&rdquo; (Price&rsquo;s Patent Candle Company). The weather was bad, but the game was pursued with spirit in spite of the heavy rain. The College went in first and got 43 runs, Messrs. Sanson and Eotheringham dis&not; tinguishing themselves by the highest scores (13 and 13). The Belmont Club then began their innings in a style which promised the College an easy victory, several wickets falling in quick succes&not; sion before the good bowling of Mr. Sanson and Mr. Bromhill, but the stand made by some of their later champions altered the aspect of the game. The chances seemed pretty evenly balanced when a brilliant catch by Mr. Wright stopped the career of the most successful hitter on the Belmont side, and finished the game leaving the College victorious by a majority of 43 against 39 runs. A fraternal tea wound up the day&rsquo;s enter&not; tainment.,A match is to come off with the &ldquo; Spot- tiswoode Club,&rdquo; at Page&rsquo;s Ground, Tufnell Park, on Saturday, July 2nd, at 4 o&rsquo;clock.,OUR CONVERSAZIONE.,London.,To describe a pleasant meeting a fortnight after its occurrence is a difficult matter, to me at least. The morning after it is the time for telling of the pleasures of it ; and I only wish, Mr. Editor, that you had ordered me to send you an account of our yester&not; day&rsquo;s walk, instead of our fourteeu-days-old Conversazione. Would I not have expatiated on the glories of Hampstead and Hendon fields, the sweet smell of the hay on the mown ones, the brilliant yellow-green on the cleared ones, dotted with white shorn sheep and glowing chestnut cows, aud then have taken you by lovely out-of-the-way lanes and
paths, between hedges of white roses and woodbine to Harrow Hill, with its view over all our London plain (but with no tea for thirsty walkers), and then to Greenford Green, through wheat and oats shoulder-high, and grass nearly ready for the scythe, till the sky flamed up with the sunset, and left us, happy and grateful, to walk home hy the gleams of the lightning. But instead of all this, it seems you want me to tell, for the benefit of country friends, after what fashion we spend the one particular evening in the year that we give to social festivity. The Conversazione is the theme. What has one to say about it ? Suppose one has a country-college man outside our big bow-windowed house in Great Ormond Street; one walks him up the steps, and into our library on the left, to buy a shilling ticket; here he sees the Secretary in a semi-glass box (carefully con&not; structed so as to make it as difficult as possible for people to get at him), hooks from floor to ceiling all round the room, and down it a long table covered with drawers of fossils. Crossing the hall he passes up a stone staircase into the drawing-rooms, two long and lofty red-papered places, with the folding-doors between thrown open ; on the walls of the first room are the pencil and water-colour drawings of students (arranged by Mr. Allen and Mr. Jeffrey), from the first night&rsquo;s trial at that puzzling smooth round hall, to the latest achievement of the Colour Class, a magnificent sunburnt Navvy, in blue striped shirt and red neckerchief, seated at ease, pipe in mouth, and spade and jacket on knee. About, are books of prints and photographs, lent us by Miss Twining, Mr. Jeffrey, Mr. Dickenson, etc. ; and Mr. Slade shows us his microscope. But the great attraction of the evening is in the back drawing-room, for there stands the grand piano, backed by songstresses and singers, in white and mauve, blue, pink, and green, and black,&mdash;members of our Women&#39;s Classes, and kind friends who come to see us once a year, and brighten us up with the sight of their faces and the sound of their voices In the curve of the piano, with his back towards us, but his face towards the bright eyes in front of him, which all watch him eagerly, stands the happy Conductor of the eveniug, and Editor of this Journal. Place aux dames! He calls on the Women&rsquo;s Singing Class to lead off. They cluster together ; their teacher. Miss Bell, takes her baton, and off they go, with a glee that delights and surprises us all, so great is the improvement since last year, so fresh and clear the voices, so spirited the singing. Then follow quartets and solos hy our guests, Mendelssohn part-songs and lightsome English ditties,&mdash;Miss Hawes, Miss Wedg&not; wood, Miss Spottiswoode, and Miss Gaskell (a name not unknown to Manchester Colleges, or indeed a far larger world, for who has not read * Mary Barton &rsquo;?) are the lady singers. Their performances are applauded to the echo, especially when our great English war-song, &ldquo; Come if you dare,&rdquo; is pealed out by Mr. Coleridge, and repeated with emphasis by the chorus. We thread our way through the singers, on to the balcony behind them, and see in the garden a grand tent that our tea-provider, Mr. Earee, has put up for us, and under which he is dispensing tea and cake to knots of people, standing about or sitting on benches, chatting and strolling up and down. A pretty sight enough, and so the singers think, for all adjourn to the garden. There, in the cool of the June evening, they give us that solemn Sanctus of the Russian Bartniansky, winding up with its jubilant Hallelujah,&mdash;and other lighter pieces, finishing with the hardy Norseman sung in full chorus, with ad libitum accompaniment by the non-musical part of the audience. Then our riflemen that areto be, strike up &ldquo; Riflemen, form !&rdquo; while a cricketer on the outside suggests that the conductor&rsquo;s place is a decidedly agreeable one, and that next time the Eleven ought to undertake it, and let each man have his innings as usual. Then we have tea, and chat, and go hack to the house&mdash;up to the second floor, the drawing-class rooms, where we see some wonderful copies of Turner&rsquo;s water-colours, and rapid studies of clouds, made by Mr. Bunney, one of our members, for Mr. Ruskin ; and which have so pleased our great and much-loved and honoured teacher that he has sent the doer of them out to Switzerland to see and paint the mountains and pines of that artist&rsquo;s home. Thanking Mr. Cormack, who has, notwithstanding his illness, brought the drawings to the College, and shown them to us, we pass into the Museum, and find Mr. Seeley who has worked so perseveringly at it, showing its treasures and expounding the analo&not; gies of the fossil specimens with their living descendants. Mr. Fleming, with his mi&not; croscope, shows us the heart-heating of a transparent animalcule kicking about in its drop of water ; and we come back to the piano as the music begins again. After the first chorus, a voice is heard calling out &ldquo; Find Hurst, and pass him forward ;&rdquo; a cheery &ldquo; Here he is, make way for him&rdquo; is answered, and one of the gems of the College brush-maker Hurst, appears amidst much cheering, and starts Dibdin&rsquo;s song of &ldquo; The
tight little Island.&rdquo; If the reader does not know the words and the tune, it is hopeless to try and describe to him the effect of the song ; if he does, let him fancy it sung by a rich manly voice, the women&rsquo;s classes and our guests bursting into shouts of laughter at the incessant rush of quaint wit, all about &ldquo;Billy the Norman, that wonderful war- man,&rdquo; &ldquo; who thought ť would be handy, to leave his Nor-mandy,&rdquo; &ldquo; and go for to live on the Island,&rdquo; etc. etc., and all of us joining in the swinging chorus at the top of our voices, and he will well understand the hurricane of. encores that greeted Mr. Hurst when the song was ended. We had it again, and then followed more chorusses, duets, and songs, the Men&rsquo;s Singing Class not daring to attempt anything after the admirable singing of the women, and only putting forward one of their members, a very big boxer, for a solo, and he, though he publicly assured us his &lsquo; heart was of oak,&rsquo; and that he was &lsquo; ready to fight and to conquer again and again,&rsquo; has since confessed that that organ of his body felt like a large pudding in his throat, and that the gaze of so many bright eyes was so terrific that he had to hold on to the piano to preserve his equilibrium. After a few more songs,&mdash;among them, &ldquo;Where the bee sucks,&rdquo; from Miss Wedgwood, and Kingsley&rsquo;s &ldquo;Three Fishers,&rdquo; from Miss Gaskell,&mdash;come &ldquo; God save the Queen &rdquo; and &ldquo; Buie Britannia then with three cheers for the ladies, three more for our guests, and one for the conductor, our party broke up at eleven, and then we adjourned to the tent and sang on till twelve, every one saying that it was the most successful gathering the College had yet had, but saying too. Why cannot we meet ottener ? Why are we only to see one another once a year ? How much longer are men-students and woraen-students, men-teachers and women-teachers to be kept so separate from one another ? Both sides would be glad to know more of each other, both know they are as worthy of trust as people who have money enough and houses big enough to meet their own friends whenever they want to :&mdash;then hurrah for another Conversazione in London at Christmas ; let us all follow Sheffield and Halifax ; and may Ancoats, Salford, and Manchester soon have a Grand United Social Meeting, and a dance if they like, and go for excursions together; and let all Members of &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; know, what all unpriggish members of older Colleges (and Schools too) do, that the social life of a College is worth as much as its intellectual training, and that any College which does only the latter part of its work and leaves the former undone, is as yet performing only half its functions.,F. THE,Storking Щпп College Hi aga mie.,No. YIIL AUGUST 1, 1859.,AFTERNOON CLASSES FOR WOMEN.,{Continued from p . 119.),What is taught in them ?,The list of subjects for each day of the week is usually appended to the prospectus issued by the more noble portion of the College ; but 1 have noticed that it has not appeared in the Magazine since the month of Ja&not; nuary, therefore I think the readers of the Magazine may be glad to be reminded of its contents and characteristics. The information it conveys is general rather than specific ; and thence it arises that but little change has had to be made in the wording of it since the first term (January, 1856). Every day there appears to be one class for the benefit of the wholly unin&not; structed, and another calculated to attract, or at least to interest, the better-informed and more studious. No division into Adult School, Pre&not; paratory classes, and College classes, is mentioned , but of course it is necessary that these distinctions should be made. &ldquo; Persons whose attainments are very different cannot be taught together,&rdquo; says the Bishop of St. Asaph, in his valuable remarks on teaching, reprinted by the Chris&not; tian Knowledge Society from his charge in the year 1844.*,The list of subjects for the Women&rsquo;s Classes comprises Reading, Writing, Arithmetic, Geography, History, English Grammar, Natural History, Domestic Economy, and the Bible, as well as Yocal Music and Needle&not; work,&mdash;-not by any means a bad programme, if the teachers do indeed go methodically and scientifically into the subjects which they profess to teach, encouraging their pupils to insist on &ldquo; learning principles,&rdquo; accus&not; toming them to be dissatisfied with whatever is superficial.f,Rather meagre, perhaps, but not a bad programme ; and yet a minute ago, when I had used the word &ldquo; attract,&rdquo; 1 felt myself obliged to with&not; draw it and substitute for it the word &ldquo; interest,&rdquo; because I doubt whether this list is sufficiently definite to be attractive to those who know nothin^&middot; of the College except through its printed papers. The men&rsquo;s list, being- far more explicit, suggests a good deal to him who looks at the board outside the house, or turns over the prospectus lent him by a friend ; but,L 3,* Bishop Short, in the above-mentioned charge, is speaking particularly of teaching in Sunday Schools, but much that he says is applicable to teaching in general. He uives itas the result of his experience that it is an &ldquo; impossibility &rdquo; to teach in one dass pupils of various attainments; and that where it is attempted &ldquo;none are reallv taught.&rdquo; J t AVhether the pupils are to feel that they are really members of a Colleue united together for high ends by other than mercenary bonds ; whether they shall feel that they are learning principles instead of merely acquiring a few scraps of miscellaneous infor- mation, must depend mainly upon the teachers, upon the principles which govern their thoughts and acts, upou the fellowship and mutual understanding that there is anion &quot;■ them.&rdquo; (&lsquo;Learning and Working,&rsquo; Preface, p. xvi.)
all that can be learned from the women&rsquo;s list is sadly vague. The man sees announced such or such a period of English History ; such a play of Shakspeare; certain defined portions of the Arithmetical and Mathema&not; tical course ; certain particular branches of Natural History, or Physical Science. The woman is only told in the most general terms that Natural History, Geography, History, etc., are taught. The names are clearly printed in small capitals, but what do they convey to her mind?,History.&mdash;I cannot tell whether the History be ancient or modern, English or foreign ; whether the class be conducted in accordance with the plan laid down by the Historical Section of the Council, and published in the second number of this Magazine, or in accordance with some other methodical scheme. Might not the period to be studied during the actual current Term b&eacute; specified? But be this as it may, I am glad that working women are, in some way or other, studying history, learning to read the secrets of the present by the light of the past, and so becoming less liable to be carried blindly and helplessly along by the oratory of reli&not; gious fanatics or political demagogues.,Natural History. &mdash;Of course it is not to be supposed that the ladies teach Natural History in all its branches at once. They must be well aware that if they choose too wide a subject, their lessons will necessarily degenerate either into what are called object-lessons,* or into a series of mildly interesting, but unscientific, essays or lectures, such as the students might read for themselves (if they thought it worth while) in the ť Leisure Hour,&rsquo; or some such periodical. I take it for granted, therefore, that each teacher restricts herself to one branch&mdash;or one small twig of a branch&mdash;of the great science, and makes her pupils enter into the exa&not; mination of it deeply and minutely. They thus gain more, and retain more, than if they went more lightly over wider ground. Would it not be well to mention in the programme what particular parts of Zoology and of Botany are under treatment at a given time ? Botany seems to me pe&not; culiarly deserving of woman&rsquo;s attention ; and I should hope that if Mr. Seeley were to address himself to the women&rsquo;s class, he might find more than one student able and willing to undertake the arrangement of the plants in the Museum.f Why simuld they not ? I can scarcely imagine a more agreeable way of fixing on the mind its newly acquired knowledge of the botanical system, nor a more suitable employment in our College for man&rsquo;s neat-handed helpmeet.,Arithmetic. &mdash;There can be no doubt that, in Arithmetic, the women, like the men, must find it necessary to have several sections ; and would it not be more satisfactory if they Avere to advertise the part of the course at which each section is working, so that those who are without might know precisely what they have a chance of learning if they please to go within,&mdash;whether the Elementary Bides, or Practice, or Proportion, or Algebra ?,The value of mathematical studies to women, is put in a strong light by some words of Lord Bacon, which formed part of the extract from the &lsquo;Advancement of Learning&rsquo; printecWin this Magazine for June ;&mdash;&ldquo;For if,* Object-lessons may be very useful in Infant Schools, for aught I know, but adults should not be taught like infants. (See &lsquo; Learning and AVorking,&rsquo; pp. 139, 140.) f See page 81.
the wit be too dull, they sharpen it ; if too wandering , they fix it ; if too inherent in the sense , they abstract it.&rdquo; But though I would wish to promote among women the study of Abstract Arithmetic, and of Geometry, I should like to feel sure that the practical applications to the necessary business of &ldquo; this workaday world &rdquo; are not neglected. I hope our Lon&not; don women have the means and opportunity of learning something about discount and interest, the systems of book-keeping, and the best and simplest form for mere household accounts, and many other things con&not; nected with money-matters. Surely this may be done while they are never&not; theless being led to think more of their inward life than of their material prosperity.,Domestic Economy.&mdash; Being, alas! profoundly ignorant of this science,,I cannot say much about it. No one will deny the great importance of it. I suppose it was introduced among the Women&rsquo;s Classes partly in order to link the female branch of the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; with the general College for women, rich and poor, which it was once proposed to establish. Under the head Domestic Economy are included medical lectures (some of which, by Mr. BarweU, on &ldquo; The Care of the Sick,&rdquo; have been published since they were dehvered) and lectures on Economical Cookery. Thus the subject is connected at some points with natural science and natural history. A previous understanding of the elements of natural philosophy and of physiology, and a knowledge of the principles of chemistry, must make the domestic economy lessons far more interesting than they would other&not; wise be.
