About English literature as other things, it may be good that when a woman de&not; sires to learn she should &ldquo; ask her husband at home,And what delights can equal those That stir the spirit&rsquo;s inner deeps When one that loves, but knows uot, reaps,A truth from one that loves and knows ?&rdquo;,but before she can do so, she must feel the &ldquo; desire to learn.&rdquo; Belonging to a class for the study of English literature may rouse that desire without satiating it ; much will still be left to be learned at home.,Needlework: Vocal Music. &mdash;Long may they prosper in the wo&not; men&rsquo;s branch of the College ! I hope the invention of sewing-machines may never have a more deadening effect on the lingers of women than the invention of musical instruments has had on their voices. To sing with the heart and the understanding we must have a living voice ; and to sew with the heart and the understanding we must have living fingers. For it is not mere sewing that is to be done, it is dressmaking, waistcoat-making, millinery, the adap&not; tation of old materials to new purposes, and much more that involves the exercise of judgment, design, and taste. Now it may be said, with truth, that working women, in general (mdess they be of the very lowest and most uncultivated class), know better than ladies how to turn old things to ac&not; count, how to make shift with poor or scanty materials. Their necessity has been the mother of their invention. But still, it is a wise saying that &ldquo; two heads are better than one.&rdquo; The gentlewoman may at any rate show her neighbour how to execute her task most tastefully. And if the in&not; struction in the Needlework Class is mutual, is not that exactly what we should desire ? The most valuable point about a Sewing Class is that it forms such a sociable meeting : it may be considered to correspond to the social teas announced by the men. The teachers of the other classes may feel it difficult to find opportunities for establishing a cordial human rela&not; tionship between them and their pupils without distracting their thoughts from the proper subject, or wasting the lesson-hour; but the fortunate head needlewoman has no such difficulty to contend with ; she must feel that she and her pupils are at liberty to employ their tongues, speaking out of the abundance of their hearts, in cultivating each other&rsquo;s acquaintance : and who knows whether that acquaintanceship may not gradually ripen into true friendship among some ?,The Bible. &mdash;All the studies I have spoken of I prize because they stir up the mental faculties, as I believe the social intercourse and musical en&not; joyment do stir up the moral and spiritual faculties, and so make the whole man more fit to receive the highest lessons of Christian wisdom. But it is not necessary that I should presume to say a word upon this subject ; the teacher has spoken for himself in his &ldquo; Introductory Lecture on the Stu&not; dies of the College.&rdquo; That lecture, I doubt not, was read with equal in&not; terest by both men and women students. To the naturally pious mind of woman it must be peculiarly delightful to be shown how all the meaning,
and all the value, of her other studies, are connected with the study of the revelation of God, the study of the nature of &ldquo; that other order or king&not; dom &rdquo; in which &ldquo;there is neither male nor female,&rdquo; but &ldquo; all are one in Christ Jesus.&rdquo;,ON THE STUDY OE MATHEMATICS.,{Continued from p. 69.),Having in my former paper gone into the mental discipline which is afforded by the study of Mathematics, I shall now describe the various things which are learnt in the course of studying them, so far as they have been already carried at the London &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, or as we may reasonably expect that they will be carried at such colleges: it would serve no present purpose to detail the bearings of mathematics on tlie higher branches of science.,The student is commonly recommended to begin with Euclid&rsquo;s geometry, though in fact that subject and algebra are so distinct that neither re&not; quires the other as a preliminary, and therefore either may be taken as a point of departure. If any one has leisure to carry on both at once, he will find it advantageous to do so, because they will occupy about equal periods, and the next step (trigonometry) will require a knowledge of both ; but if only one can be taken at a time, there is this advantage in beginning with Euclid, that, as geometry appeals to th eye, it is more easily remem&not; bered, whereas if you begin with algebra, ana then intermit it while reading Euclid, there is a chance that your algebra may be somewhat rusty when you want to use it in trigonometry. I believe that there are very few members of the London college who can be recommended to attempt their Euclid and algebra together, since&mdash;speaking exclusively my own opinion, but that an opinion founded on observation&mdash;I believe there are very few who have the leisure to prepare out of class for more than one class of any kind, still less for more than one mathematical class. The student shall therefore be supposed to commence with Euclid, and to carry it through the first four books, the sixth, and such parts of the fifth, eleventh, and twelfth as are now read : about a year&rsquo;s work for an average man, having only his evenings, and, it is to be hoped, some human interest to occupy a large part of them. In this course then he will have learnt the properties of triangles and quadrilaterals, and of regular (that is, equilateral and equi&not; angular) pentagons and hexagons ; of parallel straight lines and of circles ; arid generally of all such figures as may be formed by combining straight lines and circles in one plane, that is, upon one level surface ; and he will have received his first notions about figures not lying in one plane, or, in other words, about bent or curved surfaces and solid bodies, by beino- at least made to contemplate different planes, whether parallel or intersecting and straight lines and angles lying in those different planes, thouo-h the complete investigation of the properties of solids and curved surfaces must be reserved for a later period.,Now by all this, first, the student will have learnt many useful properties ot common figures which every one must meet with more or less and winch those in particular must meet with often whose work has anythin^
to do with making, shaping, or designing. Secondly, his eye will be trained to an appreciation of fonn generally, to an extent which it is not possible for one to understand whose conceptions of form are so vague that he can&not; not even conceive how much clearer they might be made. Thus, in geome&not; trical problems, one of a beginner&rsquo;s chief difficulties is to draw the figure. Do that for him, and he will soon give you the solution ; but if the figure involves something even no more recondite than a few triangles in different planes, or a cube drawn in perspective, his first efforts to represent to the eye the conditions which are expressed in the words of the question com&not; monly result in scratches which only bewilder himself. But after being taught by Euclid&rsquo;s geometry to analyse the more elementary figures into the various straight lines and circles of which they are composed, and again to put straight lines and circles together in various positions relatively to one another, and even, to a certain extent, in different planes, so that they will require something like a perspective representation, the following re&not; sults will appear :&mdash;Not only will the student be master of the figures which have been thus put before him, but his conceptions of form will have been so improved that he will be able to imagine clearly and correctly many a figure not even within the range of his Euclid, and even to represent it so as at least to suggest his meaning to himself, or to another. Of the figures so imagined many a property may become apparent to him by the mere force of his clear and accurate conception of the figure to which it belongs, and thus many a useful arrangement may be hit upon, the full argumenta&not; tive demonstration of which may as yet transcend the powers of his mathe&not; matics. And verbal descriptions of things, localities, and figures which he has not seen&mdash;which descriptions must always remain in constant use, how&not; ever widely the art of drawing may be extended&mdash;will no longer present to his mind&rsquo;s eye that meaningless confusion which it is greatly to be feared that they present to many ; he will be able both to understand them him&not; self, and to give them, when necessary, to others, with such precision of language as may call up a corresponding image in their mind&rsquo;s eye.,The student passes next to algebra, which, under the same conditions of an average working man with average leisure, will take him another year. This is universal arithmetic, the science which proves general truths about numbers, expressing them by letters for which numbers must be substituted when particular applications of each general truth are wanted to be made. Here he will gain, first, a familiarity with many processes which will shorten arithmetical calculations, as well as a tact in choosing that process which will most shorten any particular calculation : secondly, a familiarity with many actual numerical results, as squares, cubes, and the sums of certain numerical series : and from both these causes he will be&not; come a much more expert arithmetician, and especially a much more ex&not; pert mental arithmetician. Often also occasions arise when a number is desired to be known which is not stated, but which is involved in other numbers which are stated, and which therefore by common arithmetic could only be found tentatively, by trying what number will satisfy the given conditions, and so guessing more closely each trial : but algebra will give the desired number at once, by an equation. Besides all this, however, the student will also learn in algebra much of which he will not see the direct use ; and, although he can scarcely fail to feel the indirect
use in the improvement and training of his reasoning powers, as well as the interest excited by the great generality of the results, he will do well also to bear the following remarks in mind, as an encouragement in the matter of direct utility. Geometry furnishes no assistance in making cal&not; culations, and yet calculations are of vital importance where many kinds of work have to be done. If you survey a country or navigate a ship, a map of the country, or a chart of the ship&rsquo;s course, is a beautiful result appeal&not; ing directly to the eye, but must be arrived at by calculation based on the observations. If you build a ship or a bridge from a model, no ruler or compasses will enable you to enlarge the parts with sufficient accuracy, but there must be calculation. If you are making some solid object, and want a working drawing of its projection on some plane, it will very often happen that calculation will have to be resorted to : almost always, unless your geometry goes beyond Euclid&rsquo;s. Now these calculations will conti&not; nually involve the properties of other curved lines besides circles, and also of curved surfaces, which properties can be more easily investigated by the application of algebra to geometry, than by pure geometry: and even where Euclid&rsquo;s figures only are involved, his geometry will not give you such an ordinary numerical result as the ratio of the circumference of a circle to its diameter, or the actual measurement, in degrees, minutes, and seconds, of the several angles of a triangle, the lengths of the sides of which are known. Such results are obtained by methods requiring the employment of those algebraic series which seem at first so abstract and useless. 1,The next question then for the student must be that which, in the histo&not; rical development of mathematics, was the next for the discoverer, namely, to combine algebra with geometry, or to complete the doctrine of form by showing how to calculate magnitude accurately. The first step in this is what is called plane trigonometry, a name which signifies in Greek the mea&not; surement of plane triangles. The subject so designated teaches the student how to calculate the measurements of the various parts of those figures of which Euclid has taught him the general properties. Now Euclid&rsquo;s geo&not; metry deals only with straight lines, and the direct measurement of the size of an angle cannot be effected without the use of a curved line, viz. the circumference of a circle. An indirect way of measuring angles is there&not; fore adopted wherein the proportions existing between certain straight lines are taken as determining the values of the angles. It is the chief business of plane trigonometry to show how to find these proportions or ratios (sine, cosine, etc.) and tabulate them, and how to apply their properties to the &ldquo; solution of triangles,&rdquo; that is, to such questions as this given that one side of a certain triangle is seven miles, and another thirteen miles lono&middot;, and that the angle between these sides is 30 degrees, find the values of the remaining angles and side. The most interesting problems of this kind are those which concern the determination of the distance of the heavenly bodies from the earth, and from each other. In connection with such questions as these are commonly also explained the methods of finding tabulating, and using logarithms , by which all actual calculations are not only made, but so immensely facilitated that in fact very large numbers could hardly be dealt with without them. It is in the higher parts of this subject that the student begins to see the use of algebraic series in the com&not; putation of logarithms, and in the ascertainment of the ratio of the circumfer-
ence of a circle to its diameter. The most important practical application however, of plane trigonometry is to surveying , and the student is thus made acquainted with the structure and use of the sextant, theodolite, and other instruments employed in surveys. But it is only surveys on a small scale that can be conducted with the knowledge as yet obtained, as of a farm or a town : in mapping a country the triangles into which the surveyor would divide it could not be considered as lying in one plane, from the curvature ot the earth&rsquo;s surface, which renders what is called spherical trigonometry necessary, a subject which we have not taken up at the London college, nor probably shall. Lest however the student should underrate the value ot what he has learnt, it may be observed that that really includes the whole principle and method of applying measure to geometrical magni&not; tude : the limitation of its use lies merely in his not yet knowing the pro&not; perties of any but plane figures.,Nor does he as yet know even all these, for before he can attack the pro&not; perties of curved surfaces there remains the discussion of those plane figures w hich are not, as the plane figures in Euclid&rsquo;s geometry, to be constructed out ol straight lines and circles alone. Of these we have at the London college taken up those simpler ones only which are known as the conic sec&not; tions, namely the parabola, ellipse, and hyperbola. They are important from their connection with dynamics and astronomy : the parabola being the curve in which a stone thrown from the hand, or a bullet shot from a gun, would move but for the resistance of the air ; the ellipse that in which a planet would revolve round the sun ; and so forth. The same curves are also still more important to the mechanic as examples of the theory of projection, being in fact the projections of a circle upon certain planes by straight lines drawn through a certain point : and there is nothing of greater practical use in art than what is called descriptive geometry, that is, the method of obtaining by geometrical construction the various plane projec&not; tions of plane and solid figures. In that manner a vast deal of labour may be saved in obtaining working drawings, where the thing to be made, being small, will need no enlargement from the drawing ; or where, the minute accuracy of calculation not being required in the thing itself, it may be enlarged from the drawing to scale. It is a branch of science which has been more cultivated in France than in England, but one in which the beneficial effects of &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt;, in establishing a closer connection between science and art than has hitherto existed in this country, may one day be most conspicuously experienced. The conic sec&not; tions also introduce the student to another application of algebra to geo&not; metry, in which it is made available for investigating the properties of figures, no less than for computing magnitudes.,There can be no necessity for dilating on the practical advantage of the science of mechanics. How great things may be done without any know&not; ledge of it is indeed sufficiently shown by the rectangular vaults of (rotine cathedrals, or the pendentives by which the Mahometans in India have earned the construction of the dome to such a pitch of ingenuity and beauty. Yet how little may be so done, in comparison with what science will effect, is shown by considering the same examples from another point of view. They are attained only by eliminating the failures, and accumulating the successful steps, of centuries of tentative effort. Science, by predicting re-
suits on safe grounds, anticipates the slow improvement of mere construc&not; tive skill : and thus one generation has seen a greater progress made in bridges than separates the nave of Amiens or King&rsquo;s College chapel from the basilica of Constantine, or the tomb of Mahomet at Bejapore from the Pantheon. We have not at the London college gone beyond the elements of scientific mechanics, but they are the grammar of the subject, and, besides, are sufficient for the intelligent appreciation of much of the com&not; mon operation of force which always goes on around us. The distinctive natures of solids and fluids, and the conditions under which forces will be in equilibrium when acting on simple bodies or machines, both solid and fluid, as the inclined plane or Bramah&rsquo;s press, with the multiplication of power by machinery which results from those conditions ; the combination of several forces acting simultaneously on a solid mass, so as to produce determinate tendencies therein, as well to move altogether in a determinate direction, as to rotate while so progressing round a determinate axis ; the laws of motion, at least in their application to the motion of an atom, as the earth may be considered in its orbit round the sun, or the moon in its orbit round the earth ; all this may be easily explained to one who has mastered Euclid&rsquo;s geometry and the conic sections : and in fact our course has included, in the first three sections of Newton&rsquo;s &lsquo; Principia,&rsquo; the full demonstration that the elliptic paths of the earth and moon result from the law of gravitation ; while the motion of a clock pendulum, con&not; sidered as a heavy atom hung by a thread without weight, might be ex&not; plained and demonstrated without any greater difficulty. Larger mathe&not; matical knowledge is required to calculate the disturbances introduced into the elliptic paths by the interfering attractions of other planets, and the motions of bodies and systems of bodies which cannot be regarded as atoms, as, for instance, that of a real pendulum of some bulk suspended by a bar of which the weight cannot be neglected. But the elementary know&not; ledge here sketched out is in many applications strictly accurate, in many more gives a near approximation to the truth, and always enables the stu&not; dent to look with an intelligent eye on the course of nature, which to one ignorant of mechanical principles must wear as confused and mysterious an appearance as the trade announcements and advertising boards to one who walks through the streets without being able to read.,The only other subject into which we have conducted our students is plane astronomy, that is, not the science of the heavenly motions as result&not; ing from the force of gravitation, which is called physical astronomy, and in which we have already described our breaking ground as incidental to mechanics, but the science in which the heavens are mapped and described, and the heavenly motions exhibited as facts without reference to their causes. Except in connection with navigation and the grander terrestrial surveys, which are not likely to be of much use to us personally, the inter&not; est of this subject is rather speculative ; but there is no other which tends more to elevate the thoughts and enlarge the mind.,So much has been done. May future years show that it is done always better, more thoroughly and solidly ; and that some at least are incited and aided to go farther, and not only conquer wider fields for themselves, but promote for the public good the alliance between science and art.,John Westlake.
CORRESPONDENCE.,CURWEN v. HULLAH.,[&ldquo; Presto &rdquo; writes at length in reply to the article in oar last number. Not having space to print his letter entire, I omit such passages as do not concern the critical points of the controversy. Perhaps an evening&rsquo;s conversation on the subject would be more in&not; teresting to our students than a prolonged discussion in these pages ; to this end I pro&not; pose that such as care about the question meet at the College on Thursday, August 5th, and talk it over together. The remainder of this letter will there obtain, as it deserves, due consideration.,I add a few notes on the chief points at issue.&mdash;R.B.L.],Dear Mr. Editor,&mdash;,* * * I think the article is too severe, and moreover contains errors, as for in&not; stance where you inveigh against the system because &ldquo; whole pages of sequences are found headed by one name as &lsquo;sixths,&rsquo; in which no distinction is made between the major and minor forms.&rdquo; This is wrong, for at page 49 of Hullah&rsquo;s Manual, on the introduction of sixths, I find him telling his pupils that &ldquo; iu the diatonic scale there are two kinds of sixth, major and minor,&rdquo; etc., and the major and minor sixths are fully explained, both in words and by examples (1). * * * *,Speaking of the number of persons who have been taught under Hullah&rsquo;s system, you say, that the few who are efficient have achieved their efficiency &ldquo; not by means of, but in spite of the method employed&rdquo; (2). * * * * Again, &ldquo; The sequence of similar (3) inter&not; vals, e. g. major thirds,&rdquo; is complained of and is described as being part of &ldquo; a hopelessly irrational method.&rdquo; Now I vote for singing sequences of seconds, thirds, and fourths, and in fact of all intervals major and minor, till they are known&mdash;till we are able to re&not; cognize&mdash;till we are able to produce the required sounds at pleasure (4). We are taught to write first by making strokes, stroke after stroke (not more pleasant certainly than sequences of seconds or thirds, but equally necessary), then &ldquo; pothooks,&rdquo;&mdash;whole pages of them,&mdash;then hangers, and so on ; and, when these are properly mastered, let&not; ters are set us to copy, composed of straight strokes, pothooks, and hangers, just as Hullah introduces songs containing only seconds, thirds, or fourths, according to the advancement made in the lessons (5).,Once more (quoting your article), &ldquo; It is not too much to say that a person must be a tolerably good musician before he can sing freely the first few exercises in the (Hullah&rsquo;s) Manual.&rdquo; If this is so, he must be a much better musician to sing the first few in Curwen&rsquo;s. Hullah&rsquo;s consist of no interval greater than a second, while Curweu&rsquo;s first exercise introduces thirds and fourths, and the second one sixths (6) ; but the pupils are requested to get a friend to set a &ldquo; pattern,&rdquo; and the exercises are &ldquo; to be repeated till fixed in the memory.&rdquo; But the reason why the exercises of Hullah are objectionable to you is &ldquo; because they are composed of series of intervals, which taken in sequence are not mu&scaron;ieľ Admitted : neither are the straight lines, or the curves, the horizontal, vertical, or oblique lines of the elementary drawing lessons entitled to the name of drawing, but when combined artistically, what then P I think this a fair comparison (7), if it is, it is one more to Hullah. I think too that it is right to learn the names of things before study&not; ing their composition, nature, and peculiarities ; but you blame Hullah for teaching the symbols of music before music itself, I think if you were an artist&rsquo;s student you would be taught the name, composition, and peculiarities (8) of colours before you were instructed how to apply them. Do not you ? Surely you do. Then why blame Hullah for doing a precisely similar thing with music ?,Further, you say the musical effect of any note depends not upon its absolute pitch, but upon its position in the &ldquo; scale.&rdquo; This can hardly be so, for it is equal to saying that a Tenor voice can sing with equal effect an air written for a Soprano (9), and vice versa. True, the notes are in the same relative position on the scale, but one set being an octave higher than the other, makes all the difference. So we find the musical critic in the &lsquo; Times&rsquo; now and then complaining of this air or that chorus being sung in a higher or lower key than was intended by the composer, and so marring the effect. There can be no doubt, I think, that in many cases as much depends on the key or pitch as on the relative position of the notes.
And now for a word about Curwen&rsquo;s Method.,If under Hullah&rsquo;s System it is, as you assert, &ldquo; a hopeless task to learn by practice the sound of every possible interval that can occur in music/&rsquo; so it must be infinitely more so under Curwen&rsquo;s ; which, to perplex completely, has re-christened every note in the scale with the following euphonious names (10), Doh, Doi, Dow ; Kay, Eoi, Row ; Me, Moi, Mow ; I ah, hoi, Fow ; Soh, Soi, Sow ; Lah, Loi, Low ; Te, Toi, Tow ; and has introduced several more (which I have come across in reading the lessons, but I cannot tell to which of the notes they are parasites), namely, Bah, Fi, Ne, Ni, Nu, Tu ! Only twenty-seven in one octave ! And yet the system is so simple that even children,&mdash;in&not; fants,&mdash;little infants who cannot read, can appreciate it ! Leggatt and Mr. Crooks, have just matricidated at the Londou University. Mr. Leggatt&rsquo;s name appears in the First Class, that of Mr. Crooks in the Second.,London.&mdash;The Cricket Club has played two matches during the past mouth. One was on July 2nd, against &ldquo;the Spottiswoode Club&rdquo;(Uueen&rsquo;s Printers), at theTufnell Park Ground, Holloway. The scores were :&mdash; College, 1st innings, 54 ; second innings, 48. Spottiswoode&rsquo;s, 1st innings, 76 ; 2nd innings, 27, with the loss of 4 wickets ; the match being thus won with six wickets to go down. The leading scores were ob&not; tained by (on the College side) Mr. Roe&not; buck, 16, and by Messrs. Holloway, Young, and Cordingly (Spottiswoode&rsquo;s), 14, 14, and 13, in the first innings. In the second innings, Mr. Dudmesh (College) headed the list with 12. The most effective bowl&not; ing was that of Mr. Young, on the Spottis&not; woode side, and of Mr. Wright, Mr. Brom- hall, and Mr. Sanson, on the part of the College.,The second match was played on Satur&not; day, July 23rd, being the return with the Belmont (Price&rsquo;s) Cricket Club, at Bush- ell&rsquo;s Ground, Keunington Lane. Owing to the morning being a thoroughly wet one, some members of the College eleven did not make their appearance, and the Com&not; mittee of the Club are highly wroth that such degenerate conduct should be exhi&not; bited by members of the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;. The College eleven lost a closely contested match by one wicket, the score being, in the first innings, College, 46,,Belmont, 51; in the second innings, Col&not; lege, 35, leaving 30 for the Belmont; and, owing to the close and successful bowling of Messrs. Bromhall and Sanson, and the improved fielding of the College eleven, nine wickets were obtained before this was accomplished. The fielding of the College side in the first innings was very indiffer&not; ent. Those who got double figures were, in the first innings, Messrs. Sanson (10), Fotheringham (13), on the College side; and Messrs. Hilliar (12), and Dyer (11), on the Belmont. In the second innings, Mr. Roebuck (14) w r as the only player on either side w&#39;ho accomplished that feat.,Finance .&mdash;The Council lately appointed a Committee to consider the financial posi&not; tion of the College, especially with a view to ascertaining how far it was possible to bring the current expenditure within the limits of the income from students&rsquo; fees. The Committee consisted of three members of the Council and three students, Messrs. Roebuck, Tansley, Litchfield, Standring, Evans, and Emslie. After holding fifteen meetings, and examining in detail the whole of the disbursements of the College since its foundation, they have made their Report, in which they advise reductions under certain heads and an alteration in a few matters of house management. Their most important recommendation, however, is for the appointment of a permanent &ldquo; Finance Committee,&rdquo; to take the entire control of the expenditure of the College. This proposal the Council has at once adopted, and has nominated a Committee accordingly, to consist at first of the mem&not; bers of the late Committee, with the addi&not; tion of Mr. Furnivall. It will eventually
consist of six members, three to be elected by the Council and three by the students ; two to retire annually in rotation. The Report also contains a proposal to modify the pre&not; sent scale of fees, reducing the class fees about 25 per cent., and charging in addi&not; tion a member&rsquo;s fee of Is. a term. This question is still under consideration, and as it touches a matter of the utmost im&not; portance, the Council would be glad to hear, through the magazine or otherwise, what the students think would be the pro&not; bable effect of such a change. Any facts from the experience of other colleges as to the relation between fees and attendance, would be especially valuable.,The usual General Meeting was held on Wednesday, the 20th July. The Principal announced the admission of two more of our students, Mr. Daniel Leggatt and Mr. Crook, into the London University; and conveyed the thanks of the College to the Finance Committee, which had just con&not; cluded its labours. He afterwards intro&not; duced to the meeting his friend, the Rev. T. White, of Dover, who gave an account of his efforts to secure to the young work&not; ing men of that town some of the advan&not; tages enjoyed by the students of our London College. Mr. Seeley gave a promising re&not; port of the condition of the Museum, tell&not; ing us that all contributions would be made the utmost use of, though many spe&not; cimens had to be put away for the present in dark cupboards ; so ranch had the Mu&not; seum outgrown the space now allotted to it. Mr. Ludlow said that he purposed to devote four or five evenings in this Vaca&not; tion, commencing on Monday, July 25th, to reading Tennyson&rsquo;s new volume of Poems, the &lsquo; Idylls of the King.&rsquo; Mr. Slade explained the modus operandi of the Botanical Class, saying (among other,things) what an agreeable relief he found it, after the busy labours of the day, to come down to the College and spend a couple of hours with his class, talking over the wild flowers and grasses, anatomizing them under a microscope, and finding out the hidden beauties of wayside weeds.,One whom our College holds in deep regard has just been visited with a great calamity. His eldest son, Walter Maurice Hughes, was drowned a few days ago while bathing in the Thames. Many of our students will recollect having seen the little fellow foremost in the merriment of those pleasant summer afternoons on which the College has enjoyed his father&rsquo;s hospitality at Wimbledon. Those who knew him had seen, at nine years old, the outlines of a character full of life and energy enough to have made him in future time such another as his father. We dare not whisper a word of &ldquo; condolence &rdquo; to a household desolated by such a loss : let us only say that their sorrow has been in some sort shared by many whom the social intercourse of the College has bound to Mr. Hughes in the ties of a cordial friendship. Of such at least the lesser grief may be softened by the,&ldquo; Trust that those we call the dead Are breathers of an ampler day For even nobler ends.&rdquo;,Vocal Music. &mdash;It is proposed to ar&not; range, after the Vacation, for occasional meetings of the College Singing Class, and the choir belonging to the women&rsquo;s classes.
