SEPTEMBER 1, 1859.,&ldquo;MHAT IS THE USE OE IT?&rdquo;,Have you never heard this question put&mdash;have you never yourself asked it, and asked it too in that spirit of triumphant assurance, which seemed to challenge the whole world to give you a satisfactory reply ? Those of us who are engaged in teaching in our &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; or other&not; wise, are often compelled to face this all-important question. It comes before us backed with such an amount of traditional authority,&mdash;it seems to appeal so directly to some of our strongest feelings and propensities, that we are apt to surrender our most cherished hopes and plans, and beat an ignominious retreat at its approach. If we cannot show the &ldquo; use &rdquo; of our teaching,&mdash;if we cannot point to some definite and tangible result to be gained thereby,&mdash;why teach at all? Now, I believe, our &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; have already helped us to grapple with this question, and, as they gather strength and influence, they will give us still more valuable help. Hitherto the most elaborate efforts have been made by the promoters and teachers of various branches of education to prove the vast utility of their several plans. To one class of aspirants, we have held out college distinc&not; tions, church-preferment, and whatever might be supposed to add to the dignity and emolument of our gentlemen-scholars. And what learned eloquence and laborious statistics have been exhibited to prove to the arti&not; san and the mechanic, how their wages might be raised, their trade in&not; creased, their means and appliances multiplied, their individual superiority over their fellows made manifest, if they would but join this or that class, prosecute this or that study ! Reverence for this great question, &ldquo; What is the use of it ? &rdquo;&mdash;a latent confession of our inability to resist its trium&not; phant logic, has lain at the root of most of our great educational move&not; ments. There have been exceptions,&mdash;neither mean nor few,&mdash;but I think this statement holds true in the main. One instance may suffice. Our Schools of Art are avowedly a commercial movement, established with the hope of proving how admirably the study of Drawing can meet the de&not; mands of this all-important inquiry. I, for one, cannot be too thankful for that grand key-note that has been struck by one to whom we all owe so much, in his &lsquo;Lectures on Learning and Working,&rsquo; in 1854,&mdash;which seems to indicate most unmistakably the position which we, as Teachers in &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt;, are bound to take.* For what does the in&not; quirer mean, when he asks us&mdash;&ldquo; What is the use of it ? &rdquo; In ninety-nine cases out of a hundred, I think he means neither more nor less than this &mdash;&ldquo; Can I get any money by it? &rdquo; or, in more general terms&mdash;&ldquo; Can I get some special benefit by it, which will give me an advantage over my fellows ?&rdquo; To such an inquiry, surely, our answer must be, &ldquo; We really do not profess to know, nor do we very much care, whether there is any use in what we wish to teach you or not. We do not see very clearly how our,M,.,&raquo;,* The passage referred to appears in the July Magazine, under the heading, &ldquo;Some late False Pretences about Education,&rdquo; p. 119 .
lessons in English Grammar, or Algebra, or Latin, or Drawing, will help you to get higher wages, or in any way raise your value in the labour-market. Most certainly we do not wish you to enter our classes at all with this hope, for we fear you will be bitterly disappointed. Nor indeed are we quite sure, that, were your anticipations of individual advantage most lite&not; rally realized, you would be any the better or happier for the change. We do not profess to make you a more perfect or useful machine ; we do try, and we hope to go on trying, to teach you to value more and more your true manhood. For, after all, do you really believe that the wages you receive, or any advantage which belongs to you separately, and which dis&not; tinguishes you from your fellows, truly express the best and deepest part of you ? There is surely something better and nobler for you and for me to think about than the everlasting seesaw of Profit and Loss. There are so many things that we are obliged to test by a market-value&mdash;so many claims upon our time and strength and brain, which must be regulated by the standard of utility, that the noblest &lsquo; use,&rsquo; the richest blessing, of our teaching and learning, of our College life and work, is, that it aifords us for one or two short hours in the week, a happy refuge from the ceaseless hum and toil of the market, the office, and the shop.&rdquo; By taking this ground, we do most effectually secure the lowest ends of education. Me&not; chanical skill will rise to a higher worth, and be invested in the eyes of its possessor with new meaning and dignity ;&mdash;the means and appliances of life will be more sacred, because he has learnt to measure life itself by a far higher standard. The more a man is raised out of his selfishness, we may gene&not; rally affirm the more successful will he be in his daily business. Nothing so readily and habitually outwits and deceives itself as selfishness. It is continually killing the goose that lays the golden eggs, and rejoicing in its own short-sighted folly. But we must say boldly, &ldquo; These tangible results are not what we are seeking for ; if we gain them, we value them only as parts&mdash;subordinate parts&mdash;of a far grander whole.&rdquo; For our own sakes, as well as for the sakes of those who enter our Colleges, we must protest against this constant tendency, to put a market-value on those gifts which God has given to us all for the common weal ; we must avow our convic&not; tion that the heights and depths of no possible question that touches the life of man can be sounded by the boastful and oft-impertinent inquiry&mdash;,&ldquo; What is the use of it ?&rdquo; J. N. Langley.,Wolverhampton.,FIVE YEARS&rsquo; TEACHING AT A &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;WORKING&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;MEN&rsquo;S&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;COLLEGE&lt;/span&gt;.,About once a week, I should think, on an average, I am catechized by some friend or other in manner following : &ldquo; How get on your classes at the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; ?&rdquo; &ldquo; What do you teach them ?&rdquo; &ldquo; Does it,do them any good ?&rdquo; and so on. Perhaps it may not be useless to set down in black and white something of what one usually says in reply to these extremely reasonable queries. For scarcely any but those who ac&not; tually spend some time within the walls of a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; know what goes on there ; and that many desire to know, is plain, from their taking the trouble to ask, I wish also to say a few words on matters too commonplace perhaps to be made the subjects of a serious discourse, but of which the experience of some years has made me feel the importance. In
most things, a mathematical class will resemble any other ; and notions sug&not; gested by the teaching of &lsquo;Eule of Three &rsquo; or &lsquo; Quadratic Equations,&rsquo; will have some bearing on the work of a class in history or grammar. Without further preface, therefore, I proceed to my string of miscellanea. One form of the cui bono question is handled in another page of the present number. If that query be put in a more special shape, I think the best way to answer it is to say exactly what is the sum and substance of the work of a class in a London College. Well then, out of the millions of this big city there have come together some dozen or twenty men demanding to be taught&mdash; (say) algebra or arithmetic. Why there are only about this number is, all things considered, rather a mystery. However, we take the fact as we find it, and set about the work in hand. Sitting at the plainest of deal tables in one o&iacute; the upper chambers of the quondam residence of an eigh&not; teenth century Lord Chancellor, we spend two hours once a week in work&not; ing problems and discussing demonstrations. It is this process which some people call a &ldquo;lecture.&rdquo; This name, however, we utterly abhor ; but it is no easy matter to make a newly-formed class understand the dis&not; tinction. When a man first comes to our College, he is apt to walk into his class-room in the solemn and discreet manner befitting an entry into a public institution, and generally for a night or two will persist in regard&not; ing his teacher as a severely official personage, whose dignity is not to be lightly trifled with. Now nothing, I believe, can really be done till this notion is extinguished,&mdash;till teacher and students have got to understand each other, and have agreed to banish the foolish mauvaise honte which makes every Englishman shy of talking to a fellow-creature. The freer is the colloquial intercourse between teacher and students, the more is learnt in the time. To establish this is not easy ; but harder still is the task of setting the students on a familiar footing with each other. There seems to be some impassable obstacle to the fraternization of a dozen Londoners, though sitting side by side week after week doing the same work. Perhaps if they were to look ahead a little, and to consider that, though they have begun as a set of casual strangers drawn from all parts of a huge city, their enti y into a College has bound them together in a common pursuit ioi, it may be, some years of their lives, the interchange of speech and thought might begin earlier. The advantage thus to be gained cannot be overrated. I have always found that the better friends my pupils have been with each other, the better mathematicians they have become ; and there are some who can testify that a mutual sympathy on the subject of a quadratic equation has frequently developed into something more valuable than even sound algebraical views&mdash;to wit, an intimate and lasting friend&not; ship. a,I pass now to another point affecting still more directly the progress of the class. The whole time allotted for each of the most important sub&not; jects m the mathematical course amounts to about eighty or a hundred hours distributed over a year. Most of this time must be spent in dis- cussing principles; very little is left for practice or illustration. It is therefore only on one condition that such a small amount of actual teach&not; ing can result in a satisfactory and thorough knowledge of what is taught. This condition is, that the student shah systematically devote a certain portion of trnie m the interval between each lesson to Working at the sub-,&Iacute;tľifW &quot;Sf ^ 1Са11 У&rsquo; tlns 18 done by only a very few of our present students. Ihe mistake most commonly committed is the undertaking too,M 2
many subjects at once. Let me therefore most strongly urge our new stu&not; dents not to begin with more than one class at a time. I have frequently seen the attempt to learn Latin and Algebra at once, fail, simply from lack of time to do justice to the two things taken together; while either alone might have been done with reasonable completeness. No rule, of course, will suit all cases. Those who have more than the average amount of leisure may perhaps advantageously extend their range of study ; but for the com&not; mon run of students, it is most certainly advisable not to undertake more than one course, until they are quite sure that they can find time to do jus&not; tice to themselves and their teachers. It would be easy to enlarge upon this topic, but I know not how to make the caution more emphatic. An idea dropped into the head on a Monday night, will not stay there till the next Monday, unless, by recalling it in the interval, you give it a chance of striking root. All of us who are not heaven-bom geniuses must submit to this law of memory and association, and if in even the most purely rational ot the sciences (such as Euclid and Arithmetic) something depends on the memory, how much more must the same mie apply to such studies as History or Languages, in which the mind has to comprehend and recollect a vast number of perfectly independent facts ! It is to meet the necessity I am now insisting upon, that we have established the &ldquo; Practice Classes,&rdquo; and I should be very glad if these remarks should lead our students to take advantage of the arrangement (which we owe entirely to themselves), more systematically than at present they seem inclined to do. It would be foolish to try to conceal from a learner the difficulty of the work he is undertaking, from a fear of discouraging his ambition : nothing is gained by such a disguise. Much disappointment would be saved, and not a whit the less really achieved, if it were possible to make the beginner under&not; stand at the outset exactly how much he will have to go through to attain a certain result. Not that I would preach the do-a-little-and-do-it-well doctrine. Nothing would be done at all in this world if we were to wait till we had done any one thing &ldquo; well.&rdquo; No subject of human thought or action, however &ldquo; little,&rdquo; but what leads, if pursued, into mysteries too manifold and complex to be more than glanced at. Our mental &ldquo; images &rdquo; must have their &ldquo; ragged edge.&rdquo; All I wish to do is to induce our students to devote a little time, before they lay out their schemes of study, to a con&not; sideration of the &ldquo; why,&rdquo; the &ldquo;how,&rdquo; and the &ldquo;how much,&rdquo; of the several subjects catalogued in our programme.,But the &lsquo; how much &rsquo; is a question which has a special interest for teachers as well as learners. &ldquo; Well, after all,&rdquo; says some spectator, ab extra of our College work, &ldquo; what do you really achieve ? What visible result have you to show after an existence of now several years ?&rdquo; A ques&not; tion very reasonable to put,&mdash;not so easy to answer; for the effect of any single operation, however small or however great, on the life of the commu&not; nity, is so inextricably mixed up with that of thousands of other influences as to defy an exact analysis. But on the principle of the whole being equal to its parts, the experience of any one teacher, simply stated, may give an idea of the aggregate of the immediate results of the work. There come to me, as I have said, at the beginning of every alternate term, twice a year, some dozen students to each of two subjects. Arithmetic and Algebra. The arithmetic class is always the largest, and lasts for two terms (about eighteen lessons) ; the algebra course occupies the whole year, four regular tenns, and usually a part of the summer vacation. Some diminution of
numbers always takes place ; perhaps one-fifth of the arithmeticians and one-third of the algebraists have disappeared before the courses are over. This falling off is due partly to unavoidable causes, such as a change of residence or other private reason, and partly to the peculiarity of the sub&not; jects, which are too strictly logical to allow any to join in the middle of the course. In the arithmetic class I generally find that about one-half arrive at a considerable proficiency. In the algebra, the proportion of proficients is not so large, but I am inclined to think the average result is better. It is easier to learn a little arithmetic than a little algebra ; but to become a safe arithmetician without learning algebra at all, is ten times harder than to go through the entire algebra course. In each of these classes I always find a few really energetic students,&mdash;sometimes one or two,&mdash;who have a decided mathematical genius. Of the intellectual ad&not; vantages of the study I say nothing at present, only recommending any pupils of my own,&mdash;past, present, or to come,&mdash;to think over Mr. Westlake&rsquo;s late paper on the subject. But I have a word to say on the comparative results attained by minds of various degrees of capacity. Experience has made me sure, that it is not those who have the greatest natural bent for this study who gain on the whole the most from its pursuit. Its chief general use is to clarify the brain, to inculcate a habit of straightforward reasoning, and to saturate the mind with detestation of all and every&not; thing like sophistry and vagueness.* Now, in some minds, this &ldquo; lesser logic&rdquo; is a native instinct, which scarcely needs any training; others, on the contrary, find an immense difficulty in grappling with the simplest in&not; ferences ; but these, if they have sufficient perseverance, are the patients most benefited by a course of intellectual gymnastics. Mathematical reason&not; ing (like all pure science) is nothing but methodized common sense ; and anybody who enters on tbe study has before him this agreeable alterna&not; tive,&mdash;if he find it easy, he will be certain to find it interesting, as he will master with ease and precision the actual knowledge to which it introduces him; if he finds it difficult, he may be sure that it must be doing him good;, the difficulties which he has to struggle with will be only a proof that his brain needs the clarifying process : and let him always understand, that it is the extreme simplicity of the subject on which he is exercising his logical faculty, which makes it suitable for the purpose. Studies of wider range and deeper interest are, in virtue of those very qualities, bad instruments of education. The simplest question of politics, e. g. the re&not; lative justice of direct and indirect taxation, or the desirableness of free trade in corn, involves difficulties a hundredfold more perplexing than the most troublesome theorem in the Differential Calculus. In estimatili o&middot; then the results obtained by teaching the elements, as we call them, of education,&mdash;Arithmetic, Grammar, and the like,&mdash;it is not fair to reckon merely the number of effective arithmeticians, or writers of correct English, produced at the end of every year s course. To the conspicuous success of the few, we must add the advantages gained by the undistinguished mediocrity which constitutes the mass. Of the latter portion of the&deg;result, only a teacher, I think, can be a judge. No examination can bring it out ; no list of certificates can take account of it. Occasionally, indeed, I have been perplexed (as no doubt other teachers often are) by a singular phenomenon, namely, the union of the most indomitable perseverance and the most hopeless inability to understand anything of the subject under&not;,let is, of all vagueness which floes not confess itself to be such.
taken. There certainly are some exceptional organizations for whom two and two do not make four, and I dare say there are some to whom a pro&not; noun and an adverb are the same thing. I know a brilliant classical scholar, of an intelligence far superior to the average of mankind, who took two years, as a little boy, to do one long-division sum, and never got it right after all. I have not yet discovered how to manage when such people come to be taught mathematics, but instances of this peculiar inca&not; pacity are very rare. After making, however, all allowances, we must admit that the ascertainable net result of a year&rsquo;s teaching is not very great. The question, whether or not it is &ldquo; worth while &rdquo; to carry it on, must be decided by every teacher for himself. To any one who doubts whether taking a share in our work would or would not prove a pleasure and advantage to himself, I can only say, &ldquo; Crede experto .&rdquo; It would take more pages than the Magazine can spare, to say in how many ways the business of teaching in our London College repays any small sacrifice of time it involves. Surely an hour is not ill spent in helping one, if it were but one man, to a small share of the advantages which we ourselves owe to quite adventitious circumstances. If the art of teaching was in itself laborious and unpleasant, this would still be so ; but the veiy cir&not; cumstances of the case make the process of instruction the very opposite of troublesome. We are certain that nothing but a positive zest for know&not; ledge can prompt the wish which brings our pupils to us. Those to whom the idea of teaching recalls only the demoniacal hubbub of a schoolroom, or the dreary monotony of the University &ldquo; Lecture,&rdquo; can scarcely imagine how inspiring in comparison is the quiet energy of a class of working men. They all &ldquo; mean work their attention never flags, except from actual and justifiable fatigue. If, as ten o&rsquo;clock approaches, you see a pair of eyes looking suspiciously sleepy, you will find that their owner began his day&rsquo;s w T ork perhaps at five in the morning, and is only anticipating by a wink or two his six hours of rest. I might quote case after case, if it were well to mention names in these pages, in which the pupil&rsquo;s effort to learn has been made at a cost, in comparison with which the teacher&rsquo;s sacrifice of leisure is a thing of quite trivial account. The mere pressure of an habitual ten or twelve hours&rsquo; work per diem makes the devotion of an evening to mental labour a more serious sacrifice than most of us are aware of. Yet many working men, in defiance of these difficulties, contrive to fight their way to a degree of mental culture, not only more intrinsically valuable, but also greater in amount, than that of most so called &ldquo; gentlemen.&rdquo; The pleasure of meeting half-way this kind of effort, is surely worth the devotion of a fractional part of one&rsquo;s leisure time. After a day&rsquo;s professional work, a couple of hours&rsquo; teaching is a relaxation ; so at least I have always found it ; and though year after year the same round of subjects recurs, yet the constant change in the class prevents the reiteration becoming wearisome. But if consolation be wanted for any occasional dulness which may creep over the work, it may be found in the unbounded warmth of the recognition with which the class meets the efforts of its teacher. No words that I can use can do justice to the depth of this feeling, as exhibited in numberless little symptoms of personal gratitude. I am almost, indeed, tempted to complain that our students will not take a more sensible view of our rela&not; tions to each other. The slightest indication on the teacher&rsquo;s part of a willingness to help them, is received almost as a personal favour. They will not understand that what we do is only the hundredth part of what
we ought to be doing, and deserves therefore only a hundredth part of the thanks they give us.,These, then, are some of the aspects of our College work. I have given but a very imperfect view of it in even these few aspects ; but my picture is at least drawn from the reality. &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; must tor the present be reckoned among the small things of the earth. But we cannot help seeing that most of the good done among men is done little by little. Your grand universal regenerators are most commonly quacks. Holloway- Pills and Spurgeon-Tabernacles have a certain stretching vastness of con&not; ception about them to which it will not as yet do for us to aspire. Suffice it if our work is at least one little ripple on the great wave of progress which we hope is to leave the world at the end of our century better than it found it at the beginning. R. B. Litchfield.,A WORKING-PAINTER AND POET.*,There are many names of working men on the bead-roll of the poets of the nineteenth century: Robert Barns, the ploughman bardie; Robert Nicoll, the grocer&rsquo;s son, who wrote his heart in his poems, and died at three-and-twenty ; Samuel Bamford, the &ldquo; Radical,&rdquo; some of the &ldquo; Pas&not; sages &rdquo; in whose &ldquo; Life &rdquo; are now History ; Willie Thom, the weaver-poet of Inverary ; John Crichley Prince, the weaver-poet of Lancashire, who, like his favourite Goldsmith, wandered over half the Continent, moneyless and sometimes foodless, &ldquo;seeking novelty and losing content;&rdquo; Thomas Cooper, Chartist and shoemaker, whose &lsquo; Purgatory of Suicides &rsquo; is a monu&not; ment of the learning, genius, and patriotism of its author; the noble brothers Bethune ; and&mdash;not to extend the catalogue of truly royal and noble authors&mdash;William Whitmore, whose poems, with an appropriate in&not; troduction by the author of &lsquo; Tom Brown&rsquo;s School-days &rsquo; (well known to the members of our London College), has just issued from the press.,The poets we have enumerated are, in character and style, sufficiently various ; but there is one quality by which they are all distinguished&mdash; that of earnestness ; they each put their heart in their poems. Not that I claim this quality for them exclusively ; earnestness can never be altogether absent from any great or true poem, for it is one of its essential consti&not; tuents ; but, in the poets of the working class, I, a working man, believe that it is present&#39; in a pre-eminent degree. One need not look far to find the reason of this. To working men in particular, life is very real, very earnest, sometimes deeply tragical. The &lsquo; Battle of Life &rsquo; is to them no sham-fight : when, for instance, a large division of the army of labour is engaged in a &lsquo; strike,&rsquo; or &lsquo; lock-out,&rsquo; they find it no Eglintoun tournament, but a hard-fought battle,&mdash;an Austerlitz or a Waterloo. And when the working man is also a thoughtful man, and moreover a poet, however lovely and graceful and tender may be the pictures with which he delights our imagination, there will ever be a background of hard and rugged ex&not; periences ; and throughout his canvas, in every line of colour, in every stroke of the pencil, an all-pervading element of human sympathy. He will feel that &ldquo; man should not live by bread alone,&rdquo; that he possesses faculties and powers of quite another kind to those which are employed to,* Gilbert Marlowe and other Poems. By William Whitmore. Macmillan, 3j.
weave calico or make steam-engines. If he has learned &ldquo; to suffer and be strong,&rdquo; while his heart beats in sympathy for those who suffer and are weak, &mdash;and being weak, are crushed beneath the iron heel of circumstance, &mdash;there will be, with the suffering and the sympathy, the power of thought, the strength of will, and the earnest purpose growing out therefrom ; and these will become an element of his life and find expression &ldquo;in thoughts that breathe and words that burn.&rdquo; For &ldquo;out of the fulness of the heart the mouth speaketh.&rdquo; Shelley doubtless spoke from his own experience, when he said that poets &ldquo; learn in suffering what they teach in song.&rdquo; We may therefore expect what we actually find,&mdash;that the poet of the working class will write clearly, earnestly, with energy, and with a purpose. If he writes at all, he cannot well write otherwise. He is in no mood to dream dreams and weave idle phantasies, to write pastorals and loves of the butterflies. Or if he dreams, it will be&mdash;as with the &ldquo; Martin &rdquo; of Whit&not; more&rsquo;s poem&mdash;a waking and ever-present dream of some Utopia , some New Atlantis, which he will strive heart and soul to transform from the realm of imagination into the world of fact. As a true man, he will do true work and no sham ; and his work, be it a pickaxe or a poem, will be made for use, for actual service, and not for the toy-shop or the boudoir. But there are two kinds of utilitarianism :&mdash;one, that can see no good in anything which does not in some way help to make the pot boil, and which therefore looks oidy to outward material benefit,&mdash;improvement of cir&not; cumstances and the like ;&mdash;the other, does not regard man&rsquo;s highest good as consisting in either praise or pudding ; it looks not so much to the ele&not; vation of man&rsquo;s circumstances as to the elevation of himself ; and it there&not; fore aims chiefly to purify the affections, to correct the judgment, to strengthen the will, to expand the intellect, to arouse and stimulate all the nobler activities of mind ;&mdash;in a word, to lead the soul to shake off its sluggishness and torpor, and awaken to a true and divine life, to be lived out, not in self-seeking, but in humble uses consecrated by the love of God and of one&rsquo;s neighbour.,We are not, in these reflections, losing sight of Whitmore&rsquo;s poems, though I am afraid the reader will begin to think so ; but let him take the book in hand and read it carefully, and I think he will not find it diffi&not; cult to trace the connection between them. He will find not only those qualities I have pointed out, as characteristic of a people&rsquo;s poet, but many others ; among them, conspicuously, a deep love of Nature,&mdash;a towns&not; man&rsquo;s love, a town-poet&rsquo;s love of her,&mdash;the love of one who sees only at too rare intervals the face of the mistress whom he loves,&mdash;,&ldquo; And all its sweetness and its sacred charm.&rdquo;,Not only to him is Nature ever fresh and young and beautiful, but all her varied pictures,&mdash;,&ldquo; Brown solitary wolds, fair pastures green,,And winding waters gleaming &rsquo;raong the trees,&rdquo; the,&ldquo; Ancient hills, rugged and bold,,And grand old woods, in many-coloured robes,&rdquo;,and,&ldquo; The still broad night, with all her starry eyes,&rdquo;&mdash; are but the dim shadows of eternal verities, the faint adumbration of the ineffable glories that lie beyond,&mdash;the symbols and hieroglyphs in which God speaks to the heart of all his rational children.
Of the poems in this volume, &lsquo; Gilbert Marlowe &rsquo; is, to my thinking, by far the best : the most skilful in its construction, the most lofty in its tone, and throughout the most equally sustained, and, more than all, the one which mirrors forth most clearly the poet&rsquo;s soul with all its human sympathies, its heavenward aspirations, and,&ldquo;Thoughts ranging &rsquo;mid the shining infinite.&rdquo;,Gilbert Marlowe is a &ldquo; representative man ; &rdquo; one who has mused much on men and things ; on the past and present ; on the pressure of evil from without, and the nestling of sin within. He is overwhelmed with the burden of &ldquo; Human Life&rsquo;s Mystery &rdquo;&mdash;the Sphinx&rsquo;s riddle which he must solve or perish. He has walked in spirit,&ldquo; Through mists of wildering doubt, when Heaven was hid,,And all things earthly were distortions strange,,And naught was firm and certain,&rdquo;,until at length, through all these black cloud-mountains, came the light which turned the leaden clouds to gold ; and Faith, like an angel-presence spread over him her unseen hands in loving benison, and he felt that,&ldquo; O&rsquo;er us all,Bends Love Divine with yearnings infinite,,To bless us evermore.&rdquo;,A faith not more simple than comprehensive and profound ; one on which the heart of the child and of the sage may alike repose in peace. A,&ldquo; Pure, large faith, as liberal as the air,,That fills all spaces between us and heaven,,And still sustains the world.&rdquo;,I have spoken of only one poem in this volume, and of that but slightly. But as there is here not space for that full analysis and those copious ex&not; tracts which this and other poems in the book merit, I can but give it my heartiest commendations, and be thankful that such poets as William Whitmore are in the midst of us to give expression to those thoughts and feelings, which in thousands of minds and hearts are labouring, but cannot find adequate utterance. I cannot however part from him without quoting the first stanza, and part of the last, of a short poem entitled &pound; Our Posses&not; sions,&rsquo; addressed to his fellow-workman, John Roebuck, a Fellow and Member of Council of our London College :,&ldquo; Smooth your brows, good yokefellow ; the clouds go by ; the heavens endure !&mdash; Into sunlights bloom the shadows&mdash;we are not so very poor !&mdash;,When stern Day, our hard taskmaster, turns, and leaves our pain behind,,Night comes like a tender mother, bending o&rsquo;er ns, loving, kind ;,With her heaven on heaven she crowns us, plumes our feet with peace, to climb Where, like stars in mighty stillness, earth&rsquo;s Immortals reign sublime.,From their heights of fame they stoop, stretch to us fraternal hands,,Lift us up to their communion, seat us &rsquo;mid their skiey bands ;&mdash;,Oh then are we rich and glorious&mdash;brothers of the wise and great,,Heirs of all the worlds they conquered&mdash;lords of time and kings of fate !,Oh smooth your brows, dear friend and comrade ; look around, above, below,_,Wide extend our fair possessions&mdash;we are richer than we know !,There is store of wealth, exceeding lands and lordships, gems and gold ;,There is value deeper, greater, than the worth that may be told.&rdquo;,T. Shorter.
CORRESPONDENCE.,THE WAR BETWEEN MASTERS AND MEN.,Mr. Editor, &mdash;On the 4th oflast June the &ldquo;Amalgamated Trades&rdquo; published a mani&not; festo in the * Builder,&rsquo; ostensibly &ldquo; To the Building Operatives of the United Kingdom,&rdquo; but in fact to the whole country. One of the most important allegations there made is, that &ldquo; The condition of the mechanic, in all that relates to his physical comfort and the social independence which should result from the employment of his mechanical energies, is retrograding to a state of miserable dependence and social slavery.&rdquo; Now if we had not been told this on the high authority of Mr. George Potter, I should have been simple enough to think that considerable improvement had been made during my own experience in London labour. There are employers who, with the help of &ldquo;bully&not; ing foremen,&rdquo; exact an undue amount of work ; but these are quite exceptional cases. The statements published by the Secretary (Mr. Potter) of the Amalgamated Trades, are either disingenuous or the result of imperfect information. Mr. Potter says that the use of machinery is one of many causes of their depression and slavery, and that the middle and upper classes, by means of their capital, enjoy exclusively the advantages which should be shared by all. Does he mean that machinery, which has caused the prices of some textile fabrics to be lowered 500 per centum in fifty years&mdash;machinery that gives daily employment to hundreds of thousands of persons? All the old distaffs and spindles in the world could never produce and support a Manchester. As to the question, &ldquo; Are we any the better off for Manchester and its manufactures ?&rdquo; I say emphatically, Yes.,Having taken up a false position, Mr. Potter proposes a remedy for the evils he de&not; plores, quite in unison with the tyrannical spirit of the Society which he serves &ldquo;not wisely, but too well.&rdquo; He ignores such principles of Political Economy as supply and demand, and what Mr. J. S. Mill calls Wages-Fund. Mr. Potter says, by shortening the hours of labour one-tenth, they will make room for many who are now unwillingly idle. How does it happen that, in spite of the alarming increase of London, there is a surplus of building labour in the market ? Why, in addition to many other attractions, wages here are the highest paid in Great Britain. Does Mr. Potter think that by in&not; creasing those attractions he will lessen the influx of country artisans ? There is a very general opinion among men of his class, that four hours daily would be sufficient for all purposes if every one were to do their share of hand labour ; they would feel a glow of satisfaction if Sir S. M. Peto were compelled to lay bricks, and Mr. Cubiti, M.P., to carry him mortar. Communism has not made sufficient progress in England for that ; but the &ldquo; Nine Hours &rdquo; movement is meant to be a step in that direction. Although further exactions on the part of the workmen may be solemnly denied at present, if their de&not; mand is granted, the first favourable opportunity to wrest another hour from the day&rsquo;s work will be seized and the struggle renewed. Although the shortening the hours of labour might at first sight appear as much the interest of the employer as the employed, inasmuch as when the workmen leave, supervision ceases, and an hour&rsquo;s extra leisure would be as acceptable to one man as to another, there is, however, this important difference : the gain to the workmen would be direct and unqualified ; but the master, bound to complete his engagements for fixed sums, would lose 10 per cent, on the cost of labour necessary to finish his work,* so that his profits would be too much reduced to be remunerative. Hence the alarm that has seized the masters, to some of whom the immediate adoption of the nine hours&rsquo; day would be ruinous. They are driven to self-defence by an imperious demand, but they can devise no better plan of resistance than imitating the very thing they condemn and writhe under. Is this how the master builders prove their superior intelligence? When their workmen &ldquo;strike,&rdquo; do they think it becomes them to &ldquo; strike &rdquo; again ? They not only strike, but widen the breach by insisting on conditions they know to be in the highest degree offensive to their workmen. At the same time they confess their helplessness by applying to Parliament and the Home Secretary, not asking them to do anything, but bewailing the hardships of their,* Our correspondent forgets a fact which destroys the force of this plea. During the earlier part of the &ldquo; Nine Hours &rdquo; agitation, the men repeatedly stated that they did not desire the change to take effect till sufficient time had elapsed to provide for the fulfilment of the masters&rsquo; existing contracts.
position. The haughty indifference which too often accompanies wealth, has been turned into alarm, hy discovering that a mandate, issuing from an obscure tavern, can inflict a loss of &pound;100 per diem on any large building firm that may be chosen by the men as a warning and an example. Certainly, Sir, there must be something radically wrong in the attempt to settle the question by a sheer trial of strength. From well-earned ex&not; perience, I may say that ten hours&rsquo; building labour is not oppressive ; the vexation of being without work, and the great distance often necessary to be walked while seeking employment, added to the contemptuous way in which some masters and foremen give you a denial, is more exhaustive and tormenting than the hardest conditions under which I ever exercised my craft. The proposed alteration in the hours of labour is much more likely, I believe, to increase the surplus supply of skilled labour in London than to de&not; crease it ; and, therefore, I conclude the Trades&rsquo; Society was not justified in making the demand. Their only tenable ground is the right to obtain the best terms they can for themselves, without violating the law.,Perhaps, Sir, you may say, &ldquo; But what have we, as a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, to do with these trade disputes?&rdquo; We may not have any direct influence in this present struggle, but we are very much concerned with what I conceive to be the cause which has led to it, viz. an ignorance of those principles which govern commercial enterprise. If we examine the sort of knowledge on which working men act, it will be found to be, On an average, personal experience in their trade, information &ldquo; picked up &rdquo; from newspapers and other ephemeral publications, with some controversial skill acquired by attending conferences and discussions on matters connected with their Societies, held almost invariably at public-houses, where accommodation is provided for them in con&not; sideration of the profits arising out of what they drink and smoke. That systematic instruction in and acquaintance with history, political economy, ethics, etc., which Work&not; ing Men&rsquo;s Colleges supply are, I am bold enough to say, entirely unknown to the great body of Trades&rsquo; Unions. When any great crisis occurs, there are of course men to take the lead, whose abilities are above the crude state and low level I have mentioned ; but a very little sagacity is needed to detect many glaring falsehoods and unsound argu&not; ments in the manifestoes of the &ldquo;Amalgamated Trades.&rdquo; While master builders adore the &ldquo; Golden Calf&rdquo; with their usual devotion, and the workmen study under the shade of Bacchus, there is not much hope that unselfish pursuits, and dry, but valuable, learn&not; ing, will receive much help from them. While, however, we see such exhibitions of fierce passion and ruinous conflict between classes whose interests are identical, as ex&not; emplified in the Builders&rsquo; Strike, it is the best possible evidence, not of the advisableness merely, but of the absolute necessity, of making earnest endeavours to instruct the work&not; ing classes on liberal principles. In conclusion, I would ask the builders if the comple&not; tion of the New Palace at Westminster were to be retarded for ten years by their obsti&not; nacy, or the &ldquo;Westminster Palace Hotel&rdquo; were never to be finished at all, would the public be distressed by it? Or if 20,000 men out on strike, and 500 masters, were ruined tomorrow&mdash;which God forbid they should be !&mdash;who would be the losers ? Is it not distressing to see our fellow-citizens foolishly divided into hostile camps, when our united efforts may any day be required to repel a really dangerous enemy ?,A Carpenter.,the builders&rsquo; strike.,Sir, &mdash;It is thought by many of our friends that a &ldquo;&lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; Maga&not; zine &rdquo; ought not to remain silent on a subject of such momentous interest to the working classes as the Builders&rsquo; Strike and Lock-out,&mdash;that, supported as our Journal is by earnest and large-minded employers ou the one hand, by working men on the other, it ought to afford a most favourable arena for the fair and impartial discussion of the various questions underlying or arising out of the present struggle. I hardly venture to share such a view. I fear it is a time when class-interests sway men so mightily that it is impossible to speak on the points at issue, and not to give offence to the one side or the other. I therefore trust that if you admit any communications (this one included) on the topic in question, you will insist upon their being strictly individual ; confine them strictly to your &ldquo; correspondence in every wise wholly disengage the cause of &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; from any responsibility which may attach to them.,Not indeed that &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; might not have much to do, in an indirect way, with such matters as strikes and lock-outs. When I see the twaddle which the
1 limes &rsquo; and such-like public instructors pretend to enforce under the name of &ldquo; poli&not; tical economy,&rdquo; I remember with regret that for the three first terms of our College life, if I recollect aright, a course on &ldquo; Political Economy &rdquo; was regularly announced in our Prospectus, and as regularly failed of calling two scholars together. If working men would really study what the great writers on Political Economy, such as Ricardo or Mill, have given as the result of their inquiries ; if they would test the conclusions which those writers have come to by the lessons of their own experience, and their own ex&not; perience again by the laws which the professed economist has sought to evolve, they would learn in time both what political economy (as now understood) can teach and what it cannot ; they would spare many blunders to their own class, and might at the same time prepare the foundations of a political economy far more deserving of the name than that of the present day, one which should take account of man as man, and not as a mere wealth-producing mechanism. As it is, when those great crises come which bring the questions of economic science uppermost among the facts of daily life, the working man is for the most part all unprepared to discuss them otherwise than from the point of view of his own particular calling, which often other working men even find it difficult to enter into, or, still worse, from the point of view of some wild, crude theory, made up of two or three facts, thrice as many assumptions, conclusions reachable only with seven-league boots, and about two dozen of the hardest words which the dictionary can supply ; add half a page of unsound statistics, dealing chiefly with millions and higher numerals, and the full stock-in-trade of a professional spouter for working men&rsquo;s rights is complete.,For myself, I would say to working men &mdash;&ldquo; Study political economy ; it concerns you, it is emphatically your science. But in God&rsquo;s name do not believe in it ; test it strictly; see where it fails ; look beyond it.&rdquo; Slowly, half-unwillingly, the general public, and even its jackals the journalists, are beginning to acknowledge that neither trade societies nor even strikes are to be reckoned as economic heresies in our present social condition. But there is a temper of mind of which I am more afraid than even of that which would restore the Combination Laws : it is the ultra-economic temper; that which is blind to the human aspects of the question; blind to that fearful oppositiou of interests and con&not; sequent enmity between class and class to which the existence of trade societies testifies ; blind to that hell-brood of miseries, sufferings, hatreds, which every strike and lock-out almost is sure to beget. All these evils are utterly incurable, I believe, if ever&mdash;with a recent writer in the &lsquo; Builder &rsquo;&mdash;we come to consider a strike only as the solution of a question of pure science, to sit by, quiet and unmoved, as he would have us do, till the one side or the other lias starved or bullied its opponent into terms. Between indifference to human suffering and that of the Roman, seeing the gladiators murder one another in the arena, there are but a few steps ; and as surely as his indifference but heralded the decay and downfall of his country, so would ours that of our own.,I do not think I ought to say much more. 1 suppose we should all rejoice if, by working nine hours a day, or eight, working men in general could obtain a livelihood without impairing the production of the country. I suppose we should rejoice still more, if by means of this limitation of individual labour, a larger number of the unemployed were set to work, and the spread of pauperism so far checked. More still, if such an end could be achieved, without diminution of the rewards of labour. More still, if the possession of larger leisure led the worker to more diligent self-improvement in body and mind. But we should not rejoice, if the attempt to enforce untoward reductions in the hours of labour were to hinder the completion of contracts for which the em&not; ployer is alone responsible ;* if the undue curtailment of profits placed a check upon the employment of capital in any given trade ; if the time won and the wages received were spent in drink, brutality, sloth, empty self-indulgence, whilst really benevolent employers were crippled in their means for the advancement of those in their employ. Such are the formidable &ldquo; ifs &rdquo; which meet one at every turn in considering any specific question of wages or hours of work, as soon as one endeavours to look fairly at both sides of it ; which should make us understand how easy it is for good men to take either view, very diffident of ourselves in condemning them for their choice ; above all things, which should make us long for some way out of the strife and struggle, some means of reconciling the conflicting interests, some passage from discord into harmony.,So much you will perhaps allow an old &ldquo; Christian Socialist &rdquo; to say upon the matter.,J. M. Ludlow.,* See Note to the other Letter.

See the Programme.],NEWS.,London .&mdash;The pleasant meeting of our women&rsquo;s and men&rsquo;s classes at our Con&not; versazione in June (p. 120), has given rise to some joint country-jaunts of certain members of the classes.* The first was a party of twenty to the Crystal Palace, when our hospitable friends, Mr. and Mrs. Hantier, gave us tea at their pretty cottage, and we spent a very pleasant evening. The second was a party of twenty-two to Box Hill a quiet stroll through woods and fern, a climb up the steep chalk down, dinner under the trees, a rush through the close-growing box for a bathe, chat and songs on the downside, tea on the cottage- lawn, looking out over the wooded and corn- gold vale to the greensand hills and Sussex downs far away, and then a measured walk to the station, with many halts and songs and long looks at the scene and the sunset, gave us a very happy day. But the brightest and most successful was our third trip,&mdash; to Richmond and Hampton Court ; our conversazione-conductor changed his b&acirc;ton into the stroke oar of a sh&aacute;llop, and in that and five other boats, thirty-four of us started from Richmond, and paddled up, while the sitters sang, to a &lsquo;tight little island &rsquo; between Kingston and Hampton, belonging to an ex-lord-chancellor, where we landed, and spread our cushions and coats under the trees. Boats went ashore for beer and lemonade, and an enormous pie was produced from the conductor&rsquo;s hamper, &mdash;but was followed, alas! by a moan of dismay, &ldquo;Good! heavens ! you&rsquo;ve forgotten the knives.&rdquo; However, our Sydenham hostess had not forgotten the knives with her pies ; and so a merry subscription dinner followed. Then came songs and chat, a pull to Hampton Court, an hour&rsquo;s look at the best of the pictures and the cartoons, a gathering in the gardens, and then some off to row the boats to Tedding- ton Lock, while the rest strolled to meet them there, through the chestnut and beech avenues of Bushey, and taking by the way a &ldquo; pitch &rdquo; on the fern. The tide soon carried us to Richmond, where we had tea,,and then we were off to the rail or drive home, though a few first walked into the park, and sat in the deep solemn gloom of the trees, talking low, looking out at the distant lights and the sheen of the reach of the river.&mdash;A dance at Mr. Eurnivall&rsquo;s on the 26th, and a picnic to Epping Forest, arc to wind up this August-month.,Most of our teachers have taken wing, one to Canada, others to Switzerland, Scot&not; land, etc. Four of our students paid a visit to Paris for the first time on the recent entry of the army of Italy; and at our Wed&not; nesday &ldquo; Tea &rdquo; of August 17th, gave us an account of all they saw and heard. The cheers for the Emperor they thought cold, but those at the sight of the Austrian standards and cannons, and the wounded, came with a will. Very much pleased with the people and the place our friends were ; and to any one who knows little or no French, two of them recommend Mr. Brown&rsquo;s Albion Hotel, 30, Faubourg St. Honor&eacute;, Cour des Coches ;&mdash;a bed, two francs ; breakfast, ditto, and you are not charged extra when you eat four shillings&rsquo; worth of eggs and bacon, as one of our cricketers assures us he did. The same evening two others of our students who had jnstreturned from a fortnight in Normandy, were comparing notes with some of our last year&rsquo;s party whose steps they had almost followed, and we heard again of the glories of Rouen, Falaise, Mortain, and Mont St. Michel, the barefoot tramp across the last- named&rsquo;s granite sand, the grace and love&not; liness of our English-built cathedral of Coutanccs, and the friendliness and good- humour of the &ldquo; hardy Northman &rdquo; Franks. Walking parties (during bits of our Vaca&not; tion,&mdash;Aug. 27 to Sept. 22) are forming for Malvern and the Wye, for Yorkshire, and the Surrey, Kent, and Sussex downs. Cannot we arrange to meet some of our fellow-collegers of the North next year, if not this ? Many of the specimens have been collected in our geological excursions since, three years ago, Mr. Alfred Tylor first opened our field-work with a lecture to forty of us in the Charlton pits where the tertiary and secondary strata join ;&mdash;and to those who have worked with Mr. Taylor in clay and sand, chalk, ganit, and weald, have jointly tramped in sun and shower, cloud and storm, morning and evening, midnight and early dawn, what living remembrances cling round the dead bones of his gift ! Not only visions of an excited amateur in a chalk-pit, white-painted, triumphant, war-dancing, but the memory of three years&rsquo; bright companionship, three years of steady friendship through all its seeming ebb and flow, three years&rsquo; progress hand in hand towards knowledge of, and love for, our English home as it is now, as it was before man lived ; three years not wholly lost, I trust, three years&rsquo; endeavour to track the working of the finger and mind of God.,F. The first was the return match with an Eleven of the Belmont Club,&mdash;a very close match, which we lost by one wicket. The scores were. College, 46, 35 ; Belmont, 51, 32, with one wicket to go down. As each club has thus won a match on its own ground, a conquering match was talked of, but the lateness of the season will prevent it, many of the College crick&not; eters being out of town for their holidays. Roebuck and San&not; son, were given, their places being then filled up ; fourteen other members of the club were chosen, and an interesting match resulted, in which the sixteen were the victors. Jackson, Sanson, and Roebuck, got off the number scored by the eleven, but, the inn&not; ings being played out, the whole side was disposed of for 44 runs. Messrs.
