OCTOBER 1, 1859.,A STUDENT&rsquo;S WIFE&rsquo;S NOTION OF COLLEGE AND,CLASSES.,When the London &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; was first opened, our hus&not; bands and brothers went off every night to Red Lion Square, and returned to rather late suppers. They seemed very pleased at going ; much more than we did at their being away from home so much. They would come home from work at seven, and by half-past they would have had their tea and started ol&igrave; for the College, to return just in time for supper. On Sun&not; day, instead of going out with us, they would be oif for a walk of thirty or forty miles : off directly after breakfast, to return about midnight. Or if they did stop at home, it would be to invite somebody from the College to come to tea. These people would come, bow to us, say &ldquo; How do you do?&rdquo; when they came, and &ldquo; Good night&rdquo; when they went; all the rest of the time would be spent talking about things we did not understand. If we asked questions, we heard about Algebra, Shakespeare, or Red Sandstone. What these things were we had no idea ; nor did our lords and masters seem to know enough about them to be able to explain them in simple words that we could understand. By asking, we found that they had no&not; thing to do with our husbands&rsquo; daily work ; that they did not earn more money by them, and the only thing they seemed to do for our homes, was, to leave us alone all the evening. This went on for about a twelvemonth, and all that we learned from the conversation of the learned Collegians on Sundays was, that all the teachers of some sort of classes (I think they called them Mathematical) wore double-breasted waistcoats and Albert waţchguards of the same pattern. We women felt, naturally, not quite satisfied with this. We had enough trust in our husbands to feel sure they would not waste their time simply for amusement. They told us that what they learnt had something to do with us and our homes ; that they were learning to be better husbands and fathers. Of course we believed this, but we certainly could not understand how Shakespeare or red sand&not; stone could make our homes more comfortable, though we had an image of one over our fireplace, and a specimen of the other on our mantelpiece. At last, however, it began to be talked of that the College was going to try and explain these things to us poor women, to show us how they did concern us and our homes. I, for one, was very glad to hear this, and hoped to attend the Classes very regularly. Before the time came, however, I had to leave London, and have lived in the country ever since, excepting only about three months. During these three months I attended several of the afternoon classes, and though I learnt but little of history, geo&not; graphy, or drawing, I learned enough to enable me to understand a little the purpose of the College teaching. I was able better to understand how our husbands&rsquo; College learning did concern us and our homes ; how there,N 2
was something in it besides hard talk and long walks ; how it enabled us both not only to understand our positions among our neighbours, but in our own home, and, above all, to each other. We were able a little to see through the darkness of consulting too much our own wishes, and into the light of our duty to ourselves no less than to our families. Henceforth an attempt was made to establish for the ruling principle of our house, not our wishes, but our duties ; and to do, not what we wished, but what we ought. This has been the result of my experience (short but pleasant) of the Afternoon Classes. I regret much that I am unable to continue my attendance, and also that ray very short attendance was not long enough to enable me to make some friends among the members.,In the July and August numbers of the Magazine is an article upon the Women&rsquo;s Classes, I do not think it is written by a woman,* in fact I rather think I can detect the style of one of the very few College friends of my husband and myself; in it the writer says: &ldquo;The business of women in this world is to clean rooms, to cook, to mend clothes, to tend children,&mdash; dreary mechanical work even that last mentioned may become if their minds are not now and then roused out of the thoughts connected with its rou&not; tine, if they are not occasionally allowed to feel that they belong to another world besides the world of pinafores and perambulators.&rdquo; If this is women&rsquo;s business in the world, why teach her geography, history, or sing&not; ing? These things will not help her in scouring or cooking. Will they not be likely to make us wish for some less &ldquo; mechanical routine &rdquo;? Is it not because these things are not the chief business of a woman&rsquo;s life, and because she and others are too apt to think they are, that some teaching is desirable which shall enable her to understand more truly what is her busi&not; ness and position ? Do the names of wife or mother convey only the no&not; tion of a scrubbing, cooking, and nursing animal ? Does the name of house-bandf (husband) mean only a money-producing animal ? Would it be fair to estimate a wife only by her ability to do domestic work ? or to judge a husband by the amount of his weekly wages ? For myself, I feel thankful that the College Classes have made me understand the true nature of a wife&rsquo;s position in her family ; to know that domestic work is all-im&not; portant in itself, but is quite subordinate to the higher duties of a woman in her house ; to feel that rooms are not built merely to be scrubbed, nor children sent into the world merely to be nursed and dressed. I believe the more this is understood the happier will our homes be.,Suffolk, September , 1859 . Elizabeth Eossiter.,* It is though ; and by one of the Teachers of our Women&rsquo;s Classes.&mdash; F. J. F. f A very popular derivation ; but the band of husband has nothing whatever to do with the nouns band and bond and the verb bind in their present sense. The bonde is the tiller of the soil ; the word is the present participle of the old Norse bua , to till ; hus is a late prefix. Etymology, like every other science, suffers from those people who find it gives less trouble, and brings more credit, to turn their own fancies into facts and ex&not; tract morals from them, than patiently to investigate the facts themselves. These im&not; moral moralists, like Mrs. Rossiter&rsquo;s teacher, want cautioning.&mdash; F. J. F.
TWO YEARS&rsquo; STUDENT-LIEE AT A &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;WORKING&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;MEN&rsquo;S&lt;/span&gt;,&lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;COLLEGE&lt;/span&gt;.,In last month&rsquo;s Magazine Mr. Litchfield, my old algebra teacher, speaks of five years&rsquo; experience as a College teacher. Of these five years, the first two were the years of my experience as a student. We began our College career together ; he as teacher, I as student. At the end of these two years my lot was cast away from the College, and since then I have been only connected with it by friendship with many of ray old chums, and by feelings of the deepest gratitude to &ldquo; Our College.&rdquo; Still I think my experience will enable me to say a few words upon each of the subjects mentioned by Mr. Litchfield, the more especially since I am egotist enough to fancy that I am myself indirectly referred to in one paragraph of his paper,&mdash;to me a grateful proof that he has not forgotten his old student, any more than I have forgotten him or any other of my old teachers, whose features are the chief and most honoured ornaments of my little country dwelling.,I think the pith of Mr. Litchfield&rsquo;s paper may be expressed in the fol&not; lowing four propositions :&mdash;,1. Students of a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; should enter fully into the social life of the College, and regard their fellow-members and teachers as fellow-labourers, each and all ready to give and take the hand of friend&not; ship, both in and out of their class-rooms.,2. Students must work at the subject of their classes out of College as well as in it.,3. Students should not commence more subjects than they have time to work at thoroughly.,4. The mere number of those who successfully complete any given study, does not measure the success of the teaching of that class.,1. With respect to the social life of the College. This has been much more fully developed since my College days. In my time there were no boxing or cricket clubs, no conversaziones, no museum, and no Wednes&not; day social meetings or picnics. A Sunday walk, and the meeting each other at the College, was the sum of the general social life of the College. Without any of them, I made acquaintance with most of the students. Ь I found them all quite ready to believe, with me, that a fellow-colleger was more than a fellow-student. To this feeling I owe many a pleasant break&not; fast-party, many a pleasant country walk, and many and many a happy evening after both daily and College work were done. Thus not only the College, but the communion of the students was &ldquo; a happy i-efime from the ceaseless hum and toil of the market, the office, or the shop.&rdquo;* 0 I be&not; lieve that I, more than most of the students of my time, have cause of gratitude for the friends the College gave me. Of my cotemporaries, most were my acquaintances, but a few have become my dear friends, whose friendship I hope to retain through life, though I can feel the grasp of their hands but once a year. The number may not be so large as &Icirc; mudit wish or believed it to be, but I feel that among those whom 1 should never,* See Mr. Langley&rsquo;s article in the September number.
have seen but for the College, I can count some few very, very dear friends. I feel that it is to this warmth of feeling exhibited by my teachers and fel&not; low-students, that I must, in part, attribute the benefit I have derived from the College. Without this sympathy from teachers and fellow-mem&not; bers I should simply have learnt a few dry mathematical rules, and a few, still more dry, of language and grammar. By means of this social feeling, the College taught me to consider its teachings as not mere definitions and rules, but as a means of enabling me to live my life better than I could do without this teaching. In a word, it taught me duty and responsibility. In the words of the most talented of my coteraporaries, now a member of the Council, &ldquo; The College taught me that there were duties to be done in the world ; and not only this, but that I myself had some of these duties to perform ; that we were not merely to vegetate, but to find out our du&not; ties, and do them.&rdquo; This great lesson can scarcely be fully learnt from a mere attendance at classes ; but it is, I believe, the chief teaching of the College, and is taught through the social education of its members. A few weeks ago I was in London, and was claimed as a friend by one whom I liad never seen, because a friend of his was a member of the College, and had heard of me there. This I felt as a full realization of the truth, that the bond of College membership is one not lightly to be broken ; that we are members of one body, both in &ldquo;the College&rdquo; and the larger college of the world ; bound to regard all as fellows, to help each other to fulfil the duties the small college teaches us we have to perform in the larger ; to feel that we are bound together in bonds of goodwill, charity, and for&not; giveness.,2. To do any good in our classes, we must work out of them. To the truth of this, in my own case I can bear witness. I never did any good at any lesson unless I had previously spent some time at it away from the College. I always made friends with the members of any class I was in, and with one or two of the fellows I always had a meeting during the week, and spent the evening over the subject to be got up for the next class night. Many a happy hour, and many a cup of tea and &ldquo; fixings &rdquo; have been spent over an obstinate quadratic or a hard rule of grammar, and many a hearty laugh have we had over the &ldquo; confounded thing.&rdquo; We have sometimes spent six or eight hours over one lesson, rather than go back to class like lazy schoolboys without our work done as it should be.,I know one instance of a student carrying his algebra book to work with him, and devoting every spare moment to it ; and of another who, after spending an evening over an obstinate binomial in vain, woke up in the night with a fresh idea, and there and then did it triumphantly. I would therefore, in the most emphatic way, say to my fellow-students, for such I hope I may call them, though I am many a weary mile away, &ldquo;Work hard out of class, or you will never make much progress. You cannot sit and have a thing pumped into you; you can only learn by hard work. Make friends with the fellows in your class, and work with them. Go to their houses, and ask them to yours. You will know them better than even at a practice class. You will not only know them, but their families, and I am sure you will afterwards rejoice.&rdquo;,3. In this I agree with Mr. Litchfield, that a student should neither attempt too many subjects at once, nor confine himself entirely to one. I
know from experience that attendance at too many classes prevents due attention to any of them ; while, on the other hand, ahnost any study will help, more or less, any other one. In one term I attended a separate class every night in the week, but I could never attend to all ; so that in two or three of the classes I passed as an inattentive fellow, who took no interest in my work. And yet this was after all the term in which I did most real work. Knowing that I had much to do, I worked hard ; and though I could not do all I had to do, I did more than if I had had less. I would recommend every one to consider what time they can fairly give to College work, and to take just as many classes as they can fairly attend to consis&not; tently with a determination never to come to a class without having spent some time at work since the preceding lesson.,4. Every student derives some benefit from every lesson, though he may not continue the study. In my first year about thirty students commenced algebra with Mr. Litchfield. At the end of the year an examination was held. Of the thirty, only three professed to have finished the course, and of these three only one obtained a certificate. Yet nothing would be more fallacious than to suppose that only this one got any good from his work. I know some of the others personally, and though (with one distinguished exception) they cannot solve a stiff quadratic, or prove the binomial theo&not; rem, I know the mental work they had done has been of immense service to them. The mind can hardly be exercised without being strengthened, apart from any actual knowledge that may be acquired.,Of my own experience I can speak a little as to the good to be acquired from only a partial attention to a study. Among my &ldquo;incomplete works&rdquo; may be counted an attendance of eight nights at a botany class. This is all I ever learned of botany till I came to live in the country. Yet this was my intellectual capital when I commenced, on the minutest of scales, to be a farmer and horticulturist. The little that I got from those eight nights&rsquo; attendance has been of incalculable use to me in my quarter of an acre of vegetables and in my flower-garden.,In the same way I attended a few nights to a geology class. I learnt, of course, next to nothing of the science, yet enough to give direction and reverence to what would otherwise have been simply stupid wonderment when standing on the granite of the Land&rsquo;s End, or when chipping serpen&not; tine at the Lizard (I trust our worthy Secretary and others recollect those days as vividly as I do).,I once began Latin, but never did much at it ; yet what little I did learn has been of immense value to me. And, still more than the mere learning, I owe to this little bit of Latin one very dear friend. He, like myself, began Latin, and, like myself, cannot read a dozen consecutive words. Probably we should never have met, to know each other, but at this class. Yet to this meeting we both owe very much of pleasure. We worked together then at Latin as we have done since at many a harder task, and to my Latin chum now I can say, as Tennyson says to him whom some of us love with so deep a love,,&ldquo; Come, when no graver cares employ,,Godfather, come and see your hoy.&rdquo;,To this little bit of unsuccessful Latin I owe the knowledge that there is at least one happy family in the great Babel, to whose fireside I have
always a truly hearty welcome. Is this a little? Was my Latin quite a failure ?,Mr. Litchfield says that teachers can best judge of the benefit derived by the unsuccessful students. I am not so sure of this. We unsuccessful students have derived many benefits from our unsuccessful studies that our teachers know not of. Many other subjects I began, but did little or no&not; thing at. And in counting the success of the class, I among others would be considered as having derived no benefit from my attendance. Far other&not; wise think I, who know how very much I have since been indebted to every lesson or lecture I have ever attended at the College.,In conclusion, I would say to my fellow-students, &ldquo;Regard every teacher and fellow-student as a brother ;&rdquo; and especially to the old students I would say, &ldquo; Welcome every new brother.&rdquo; A new member lately remarked, &ldquo;Every one nods to me, but Mr. Tansley shook hands with me, and I believed in him at once.&rdquo; The members ought not to be mere acquaintances of the moment, they ought to feel bound together by a closer tie. But while doing this, while mixing to the utmost with both teachers and students, do not neg&not; lect actual class work. The social teaching is the most valuable, when built upon a good foundation of actual intellectual class work, but by itself it will be of comparatively little use. To my knowledge the teachers work hard out of College at College work, and it would be a poor return to them and little use to you to accept their trouble in teaching, and their society out of class, if you do not, on your part, work hard at the actual work of the class. Work by itself will be of more benefit than play by itself : the two combined will be perfect college teaching.,William Rossiter.,Suffolk, September , 1859 .,HISTORY OF THE HALIFAX &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;WORKING&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;MEN&rsquo;S&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;COLLEGE&lt;/span&gt;. (communicated by the senior tutor.),The education of the half-timer in the manufacturing districts ordinarily terminates at thirteen years of age. Seeing the insufficiency of the instruc&not; tion imparted under such circumstances, a benevolent gentleman at Hali&not; fax determined to tiy the experiment of founding a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, dignifying the advanced class of evening school with such a title, not from any vain desire to imitate the collegeskor excellence of the country, but from seeing that the repugnance to go &ldquo; to school &rdquo; manifested by an adolescent or adult often springs from the name ; and also from the higher motive, that by taking an active part and interest in the working of the Institution, he might assist to break down those gigantic barriers fast rising between the employer and employed, which threaten, unless some effectual stop be interposed, to sap the very foundations of the character we formerly pos&not; sessed of being a strong, because a united people.,The &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; for &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&lt;/span&gt; at Halifax was commenced in the spring of 1856 , with a staff of three paid tutors, and a committee of practical working men, who might give countenance by their presence, and assist-
ance in the course of study, to the young men. One fundamental point in the arrangement was the separation into two divisions, a senior and a junior. Into the former, young men of eighteen and upwards are admitted ; those from thirteen to eighteen being in the other,,A Young Women&rsquo;s Institute, on similar principles, was begun at the same time, and both are fairly fulfilling their respective work ; the object of the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; being to educate the many, and to assist the few to raise their position by dint of harder application ; that of the Young Women&rsquo;s Institute, to fit the females to discharge the important duties of after-life in a more intelligent way than they could without such help.,Medals for good conduct and regularity of attendance ; Certificates for fair proficiency in the subjects chosen by the young men, and Prizes for excellence in particular branches of knowledge, are the incentives to exer&not; tion. The medals, certificates, and prizes are, besides, no small recom&not; mendation in case of trying for appointments. Again, from the College being in union with the Society of Arts, many of the young men have at&not; tended the examinations of that Institution for three years past, and the cei tifica tes are looked upon, and properly too, as a great inducement to exertion in learning. Many are the sacrifices of pleasure, sometimes too of present profit, made by the workers in the classes to gain these rewards. Some will cynically point to this as a low motive to exertion. Let such honestly examine themselves and inquire whether the gratification of their love of learning is the only end they have in view in exercising such self- denial as may seem necessary for the attainment of their object. It is a refreshing sight to enter a room where one of the classes of this College is at work, and to see the earnestness with which the students toil,&deg; the chagrin manifested when other though unavoidable engagements have de&not; tained them from the commencement of the lesson, or compel them to leave before its termination.,It is tiue that the masses are not yet affected by this movement, but we must bide our time. Will these young men, although forming &rsquo;perhaps only one-twentieth part of those who should be under instruction, when they become the heads of families, regret the time thus spent, as lost, or the knowledge acquired as dross ; and, more important still, will they not&mdash; seeing the good they have gained from the mental and moral discipline they have undergone influence all over whom they have power, so as to cause the numbers of alumni to increase ? Literary and Mechanics&rsquo; Insti&not; tutes very well in their way although fulfilling their purpose, do not educate &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;men&lt;/span&gt;. A &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; cannot exist except as a place for mental discipline. 1,As stated above, the College is, in all its departments, fairly fulfilling its appointed work. There is an examination before Easter in each year conducted by gentlemen from a distance, who report the results and upon whose reports the award of certificates and prizes is based At the Society of Arts Examinations, the following are the certificates that have been gamed during the three years past.,In 1857,5 Third Class in Arithmetic. 1 Second Class in Algebra,2 Third Class in Algebra. 1 Third Class in Geometry
In 1858 :&mdash;,2 Third Class in English Literature.,3 Third Class in English History.,1 Second Class in Geography.,6 Third Class in Arithmetic.,In 1859,4 Third Class in Eng. Literature.,1 Third Class in Arithmetic.,2 Second Class in Arithmetic.,1 First Class in Arithmetic.,1 Second Class in Arithmetic. 1 First Class in Book-keeping. 3 Third Class in Algebra.,2 Third Class in Algebra.,1 First Class in Book-keeping.,3 Second Class in Chemistry.,1 First Class in Chemistry.,It will be seen that, in contradistinction to many institutions, which send up a few (often only private students allowed to compete in the name of members of a Mechanics&rsquo; Institute), the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, Halifax, sends up a considerable number.,Lectures from strangers, as a rule, are considered too desultory a mode of learning to be much encouraged. Occasionally one is delivered as a sort of stimulus and rallying-point for the members of the various classes ; but the young men are taught to look much more to steady, systematic, in&not; dividual exertion, as the surest means of attaining knowledge.,A well-selected library of above 4,000 volumes, containing readable as well as didactic works, is also attached to the Institution. Its valuable contents are frequently applied to by the students. A news-room also affords reasonable means of gaining acquaintance with the passing events of the day.&#39; Nor was the Founder unmindful of the necessity, after a hard day&rsquo;s work, in some constrained position perhaps, of some place where physical development might be attended to occasionally, rather than the culture of the intellect. A gymnasium, well supplied with apparatus for exercising the muscles, etc., cricketing requisites, bowls, quoits, etc., was therefore sup&not; plied. This, especially during the summer months, is very fairly attended. In connection with it are prizes for batting, bowling, quoiting, etc., as are also rewards for the b^st entomological and botanical specimens collected by the students themselves&mdash;the botanical specimens being restricted to ferns and grasses. The botanical class, self-taught, and whose members gain both instruction and health, chiefly by making incursions into the neighbouring heaths and woods, is becoming more attractive every year. The young women, too, have prizes awarded for proficiency in homely dressmaking and millinery, and are taught the art of simple cookery. Nor are these last by any means unimportant matters. Perhaps more of,the dissoluteness and recklessness of living among husbands in the working class is produced by want of good management in their wives than by any other cause. Hence, what can be more important than to teach young women neat and thrifty modes of turning everything to the best account, whether it be the print or stuff for their own dresses, or the small joint for the family dinner ? No one who is not practically acquainted with the matter, would credit the necessity for instruction in such matters that exists among the young women employed in mills.,This reminds one of another important feature in this Institution, viz. the means employed for affording that due intercourse between the mem&not; bers of the two great, branches&mdash;the Working Man&rsquo;s College and the Young Women&rsquo;s Institute&mdash;which ought to be considered an essential
feature in such a work as is here undertaken. Conversaziones , occasional lectures, soirees, and the award of prizes gained at local flower-shows, are severally taken advantage of to foster that brotherly and sisterly sympathy which must in the end be looked to as the main instrument for human&not; izing and softening the naturally rugged and&mdash;from the desire to improve their minds&mdash;the selfish manners of the young men, and elevating and rendering more stable the social condition of the women. Nothing is more hurtful to the well-being of this large section of the community than those half-clandestine meetings between them which have only the sanction of their companions, in which no genial sentiments or improving ideas are interchanged, but which are usually spent in profitless conversation, being indeed the only means left for the enjoyment of that divinely-implanted instinct for communion for which man craved even in Paradise, and to encourage which God made a helpmeet for him. Class-teaching has not been tried ; it might do under certain conditions. As to the intellectual part of the question,&mdash;one slight difficulty overcome, viz. the repugnance on the part of those not well informed in a subject to be listened to by those of the other sex,&mdash;the benefit to both would be undeniably very great. The young men naturally wish to keep ahead of those who conventionally have been esteemed intellectually inferior ; but the quickness of women in matters purely mnemonica! and mental gives them an advantage ; and these two causes combined would stimulate both to put forth all their energies, but whether the moral and disciplinary effects would be good or bad is a question remaining to be answered. The elder portion of the students are thoughtful and eminently careful in their behaviour; but much could not be looked for from those whose ages range from thirteen to eighteen. In public assemblies at Haley Hill, as soirees, etc., both lads and lasses of that age are found to be somewhat boisterous ; but they are fast improving in propriety of behaviour, and this good result is here looked upon as the natural fruits of these reunions.,On these occasions the Founder is ever the foremost to promote not only the festal and intellectual, but also the amusing part of the pro&not; gramme. A speech which makes all feel of one kith, is perhaps a greater means than even a well-supplied table, of setting at ease, and of breaking down those artificial distinctions which, although they must exist, need not necessarily be always prominently brought forward. Nor is this done in a patronizing, condescending manner; but so that the poorest feels that an active benevolence prompts the homely phrases employed. The slight attempts at familiarity displayed by those thus benefited never seem to distress his kindly heart, nor to cause any abatement of the bonhomie which endears so much.,One word in conclusion, Mr. Akroyd, the founder of the Halifax College very wisely judged that voluntaryism was not the mode by which teachers were to be secured (at least as far as this district is concerned); therefore, while inviting the voluntary assistance of those who could and would afford the time and patience, he maintained the uninterrupted working of the scheme by paying his teachers. No man, however well intentioned, can be ex&not; pected, night after night, to leave his comfortable fireside, to teach in a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, or any other place, after perhaps a hard day&rsquo;s work. He who looks for such, under ordinary circumstances, looks for simple im-
possibilities. Mark, mider ordinary circumstances. Men may be, and thank God are, found, who devote themselves &iquest;n this way to his glory ; but the exceptional nature of the case proves the truth of the above assertion.,HINTS ON &Lambda; FEW PRINCIPLES OF INSTRUCTION OF WORKING WOMEN, SUGGESTED BY A TEACHER.,There is much education of working people which has for its object the communication of that simple knowledge which is needful in the every-day business of life. Too often, if anything more than this is undertaken, it is simply with the notion that further knowledge is a mark of social rank. In the opinion of some, the object first mentioned is inadequate ; in that of others, the motive of the latter is reprehensible ; but there is a third result of study to which it were desirable that the attention of teachers should be far more directed than is usually the case ; namely, that important part of their work which consists in opening out to their pupils fresh sources of joy, both by revealing facts new and wonderful to them, as in natural history and geography ; by calling out new powers, as in arithmetic ; and by cultivating such feelings as the delight in music and colour. To those who feel it right to awaken such power of delight in every human being, and who feel that such studies would form a ground of sympathy amongst all people, the following suggestions are addressed.,Singing, it is true, is.already recognized as an important part of all plans of instruction for working people ; it always is an intense pleasure to them. That this enjoyment should be far more widely spread, would it not be well that singina; classes should constitute themselves into choirs to give concerts in wretched neighbourhoods? Teachers and pupils would find this joint work good and delightful. Concerts given to the working- classes by ladies and gentlemen have been productive of much pleasure, but it is evident that this exercise of their power would give fresh zest to all pupils ; and clergymen would probably be glad to throw open their schoolrooms, that their poorest parishioners might enjoy such a treat.,Drawing affords a marked instance of the two motives of study above alluded to. It is taught as a means of livelihood, or as a mere accomplish&not; ment, but, as Mr. Ruskin has pointed out, it is a valuable means of leading people to notice, and consequently to delight in, truth and beauty. The serious study of drawing requires a very able master, and if drawing from casts is attempted, a room must be set apart where they can remain undis&not; turbed ; also such arrangements must be made as will secure the same light at several successive lessons.,In cases where these conditions are unattainable, something may yet be done to bring out the particular value of drawing under consideration. If, for instance, the teacher has a slight knowledge and a great love of truth, she will find herself able to interest her pupils much by procuring for them leaves, sprays, and flowers, which they can draw in pen-and-ink outline at one lesson. The name of the flower and date of the drawing should be written, and where possible, all facts about the plant related,&mdash;its character and habits, any description of places with which it is connected in the
teacher&rsquo;s mind ; and if any quotation about it can be found and written by the pupil at home, the plant will have for her henceforward an individuality and preciousness not easily forgotten. All this the teacher will do natu&not; rally, if she really knows and loves the plant, and will show how every loss of truth is also a loss of beauty, every false line misrepresents the plant,&mdash;the spring of the stem, the sharpness of the thorn, and the droop of the flower being each essential to its character. All this is simply suggested as a preparation for the harder work of more complete rendering of form (as in the shaded cast), when the pupil is interested in such instruction, and the teacher able to undertake it.,Heading and AVriting classes can be worked hard at once, taught thoroughly and quickly ; the women who come to learn will be found quite impressed with a sense of the importance of both, willing (if they have enough hope of success) to work with thought and energy at the dry de&not; tails of both. But when they have mastered these two keys of knowledge, they surely should be used to unlock the great and noble stores of&mdash;,English Literature.&mdash; It is easier to touch and to excite than to calm. There is much danger in over-excitement, but the danger is not averted by leaving people in the apathy of ignorant dullness ; this breaks out into the wildest and lowest kinds of excitement ; the danger is not averted by leaving people to brood over themselves, and what is immediately around them ; excitement is often sought to dispel a sense of the gloominess of these. The danger is only thoroughly overcome by quiet trust in God, and by self-forgetting sympathy with men. In order that these may be promoted, should not all having the power of teaching seek to awaken in their pupils right feeling about human sorrow and joy, loving study of God&rsquo;s works, extended knowledge of what has been? And would not the study of even one great author do more to cultivate these than thousands of the miserably dull books that we substitute for the more miserable non&not; sense of much fiction ? Not that all fiction is unhealthy reading ; and the uncompromising and unqualified disapprobation of it has done much harm. AYe want truth. Precisely because we do, it is better to have a book where the author presents his imaginary people as living than one in which he presents his real people as hopelessly dead, or only useful to illus- tiate a tute maxim. Eiction is injurious when it is read from a mere excitement about incident. The feeling produced by such reading was well expressed by a woman, who said that &ldquo; she had read eveiy book in,-library, and now she was tired of reading she did n&rsquo;t know what to,do and by another, who said &ldquo; she had got all the good she could out of reading.&rdquo; This exhaustion is never felt by those who have been led to find in a work of fiction something besides incident. AYitness the eager&not; ness and delight with which all facts connected with a story are seized by those who have not dulled their powers by a restless pursuit of excite&not; ment, an eagerness never called up by books which give facts unconnected with human action. AYithout such living presentation of fact as is to be found only in great authors, and pre-eminently perhaps in works of fiction, it is utterly hopeless to expect that some kind of knowledge should either impress itself on the memory, or exert much influence on the life. AYhat class reading the description of the storming of Front-de-B&oelig;uf&rsquo;s castle in &lsquo; Ivanhoe,&rsquo; when their interest had been intenselv fixed on the drama there
enacted, but would wish to understand the plan of a Norman castle, and would retain a clear idea concerning it ?,A quieter interest, but one as deep, is awakened in every rightly-toned mind by books of science, and the balancing study of these prevents the mind from degenerating into an exclusive interest in human action as set forth in works of fiction. 1 have never yet had an opportunity of studying with a class the great prose works of English literature, my experience being only of such extracts as could be repeated at work or during walks in the fields. I cannot but think, however, that careful reading of our great prose writers with a class would be well repaid. A knowledge of poems has been very precious to some few of those whose lives are for the most part passed in hard work and much thought about tangible things. Poems have spoken to them as spiritual beings ; they have presented to them beautiful scenes, they have realized to them the lives of others. A friend, who has had great experience in education, quite agrees with me in thinking that it would be well to choose poetry for a reading class, when it is tolerably advanced. Not only inaccuracies both of hesitation and accent are then most readily noticed, not only opportunities occur of explaining words not frequently met with, but in poetry perhaps is best cultivated that loving carefulness of thought, without which all reading is useless, and that tender observation of detail which is so essential to the understanding of all great authors, and which (as in drawing from nature) fills us with awe and admiration of the perfection and harmony of the minutest parts, sure in their inexhaustible beauty to inspire us with an ever-deepening and ever-extending interest and love.,Space does not admit here of enlarging on a point of primary impor&not; tance, namely, that as in every class some members will usually be found both competent and desirous to communicate to others the knowledge they have received, opportunity for their doing so should, if possible, be afforded. This feeling constitutes a strong stimulus to perseverance, and there can be no purer dedication of power. The readers of &lsquo; Tom Brown&rsquo; will remember the influence on his life of having Arthur put under his charge. Institutions where classes are carried on might endeavour to find where such help as their pupils can render would be valuable. If possible, this&#39; should be in their immediate neighbourhood; for it is to be ob&not; served, that there is a tendency to relax study in the sense of usefulness, and new-found delight in teaching. This should be guarded against, and those who have commenced teaching should be strenuously exhorted to continue learning, O. H.,CORRESPONDENCE.,PRIZES OR NO PRIZES.,Dear Mr. Editor,&mdash; The question of Prizes has been mooted in our Council. Some of our Council are anxious to give prizes to our students, some have objections, while all of us wish to see the question more fully discussed before taking any practical step. The London College does not give prizes,&mdash;the Manchester and Halifax Colleges, I think, do give them. Will you, or some member of your London Council, give us your
reasons for not giving them, and some Manchester friend give us their reasons for coming to a contrary resolution, and also state the principles on which they award them, and the effect among the students, so far as it comes under their knowledge ? Still better would it be if, in addition, some of the students would give us their opinions, and the results of their experience, on either side of the question, in the next number of your Magazine.&mdash;I am, dear Mr. Editor, yours most heartily,,J. N. Langley.,Wolverhampton , 15 th September , 1859.,NEWS.,Salford. &mdash;The College is going on very well indeed in all its departments. We have had several excursions this summer, but not on a large scale. This Circular has been issued by the Treasurer and the Honorary Secretaries of the College and the Mechanics&rsquo; Institution;&mdash;,&ldquo;We are desired by the Board of Directors of the Salford Mechanics&rsquo; Institution and the Council of the Salford &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, to solicit the favour of your obli&not; ging them with a subscription towards the fund for enabling them to pay off the mort&not; gage debt and liabilities on the Institution, amounting to &pound;650. If this effort to liqui&not; date the debts on the Mechanics&rsquo; Institution be successful, it has been agreed to amalgamate the two Institutions, and to continue all the classes under the name of the &lsquo; Salford &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;.&rsquo; We are happy to be able to inform you that promises of subscriptions have already been received to the amount of &pound;502 (up to Sept. 9), on condition that the whole debt is now liquidated. The building now occu&not; pied by the two Institutions was erected in 1858, on freehold land presented by E. R. Langworthy, Esq. The value of the land, buildings, furniture, and fittings, is upwards of &pound;3,000, If the liquidation of the present liabilities can now be accomplished, Salford will possess an educational Institution, entirely free from debt or permanent charge of any kind, capable of providing to a considerable extent for the educational wants of the borough : and there is the best reason to believe that all the expenses of its manage&not; ment will be covered by the annual, quarterly, and monthly subscriptions of the mem&not; bers, and that it will be entirely self-supporting. Reports in next Magazine. *,London.&mdash; To our Epping Forest Picnic we had a party of twenty-nine, increased by two subsequent arrivals, to thirty-five. The train took us to Loughton ; thence we strolled through the Forest to Highbeach, and wandered about the glades in search of a place to pitch. A cry from one of our outlookers made us all troop up to his tree, and there we stood on the short-turfed edge of the sand-hill, looking down a ferny combe and over the feathered tops of the trees on to the rich green meadows beyond, and to where in blue haze, the high clay-ridge met the clouds. This was clearly the spot ; so we &rsquo;cut heaps of fern for the ladies&rsquo; seats, spread the cloths at their feet, and Mr. Fotheringham&rsquo;s
delicious pies were soon consumed, though a spoon, instead of a knife, would have ladled out the plum-juice quicker. Songs and chat followed, as we looked at the view before us, and then a stroll to our tea-place, a wash, and a walk through the forest-glades, past fern- beds (red-brown in the sunlight) to a little sheltered nook, where we stopped, and lay looking up through the lovely leaves and the delicate tracery of their branches to the blue sky above, while our singers gave us tender-toned songs ; and all was peaceful and happy. But up to the hill again for a dance and a ride, and down to the vale for a merry tea on the green ; then a lie-dowm for a glorious sunset and a chat over future plans, with a qidet walk hack in the gloaming, till the singers coaxed out the stars. For accounts of the next trips&mdash;thirty-six to Leith Hill, the finest point south of the Thames, and the scramble up its long back ; twenty-six to Richmond, and their row and stroll in the park&mdash; we have no room, but must report that the General Meeting , preceded by its usual Tea, was held on September 22ud, Mr. Furnivall in the chair. The speakers were the Chairman, Messrs. Taylor, W. Sutton, Hurst, Litchfield (who spoke on his new Political Economy Class), Hughes (who promised to resume his Sparring Class and Wednesday Teas at once), Tansley, Hales, Leggatt, Townsend, Wright, and Roebuck (who read some most eloquent passages from Mr. Tansley&rsquo;s letters describing his late Wye tour). On Friday, the 23rd, we had Mr. Litchfield&rsquo;s account of his month in Switzerland, Turin, Milan, Padua, and Venice. To it he, like Mr. Ruskin on previous occasions, admitted ladies, and, com&not; plimenting them on the delightful contrast they presented, in face, to their Continental sisters, in voice to their American ones, he chatted us on, brightly and easily,&middot;&mdash;reading at times grand bursts from Ruskin, &mdash;through glories of blue lake and dazzling snow- peak, all ablaze iu the sunset, through foul villages, over glaciers, up the St. Bernard Pass to its inn and its deadhouse, and so to Italy&rsquo;s plains ; Piedmont and Lombardy, wdth their life and hopefulness; Milan&rsquo;s marble minster and the faint memories of Da Vinci&rsquo;s hand; through Verona and Padua to Venice, whose ancient glories he sketched, but made us sad with her desolation now. Not for ever though are the Austrians to be there. Freedom is to come from Victor Emmanuel and Garibaldi and from the awakening spirit of the nation ; and the Bride of the Sea shall live her life again,&mdash;not in selfish indulgence, but in noble doing of duty. The saying and the sayer w ľ e cheered, and went off to our merry tea, well pleased with our evening&rsquo;s trip. F. J. THE,ffiW&uuml;ng Piens Coli ege aga mie.,No. XI. NOVEMBER 1, 1859.,TO OUR SUBSCRIBERS.,The Magazine does not pay its expenses. This, I suppose, will not emprise many of our Subscribers. No one conversant with the nature of periodicals devoted to a specialty could expect that a publication like this could be produced otherwise than at a loss. I state the fact now, that as our first year draws to a close, the friends of the various Colleges may con&not; sider on what footing the Magazine is to be carried on, if it is to exist any longer. We should be glad to hear from the governing Bodies of the country Colleges, if they are willing to contribute to a Guarantee Fund. We have made up our minds in London that a continuous record of our College life does help to make that life more vigorous, and should be sorry to give up the chief effective means of establishing a connection with other Colleges. So long as the Magazine reflects, as it ought to do, the daily work of the various Colleges, it cannot be made more entertaining than that work itself is. That we are far more stupid than w r e need be, I have no doubt ; but an increased circulation, which anybody can promote with a very little trouble, would probably mend this defect as surely as it would rectify our balance-sheet. The College Finance Committee will consider at its next meeting whether the expenses of publication can be reduced.,The Editor.,OUR LEISURE HOURS.,- If we can teach ourselves or our students rightly to estimate the value of our leisure hours, I think our &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; will have done no little good, albeit it will be done in a quiet, silent way, not at all in the fashion of those &ldquo;grand universal regenerators,&rdquo; which Mr. Litchfield very plainly put down as &ldquo; quacks &rdquo; in the last number of the Magazine. The fact that we have generally so much more freedom in the employment of our leisure hours than in those devoted to our daily work, gives to them a value for the formation and development of character which cannot be estimated too highly. The lawyer must go to his chambers, the tradesman to his shop, the mechanic to his factory, but, the hours of business over, each must sink shall we not rather say, rise ,&mdash;to his simple state of man&not; hood ; each may turn to the pleasure or occupation which suits him best. In the vast majority of cases, the day&rsquo;s work is done simply because it must be done, without much thought, or heart, or enthusiasm. A certain tiade is adopted rather fiom accident than from choice, and day by day we go on grinding at it, neither liking it nor disliking it, but simply accepting it as a stern necessity which we cannot evade. I do not say this should be so, or that it is so in every case. Far from it. It is the glory of England
that so many of her sons,&mdash;especially among the professions, where the money returns are often very inadequate,&mdash;are silently and willingly devoting their best and noblest powers to their daily calling, with an enthusiasm which no money can repay, impelled by a pure love of their work, and by the reverent belief that it is not theirs only, but that He who had called them to it, will reward and bless them in it. But generally the fact is as I have stated, and so it will continue to be, certainly for some time to come, de&not; spite all we may do and hope to the contrary. But still a vast field lies open to us. While our daily work is what it is, it can only be viewed as that which procures us the means of living, but the real life comes out in the fair play of our leisure hours. Here the true character, the stamp on the man, is manifested. Whatever the man does, he puts his heart into it, when the pressure of stern, unloving compulsion, the dull monotony of the day&rsquo;s work, is removed. It is one of the boasts of the present day, and, in the main, I believe it to be a just one, that the amusements of the people are far more varied and far healthier than they were eighty or a hundred years ago. But when one thinks of the vast amount of evil still left un&not; touched, of the vast amount of good still undone, we cannot help feeling that the time for boasting has not arrived yet. Now I cannot help hoping that our Colleges are doing something, however little and unobtrusive it may be, to aid in this work of teaching men to use their leisure hours more wisely and profitably. I do not mean teaching working men only, as though they were the only men who wanted teaching : we all want it ; and I do not think we can obtain it in a more effective way than in the hearty fellowship of work and feeling which is promoted by our classes. How much valuable leisure is frittered away simply from a want of purpose ! We do not know what to do, so we go on doing nothing at all, and the time slips by, leaving us weaker and feebler in character and determination. Without pretending to sermonize on the value of time, and the importance of improving each moment, yet I do think it is a desirable thing for a man to have something like a purpose before him in his hours of free relaxation. Let it be a good hearty romp with his children, or a game at cricket or foot-ball, or attendance at his College class,&mdash;but let there be something to do, and let it be done. This we do supply for one or two hours a week at all events, and I do not think any man, whether as teacher or student, can regularly attend his College class from week to week, and, as in some cases, we can say, from year to year, without deriving a benefit quite inde&not; pendent of the special subject of study carried on by the class. At a given hour on a given evening, I have to be at that College, with my class ; it is a necessity, not the unloving necessity of compulsion, but the hearty ne&not; cessity of a self-imposed duty&mdash;the more binding just in proportion as it expresses the action ot my will and affections. I have to be there to cany out a fixed aim, to prosecute a special study. I know what I have to do, and I try to do it. This regular, systematic prosecution of a study is, as every one&rsquo;s experience will amply testify, a widely different thing from at&not; tending lectures&mdash;a habit which may spring from sheer idleness, and want of purpose, and may in turn help to produce them. I think, then, that those who will make up their minds to attend a class regularly for, say, one year, will find that th^y are acquiring a habit which is invaluable for the true formation and development of character, in addition to the benefit
derived from the study they are pursuing. But why come to a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; to learn this ? Surely, there is fixedness of work enough in one&rsquo;s daily occupation to supply any number of men with &ldquo; a fixed pur&not; pose &rdquo; for their whole lives, without shutting oneself up in a class-room to repeat prosy lessons, or stumble over dry books ? Yes ! but there is one vast difference between the work of the shop or factory, and that of the College. The one is fixed for you, the other by you. The one must be done, under peril, more or less immediate, of starvation ; the other is done because you love it, because you find the doing of it makes you a better man, more genial, more tolerant, more fitted to help others. The one is the inevitable destiny of your position ; the other the willing, hearty conse&not; cration of your leisure hours : and where is the man who does not know that the lesson which is brought home to him by compulsion is too often learnt only to be forgotten, while that which is suggested to him in the exercise of his own free will, is treasured up for daily practice and blessing ?,Another test which may be applied with safety to the employment of our leisure is, the influence exerted on home life. We cannot be very well or wisely occupied, if we are not taught to value home, and home- duties, and home-comforts, more highly. But is not this just furnishing an objector with the very argument he wants to urge against us ? There are temptations already far too numerous to draw men away from their homes, that you should add another to the list under the specious pretence of doing good. True ! we do ask a man to come away from home to our Colleges once or twice a week, but it is that we may send him back to his home, not only with something to do there, but something which will interest him in the doing thereof. Because he has been with us one even&not; ing, he must remain at home for the rest of the week to finish the work commenced with us, or to prepare for the next meeting. Home-life is more valued, because it has received a new interest, it has been invested with the charm of a new duty. And I cannot doubt that to the man thus occupied quietly at home, the lisp and prattle of children, or the chat of &ldquo; loving spouse,&rdquo; will form no interruption, but only cause his eye to glisten with a brighter glow, and his heart to beat with a keener, richer throb. I hope I am not drawing a picture purely from imagination. I believe many of our men could more than confirm all I have said. In another way, too, I believe our Colleges are blessing us in our leisure hours, by the true and happy friendships to which they give rise. The very name we bear pledges us, as we have often been well reminded, to the cultiva&not; tion of this social bond of fellowship, as the distinctive element of our work. Mere teaching and learning by no means express our purpose. These are not so much the end at which we aim, as the means to the end! We believe, rightly or wrongly, that to fall into the condition of mere in&not; dividuals, with hopes, interests, fears, and joys, apart from our fellows, is the death of all true manly life ; and we believe too, that the temptation not only to fall into this condition, but actually to choose it as our own, is constantly near to every one of us. We believe moreover that the hearty engagement in some such work as our Colleges afford, may be one of the means which God may use to help us to surmount this temptation, and we fancy that our experience confirms us in this belief. One of the necessary
results of a belief of this kind must be the spontaneous grotvth of special friendships, which, based as they are on a community of work and thought, may bring incalculable blessings to all parties. The workshop will pro&not; vide companions enough, so will the club and the now celebrated &ldquo;union.&rdquo; But we believe many a man has turned with glad heart from all these to enjoy the quiet walk or read with his &ldquo; College chum.&rdquo; It is generally admitted, that no friendships are so entire, so all-comprehending, as Col&not; lege friendship. There, every thought, every care, is shared. There seems no inner circle from which the friend is debarred. To aid and abet the growth of unions like these, to strengthen in our own hearts and in each other&rsquo;s, the conviction that the richest, deepest life we live is where we are living, as it were, for another, is, I think, no mean or contemptible work, and this I firmly believe our Colleges are doing.,These seem to me some of the indirect results of our work upon the employment of our leisure-hours,&mdash;results, surely, affecting the very foun&not; dation of man&rsquo;s being, penetrating to the very heart&rsquo;s core of life and character. The profitable employment of our hours of work affects our social &ldquo; position,&rdquo; but it is in the use we make of our leisure hours, that life and character are most vitally influenced. It is true of all times and all hours, that if the spirit of good is not working in our hearts the spirit of evil is ; but the words of South are especially true of our free leisure hours, that &ldquo; the soul&rsquo;s play-day is always the devil&rsquo;s working-day, and the idler the man, still the busier the tempter.&rdquo;,J. Meanwhile a third man rides con&not; tinuously on a donkey the same distance in the same time : how r fast does he go ?,ENGLISH GRAMMAR EXAMINATION.&mdash; Professor Masson&rsquo;s Paper.,1. WOLVERHAMPTON &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;WORKING&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;MEN&rsquo;S&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;COLLEGE&lt;/span&gt;.,Second Annual Report,: Sept. 30,,1859.,During the past year&mdash;the second year of,our history,as a College,&mdash;the followiii],classes have been taught ;&mdash;,1st Term.,2nd Term.,3rd Term.,Bible Class.&middot; .,22,24,12,Arithmetic, Sect. I.,8,Arithmetic, Sect. II.,7,16,8,Latin, Sect. I.,12,8,8,Latin, Sect. II.,15,10,English Grammar, Sect. I.,English Grammar, Sect. II.,5,6,5,Geology and Mining.,5,5,4,Preparatory Class.,21,5,Total number of Students . . .,58,71,01,It will be seen that there have been only three terms this year, instead of four, last year&rsquo;s experience having proved the advisability of closing the College entirely during the summer months. This fact, together with the necessary diminution of entrance fees, will quite account for the smaller receipt of fees for the past year,&mdash;the amount being &pound;30. 10s. during the past year, compared with &pound;51. 5s. for&rsquo; the previous year. The English Literature Class has not been re-formed this year, for want of a sufficient number of students, but it is hoped that one of the results of this meeting will be seen in the renewed interest and vigour of a large class on this important subject under the able guidance of the Rev. J. Lloyd. A second Latin Class has been formed for the elemen&not; tary study of the language, and has thus far been most successful.,The attendance is generally very regular, and the interest unflagging. Many of those who joined us at the opening of our College two years ago, abide with ns yet, among our most persevering students, and most zealous friends. The College work and associations have become a part of their very life, which they would not willingly give up, and which they will never forget. At our last meeting, in October, 1858, it was announced by the Rev. T. H. lies that a copy of the &lsquo; Lifeof George Stephenson&rsquo; would be given to the writer of the best essay on auy subject selected by the Council. The subject chosen was, &ldquo; The Influence of Machinery on the Prosperity of the People.&rdquo; Three essays were sent in : the prize was awarded to Thomas Morris, puddler, in the Shrubbery Works, but another was so nearly equal, that the adjudicators determined to give a second prize to Mr. Farnworth, a shiugler.,The College has during the past year united itself with the Society of Arts, especially with a view of giving the students the opportunity of availing themselves of the ad&not; mirable examinations annually conducted by that Society. Only one student presented himself for examination this year, James Henry Butler, and he gained two third-class certificates for Algebra and Bookkeeping, and a first-class one for Arithmetic. Several of our students we hope will come forward for the examinations next May, so that a local Board will be formed and the examinations held in this town.,Lord Wrottcsley, our first President, having retired from his office, the Council unani&not; mously resolved to invite the Earl of Dartmouth to take his place. Without referring to the general claims which his Lordship possesses, as the willing helper in every good work, it may be enough to state that to him mainly our College Library owes its present value and importance. To this invitation his Lordship most readily yielded assent, and he comes amongst us this evening to testify by his kindly words to the interest he takes in our work and success.,In common with all classes of our townsmen, we have had to regret the loss of the late George Edwardes, Esq., who took a warm interest in the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; from the first
hour of its existence, and whose name is enrolled in our books as our first Vice- President.,The different &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt; in the country possess now a Magazine of their own. To the exertions of the Council of the London College the origin of this Magazine is chiefly due. Already, it has proved a valuable medium of intercommunica&not; tion between the different Colleges, and strengthened that bond of fellowship which those engaged in a common work, and animated by a comraou spirit aud hope, ought ever to feel. To those who are interested in these Colleges, but are unable to take any active part in them, this magazine might prove a welcome monthly visitor, telling them what we are doing, and how we are doing it.,Our College is not self-supporting, aud the Treasurer&rsquo;s statement reveals a small balance against us. After all, we do not require very large subscriptions, nor do we wish to canvass for them, but we are sure our claims have only to be presented to meet at once with a ready aud kindly response. We should be glad indeed to see the number both of students and teachers greatly increased. Our past experience enables us to ap&not; peal confidently to both classes, for we believe that any who will join us with a willing and earnest spirit, either as teacher or student, will find his work with us a true and increasing pleasure. We believe that the spirit of fellowship between man and man, which our College work and life has already awakened, and which we hope it will still more fully call forth, will help us each to do our daily work in a braver and more kindly spirit, and to serve more faithfully aud fully the God in whose image we are each created, aud from whom &ldquo; all holy desires, all good counsels, and all just works do proceed.&rdquo;,Abstract of Account, for Year ending Sept. 30th, 1859.,Receipts.,&pound;. s. d.,Pees for past year . . . . 30 10 0,Subscriptions.17 19 0,Magazines on account ... 2 0 0,Looks sold.3 17 0,Balance due to Treasurer ..1205,&pound;66 6 5,Expenditure.,&pound;. s. d.,Bent. 20 0 0,Gas.5 17 0,Coals for one year and a half .16 16 0,Society of Arts Subscription .220 Assistant Secretary&rsquo;s Salary ..400,Additional Tables.14 3,Hire of Exchange, Oct. 1858 .3 18 6,Printing, Books, etc.6 13 0,Magazines.4 7 8,Incidental Expenses .... 1 8 0,&pound;66 6 5,CORRESPONDENCE.,A SCOTCH BAKER AND A LIVERPOOL POLICEMAN.,Mr. Editor, &mdash;Some of our readers may be interested in the following extract from a letter lately received by me from the rector of the village in Surrey in which I live. Writing from Morayshire, he says :&mdash;,&ldquo; The question of the Silurian rocks and fossils found on the north-west coast will be brought forward at the meeting of the British Association at Aberdeen in a few days. It is a vexata qu&aelig;stio with geologists. Meanwhile I have collected some of the fossils in question, with some others, ť was also fortunate in meeting with the two persons whose names have become familiar to geologists in connection with these questions,&middot;&mdash;- Mr. Dick, of Thurso, and Mr. Peach, the head of the Customs at Wick. The former is a character rare in any place but Scotland. He is a baker, in just such a position as one of the Ripley bakers, who tucks up his sleeves, aud wears an apron, aud is ever be- whitened with flour. Aud yet this man discourses learnedly on geology, physiology, aud
is composing a treatise on the Northern Flora;&mdash;pretty well for a small baker 1 There is another of the same sort near this place, at the town of Tynet, who adds also to his scientific knowledge, studies in French and Italian literature. He is a man who oc&not; cupies the position of Cutt, the small shopkeeper on our common. These men are rare with us, and being thrifty men in their trade, show that it is possible to unite with this thriftiness something of a higher character,&mdash;a fact not always allowed.&rdquo;,Rare indeed such men are, and must he, yet by no means is the kind non-extaut in England.&mdash;Walking a short time ago along one of the Liverpool docks, and watching the ships loading and discharging, 1 came upon a ship with a crew of tawny-faccd men, evidently foreigners. A dock policeman was standing by,&mdash;a functionary armed with a cane, and charged to keep order and honesty. I asked him what countrymen they were. &ldquo;Portuguese, Sir.&rdquo;&mdash;&ldquo; What sort of seamen are they ?&rdquo;&mdash;&ldquo; Very fair seamen.&rdquo;&mdash;&ldquo; Well, so they ought to be, if they&rsquo;re like what they were three hundred years ago.&rdquo;&mdash;&ldquo;Yes,&rdquo; answered he, &ldquo; in the time of Vasco de Gamma.&rdquo;&mdash;Vasco de Gamma, or Vasco de Gama ! I ^wondered how a policeman came to know of him, that gallant navigator, first rounder of &ldquo; the Cape of Good Hope ;&rdquo; and began to ask my friend other questions. I soon became listener chiefly, and he talked : of the Spaniards, &ldquo; what a fine people they once were, how lazy and good-for-nothing now ;&rdquo; of the Greeks, now &ldquo; such degenerate cut&not; throat villains,&rdquo; once &ldquo; everything that was noble Thermopylae, &ldquo; what a majestic defence that was ; the Retreat of the Fen Thousand, &ldquo; where can you read of anything finer? and of much else to the same effect that I cannot now remember. &ldquo;And was England,&rdquo; he said, &ldquo;going to fall too, like Greece and Spain ?&rdquo;,This policeman, receiving perhaps twenty shillings a week, and pacing up and down the Liverpool quays eight hours out of every twenty-four, had these thoughts in his head ; and could discourse upon them right well, quite eloquently it seemed to me. He was, he said, very fond of reading as a boy ; liked nothing so much as history and bio&not; graphy ; and was fond of a book still. With him, reading had not been in vain,&mdash;as with some who have paid hundreds of pounds to be made to read. Clearly some of the great tacts o&iacute; the world&rsquo;s history, and the truths they witness of, had taken a living hold of his imagination ; and now, as I had seen, in the midst of his daily task, and by its incidents called to mind, they were sometimes present with him. He could speak,Of brighter images by books impressed Upon the memory, faithfully as stars That occupy their places, and, though oft Hidden by clouds, and oft bedimmed with haze,,Are not to be extinguished or impaired.,Is it not encouraging to us, that without &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt;, &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;men&lt;/span&gt; like these are now living on both sides of Tweed ?,September 1Ш, 1859. Truly yours, V L.,PRIZES OR NO PRIZES.,Dear Mr. Editor,&mdash; Mr. Langley has lately expressed a wish that some of the stu- dents would give &ldquo; their opinions &rdquo; and &ldquo; the results of their experience on the subiect.&rdquo; As I have some knowledge of the examinations and of most of the examiners of our London College, I have ventured to write a few lines on the question.,Here we have no prizes, certificates of competency being alone given to the successful candidates ; we have, therefore, no knowledge of the practical working of the prize- system, and shall in consequence feel grateful to our Manchester friends if they will,* [ &middot; &amp;lt; &Alpha; &deg; 1 &Gamma; ^ mei r er m 0f ? Ur Councilsaid Mr * Langley. None can reply more ap&not; propriately than Mr. Tansley, in both characters. How far his colleagues in the Council will agree with him we shall not know till the question comes on for discussion. Some of us, I think, will feel that he is riding a good principle a little too hard. Our next number will contain Mr. Greenwood&rsquo;s account of the examination and prize system as tried at Manchester.&mdash;E d.] 1
give us their experience and thoughts on the matter, and so help us to form an opinion on grounds not merely speculative.,At present, so far as I am able to judge by means of what I know of our London College, and what I have thought upon this subject, it docs not seem to me that it would be well to urge the members of our Colleges to study, or to complete a study they have begun, by holding out to them any premiums beyond the acknowledgment, by means of certificates, that they have worked fairly and honestly at their subjects, and have ac&not; quired a certain degree of &ldquo; competency therein.&rdquo;,For all who have tried to understand why such Colleges as ours were instituted, and what their aim is, must have discovered that Mr. Maurice and his colleagues did not form a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; for &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;men&lt;/span&gt; iu order that the members of it might increase their in&not; comes, but that by working together as learners and teachers, working men and men of other ranks might become happier and better; and that, by being bound together in a Col&not; lege, they might feel that they have a common object and common duties. We, of the Lon&not; don College, are frequently reminded of this, and Mr. Langley&rsquo;s article, in a late number of the Magazine, gives ample proof that the same principle is fully believed iu at Wolver&not; hampton. Now, is it not contrary to this principle to hold out a prize, especially if it be a money prize, as the first result of having completed a course in any branch of study ? I cannot but think it is. There appears to me to be an obvious contra&not; diction in telling a man that you do not teach him in order that he may gain money, but that you will give him some if he acquire a certain amount of knowledge. Moreover, whetherthe prize be money or anything else,&mdash;books, for instance,&mdash;men may look forward to the prize as the end for which they are studying, and so, losing sight of far nobler ends, come to regard their work at the College as something which is to benefit themselves individually by increasing their incomes or adding to their libraries. It may be said, &ldquo;But after all there is no great harmin men, especially working men, to whom every penny is of consequence, trying to increase their incomes or add to their libraries.&rdquo; Certainly not, provided it can be done without interfering with other, more sacred, ob&not; jects ; but when men, in order to earn their daily bread, are obliged to spend ten or more hours a day in money-getting, the few hours a week that they can then spare from their home duties are not too many to be spent iu educating themselves so that they may be better able to grapple with those subjects which all men ought to think out for them&not; selves, and I am sure this work can be done more thoroughly if it be entirely free from that &ldquo; everlasting see-saw of profit and loss &rdquo; which must necessarily form part of their daily lives.,During my four j&#39;ears as a student, 1 knew nearly all our certificated men before they had won their certificates, and I have joined in many an anxious discussion in which the chances of passing the next examination have been very seriously considered ; I have also joined in many an evening&rsquo;s hard &ldquo; grind&rdquo; to increase these chances. At these grinds, a few men who are going through a regular course together, meet on certain nights in the week (in addition to their regular class-work) to read for the examinations. I may just say here that we have always found this a capital plan : most of the men who have read in this way have been successful ; and, what is better, many of them have been intimate friends ever since. After a hard day&rsquo;s work at the bench or desk, many men have had to walk long distances through rain and snow, and yet I never heard any one complain that the certificate was too small a reward, or wanted the addition of money or books to make it worth having. On the contrary, every one ot my fellow-students that I have yet spoken to on the subject, has expressed his belief that prizes are not necessary.,There is a privilege attached to our certificates which is highly valued by all who have gained it, and which enables working men to help in educating their fellow-work&not; men. I refer to the fact that our Preparatory and Practice Class teachers are chosen from the certificated students. Alluding to this privilege, a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; friend of mine (a &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;man&lt;/span&gt; and a C.S.) said, &ldquo; Our Colleges need hold out no other prizes to their students while they offer such as these ; first a certificate, accompanied with the privilege of sharing in the College work, and ultimately a Membership of the Council, which carries with it a fuller share of this work.&rdquo; This is quite true, for if &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;men&lt;/span&gt; benefit at all by &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; teaching, they will feel a deep interest in the welfare ol their class ; they will know that the greatest good must follow from the education of that class, and they will believe it to be a blessing to themselves to be able to take a part, however small, in helping forward that education.,London , October 1859.,George Tansley.

The chief feature was the distribution of prizes and certificates to the successful stu&not; dents. The Report, read by the Principal on behalf of the Council, gave the follow&not; ing statistics of attendance during the past term. Total entries 128, consisting of 40 operatives, 66 clerks, etc., 17 shopkeepers, and 5 teachers or librarians. Entries for the several classes:&mdash;Greek, 18; Latin (elementary), 19 ; Latin (advanced), 24 ; English Language, 31 ; Logic, 17 ; Geome&not; try (elementary), 11; Geometry (advanced),,5 ; Algebra, 8 ; Arithmetic, 13; Mechanics,,6 ; Chemistry, 13 ; Physical Geography, 3 ; Physiology, 9. The average attendance had been at least as good as, if not better than, during any previous term ; and the exa&not; minations (the first held) have given a bet&not; ter than usual test of the spirit and suc&not; cess with which the studies have been pur&not; sued. The plan pursued in the examina&not; tions has been such that no prize or certi&not; ficate had been awarded for comparative excellence ; each represents a positive amount of satisfactory knowledge and work. The result of the examinations will give the Council an opportunity for cousi- &#39;dering, wdth a view to action, how best to form a body of students who may have a permanent connection with the College, and, if possible, take a share in its management.
