The older members are applying themselves with greater zeal and diligence to their severals tudies, a re&not; sult fairly and mainly attributable to the stimulus given by the recent examination, and the award of prizes and certificates. No precise return of the numbers regis&not; tered tor the present term can yet be given.,From the address of the Treasurer, Mr. Alfred Neild, it appears that the financial position of the College is not very good : it is somewhat in doubt. The receipts for the year have been &pound;88. 2s. bd&bdquo; against &pound;99. 19s. od. Both the Council and stu&not; dents, especially the latter, are anxious that the College may become self-support&not; ing. This feeling was strongly expressed at the meeting. Mr. J. W. Southern (whose name appears in the list of ho&not; nours), in seconding a vote of thanks to the teachers, said he was sure they were all so grateful for the instruction given, that they would each strive to increase the number of students&mdash;so as not in future to have such Reports read ; and another student said he was sure that when once the body were brought into communication with the Council, there would be such a feeling raised that there would be an end of any pecuniary difficulty.
Salford .&mdash;The opening meeting of the present term, held on Sept. 28th, was one of more than usual importance, as the chairman, Mr. Chadwick, was able to an&not; nounce the completion of the arrangement by which the Mechanics&rsquo; Institution has merged into the College, and the latter ob&not; tained possession of the building. The man&not; ner of the amalgamation as stated by Mr. Chadwick has been as follows;&mdash;The direc&not; tors of the Mechanics&rsquo; Institution agree to transfer all their Institution to the Council of the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; on these conditions, namely, that all their classes should be continued; that six of the di&not; rectors should be nominated by them for one year only, and should be appointed on the Council of the College, in order to af&not; ford their assistance in the management of the classes of the Mechanics&rsquo; Institution to be transferred to the Council of the Col&not; lege, but at the expiration of the current year the constitution of the College to be altered, by the addition of a rule making it requisite to appoint six independent mem&not; bers of the College, for the especial purpose of being a sub-commiltee to take charge of the juvenile classes in connection with the Mechanics&rsquo; Institution ; and that the Coun&not; cil of the College should be required to raise the requisite funds to clear the Insti&not; tution from debt and to pay off the mort&not; gage. A further condition is that the &ldquo; Temperance Committee,&rdquo; a body which under the old title-deeds had a partial right of occupancy in the building, shall be allowed the use of the principal room on Saturdays and Sundays, taking a lease for seven, ten, or fourteen years. To pay off the mortgage and put the building in order, required a sum of &pound;650. This the Council undertook to raise; and &pound;580 has already been collected. Among the contri&not; butors are Mr. Langworthy (President), &pound;100 ; Mr. Harvey (Mayor of Salford), &pound;50; Thomas Walker, Esq., &pound;100; the Earl of Ellesmere, &pound;25 ; Sir B. Heywood, &pound;25 ; Arthur Heywood, Esq., &pound;25 ; Aider- man T. Fuller, &pound;40 ; besides many more do&not; nors of sums varying from &pound;20 downwards. We are now making further efforts (said Mr. Chadwick) to raise not only the re&not; maining &pound;70, but we hope we shall be able to raise &pound;100 or &pound;120, so that we can thoroughly beautify the building, have it surveyed and examined throughout, see that it is in thoroughly good order, and, when it is free from debt and in perfect order, we expect that we shall be able to make the Institution perfectly self-support&not;,ing in every respect. The Council intend every year to make an estimate of the ex&not; penditure and charges on the classes, but each class is not to exceed the amount of the estimate during the year, never incur&not; ring a debt for any alterations or extraor&not; dinary items without having first the money in hand to do it with. In asking for these subscriptions we have almost pledged ourselves that this shall be the re&not; sult ; and we therefore now publicly state, as the condition on which we have obtained money, that we have pledged our succes&not; sors never to get into debt and never to exceed their income Although the whole of this money has not been received, we were so desirous of making the transfer complete, that we have paid off the mort&not; gage, and are now able to show you the cancelled deed, the money having been paid ; and we are now for the first time assembled in a building which is in reality and in name, &ldquo; The Salford &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;.&rdquo; (Cheers.) To enable this to be done, the building had to be bought in the name of Mr. Langworthy ; Mr. Lang&not; worthy has accepted the trust-deed from the trustees of the Mechanics&rsquo; Institution, and has purchased it in his &omicron;&lambda;&nu;&eta; name, and then executed another deed, conveying the property to the trustees of the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt;, as stated in our prospectus, on the conditions that this building shall be for ever hereafter used for the purposes of the Salford &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; ; and the only conditions not mentioned in the trust-deed are those stated in our con&not; stitution, that the building and the College are intended to enable the working classes to improve their education and acquire know&not; ledge. It was thought, in conveying the Institution to the trustees for the bene&not; fit of the College, it was not necessary to put forth all those extraordinary restraints and conditions which would be required for institutions used principally for young men and youths ; and especially when the de&not; mocratic element in onr College places the management of the Institution in the hands of the members themselves. It is hoped that the working men of Salford will always take such an interest in the Institu&not; tion, which is now their own property, and as much the public property of the working men of Salford as Feel Park and the Free Museum and Library&mdash;it is hoped that they will not only manifest a desire to acquire knowledge themselves, but a desire to be able to disseminate ihat knowledge and to assist others to acquire it; that they will
value this Institution according to its real worth; and that they will be proud of it, and have an affection and regard for it, never being ashamed of acknow&not; ledging their interest in it. The Coun&not; cil hope that thousands of working men, who have not had the opportunity of acquiring a good education in their youth, will avail themselves of its privileges now, and thus make themselves fitter to take an active and important part in all matters re&not; lating to this borough, and in relation to the public opinion of the country at large. It is freely open to all. We wish working men, without restriction or restraint, to acquire knowledge here, being convinced that the more knowledge they have, the better position will they be in to exercise their functions to advantage, in a political and moral sense. As a member connected with the College, I am truly thankful that the Institution has managed so satisfac&not; torily, The democratic element of our Institution I believe has been eminently successful. In other Colleges, not only in neighbouring towns, but in London* and other places, the Councils of Manage&not; ment are entirely entrusted to the teachers and patrons. I may say that in the Sal&not; ii* Mr. Chadwick will be glad to hear that this is not quite the case with our London College. The students have prac&not; tically a considerable share in its manage&not; ment. Like the &ldquo; gentlemen of the House of Commons,&rdquo; they control the exchequer, a majority of the Finance Committee being elected by them. Besides this, we recruit our Council from among them, as fast, that is, as the present strictness of our examination tests will allow. The students too have a general standing Committee of their own, to secure a constant communi&not; cation with the Council. As for &ldquo;Pa&not; trons,&rdquo; we know not the name. Nobody has the least to do with our management who is not at work with us. I mention these points that our Salford friends may not think we are a set of bigoted oligarchs because our constitution is not quite so democratic as their own. We watch, in&not; deed, with the deepest interest the work&not; ing of the principles of self-government ; and so long as it shows results so inspiring as the present condition of the Salford College, we shall be delighted to confess that, whether theoretically the best or not, it at least gives free swing to the un&not; flagging energy of the North.&mdash; Ed.],ford &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; the very op&not; posite is the practice. Although our vice- presidents and trustees are members of the Council of the College, and have a voice in the management of the Institution, every member of the Council, every dele&not; gate and representative of every class, will bear me out in saying that in their bauds rests the sole respousibility of the Work&not; ing Men&rsquo;s College. I can only say, in conclusion, that I believe the Institution in every branch is being carried out with every success, and that our ardent desire that it may disseminate good, and be a blessing to the town, is being realized.,It was afterwards stated that the num&not; ber of students during the past term had been 165, with 194 entries to classes. The business of the evening was enlivened by some pleasant old glees, sung by the Col&not; lege choir. This, and the presence of a goodly array of the members&rsquo; fair friends, contributed to secure a most agreeable evening.,Ancoats .&mdash;From a most interesting Re&not; port of the A.C. Cricket Club, which we have not space to print, it would appear that the Club has had a most merry and successful third season, has played nineteen matches, won ten victories, proved itself superior in three drawn-battles, and paid its expenses. At a meeting under the presidency of the Rev. Canon Richson, prizes were given 1o the most accom&not; plished batsmen, Mr. Moore and Mr. Underwood. The following extract will give an idea of the cheery way in which the Club goes about its work :&mdash;&ldquo; An&not; other match was played on a delight&not; ful day in July. Some thirty of our members, with their wives and intended dittos, .engaged an omnibus, and drove to the beautiful park at Bowden, where sides were chosen, and the afternoon spent iu cricket and other games, interspersed with vocal and instrumental music, by profes&not; sionals and amateurs, and kept up until approaching night compelled a reluctant separation. So, after a hearty tea, the whole party returned home, highly de&not; lighted with their day&rsquo;s pleasure, and long&not; ing for another opportunity to repeat the pleasing experiment ; the ladies declaring they saw more sense in an out of that sort, than in a lot of men selfishly keeping themselves to themselves for a whole day.&rdquo; The Club desires &ldquo; to measure bats with the Southern Colleges.&rdquo; Heaven help the Londoners !

THE,W&oslash;rking Цепя College Цэдше.,No. XII; DECEMBER 1, 1859.,THE EDUCATION OF WOMEN IN &ldquo;&lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;WORKING&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;MEN&rsquo;S&lt;/span&gt;&rdquo;,&lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;COLLEGES&lt;/span&gt;.,The necessity for educating working women, as an abstract question, has long ago been demonstrated. The old notion, that if the boys of a family received school teaching, all that was needful to be done had been done, is now exploded. The prevalent evil, in the case of parents whose means enable them to educate their girls, is that, in their anxiety to make that education tell , they provide teaching much more of an ornamental than of a useful character. This remark, however, does not apply to any great ex&not; tent to women of the working classes, who are too frequently left without anything which can fairly be called education, and it iş about them I wish now to write. A great deal has been said and written of the social good which would result from the education of those who are, or are to become, the wives of the men of the present generation and the mothers of the next. In addressing the readers of the &lsquo; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; Magazine,&rsquo; I have no wish to enlarge upon this topic, except in so far as our College work creates, as it appears to me, a necessity of its own for the education of women of the same class as that to which our present students belong.,That work I take to be the education of the mental, moral, and physical capacities of the men of whom the College consists, the refinement of&quot; their perceptions and sympathies, the development of their sense of responsi&not; bility to their families and country, and of all the duties which grow out of their social position ; the fostering, indeed, of all that is essential to the formation of truly earnest, honest, thoughtful men. To a considerable ex&not; tent I believe the College succeeds in this ; to some extent also, I am forced to believe it fails. But inasmuch as it does succeed to that extent, does it create in the minds of the recipients of its teachings a desire for commu&not; nion with human beings of like tendencies, and absolutely precludes them from finding pleasure in the society of any who have not these tendencies. Much of the happiness of their future lives will depend upon the gratifica&not; tion of this desire, their domestic happiness, perhaps, more than any other kind, depending upon it. There is always a gulf between an educated and an uneducated person which no courtesy can completely bridge over, which must always prevent a complete communion of tastes, thoughts, and pur&not; suits&mdash;necessary surely in a domestic circle more than anywhere ; a gulf which is ever widening as the difference between their educational con&not; ditions increases. Such considerations as these, independents of more general reasons, force me to think that it is necessary, even for the realiza&not; tion by the men of all the good which should be derived from the teach&not; ings of our College, that women should, either with us or elsewhere, receive the same kind of teaching as that which our College gives. With us by all means, I say, if that teaching can be given without detriment to the,p
present real usefulness of the College, as I believe it can ; and not merely without anything which would be detrimental, but with much that would be helpful, much that would tend to the complete realization of all that the most cordial well-wishers of the College hope for from its action.,This is not a new subject with either teachers or students in our London College. In 1857, on the commencement of our present &ldquo;Women&rsquo;s Classes,&rdquo; the College &ldquo; Report &rdquo; says :&mdash;&ldquo; Our pupils would have been quite willing that the women should have been instructed, if we had thought fit, in the same classes with themselves. This plan, after careful considera&not; tion, has not been thought advisable.&rdquo; I am sensible that good reasons prompted that decision, reasons which, if they still exist, will doubtlessly be sufficient to set aside the idea of opening the evening classes to women now. On the other hand, it is quite possible that nearly three years&rsquo; addi&not; tional experience of the working of our College and of the women&rsquo;s classes may have removed or modified the difficulties which then existed. At least no harm can come of the discussion of such difficulties, if they still exist ; and it is perhaps due to those who desire the change, that whatever objections there are, should be plainly stated and considered.,This then brings me to say that in writing this paper I have the hope that a careful reconsideration of the subject will show that there are no real grounds for the exclusion of women from our evening classes. Before going on to say a few words about some of the difficulties which are sup&not; posed to stand in the way, and to show, as I hope to do, that the gravest of them does not afford much ground for apprehension, it will perhaps be as well to consider what the College has done to educate working women. By the last Report I find that, exclusive of the vacation term, &ldquo;an average of seventy members of the Women&rsquo;s Classes &rdquo; was obtained, each member attending on the average three classes weekly. But we are told in the article on &ldquo; Afternoon Classes for Women &rdquo; (Mag. No, VII.) that &ldquo; they &rdquo; (the classes) &ldquo; are not attended by those who are commonly called working women ; that is to say, not by paid workers, engaged in trades or crafts. For such, the only time for study must be not afternoon, hut night.&rdquo; The writer goes on to claim the appellation &ldquo;working women&rdquo; for those wives, sisters, or daughters of working men &rdquo; whose business in this world is to clean rooms, to cook, to mend clothes, or tend children.&rdquo; Certainly such women can, in a most emphatic sense, claim that appellation, but I must be permitted to say that from what I have seen and heard of the members of the afternoon classes, I judge them to be, in the main, not the wives, sisters, or daughters of working men, restricting the application of the term &ldquo; working men &rdquo; to those who are usually designated by it, but that they are, as compared with such and with wages-receiving working women, what the tradesman and clerk are to the mechanic and labourer.,The writer of &ldquo; Afternoon Classes for Women,&rdquo; besides informing us that the students in those classes are not &ldquo; working women,&rdquo; as they are generally understood, goes on to say that the programme o&iacute; studies is too meagre ; partly that it is not sufficiently explicit on the nature of what is taught in the classes, and that subjects do not appear in it at all which it would be well to teach. I presume the writer of that article, and indeed all the teachers of the afternoon classes, think it desirable that their lists of classes should, if practicable, be nearly as comprehensive as that offered to
the rougher portion of the College. AYith the addition of the specially feminine studies&mdash;cookery and needlework&mdash;the subjects taught in the three &ldquo; divisions &rdquo; of the College would serve equally well for women. There would probably be some portion of that teaching which women would not care to receive, but it would not be deficient in any essential part of a woman&rsquo;s education. The question then stands thus at present : Women&rsquo;s classes exist which are not available for actual working women, to whom, for reasons I have referred to above, it is especially desirable instruction should be given ; these classes are not sufficiently numerous and compre&not; hensive for the purpose intended. On the other hand, the men&#39;s classes, which, so far as time is concerned, would be as available for working women as men, much more nearly meet the requirements of women who desire a complete and systematic education. The question to be solved is, can our College throw open its doors to women, to learn side by side with the men who come to it ? It is evident that, at present, that is the only method by which our cuniculum of students can be made available for women.,The difficulties in the way of admitting women to the evening classes, if I at all understand them, are not numerous ; that which gives most anxiety, is, I presume, the fear that unpleasant things might occur from the levity of students of either sex. On this point, fortunately, the Magazines (Nos. IV. and YI.) furnish us with most important testimony, in the history of the People&rsquo;s College at Sheffield, written by Mr. Rowbotham. This testimony is the more valuable that it relates to men and women who are, class for class, the same as those whom we now have, and should get under the ar&not; rangement. I suppose, and I feel myself justified in assuming, that a mere difference of place will not make much difference in the result, and that in this particular human nature is pretty much the same, whether located in Sheffield or London. I shall not occupy much space in quoting what Mr. Rowbotham says, nor in remarking upon the particular rules which the Sheffield College adopted in this matter, though they are well worth con&not; sidering, I trust that all who feel interested in the question will turn to their Magazines and read for themselves. I will content myself with say&not; ing that on the reorganization of the College, consequent on the unavoidable retirement of its founder and Principal, the Committee of Management, consisting of the elder students, with six years&rsquo; experience to guide them, determined upon the continued admission of women to the classes. More&not; over Mr. Rowbotham, writing with ten years&rsquo; additional experience, makes the following important statement :&mdash;&ldquo; There has not been a single occur&not; rence to call into question in the remotest degree the discipline of the In&not; stitution and the decorum practised by the students towards one another.&rdquo; While referring to the history of the Sheffield College, I must be allowed to give vent to the feelings which its perusal has aroused in me. I cannot call to mind anything I ever read which gave me more unmistakable plea&not; sure than that &ldquo; history.&rdquo; We, of the College in Great Ormond Street, feel ourselves to be singularly fortunate in possessing the goodwill and active assistance of a body of men who, by ability, educational qualifica&not; tions, and sympathies, are pre-eminently fitted to be our teachers ; yet I must say, even though I should appear to be wanting in gratitude for all the good which I feel has been conferred upon me, that we have less cause for gratulation than those men at Sheffield, who by their own energy and man-
ful independence have won for themselves all the good which their College gives them. Their meeting in the humble room &ldquo; illuminated by a half&not; penny candle stuck into an earthenware inkstand,&rdquo; has for me higher hope for the good of my own class than many more pompous assemblies of loudly philanthropic purpose ; and as an illustration of the self-reliance of English working men deserves every working man&rsquo;s respect, nay, veneration.,The experience of the Sheffield College is, I feel justified in saying, most important, indeed I may say conclusive, on what I conceive to be the very vital part of the question I am considering. It unmistakably shows that men and women can study together, can associate together for all the legitimate purposes of a College, without any other results than those most to be desired. I agree with the writer of the account of the Halifax College in the October number of the Magazine, that the association of women with men in their classes would act as an incentive to the latter to work the harder. They would very naturally desire to distinguish them&not; selves in the eyes of fairer class-mates, and for the honour and reputation of their sex they would strive not to be outdone by them. Some classes would thrive the better with an infusion of the feminine element. Notably the singing classes, in which an occasional mingling of the men&rsquo;s and women&rsquo;s classes is now being carried out. Besides, there is no blinking the fact, that a portion of the members of the College do already seek the society of the female friends of their fellow-students, either in their studies or their pleasures, as excursions, etc.; and it appears to me irthat kind of as&not; sociation is to be had, it will be the better for both men and women, that it should be not less in their work than in their relaxation.,The only considerations which occur to me at this moment, are those of space and teachers. Can one or two rooms, and the teachers, be found for the new classes which would be required ? Would our present staff of teachers object to receive women in their classes ? I believe an affirmative answer may be given to the former of these questions, and I doubt if the latter would be at all a troublesome one. Our Principal already takes part in the teaching of women in the afternoon classes, and I think not many of our teachers would hesitate to follow his example in this particular, while I am convinced that some of them would accept with pleasure such a cheerful addition to their classes.,Nearly all our class-rooms, as every member of our College knows, are capable of receiving a greater number of students than now come to them, and if the three hundred who attend weekly could be increased by one- half by the admission of women, the good effected in the College would, I am hopeful, be increased in a still greater proportion, from the softening and refining influence which such an importation would have on the naturally rugged characters of the men. Let me ask, in conclusion, that all who may give their attention to the subject of this paper will carefully distin&not; guish between real conviction and mere prejudice, with which, I fear, the matter is peculiarly liable to be regarded. Let us have constantly before us the conviction that this is a subject closely related to some of the greatest social deficiencies of our day ; evils which are pressing with ever-increasing force on the attention of earnest-minded men, evils which must be abated, and in the abatement of which education must undoubtedly play a not inconsidera&not; ble part.,John Roebuck.
THE COLLEGE EIFLE CORPS,Has made a good start at last. On the 3rd of November I received a final note from the Lord Lieutenant&rsquo;s secretary, stating that he would certify us as soon as we could show that we had obtained a practice ground for rifle-shooting of not less than 300 yards in length, a secure place for the custody of the arms, and a competent person engaged to take care of them. The two latter requirements we might easily have met; the first was out of the question. Rifle-grounds 300 yards long, close to London, are too expensive for the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; Corps at present. It seemed therefore that we had no hopes of getting on our legs by ourselves. The next point was to find somebody to hold on by while we were getting our legs.,I tried several established corps, and was very nearly coming to terms with the Marylebone, who are already sanctioned, and have a good prac&not; tice-ground. They offered very favourable terms in most respects,&mdash;to reduce our subscription, to help us out of their funds to some extent, and to give us longer time for payment than their own members, and we were to form a separate company of their corps. They could not however dis&not; pense with their dress uniform. This they required us to get within six months, besides a shell jacket and cap, costing 10s., for immediate use. As I found that their uniform cost L3. 15s., whde the uniform worn by Lord Elcho at Hythe, which has been approved and adopted at the War Office as the best yet out, costs 28s. only, I felt that there was nothing more to be said.,When my attempts at hooking on to richer corps had failed, I went down to the War Office to consult with Lord de Grey (who, as you all know, was one of the earliest and warmest supporters of the College) as to what could be done. He cannot ask the Government to help us by the issue of rifles, or sending any of our number to Hythe, until we have satisfied the Lord Lieutenant on the points above referred to ; but as he has the whole organization and direction of this volunteer movement under his control, and is therefore in constant intercourse with the captains of corps, he is going to try to make terms for us with some corps in the Metropolitan district which is already sanctioned, so that we may be able to return a proper ground, and obtain her Majesty&rsquo;s sanction as soon as possible.,Meantime, however, he has been able to do for us just what we want. At this time of the year we could not go out for rifle practice in any case ; what we can do is to get thoroughly well drilled, so that when we do get to rifle practice, we may be able to give our whole time and energies to it, having mastered thoroughly all the first steps of a rifleman&rsquo;s training ; and there is nothing to hinder us now. Lord de Grey has obtained for us the Secretary of State&rsquo;s sanction to drill, and we have a very fair parade ground of our own ; so now let us see what the &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;College&lt;/span&gt; is made of.,I am very well satisfied with our start. It is a week since I obtained the Secretary of State&rsquo;s sanction to drill, and we have in that time lighted the garden with gas, and had three nights&rsquo; drill of not less than two hours each. We have appointed two secretaries of the corps, who have gone
carefully through the old list, and have taken new names, and we muster now 72 strong, AA&#39;e have opened a subscription list, which has been headed by Mr. Vernon Lushington with a subscription of two guineas. IS ext Wednesday (23rd November) Ave have our first general muster to settle the amount of subscription, hours of drib, and all other matters of detail. It is pretty clear to me that it will depend on ourselves Avhether Ave ever get enrolled or not. If in a few months (say February or March) Ave can march out on to any parade-ground in the kingdom, and go through all the usual movements of a light infantry company as well as a regiment of the line (and I for one will be con&not; tent with nothing less, and Avon&rsquo;t march out with our company till Ave can do this), Ave shall get practice-ground and rifles fast enough, never fear. As I said above, I am very Avell satisfied Avith our three preliminary drills on the Avhole, but there Avere many men not doing their best. There Avas a good deal of slovenly Avork, Avhich need not have been ; the men could have done better if they had tried. Every man can hold his head up, every man can keep his hands to his sides. Noav it may seem absuľd to sup&not; pose that a man Avili make a bit the Averse rifleman because he swings his arms a little in marching, or commits any of the other five-and-twenty small sins in carriage, position, etc., against which Sergeant Reed has been protesting this last week. Nevertheless, those &alpha;&alpha;Ί&iota;&omicron; ought to know best tell us that it is not absurd ; that the fact is jusl so ; and when you
come to think of it, reason and common sense is wholly on their side. If you want to make 100 men into one man for all purposes of marching, facing, wheeling, etc., which I take it is the object of all drill, it does seem to me as plain as the nose on one&rsquo;s face, that you can&rsquo;t be too par&not; ticular about the least error in any one of these little matters. It is no proof of manliness or superiority to do the goose-step or stand-at-ease like a sloven ; it is a proof of nothing but gooseishness. Some of you may say, &ldquo; But we are told that drill is of no consequence to volunteers, pipe-clay is to be discarded ; intelligence and good shooting is to be the thing.&rdquo; So, I admit, write the * Times&rsquo;* and other papers, and I say they write mischievous bosh. We mean to go in for all the intelligence and good shooting part of the business too ; but will any man in his senses (except the we of a newspaper, which isn&rsquo;t a man and isn&rsquo;t in its senses) tell me that 100 intelligent, good shots won&rsquo;t be ten times as confident and available if they are perfectly versed in all light infantry movements P It is wasting words to talk about it. What we shall aim* at, if I have anything to do with the discipline, is to get into such order that if the Trench land we could march in a body to the War Office to volunteer into the line for the campaign, and that when we were inspected, no command&not; ing officer would hesitate for a minute about sending us to the front.,So now, my fellow-volunteers of the College, let us go into this business &ldquo; a header .&rdquo; We hear that there is a chance of Messrs. W. and J. Spot- tiswoode&rsquo;s joining with 100 men each out of their establishments, and other rumours of working men&rsquo;s companies anxious to come to us, and of the wish of one and all of these to be enrolled along with us in a workino&middot; man&rsquo;s regiment. There is no room here for speculations, but it is S tempting subject for castle-building. I can only say for myself, that if we and they go on well with what we have in hand, I really don&rsquo;t see what may not come of it in the future of all the good things ivhich many o&iacute; us have been experimenting after these dozen years.,Thomas Hughes.,SOME USES OF IT.f,Mr. Langley&rsquo;s recent paper on &ldquo; What is the Use of It ?&rdquo; met this ques&not; tion, m its ordinary acceptation, on the right ground. &ldquo;We do not pro fess to know, nor do we very much care, whether there is any use or not in what we wish to teach you.&rdquo; But there are men to whom the question comes with a deeper force and meaning; men who feel there must be a use in their College-work far higher than any money-value can give; men who are not so much concerned to put the question, as to seek earnestly and reverently the answer. J,The feeling which prompts this search seemed to pervade so stron &raquo;ly all the speeches made by working men at our late annual and social gathering,Srnce this article was written, I am glad to see that the &lsquo;Times&rsquo; has fas usuali come clean round, and is now my way of thinking. Sec article in &lsquo; Times &rsquo; Nov 2&#39;i T Communicated hy a Member of the Council of the Wolverhampton Colle-e &rsquo; &#39;
that my attention was powerfully drawn to the subject, and I purpose hy&not; ing, as far as I can, to interpret it. At these meetings I heard several of our students say publicly what they thought of the College, and what they had got by it, and I observed that nearly every speaker recommended to intending students the particular study he had himself taken up. &ldquo;Begin with English grammar,&rdquo; said one man, &ldquo;that will enlarge your minds. 1 found it helped me to think, and gave me something to think about ; be&not; sides, I am sure every man ought to try and speak his own language rightly.&rdquo; &ldquo; I would recommend the Algebra Class,&rdquo; said a second, &ldquo; to every man who, like myself, wants a good exercise for his mind. It is something worth tackling with ; you are obliged to fight your way inch by inch, and keep up what you leam, or you are soon floored.&rdquo; And so with others. Each honestly believed his ovvn study the best for him, not be&not; cause that study made him a better grammarian or mathematician than his neighbour, but as I conceive on much nobler grounds. The reason each felt and endeavoured to shadow forth dimly in words was, in all cases, I believe, precisely the same. Is it not this,&mdash;and I appeal to each man for the truth of what I say,&mdash;that the application which any study, in a greater or less degree, demands, compels the student to adopt a certain order and arrangement in thinking no less than in doing? One fact has to be linked with another, in order to show the relative bearing of each. Each fact ap&not; pears with a new meaning or a different application in different situations, or loses all meaning if unconnected or misplaced. Hence, almost without knowing it, the Grammar, the Algebra, the Geology, the Latin, or the French a man has been exercising his brain upon, trains him in those pro&not; cesses of investigation and reasoning which lie at the base of all life. The practical and immediate use of this to the workman, and indeed to every man, is mainly to help him to understand what people say, to understand what books say, to understand what nature and their own experience say, and to think rightly, speak rightly, and argue rightly on ail questions that interest them ; all which vre cannot do till we pierce through the words we hear and utter, and probe the mind that lies behind those words.,But, says one, Cannot I understand ray newspaper, for instance, unless I can solve an equation P In answer to this, I simply appeal to the expe&not; rience of every man who has begun his thinking life by the earnest study of any subject, no matter whether by books or without them, whether the subject be Religion or Politics, Nature or Art, Science or Language.,A few evenings ago I was asked by a student in my French class a question about a passage he did not quite understand in Bacon s Novum Oiganum. The position of a point, or the meaning of a prefix, made all the difference.,I had only to call his attention to that, and the meaning was clear to him.,I am not sure that I mid a word about it beyond that. The French or the Latin he studies had nothing to do with it ; there was no possible as&not; sociation between the subjects ; it might have been an Essay on Strikes, or a receipt for boiling potatoes, but who can deny that the labour and pa&not; tience and fixity of attention required to produce a good French exercise or a readable translation of Cicero, was not the best possible training that man could have for taking a part in the common questions that interest people, and extracting therefrom hints, ideas, embryos of thought for idle moments ? And just as a similar labour and patience are necessary for the
mathematical or geological student, so are the results the same for him. \V&#39;hat he does earnestly and ploddingly under the direction of those who have preceded him in any given study, and with the co-operation of others struggling with him through the same difficulties, if it do not actually ex&not; pand his mind, at least makes him know the resources and capabilities of what he has, imparts an order, a regular sequence to the chaotic mass of information he possessed before, determines the amount of what he knows or thinks about, assigns a place to every fact or idea, and binds the whole into a useful and usable machinery of mind, that differs from material ma&not; chinery in the faculty it possesses of ever increasing its own powers and extending its own range of operation.,But I may be asked, &ldquo; Might not this machinery of the brain be kept up to the working pitch without coming to such a place as your College, and learning something dry for an hour or so, when one could do it at home just when one liked and how one liked?&rdquo; This objection has been made and answered repeatedly ; it touches closely upon the question of the indirect advantages of College-life as contrasted with individual effort at self-culture ; and here it will be enough for me to add :&mdash;I admit the force of your objection if you think that man&rsquo;s being has its compietesi develop&not; ment in the culture of the intellect alone, and if you can succeed in inakiim us forget we have hearts with human sympathies and human wants, that struggle for utterance, and find their finest expression, not amid the gloom which accompanies bodily or mental suffering, but in the labour and plea&not; sure, the fears and the hopes of our ordinary life.,There is again another point which we must keep in view. The subdi&not; vision of all labour is rapidly tending to reduce men to the condition of the simplest tools they employ, each working monotonously at the one un&not; varying round of cutting or shaping or finishing his own speciality ; a state of things that, after a certain point, makes no demand on the intel&not; lectual powers of the mass of men, and forces upon them involuntarily the b&eacute;li&eacute;t that the head will contrive and the fingers work just as well without keeping up the machinery of the brain in proper working order. What we want to counteract this tendency, particularly those of us who live in laro-e towns, among so many evidences of the powers we hold in command, is to be reminded, that just as we impose an unfailing order and precision of operation on the instruments and appliances by which we make our will felt in every part of nature s kingdom, so ought we to recognize the prin&not; ciples of submission to order and law in that subtle, invisible mind of ours which guides the will, i aises it above the instinct of the lower animals, and exists to prove our kinship to God himself. We know our material bo&not; dies are subject to laws which we cannot set aside : our lungs must rise and fall, and our pulse beat in obedience to a necessity which we feel to be no hardship, and under which we can realize a sense of the most complete liberty. And the more we get to know of what is going on in the world of the geologist, the botanist, the astronomer, the more do we see that har mony and obedience to law reign everywhere, and that God&rsquo;s vast universe of things, seen and unseen, is linked together in all its parts by a succession of systems complete m themselves, and yet severally dependent on all the rest&middot;,If, then, we let confusion and discord run riot in that boundless world
of mind placed under our control, and given us for the exercise of our di v inest lordship, we prove ourselves unworthy of the noblest and most sacred treasure we have been entrusted with, and forfeit all claim to rank among the sons of God and the lords of His universe.,S. The Principal presided, and explained the arrangements as to new classes ; alluding especially to those to be conducted by new teachers, Mr. Allbutt, Mr. Bowen, and Mr. Pranks He then awarded two &ldquo;Cer&not; tificates of Competency,&rdquo; one in Algebra to Mr. J. Mr. Hughes, at the close of the meeting, made some remarks on the fact, that for some time past the practice of having prayers after the classes had fallen into disuse, saying that this omission had been particularly painful to him, as he had in a maimer taken upon himself the task of guiding the social life of the College. This, he said, he could no longer undertake to do, if united worship, the highest of social acts, was to be given up.,Arrangements have now been made for holding the prayers regularly. Saturday, 4.30 to 6.0, 1st and 2nd to&not; gether.,The number of members is at present 72.,CORRESPONDENCE.,Examinations and Prizes.,Sir, &mdash;In several recent numbers of the Magazine, attention has been drawn to the subject of examinations, and to the comparative merit of various systems ; and as iu the Manchester College we appear to have differed from most other colleges in offering prizes as well as certificates to successful candidates, I venture to hope I may contribute something towards the discussion of this question, if I give you a short account of orn&not; iate examinations, and of the reasons which, I think, justify the course we adopted. It is, perhaps, too early at present to speak of any experience derived by us from so recent a trial.,In June last we completed our first full session ; and this seemed to us the fitting occasion for holding our first general examination. Some unavoidable delay arose in maturing our scheme, and hence the notice given to the students was but short. In consequence, we believe, of this delay, a much smaller number of our students entered the lists than will probably be the case in future. Students allowed the question of entry
or no-entry to act as a self-imposed test of qualification ; and to this is to be attributed the large proportion of honours won to the number of candidates&mdash;at least in most of the classes. In future, I hope that more will face the examinations, in the conviction, that to an honest worker the next best thing to success in an examination is failure in one ; for assuredly there are few means so efficacious for finding out one&rsquo;s weak points.,Owing to the necessity of closing our session in June, the examinations were crowded into one week, and thus a very insufficient time was allotted to each subject ; scarcely enough to enable the candidates, most of whom were strange to the method, to bring out the knowledge they possessed.,these two circumstances, the comparatively small number of candidates and the shortness of the time given, were drawbacks to the success of the examination as an experiment ; still the results were generally very satisfactory to the teachers,&mdash;with the exception perhaps of the Mathematical Classes. Literature .,4,The Literature to Alexander Pope,3,professor Scott, the teacher of the Logic Class, reported that he did not remember having ever taught a class which gave him greater satisfaction. For myself had I in my class (Latin advanced) followed a frequent rule of awarding a certificate to all who gained more than one-half of the total marks, I should have signalized every candidate but one. I have no other reports by me, but several were not less satisfačtorv. On analyzing the results, it was discovered that rather more than one-half of the honours gamed were gained by men of the operative class, though these formed considerably less than one-half of the whole number of students. ^ S,I have read the interesting argument of Mr. Tansley in your last number on the question of Prizes or no Prizes?&rdquo; and I fully admit the elevationand Tunduess of the punuples he lays down as the true foundation of zealous study, but I cannot assent to the conclusions he draws from them against the expediency of prizes. In our case I
believe the Council adopted the prize system, not after any careful discussion of it, but merely because no sufficient reason presented itself for departing from the usual form. We claim, as do all our coadjutors in &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt;, for &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;men&lt;/span&gt; a vir&not; tual equality, qua students, with the members of other classes. We assume them to be open to the same influences, and subject to the same aspirations, if incidental and super&not; ficial obstacles can be cleared away ; but it seems to me, that to assume for them that they are to be wrought upon, hot only by all high and healthy motives, but by the highest motive only, besides exposing them to an unfair test, betrays a suspicion that that first assumption is ill-grounded. I, for one, agree entirely with Mr. Tansley, that nothing could be worse in principle, or more mischievous in practice, than to offer money prizes, or prizes in any form in which they could be supposed to measure or represent the advantage gained by the successful candidate. But it was because the possibility never occurred to us of such an interpretation being given to our book prizes that we did not hesitate to adopt them.,There are two approved methods of classifying candidates in an examination. One, in which the certificates are ranged in groups or classes, all who receive certificates o&iacute; the first class being considered as of equal merit, and in like manner those who receive certificates of the second class, and so on ; the other, in which each separate certificate is numbered according to the candidate&rsquo;s place in the list ; and of this latter plan prizes are the natural complement. Of these methods we chose the second, partly, I think, be&not; cause of the difficulty of securing that the relative value of first-class and second-class certificates should be the same in the several classes.,The theory of prizes 1 suppose to be this, that, while on examination certificates of honour are awarded to all who pass beyond a certain minimum of marks, prizes, either books or medals, are given to the one or two who reach a conspicuous degree of excellence, not as a reward for passing him immediately below (who may be still higher above the next), but to signalize the student who, by outstripping all the rest, shows to what point all might have reached. The use of the prize as matter of discipline is this, that the broad distinction beyond the foremost and all the rest acts as a sparto all competitors to aim high. If certificates are given to all who pass beyond a certain standard, the dif&not; ference between first, second, and third will not be striking enough to tempt all to great efforts. If certificates were given to the first only, in that case they would, I doubt not, like the olive wreath at the Olympic games, prove sufficient to call out the greatest emu&not; lation.,A medal is a very good form of prize, in some respects perhaps the best ; but a book, besides being much cheaper, is even less in danger of being confounded with a bonus or extra-work. Again, if the book is, as it should be, a capital work on the subject of ex&not; amination, it will be of positive service educationally to the winner, who will be surely tempted to use it well. I well remember, through more than twenty years, receiving Forcellini&rsquo;s Latin Lexicon as a Latin prize, and few books have been used by me in my maturer studies (it lies on ray table as l write) oftener or with greater benefit. I doubt if I should have made equal use of those bulky quartos, at least in those days, had they come to me in any other way.,The prize-book again, like the medal, with its appropriate binding and stamp, will sometimes, with no breach of modesty on the owner s part (a certificate must be shown, the prize will show itself), remind relations or comrades of his well-proved merit, and keep alive in himself a spirit of self-respect and of honourable ambition.,I might suggest some reasons which add special weight to the above considerations in the case of our &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Working&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Men&rsquo;s&lt;/span&gt; &lt;span class=&quot;hitHighlite&quot;&gt;Colleges&lt;/span&gt;. At all events, if we refuse them any positive weight, they have a negative value discountenancing a departure from the ordinary cus&not; tom 0 The members of these colleges are not living, as those of ordinary colleges are, in an atmosphere of eager emulation, with the excitement of traditional feats of prowess to imitate, and they will seldom possess books of their own in any great number. Fur&not; thermore, the distinctions they win will not, for long, command extensive lecognition. Those who know, as we know, amidst what difficulties and discouragements many of our students courageously work on, will feel that in these instances he who evinces the high&not; est merit performs areal service to others, and that the &ldquo; prize of worthiest worth should be, not less, but more conspicuous than elsewhere. The indifferent observer may indulge in the sneer &ldquo;in tenui labor,&rdquo; but even he would be compelled to admit &ldquo;at tenuis non gloria.&rdquo; I hope that the time is not far distant when our Colleges by a closer union
among themselves will secure a wide recognition for the merit of their more successful members ; and already I see with great satisfaction that one of your own students has taken the degree of B.A. in the University of London. Meanwhile, I contend for the wisdom of retaining all lawful and usual incentives to a generous emulation within the circle of our own classes. Mr. Tansley&rsquo;s argument, if pushed to its legitimate conse&not; quences, would, I think, go the length of discountenancing emulation in every shape, even that of graduated certificates ; and I doubt if he is prepared for this. I believe, Mr. Editor, that we shall not exorcise ambition of its evil spirit by turning our backs upou it, but rather of what it has that is noble. I do not forget that Quintilian fears that am&not; bition itself is a vice, while he quaintly asserts that it is the parent of many virtues ; nor that it was from Tacitus that Milton derived his judgment that Fame was the &ldquo; last in&not; firmity of noble mind. But I cannot fear that I shall be charged with advocating a lower morality than theirs. Such a judgment was natural enough in men who lived at Rome under Nero and Domitian, when ambition could end in nothing but the aggran&not; disement of the individual ; it is little suited for England in either the seventeenth or the nineteenth century. Is it not better, because truer, to admit with Milton himself that Fame, though in itself neither a virtue nor a vice, is yet,&ldquo; The spur that the clear spirit doth raise,,To scorn delights and live laborious days&rdquo; ?,And then to trust to other principles, still more deeply rooted in our nature, to show that the true plant &ldquo; grows not on mortal soil,&rdquo;,N or in broad rumour lies ;,But lives and spreads aloft by those pure eyes And perfect witness&rdquo;,Of Him who judges all !,It has been from the beginning stated to be an integral portion of our scheme to place on the Council some of the most distinguished students, imitating in this the essentially democratic character of the old Universities. The prospect of being able soon to carry this into effect gave additional interest to our examinations ; at the same time it supplies us with a further reason for aiming at the most perfect system of examining. We are at this moment engaged in assisting our students to form a Committee of their own number for communication with the Council. Still we believe that only by placing on the Coun&not; cil a number of the most highly qualified and experienced students can we secure for our College a vigorous and continuous life.* We shall therefore be glad, Mr. Editor to hear some account of your system of creating Associates, and of the qualifications for&rsquo;electiou to your Council.,I am, dear Sir, yours sincerely,,J.
